->**JESUS** <term>IHS: A monogram for the name of Jesus, known as a Christogram. It is derived from the first three letters of "Jesus" in Greek (iota-eta-sigma). For the Society of Jesus (the Jesuits), this symbol represents their order and is traditionally depicted with a cross rising from the "H" and three nails below it, all surrounded by a sunburst.</term><-

# Giordano Bruno of Nola
# ON THE TRIPLE MINIMUM AND MEASURE
### ON THE PRINCIPLES OF THREE SPECULATIVE

*To the most illustrious and most reverend prince* HENRY JULIUS, *Duke of Brunswick and Lüneburg, Bishop of Halberstadt.*

<note>The "Triple Minimum" mentioned in the title refers to Bruno's philosophical theory of "minima": the physical atom (the smallest part of matter), the mathematical point (the smallest part of a line), and the metaphysical monad (the fundamental unit of existence).</note>

## JOHANNES WECHEL SENDS GREETINGS. <note>original: "S." (Salutem dicit), a standard Latin greeting meaning "wishes health" or "sends greetings."</note>

**E**ven pagan antiquity recognized how foul the crime of ingratitude is, most illustrious and reverend Prince. For this reason, even <term>GIORDANO BRUNO</term> <note>The author, an Italian philosopher and former friar known for his theories on the infinite universe, who was eventually executed for heresy.</note>, when he considered how generously and honorably he had been treated by your Reverend Highness <note>original: "R. C. tua" (Reverenda Celsitudine)</note>, thought he must not fail to display some proof of his gratitude toward your Reverend Highness publicly before the eyes of all men. Therefore, among the other monuments of his industry—some of which were already finished and others only conceived in his mind—he judged <note>The original Latin "iudicavit" is split by the page break; "iu-" appears at the bottom as a catchword.</note> these books *On the Triple Minimum and Measure* to be most worthy of your illustrious Highness...

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of Giordano Bruno's work to Henry Julius, Duke of Brunswick-Lüneburg. Written by the printer Johannes Wechel, it praises the Duke's patronage and introduces Bruno's gratitude.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Henry Julius, dedication, Johannes Wechel, Brunswick-Lüneburg, patronage, gratitude, 16th-century printing</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle from the printer Johannes Wechel to Duke Henry Julius. The previous page ended with the author, Giordano Bruno, deciding that his work was worthy of the Duke; this page explains the circumstances of the book's production and Bruno's sudden departure.</meta>

# DEDICATORY EPISTLE.

dedicated <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...judged [the books] most worthy to be dedicated to your illustrious Highness."</note> it to you. Having undertaken the work, so that he might complete it as accurately as possible, he not only engraved the diagrams <note>original: "schemata"</note> himself with his own hand, but also acted as a proofreader <term>corrector of the press</term> <note>In the 16th century, "correctors" were scholars or the authors themselves who checked the printed sheets for errors before the final run.</note> for the work itself. Finally, when only the very last leaf of the work remained, he was snatched away from us by a sudden turn of events <note>This refers to Bruno's departure from Frankfurt in 1591 to return to Italy, where he was eventually arrested by the Inquisition.</note> and was unable to put the finishing touch <note>original: "extremam manum," a Latin idiom for the final polish or completion of a work.</note> to it as he had for the rest.

Therefore, he requested by letter that we should fulfill on his behalf and in his name what fortune did not allow him to do. Lest we should seem to have denied this duty to him in his absence, we dedicate, offer, and commend this book—which was intended for Your Reverend Highness <note>original: "R. C. T." (Reverendae Celsitudini Tuae)</note> by him and refined by our own labor. We humbly pray that you might kindly and graciously receive both the author’s gratitude and our own diligent efforts, and that you continue to support learning and the promoters of literature with the same favor you have already shown. That you may be able to do this happily for a long time, we pray from our heart to God, the Best and Greatest <note>original: "Deo opt. max." (Deo Optimo Maximo), a standard formula for God, meaning "To the Best and Greatest God."</note>, the source of all good things.

->Frankfurt, on the Ides of February, 1591.<- <note>The Ides of February is February 13th.</note>

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark in bottom right corner.</meta>

<summary>In this conclusion to the dedication, the printer Johannes Wechel explains that Giordano Bruno personally engraved the book's diagrams and corrected the proofs before a sudden departure forced the printer to finalize the dedication on his behalf. Wechel asks the Duke to continue his patronage of scholars and prays for his long life.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Johannes Wechel, Henry Julius, printing process, diagrams, proofreading, Frankfurt, 1591</keywords>

He challenges Aristotelian physics by arguing that matter cannot be infinitely divided and that death is merely a change in the arrangement of these eternal minimums.</summary>

## INDEX OF CHAPTERS.

15. In what order and by what names of parts the circle is divided (p. 128)

### IV. ON THE PRINCIPLES OF MEASUREMENT AND FIGURE.

1. *Progression from the monad <term>monad: the fundamental unit of reality and the "one" in Bruno's philosophy</term> to a few things, thence to very many, up to the innumerable and the immense* (p. 130)
2. *On the folding-in <note>original: "implicatione"</note> of all things, and the unfolding into all three types of figures* (p. 135)
3. *The construction of the Atrium of Apollo* <note>These "Atriums" refer to Bruno’s complex mnemonic and geometric systems, where different gods represent specific types of knowledge or forms.</note> (p. 137)
4. *The construction of the Atrium of Pallas* <note>Pallas Athena, representing wisdom and strategy.</note> (p. 139)
5. *The construction of the Atrium of Venus* (p. 142)
6. *That every figure, whether unfolded or folded, is found in these* (p. 143)
7. *Definitions* (p. 144)
8. *Axioms of the art in themselves, and of the terms with manifest meaning* (p. 148)
9. *Theorems to be demonstrated by an axiom or by the definition of a term* (p. 150)
10. *The demonstration of theorems* (p. 153)

### V. ON MEASUREMENT.

1. *The archetype of truth; I shall experience the shortest line, which is also a straight line* (p. 159)
2. *Of equality: From a given point or in whatever way, I receive a line equal to a given straight line* (p. 161)
3. *Temperance and modes; I cut away the remainder from the greater* (p. 162)
4. *Distincter, The Edge <note>original: "Acies"</note>; I comprehend length without width by sight* (p. 163)
5. *Division <note>original: "Schisma"</note>, Leveling: I bisect the length, and find the middle point within it* (p. 165)
6. *The Distributor: I divide a given line into many parts by a certain ratio* (p. 166)
7. *The Detractor: I take away the commanded part* (p. 169)
8. *Apposition; From a given point I draw a parallel above a given straight line, or I apply parallels as I please through a circle* (p. 171)
9. *The Ascending Vertex: We provide a perpendicular line upon a given point* (p. 173)

### SECTION II. ON THE ANGLE.

1. *Distinction of the angle* (p. 176)
2. *The Isosceles: I make an isosceles angle and triangle* (p. 177)
3. *The Erector: I establish a right angle at any given point* (p. 177)
4. *The Square <note>original: "Norma," referring to the mason's tool or the geometric concept of the right angle.</note>: A right angle upon the extremity of a given line* (p. 178)
5. *The Intercessor: I give a right angle upon a given point within the ends of a line* (p. 179)
6. *The Adactor: I shall raise a right angle at the point of contact of a circle and a tangent line* (p. 180)

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the main text of Giordano Bruno’s "On the Existence of the Minimum," following the detailed table of contents found on the previous pages. This work represents Bruno's mature philosophy where he attempts to reconcile mathematics, physics, and metaphysics through the concept of the "minimum" or the atom.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF GIORDANO BRU-
# NO OF NOLA, ON THE 
# EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM.
## BOOK.

### CHAPTER I.

->Preludes concerning the intention, efficient causes,<-
->means, and the method of proceeding.<-

*As the mind of nature, implanted in the subjects of things,* <note>original: "mens naturae." Bruno refers to the Universal Intellect, a divine force that organizes the material world from the inside out.</note>
*Brings them forth to be graded by its own moments, and,*
*Blending them in fit*
*Orders, distinguishes them deeply by numbers:*
*So the work of reason, so the vivid force of the sharp*
*Human sense, pre-conceives at the very*
*Gates the parts to which it may apply those*
*Measures by which it may weigh them; wandering far from the boundaries* <note>The "gates" represent the initial points of perception or the basic principles of understanding.</note>
<margin>10</margin>
*Of these gates, a vast crowd of sophists, nonetheless blind and bereft,* <note>Bruno frequently used the term "sophist" to describe the academic philosophers of his time who he believed were obsessed with logic games rather than the true nature of reality.</note>
*Has led minds further away than they could, through shortcuts*
*Or gentle marks, grasp better teachings,*
*And of their own accord turn toward the most pleasant sunrise of light:*
*From which arises whatever nourishing nature skillfully*
*Depicts with colors and thunders with voices.*

<summary>This page serves as the formal opening of the treatise, featuring an introductory poem in Latin hexameter that describes how the human mind mirrors the "mind of nature" by using measurement and number to understand reality.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Nola, Minimum, Atomism, Mind of Nature, Sophists, Philosophy of Mathematics, Renaissance Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page transitions into the poetic invocation of Bruno’s cosmological vision. While the previous page introduced the philosophical concept of the "Minimum" (the smallest unit of reality), this text begins his praise for the "Immense"—the infinite universe. The catchword "Illius" on the previous page would have led into the following discourse on the nature of light and the infinite.</meta>

# GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA.

*O you who kindle eternal flames in the mortal heart,*
*And have commanded my breast to rise with such great*
*Light and to glow with such great fire;*
*By which I—with the shadows driven back on every side,*
*And having mastered the sluggish weight of the delaying mass—*
*Shall traverse the immense world with senses lifted high.*
<margin>20</margin> *All-seeing light, light that makes all things seen,*
*Raising up minds, snatching the senses above the heavens:*
*You who, shaking off my lethargy, have granted me to be awake,*
*Whom sight generates, who rises to the sight, and by that*
<note>original: "visus." Bruno plays with the idea that the "Light" is both the source of vision and the object being seen.</note>
*Preserved, you live for us, and you preserve all*
*Living things, finishing the hardest tasks with a most gentle strike;*
*You reveal whatever Earth, Water, Upper Air, and the Abyss*
<note>original: "Tellus, Unda, Aethera, Abyssus"—the traditional four elements or regions of the cosmos.</note>
*Comprise. Therefore the common crowd might call you blind,*
*Lacking light and eyes, and the mindless might call you "without a mind."*
*There shall be no place, no lot, no time, and no age*
<margin>30</margin> *To move me from my station, which might prove me wrong, O you who*
*Suggested to these eyes a "whole" enclosed by no margin,*
<note>A central tenet of Bruno’s philosophy: the universe has no outer boundary or "edge."</note>
*And showed that the shining stars are true worlds.*
*I have known that the face of the Earth is nonetheless*
*Shining—a truly holy and venerable star.*
<note>Bruno was one of the first to argue that the Earth, seen from space, would shine like any other planet or star.</note>
*O how often has your wing-feather (O Divine one) been placed beneath me*
*As I was falling, and how often when I was pressed by the weight of cares.*
*Lest I should throw myself headlong, your (greatest) virtue*
*Held me back. Truly, you were present in the likeness of the starry*
*Sky, clothed in light, driving away the clouds of insane fury,*
<margin>40</margin> *Dispersing the turbulent phantasms with the spread*
*Feathers of your wings, which with a thousand colors*
*Make the world tranquil. Whence the Earth, adorned with a green*
*Back, meets the sun with radiant waves,*

<summary>Bruno invokes a divine, all-seeing light that allows his mind to transcend the physical body and perceive an infinite universe. He argues that the stars are independent worlds and that the Earth is itself a shining, holy star among many.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, infinite universe, plurality of worlds, celestial light, Earth as a star, Neoplatonism, cosmology</keywords>

## ON THE SMALLEST THINGS THAT EXIST, BOOK I.
->A 2<-

*Presenting her face to that one and to you, and to yours,*
*She turns herself entirely toward them, through every part.*
*Why then should I be found to have professed my secret loves*
*So timidly, when Earth, Sea, and Sky celebrate them together,*
*And mother Nature exalts them above all things? Rather,*
*Burn my weary breast (O my life), I shall pray;*
*Burn it more, and do not spare your quiver of arrows against me;*
<margin>50</margin>
*Let your thousand wounds make me into one single wound.*
*Truly, in this way I shall see myself turned into one single light,*
*And I shall be a single eye, seeing the whole, encompassing all things,*
*Beholding with a simple gaze the present, the past, and the future.*
*I shall look at once upon what is above, what is below, and all things around.*
*As for what remains: I now beseech you from the bottom of my heart*
*With prayers poured out from my center: Turn your mind,*
*Behold what Earth <note>original: "Tellus"</note>—amidst her better things—holds within her curved*
*Mass; what she compares to the great stars of heaven.*
<margin>60</margin>
*Behold the House of Julius <note>The "Julias" or "Iuliadem" refers to the lineage of Henry Julius, Duke of Brunswick-Wolfenbüttel (1564–1613), Bruno's patron.</note>, born from the high blood of Kings,*
*Whose martial <note>original: "Mauortia," a poetic reference to Mars, the god of war.</note> standards have imposed the yoke of empire*
*Upon the unconquered peoples of Europe; and have shaken the borders*
*Of burning Libya and Asia, and who once—*
*By Latin <note>original: "Latiâ"</note> valor—restored the trophies*
*Snatched from German men. This is Henry Julius,*
*Whom you know better than can be recorded*
*By my humble verses. You know him because the triple Grace,*
*The peaceful wit, the charms <note>original: "Veneres," literally "the Venuses" or beauties.</note>, and every honor*
*Acknowledge you alone as the giver. Through you, all these things*
<margin>70</margin>
*Are stored within a single breast. If you also behold sublime things*
*With your nourishing eyes, you know him, to whom the sister*
*Of the one holding the scepter of Denmark <note>original: "Dania"</note> is joined,*
*And whose wife is the excellent sister of that other nymph*
*Belonging to the great Hero-spouse, whom bright Britain <note>original: "Britannia"</note> [hails] as king.*
<note>This passage refers to the Duke's high-level political connections: Henry Julius married Elizabeth of Denmark, whose sister, Anne of Denmark, was the wife of King James VI of Scotland (later James I of England).</note>

He gazes up, he who as a relative also celebrates the marriage
While present, standing by at the sacred wedding of the high Duke.
I ask you: do not withdraw him, turned away, from our Muses,
While meanwhile he feeds the eyes of the divine Nymph
With his gaze, and remains unhindered by your charms <note>original: "vinclis tuis blandissime," referring to the "sweet chains" of attraction or inspiration.</note>
And conceives heroic acts in his depths,
<margin>80</margin> And wisely fulfills the duties of the illustrious scepter;
May he not leave the products of my talent neglected,
Nor despise them as if they were gifts of an abstruse Minerva <note>Minerva is the goddess of wisdom; "abstruse" here suggests knowledge that is hidden or difficult to grasp.</note>:
But just as sometimes, with lighter effort, he traverses 
The pathless woods (pressing the back of a foaming horse)
And delights in staining his spear with the blood of wild beasts;
Guiding the brass-footed <note>original: "Æripedem," a poetic term for a horse with tireless or bronze-hard hooves.</note> beast at the command of the spur, 
And skilled with the rod to direct his swift course 
According to the master's wishes; 
Whether he wishes to go to the circus, or if a brave spear 
Meets another brave spear, and it pleases him to contend with strong muscles,
<margin>90</margin> And other things of this sort (all of which he performs so gracefully, 
Vigorously, and truly with Jove-like <note>original: "Iouia," referring to Jupiter, the king of the gods; implying a kingly or divine excellence.</note> virtue);
So also, at certain hours, let him not neglect these studies,
Being of a sublime nature, and most worthy of them first of all;
So that they may be seen clearly once they are understood.
However much that man may excel in physical appearance,
Stand out in muscle, and consist of strong force,
And contend with wonderful, agile dexterity:
Yet these are such things for which he need not fear any single man,
And for which one man will be enough for any other man,
<margin>100</margin> Things which troublesome disease consumes, and age 
Wears away; they are not from us, and are hardly ours even while they exist.
But the gifts of the mind, by which one man stands above a thousand,
And by which once, with great virtue and with Mars <note>original: "Marte," the god of war; here referring to military success or conflict.</note> being favorable...

*They conquered the world and remain famous through the ages;*
*There are those whose glory, in this maturing age, moves*
*Like a great gathering, appearing as grand as anyone*
*Holding a scepter equal to or even greater than*
*The ancients; yet I believe such a mind belongs*
*To this man, that he would judge it unworthy to assign a limit*
<margin>110</margin> *Or any measure to high virtue. Nay, rather,*
*He pushes himself into the infinite, and works his way inward*
*(Rising more and more into the form of the Gods,*
*And those honors which this fated lot of ours can bear),*
*He deems that no single life could ever be enough.*
*Therefore, I bring back things discovered by no small labor,*
*Which you will find described in the disks of the sun* <note>original: "solis disco." Bruno often used circular diagrams—"disks"—to represent his cosmological and geometric theories.</note>; *let neither*
*The mindless trickery of sophists* <term>sophists: scholars who use clever but fallacious arguments to prioritize winning a debate over finding the truth</term>, *nor the zeal of priests*

*Of a great prince, once they have been thoroughly examined.*

*Discord casts the vast elements together*
*Into a single mass.* <note>A reference to the end of a cosmic cycle or the "Great Year," when the universe returns to a state of undifferentiated chaos.</note> *But you, O ILLUSTRIOUS DUKE,*
*Kindle for yourself that light of sublime intellect*

*They can be believed most worthy of your distinguished Genius.*

<margin>130</margin> *Worthy to approach, however proud he may be of his titles,*

*“less Latinly” said, so as to disparage these high matters* <note>Bruno is defending his "philosophical Latin," which often ignored the strict, elegant rules of classical humanism in favor of technical precision.</note>

He claims the authority to revive ancient wisdom and even invent new terminology, placing the philosopher’s insight above the rigid rules of traditional scholars.</meta>

*...having been described. But let him consider these to be words and voices of the Gods,*
*adapted to these meters, fitting these very meanings—*
*however unwillingly—since our thoughts will give their own weight*
*to our words once malice, having been suppressed,*
*has yielded; and new arts will begin to be understood,*
*what they hide, and what they reveal. Then, by the power of merits,*
<margin>140</margin> *many things will be reborn which have already fallen, and those words*
*which were once held in honor shall fall; for such is the decree of usage.* <note>Bruno is paraphrasing Horace’s *Ars Poetica*: "Many words shall be reborn which now have fallen, and many shall fall which are now in honor, if usage so wills it."</note>
*We ourselves shall be the source of usage and principle, when we dig out*
*from the depths of darkness the dogmas of the ancient wise men,*
*which were once most clear in their ancient words. For the sake of novelty*
*(if there be need) in these matters, from whatever source they may be*
*most conveniently drawn, we shall even be the authors of new words.*
*Let grammarians deal with words; but let words serve us.*
*Let them observe the usage which we ordain for them.*
*Nor should that foolish and impudent race, with their violated minds,*
<margin>150</margin> *confound the species or disturb the whole genus, lest everywhere*
*the arts be dragged back onto the cobbler’s bench of the grammarian.* <note>original: "crepidam grammaticalem." A "crepida" is a Greek-style sandal; Bruno is mockingly suggesting that grammarians try to fit the vast "arts" of philosophy into the narrow "shoe" of their linguistic rules.</note>
*For you, O Muses, from whatever direction you come*
*as companions, Wisdom—that high lineage—will receive you as welcome guests,*
*even if the words of the Cumaean Sibyl* <term>Cumaean Sibyl: A priestess of Apollo whose prophetic utterances were famously cryptic and written on leaves.</term>
*or the funeral pyres of Evander* <note>Evander was a legendary hero who brought Greek culture and the alphabet to Italy before the founding of Rome. Bruno is referencing the most ancient, archaic roots of language.</note> *are brought back from the scrolls: all are approved.*
*Let those who compose verses to produce no real fruit* <note>original: "ad nullas fruges." Scholars who write technically perfect but intellectually empty poetry.</note>
*display no refined specimen, simply because Ovid and Virgil*
*yield fragments of patchworks* <term>patchworks: (Latin: "centones") poems made up of various lines or phrases taken from other authors.</term> *everywhere. I shall see*
*the struggle of panting Hercules, expressed in living forms,*
<margin>160</margin> *as he presses Antaeus* <note>In Greek mythology, the giant Antaeus was invincible as long as he touched the Earth; Hercules defeated him by lifting him into the air. Bruno uses this to represent the triumph of his "higher" philosophy over grounded, materialistic thought.</note> *with redoubled strength.*
*I shall see the fall of the world, when Phaethon handled*
*his father’s chariot.* <note>Phaethon attempted to drive the chariot of the Sun but lost control, nearly destroying the earth. Bruno often uses this myth to describe the danger and glory of seeking infinite knowledge.</note> *Just like the burning palace of heaven*
*to be beheld. Just as the sea surges toward Olympus with a smoking whirlwind.*

*He paints it. Just as Atlas can scarcely support the glowing sky upon his shoulders:*

<note>In this poem, Bruno argues that modern greatness is essentially the same as ancient greatness, merely wearing different historical "clothes." He justifies his use of classical imagery to describe contemporary figures.</note>

**M**ind above all things is God. Mind implanted in all things is Na-
ture. Mind pervading all things is Reason. God dictates and or-
dains. Nature executes and creates. Reason contemplates and dis-
courses. God is the <term>Monad</term> <note>original: "Monas." In Bruno's philosophy, the Monad is the ultimate, indivisible unit of reality—both the mathematical "one" and the divine source of all existence.</note>, the source of all numbers, the simplicity
of all magnitude and the substance of composition, and an excellence 
above every moment—innumerable and immense. Nature is 
number that can be numbered, magnitude that can be measured, a moment that is 
attainable. Reason is the numbering number, the measuring magnitude, the 
estimating moment.

¶ 8 God flows through nature into reason. Reason is lifted up through 
nature to God. God is Love, the Efficient Cause, Brightness, and Light. Na-
ture is the beloved, the object, Fire, and Heat. Reason is the Lover, 
a certain Subject which is kindled by Nature and illuminated by 
God.

¶ 49 Sense is an eye in a prison of darkness, looking out at the colors 
and surfaces of things as if through bars and holes. Rea-
son is as if looking through a window, deriving light from the sun and 
reflecting it back to the sun, just as it is observed in the body of the moon. In-
tellect, in the open and as if from a high watchtower, is an eye everywhere above 
all particularity, crowd, and confusion; and in the 
distinction of species, it contemplates the very sun shining forth in the universe.

¶ 76 Reason would easily lift itself into the mind were it not 
distracted by tossing in an ocean of various passions: for nature is 
arranged so that it is equipped and dispatched by the different faculties of the soul 
toward as many works and effects through various operations and acts. 
For man is a Republic <note>original: "Respublica homo." Bruno views the human being as a "microcosm" or a "little state" composed of many competing parts that must be governed.</note> composed of great and wonderful variety: 
in which there are so many arts, statuses, conditions, ranks, orders, 
workshops, instruments, masteries, and ministries; and therefore he is only 
finally able to gather his whole self into one single object; 
and that the most worthy. This is achieved whenever the intellectual will...

<sig>A 4</sig> <note>This is a "signature mark," used by early printers to keep the folded sheets of a book in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Bruno defines the hierarchy of existence: God (the source), Nature (the medium), and Reason (the observer). He describes the human struggle to move from limited sensory perception to the "sunlight" of pure intellectual understanding.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory aphorisms from the previous section, where Bruno established the roles of God, Nature, and Reason. He now transitions into the ethical and methodological requirements for a true philosopher before beginning his formal investigation into the "Minimum."</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

<note>The original printed text has a large blank space here, intended for a decorative initial "V" to be added by a rubricator or illuminator.</note> The **W**ill, the governor of all substance, is joined to the highest principle. From this follows the perfection and nobility of the whole, just as when the body of a city concurs with the will of its prince according to the specific nature of its parts; even if it aims at nothing higher or external, it is understood to be in the favor of that prince, in whose love and favor the prince himself rejoices.

¶ 98 Regarding a specific kind of happiness, we ought not to be more wise than is fitting; but regarding happiness in itself (since knowing belongs to the category of "the good"), no more a limit should be prescribed for the act of knowing than has been prescribed by Nature and God for its power—for God willed that no power should exist in vain.

¶ 122 He who desires to philosophize must begin by doubting all things. He should not define one side of a contradiction before he has heard those who argue the point. Having thoroughly examined and compared their reasons, he should judge and define not based on hearsay, reputation, the number of supporters, the age of the idea, or titles and ornaments; rather, he must judge by the strength of a doctrine that is consistent with itself and with reality, and by the light of reason and plain truth.

¶ 128 Voices and words do not serve wisdom and goodness any more than they serve ignorance and iniquity. Truth and teaching rejoice in the simplicity of these words; whereas laziness, cunning, and vanity—with a kind of mercenary anxiety—grow insolent through the ornamentation and variety of language.

¶ 152 Let the Rhetorician be hissed out of the forum if he lacks eloquence and persuasion. Likewise, let anyone who, having abandoned the proper use of words, makes a display of "emasculated" <note>Bruno uses "emasculated" (original: *emasculata*) to describe speech that is weak, overly flowery, or lacking the "manly" vigor of direct truth.</note> speech in the halls of our divine lady <note>Philosophy</note>, be exiled. Let him be cast out not merely as an ignorant man, but as one utterly unfit for understanding. And if anyone attempts to exclude simplicity and the proper use of words from this non-popular <note>Bruno distinguishes his deep philosophy from "popular" or common philosophy which relies on easy rhetoric.</note> genre of philosophy, let him be held more guilty of parricide and sacrilege than if he had profaned the sacred images of the gods. For Saturn, Mars, Neptune, Ocean, and Hercules, for the sake of their true majesty, wished to be depicted naked—with only the simplest weapon (to distinguish their deity)—rather than being dishonored by fine linen, purple cloth, rings, earrings, and the fringes of curled hair.

## That the Minimum is the substance of numbers, of
## magnitudes, and of all elements
## whatsoever.

**B**ut let it suffice for me to seek the light in proportion to the weight of things,
and to approach the temple built of solid adamant...

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM, BOOK I</header>

# ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM, BOOK I

<note>This section is written in Latin hexameter, a style of epic poetry. Bruno uses verse to elevate his philosophical arguments to the level of a cosmic epic.</note>

*To raise up through the ages one who has professed such better things,*
*And from whatever vein God has ordered to arise;*
*Now I shall begin to sing. The MINIMUM is the substance—*
*And you will find that very same thing, in the end, to be great above all else.*
*From this comes the monad <term>monas; the fundamental unit of being, representing both the number one and the metaphysical unity of God</term>, from this the atom, and from this the Spirit*
*poured out everywhere, consisting in no bulk or mass, but*
*constituting all things by its own signs; it is the whole essence,*
*if you look closely at things, this is what it is at last, and the matter itself.*
<margin>10</margin>
*Since the minimum integrates all things in such a way that*
*if it is not laid as a foundation, not a shred of anything else will exist.*
*Let there be no monad, and there will be no numbers:*
*For it constitutes the species, establishing every genus.*
*Therefore, in all things, it is the first foundation, from which*
*God, and Nature the mother, and Art <note>original: "ars"; here meaning human skill or science that imitates nature</note> highly unfold*
*that which persists above every genus, and that which is in every genus.*
*Therefore it is celebrated as constant in all things, and upon this foundation*
*it encloses the finite, and permeates the wide infinite,*
*effecting, binding, integrating, and propagating*
<margin>20</margin>
*whatever is composite, and whatever simple thing is created,*
*hanging from an immense age: because all the greatest things*
*are from the minimum, in the minimum, toward the minimum, and through the minimum.*
*This, Nature the mother, and the handmaid-order of Art,*
*set down when composing, and into this they resolve when observing:*

->*Just as the letter and the accent—whose final part is the point—*<-
->*store infinite words in a small number;*<-

*The point is given first; wherefore the whole substance is here in those letters.*
*Not otherwise is the first simple substance of things,*
<margin>30</margin>
*the smallest body for all bodies, which all things eventually*
*reach when dissolved. Nothing disagrees in this one point.*

->*If you look at the appearance, all things are ultimately that very thing,*<-

<summary>In this opening verse, Bruno argues that the "Minimum" (the smallest possible unit, such as the atom or monad) is the essential building block of the entire universe, asserting that all complex things are ultimately composed of and return to this single, simple substance.</summary>
<keywords>Minimum, Monad, Atom, Metaphysics, Nature as Mother, Unity of Substance, Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic and philosophical exposition of the "Minimum" from page 9. Bruno moves from the general praise of this fundamental unit to a specific definition of how the Minimum (as the Monad and the Atom) functions as the immortal substance behind all physical and mathematical reality.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

> *Just as the monad <term>monas; the unit "one," which Bruno views as the metaphysical source of all numbers and existence</term> is every number; for through its whole body*
> *it shines forth, and every succeeding number is believed to come from it.*
> *So the substance of things varies in nothing from this; indeed,*
> *it is utterly immortal, which no power creates,*
> *nor destroys, harms, diminishes, or increases:*
> *Truly, all things created are born from this and are dissolved back into it;*
> *from this, things that grow are increased, and having been thinned out,*
> *they fail back into this, revisiting their own limits and their origin.*

**T**he Minimum is the substance of things insofar as it is distinguished from the category of quantity, but in terms of bodily magnitudes, it is the principle of quantity. It is, I say, the matter or element, the efficient cause, the end, and the whole; it is the point in magnitudes of one and two dimensions. The atom is understood in a "privative" sense <note>meaning the smallest part of a body that cannot be divided further</note> in bodies which are primary parts. The atom is understood in a "negative" sense <note>meaning something that is indivisible because the whole of it is present in every part, like a sound or a soul</note> in those things which are "whole in the whole" and in every individual part, such as in a voice, the soul, and things of that kind. The monad is understood "rationally" in numbers, and "essentially" in all things. Hence, the **Maximum** is nothing other than the **Minimum**. Take away the minimum from every side, and there will be nothing anywhere. Take away the monad from every side, and there will be no number anywhere, nothing countable, and no one to count. Hence, the "Best and Greatest" <note>original: "optimus maximus"; a traditional title for God</note>—the substance of substances and the very being by which things exist—is celebrated by the name of the **Monad**.

¶ 22 The subject and object of Nature and Art <note>meaning human skill or science</note>—composition and resolution in both action and contemplation—arises from the minimum, consists in the minimum, and is reduced back to the minimum. ¶ 26 Nor is it necessary for there to be many kinds and shapes of minimums, just as there need not be many kinds of letters for innumerable species of words to be composed from them. Although for Democritus and Leucippus <note>ancient Greek philosophers who first proposed that the world is made of atoms</note> they consist of only one type of shape, the diversity of forms necessarily follows from spherical atoms due to the differences in the void and the solid, and their various positions and orders. For us, however, a simple "vacuum" <note>empty space</note> along with atoms is not enough; for there must be a certain matter by which they are glued together. But perhaps those thinkers also took "vacuum" to mean "air," though we do not believe that to be the case.

¶ 31 Number is an "accident" <note>a non-essential quality or property</note> of the monad, and the monad is the essence of number; likewise, composition is an accident of the atom, and the atom is the essence of the composite. The monad is the principle of number along with the number itself, just as the atom is the principle of magnitude along with the magnitude itself. These are, in a reductive or primary sense, in the category of quantity and are accidents of substance—which is the "antecedent monad," truly and in itself the minimum principle of magnitude, *within* which all things exist, rather than *out of* which they are made. And in this monad, all things are one, just as in the truth of the atom all atoms are one according to their species, even if they are many according to their number. Therefore, to one looking at bodies, the substance of all things is the smallest body, or the atom; but to one looking at the line and the plane, the minimum is the point.

<meta>Catchword: CA- (likely beginning "Caput" or "Chapter")</meta>

<summary>Bruno defines the "Minimum" as the immortal, indivisible substance of the universe, identifying it as the Monad in mathematics and the Atom in physics. He argues that the greatest things (the Maximum) are ultimately composed of and identical to these smallest units (the Minimum).</summary>
<keywords>Monad, Atom, Minimum, Maximum, Democritus, Leucippus, substance, indivisibility, geometry, metaphysics</keywords>

Having established that the Minimum is both the smallest point and the source of the whole, Bruno now applies this to the human condition, arguing that because our true essence is this indivisible "atom" or soul, physical death is merely a change in the outward flow of matter, not an end to existence.</meta>

### CHAPTER III.

From what has just been said, it is concluded that death does not pertain to the substance of the body, and much less to the soul.

---

> **I** *Now, foolish one, fear the threats of death and fate.*
> (*They say the sacred words of the Samian parent* <note>original: "Samii... parentis"; a reference to Pythagoras of Samos, who taught the immortality and transmigration of the soul</note> *go unheard*)
> *Go, tremble at the voices of fools, and let the dreams of the common crowd*
> *strike a fatal terror into you, as if you were truly*
> *nothing more than a composite thing consisting of these physical parts.*
> *Does not the very mass of your body flow rapidly through changing time,*
> *coming to you continually altered by parts newly*
> *acquired from without, while the first parts pass away?*
> *Is the matter of your body now the same,*
> <margin>10</margin> *in its parts or as a whole, as it was just a little while ago?*
> *Did the same blood, flesh, and bone of the boy persist*
> *into his youth? Are not all things different and foreign*
> *to the grown man? Do not the limbs flow, and being renewed, cast off*
> *their consumed appearance (just as the growth of nails and hair*
> *is plain to the senses), while a single nature continues*
> *at the center, in the middle of the heart;* <note>Bruno identifies the "center" or "monad" of the person as being located in the heart, acting as the unchanging governor of the shifting physical body</note>
> *to which you refer all your governance, and in which you are one and the same,*
> *no matter how many countless crowds of people and*
> *arising accidents are constantly present around you with varying effect?*
> <margin>20</margin> *Know that that center is you yourself; it holds the middle,*
> *the end, and all the parts, without having any parts itself;*
> *its substance is a body either minimal, or no body at all,* <note>Here Bruno describes the soul/mind as a "minimum," an indivisible point of reality that acts as the anchor for the physical form</note>
> *which no power of nature shall tear apart,*
> *which lightning bolts do not reach: an atom that the point*
> *of a flame cannot violate, just like the first-principles by which [all things exist]*

<summary>Bruno uses poetic verse to argue that the human body is in a constant state of flux, meaning our true identity and "substance" must reside in an indivisible, immortal center (the soul or atom) that survives physical change and death.</summary>

*The body is a compacted thing; only the order, place,*
*and function of its parts are ever changed, while the simple*
*substance of things remains entirely unmoved.*
*Furthermore, no composite thing is true substance;*
<margin>30</margin> *but rather that which you compose—the ultimate part of composites*
*around which you build yourself. Thus you will see*
*that you are in no way inferior to the underlying body,*
*(which is the first thing, falls back into nothing prior, and is perennial,*
*integrating things now here, now there, just as now your limbs—*
*which you move—serve you in a certain and ordered sequence.*
*For (as we have shown at length in our Physics* <note>original: "Physicis"; likely referring to his work *De Physico auditu* or his general lectures on natural philosophy</note>*) from here comes*
*the origin of life, and the ripening of our physical mass.*
*Just as the expansion of the center unfolds itself into a great circle,*
*the architect-spirit,* <note>original: "spiritus architectus"; the soul viewed as the master builder that organizes matter according to a divine or natural blueprint</note> *having gathered atoms from all sides,*
<margin>40</margin> *governs itself entirely by being infused, right up until*
*the time when, the numbers being fulfilled, or the thread*
*of the body broken, it withdraws itself into the center, and from there*
*it introduces itself anew through the wide world; and this we*
*are accustomed to call death; because we proceed into an unknown light; and although*
*it is granted to few to perceive how this living of ours*
*is a perishing; and that to die in this way is sometimes to rise to true life:*
*for not all climb out of this body,*
*for they also fall into the enclosures of a greater depth,*
*driven by their own weight, lacking the divine flame.* <note>Bruno suggests that souls lacking "divine flame" or intellectual light may sink into lower forms of existence rather than ascending.</note>
<margin>50</margin> *Therefore the mind, by clustering bodies around the minimal atoms,* <note>original: "minima... tomorum corpora"; refers to the indivisible points or atoms that form the basis of his cosmology</note>
*wraps itself as if in a blind order within them,*
*shaping limbs for itself as if in a fatal prison,*
*so that soon this whole body may diffuse itself into everything;*
*and again from the whole and the all, the spirit, having been lulled to sleep,*
*migrates back, stirred from the expanded thread into the heart:*

He argues that 'death' is merely the spirit withdrawing into its center before re-emerging into the world, suggesting that physical life is a kind of perishing while death can be a transition to true life.</summary>

<header>ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM</header>

> ...from whose center it finally pours itself into the breezes,
> and awaits new chances, uncertain in its outcomes;
> And let the dark order of Nemesis <note>original: "Rhamnusidis"; an epithet for the goddess Nemesis, referring to her sanctuary at Rhamnus. She represents inescapable destiny or retribution.</note> unfold itself.

---

<margin>1 & 50.</margin>
**S**ince the substance of things is not at all dissolvable, Pythagoras felt that death is not to be feared, but rather a transition to be expected. Indeed, dissolution itself is merely something that happens to a composite thing. No composite thing is a true substance, but is something added from the outside. Otherwise, we would change our substance at every single moment, since the flow of matter out of and into our bodies is continuous. Therefore, we are what we are solely through the indivisible substance of the soul, around which—as if around a center that is everywhere—the entire clustering and gathering of atoms <note>original: "atomorum"; Bruno uses atoms to describe the smallest building blocks of matter that the soul organizes.</note> takes place. From this, through birth and youth, the architect-spirit <note>original: "spiritus architector"; the soul acting as the master builder of the physical body.</note> expands into this mass in which we consist, diffusing from the heart. Finally, folding up the threads of that "web" into the heart once more, the spirit withdraws, so that it may eventually recede and depart by the same path through which it had proceeded, and by the same gate through which it had entered. Birth, therefore, is the expansion of the center; Life is the consistency of the sphere; Death is the contraction back into the center.

<margin>¶ 29.</margin>
There is also a most powerful argument for our immortality from this principle: that the substance which builds, clusters, and unbinds; which orders, gives life, moves, and weaves; and which, like a miraculous craftsman, is set in charge of such a great work, should by no means be of a lesser condition than the bodies that are clustered, unbound, ordered, moved, and taken up for its use. These bodies possess a substance that is truly eternal, yet <note>Aristotle and Galen</note> most stupidly defined the soul as a mere "accident" <note>a non-essential property</note>, or as an entelechy <note>original: "entelechia"; the realization of a potentiality</note>, an energy, a harmony, or a temperament.

<margin>¶ 43.</margin>
According to the condition in which the soul conducted itself in one body, it is prepared to inhabit another, as said by Pythagoras, the Sadducees <note>original: "Saduchimi"; Bruno's idiosyncratic reference here likely links certain Jewish philosophical traditions with the transmigration of souls.</note>, Origen, and many others from the school of Plato. Therefore, that change of seats is not something accidental or random, like the movement of the parts from which the physical mass is joined together. For this reason, some souls pass through human bodies, others are taken up into the bodies of heroes, while others are cast down into worse conditions. These are the views of those who understand that all things, except for the One, are driven by a certain eternal revolution.

### That all things in some way go in circles and imitate the circle.

*From this, observe how forces everywhere and all bodies*
*offer the pattern of a sphere—the sphere of the cycle, and that of the center.*
*For a cycle is nothing but a visible center,*
*and a boundless globe is nothing but a center everywhere.*
*Therefore, here is the center in its simplest form, and the <term>minimum</term> <note>original: "minimum"; the smallest possible, indivisible unit of reality, serving as the building block of both matter and spirit.</note> through all things,*
*pouring itself through the whole: true, one, always, in everything.*
*And every composite thing will be recalled into the minimum so that it may exist:*
*since the minimum of nature encloses the powers of any large*
*sensible thing, and unfolds them from on high:*
<margin>10</margin> *For the underlying minimum excels in strength more wonderfully*
*than any mass that has grown into a large bulk by a gathering of parts.*
*Truly, the power that enlarges the underlying body,*
*strengthening it, and binding its parts into one—*
*preserving itself and all these things around itself—*
*is the minimum, or indeed something consisting of no mass at all.*
*Finally, in its simplicity, the one <term>monad</term> <note>original: "monas"; Bruno’s term for the ultimate, indivisible unit of existence. It is both a physical atom and a spiritual soul-unit.</note> of monads has been found,*
*which embraces the many and the great, and moves to integrate*
*everything within itself. Giving being to all entities, it is also God,*
*existing as the whole, the infinite, the true, the all, the good, the one.*
<margin>20</margin> *Those things which consist of the composite, the separate, and the sensible,*
*and which dimension fuses together in a scattered way,*
*are merely one thing after another, up to the body of minimums*
*which never allow themselves to occupy the same space,*
*here where matter fluctuates with outspread wings;*
*In the True, the One, the Being, the Eternal, the Simple, the Same,*
*they do not establish a number <note>i.e., they are not divided into multiple parts or quantities.</note>: Simple, Being, One, and the Same.*
*Hence opposites create a name proper to it:*
*The Beginning, the End, the Middle, the Extreme, Nothing, and Everything.*
*To which the species of things are likened by certain degrees.*
<margin>30</margin> *From which the same is long, wide, and deep in the immense;*

### ON THE FREE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM

And the center is one in the whole, the whole itself, and throughout everything:
For this point is everywhere, without end, and only so great,
And therefore <term>Xenophanes’</term> <note>original: "Xenophani"; Xenophanes of Colophon (c. 570–475 BC) was a pre-Socratic philosopher who described the Divine as a single, motionless, and spherical "One." Bruno adopts this image to describe his infinite universe.</note> sphere was equal in all directions.
And even a finite globe, though it is not so great everywhere,
is nevertheless a single thing measured from its middle in three dimensions:
Forward and upward, backward and downward,
and right and left. If you observe the circuit of the all-parent 
Earth, where it prepares itself for the daily light,
any point is both Sunset and Sunrise,
<margin>40</margin>And the hinge of midnight, and the hinge of midday,
or any other distinction you might name.
Furthermore, opposites meet in a single point of view
where they do not remain separate by any depth,
but are connected in a cycle and a globe, and in a concave form,
which scarcely any intention or action could divide;
substantially, they cannot exist or be perceived separately 
by any logic. Is not, moreover, one 
and the same point, when a line rises from a single plane, 
simultaneously both the obtuse and the acute 
<margin>50</margin>angle of the unfolding line—perhaps not separated by any chance, 
since that point certainly stands beneath both of them, 
and they vanish into it and arise from it at the same time?
What difference is there between the concepts of a <term>minimum arc</term> <note>original: "arcus minimi"; in Bruno's geometry, as a curve becomes infinitely small, it becomes indistinguishable from a straight line.</note> and a <term>chord</term> <note>original: "chorda"; a straight line segment whose endpoints both lie on a circular arc.</note>,
if they know no distinction, neither this greatest nor that?
How does a minimum circle differ from its own diameter?
How far apart are the greatest straight line, the arc, and the entire 
circle, since the larger the arc is, the more it shares the nature of a straight line?
Therefore, that which unfolds itself into an infinite mass, and 
<margin>60</margin>that which is contracted into a minimum, cannot vary in their essential form.
The center (I say), the cycle, the chord, the area, the diameter, the arc—

<meta>catchword: Etra-</meta>

<summary>Bruno explores the "coincidence of opposites," arguing that at the scale of the infinitesimal minimum, geometric distinctions like straight versus curved and temporal distinctions like sunrise versus sunset disappear into a single unity. He uses the spherical philosophy of Xenophanes to illustrate how the infinite and the minimum share the same essential nature.</summary>

*And the rays come forth with no distinction before us,*
*Whether all things are conceived as the smallest or the largest.*
   *Conceive of something infinitely sluggish; that which is slow is at rest.*
*But it is also necessary that what is extremely swift,*
*Moving in a flight without end, should also remain at rest.*
*A sphere spurred by the highest force*
*Will be moved in a single point of time, and as soon as it is moved by one point,*
*The beginning and the end of the motion are simultaneous, as is every point of contact.*
<margin>70</margin> *For if it turns itself in a circle, it moves and stands still*
*In equal measure; for it goes and returns simultaneously, and having returned, it is at rest.*
*That which is simply slow and that which remains forever are the same;*
*Going and returning at once—that is both motion and rest.*
*Therefore, rest and motion are most fully present together in that*
*One, which is moved the least and the most at the same time,*
*Stepping from end to end, yet utterly immobile,*
*And it remains simultaneously whole in all things and above them.*
*Thus there is nothing besides the <term>monad</term> <note>original: "monaden"; from the Greek *monas*, meaning unity or a single point. For Bruno, the monad is the fundamental, indivisible unit of existence, both physical and metaphysical.</note>: because bodies are nothing*
*Short of the atom; no plane should be thought of outside of points,*
*Number is nothing besides the monad; finally, all things are nothing*
*If the One does not exist. Therefore, no one shall contemplate—whether you*
<margin>80</margin> *Are a <term>Physicist</term> <note>original: "Physicus"; in this context, a student of nature and the physical world.</note> now, or one applying yourself to any study of Mathematics,*
*Or one grasping at the first sublime truths of <term>Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Sophia"; Greek for wisdom, often referring to the highest philosophical or divine knowledge.</note>—*
*Without this principle. Indeed, the more deeply that person*
*Yields to study, and the more they direct their light toward things above,*
*The more it is worth the effort for them to gather themselves, and*
*Casting aside multiplicity <note>original: "numerum," literally "number." Bruno suggests that to find truth, one must move from the many (numbers) back to the one (the monad).</note>, turn themselves toward the monad, and*
*So enter into the monad itself that they draw it into themselves.*

***

The <term>Minimum</term> <note>The "minimum" is Bruno's term for the smallest possible unit of matter or space—the atom or the point—which he believes contains the power and blueprint of the whole universe.</note> is the most powerful of all things, since it encloses every moment, number, magnitude, and virtue. It is the nature of the minimum to compose, increase, and form, and finally to be the thing composed, formed, and large, even unto the <term>maximum</term> <note>Bruno's "Maximum" (the infinite universe/God) and "Minimum" (the atom/monad) are "coincident"; the infinitely small and the infinitely large meet in a single divine Unity.</note>, which we have expressed more clearly elsewhere as coinciding with the same.

<summary>Bruno explains that at the level of the infinite and the infinitesimal, opposites like motion and rest coincide. He argues that the "monad" (the one) is the essential building block of all math, nature, and wisdom, containing the power of the entire universe within a single point.</summary>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno’s metaphysical argument from page 16, where he established that motion and rest coincide at the scale of the "minimum." Here, he extends this principle to the nature of God and the structure of the universe, arguing that all distinctions vanish within the fundamental unit of existence: the Monad.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>ON THE MINIMUM OF EXISTENCE</header>

<margin>16.</margin> God is the monad of monads, namely the entity of entities: for which reason, even among common philosophers, **Being** and the **One** do not differ. Just as, therefore, through the <term>monad</term> <note>original: "monadem"; the fundamental, indivisible unit of reality—analogous to a mathematical point or a physical atom.</note> all things are one, so also through the monad they exist: since that which is not one is nothing at all.

<margin>20</margin> In the minimum, simple Monad, all opposites are the same: even and odd, many and few, finite and infinite. Therefore, that which is the minimum is the same as the maximum, and everything that lies between them.

This is shown first in **God**, who is said to be the same everywhere and nowhere; the foundation beneath all things, governing above all things; not included within all things, not excluded from outside all things. He is all things through excellence and comprehension, yet nothing by way of definition; the beginning producing all things, the end limiting all things. He is the Medium connecting and distinguishing all things, the center everywhere, the most internal of internal things. He is the Extreme nowhere, because he measures and concludes all things, himself immense and unmeasurable; he in whom all things are, and who is in nothing—not even in himself, because he is indivisible and simplicity itself—but rather, he *is* himself.

<margin>30</margin> Secondly, this is shown in the **universe**, where length, breadth, and depth are <term>indifferent</term> <note>original: "indifferentia"; meaning they become indistinguishable or lose their separate character at the scale of the infinite or the infinitesimal.</note>: and which has its center everywhere. <margin>34.</margin> Thirdly, in a **particular sphere** or world, in which the indifference of dimensions is perceived from a single center. <margin>37.</margin> Fourthly, in the **external circuit**, in which, upon the universal surface of the earth, every point is simultaneously the point of the east, the west, the south, and any other direction of that kind. <margin>42</margin> Fifthly, in the **circle** which is the boundary or periphery, in which neither reason nor nature subjectively distinguishes the concave from the convex.

## THE COINCIDENCE OF THE ANGLE.

->(Geometric Woodcut Illustration)<-

<margin>47.</margin> Sixthly, in the boundary of an **angle** upon the plane or line AB from the point C, upon which the straight line CD, with its end fixed at C, now in...

B

<summary>Bruno argues that God, the universe, and geometric shapes all reveal the "Monad," a fundamental point where opposites like maximum and minimum, or concave and convex, become identical. He defines God as the "monad of monads," existing as a center that is everywhere yet nowhere.</summary>
<keywords>Monad, God as Center, Coincidence of Opposites, Infinity, Geometry, Indifference, Giordano Bruno, Metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno’s exploration of the "coincidence of opposites," a core pillar of his philosophy. Having previously discussed God as the Monad, he now uses geometry and physics to prove that at the scales of the infinitely small (the minimum) and the infinitely large (the maximum), all contradictions—such as straight lines and curves, or motion and rest—dissolve into unity.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
->**JORDANUS BRUNO OF NOLA**<-

...whether it inclines or rises: there, from that same point C, the acute and obtuse angles—whether considered by their extreme or middle proportions and falling upon the line AB along with the straight line CD—vanish simultaneously. Thus, the most acute and the most obtuse angle are born together as the same line rises. <note>Bruno is describing a geometric limit: as a line becomes perpendicular, the difference between the "sharp" and "blunt" angles on either side disappears into two right angles.</note>

¶ 54. Seventhly, in the smallest arc and the smallest chord, you cannot fashion any difference at all, just as the largest arc and chord also become the same. Indeed, the more an arc (such as CD, EF, or GH) increases in size, the more it approaches straightness, so that finally the arc of the greatest possible circle must be the same as the straight line IK.

¶ 59. From which it follows that an infinite circle, an infinite straight line, an infinite diameter, a field or area, a center, and anything else do not differ: just as they do not differ in a point, which is the smallest circle. <note>This is a radical claim: for Bruno, the universe is so unified that at the level of the infinite, a circle's center is the same as its circumference.</note>

---

¶ 65. Eighthly, in the greatest and swiftest motion, and that which is simply slow—or rather, the greatest rest—these must entirely be one and the same. For that which is "simply fast" <note>original: "simpliciter velox"; meaning absolute or perfect speed.</note>, moving from A to B and back from B to A, is simultaneously in A, in B, and in the whole circuit; therefore, it remains still. 

Hence, **Divine Wisdom**, which reaches all things and is in all things, is called the "most mobile" of all because it remains everywhere, and the "most immobile" because it reaches from end to end most swiftly and arranges all things within its own boundaries everywhere. <note>Bruno is likely referencing the Biblical Book of Wisdom 7:24: "For wisdom is more mobile than any motion; because of her pureness she pervades and penetrates all things."</note> Motion is attributed to Wisdom because it is the vegetation and life by whose power all things are moved; rest is attributed to it because it is eternity itself and the substance in which and through which all things exist and remain.

¶ 76. Therefore, a line is nothing but a point in motion, a surface nothing but a line in motion, and a body nothing but a surface in motion. Consequently, the **mobile point** is the substance of all things, and the **stable point** is the whole. The same judgment applies to the atom, and in the first and most primary way to the <term>monad</term> <note>original: "monade"; the fundamental, indivisible unit of reality that is both a mathematical point and a physical soul-atom.</note>; whereby finally the minimum, or monad, is all things—that is, the maximum and the whole.

¶ 79. If, therefore, the contemplation of nature follows in the footsteps of nature itself, it must begin from the minimum, persist in observing the minimum, and end in contemplating the minimum.

<summary>Bruno argues that at the scale of the infinite or the infinitesimal, geometric distinctions (like curves vs. straight lines) and physical distinctions (like motion vs. rest) disappear. He concludes that the Monad is the fundamental substance of the universe, and God is the "most mobile" yet "immobile" center of all existence.</summary>

While the previous page focused on the mathematical "coincidence of opposites"—where lines, circles, and points merge—Bruno now explains why this mental exercise is essential for the perfection of the human mind and its interaction with the physical world.</meta>

# CHAPTER V.

## A Digression restating the relationship of this 
## contemplation to practice.

**T**herefore, one who investigates the principles of measure and shape, aiming for various goals along a single path—He who creates while measuring and measures while creating—must first study the nature of the *minimum* <term>the minimum: the smallest indivisible unit of reality, equivalent to an atom in physics or a point in geometry</term> and grasp its solid foundations. All contemplation takes up this additional task, and the present action is not without profound reason: for the "fathers of Wisdom" <note>original: "patribus Sophiæ"; likely referring to ancient sages such as Pythagoras or Hermes Trismegistus</note> primarily sought the perfection of the human mind. If practice also bears witness to this perfection <margin>10</margin>, the light is better able to pour itself outward, so that it might descend from the order of the mind down to the senses—just as the senses are accustomed to climb the stairs <term>original: "schalas"; the metaphorical "ladder of being" or steps of logic</term> to higher thresholds, rising through the intersections of middle reason. 

Therefore, we teach by working within the "light of nature" <note>the innate human capacity for reason and observation, as opposed to divine revelation</note>, so that students, by reversing the process, may be moved toward its sublime vision—that light which shines even in the dark and, spread deeply through the infinite, offers itself to all according to their capacity, and as much as each person opens themselves to it. The mere practice of study is not as significant as the *reason* and the *end* (the goal) <margin>20</margin>, for the sake of which these things are carefully detailed. 

After the dawn has chased away the clotted darkness, the ethereal sun will show its head with brilliant light; for this reason, there is a need for certain steps and a certain order: first, let the primary foundations of falsehood be cleared away—those errors which have begun to extinguish the light of nature and disrupt the clear path of reason.

<sig>B 2</sig>

<summary>Bruno argues that studying the fundamental "minimum" is not just a mathematical exercise, but a way to perfect the human mind and align it with the "light of nature." He suggests that true wisdom requires a bridge between abstract reason and sensory practice.</summary>

1 From what has been stated above, it is necessarily inferred that the contemplation of the **minimum** <term>the minimum: the smallest, indivisible unit of reality; the "building block" of the universe</term> is both necessary and must be established before all else, prior to the sciences of nature, mathematics, and metaphysics. For many disciplines, though they use different methods, are concerned with magnitude—especially **Geometry**, which gives shape through measuring and measures through the act of shaping.

¶ 6 Here, however, although theory is linked to practice, and practice to theory, the primary goal set before the eyes of the mind is the **light of truth**. This truth is sometimes revealed by the very work and its results, and it alone remains identifies with the banner of eternity—immobile and unchanging.

¶ 22 However, so that intellects may be better prepared to receive the truth, we must proceed by demolishing the foundations of falsehood <note>Bruno often uses the metaphor of "demolition" to describe his critique of traditional Aristotelian philosophy, which he believed blocked the path to true scientific understanding</note>.

## CHAPTER VI.
### To those who accept that the continuum is infinitely divisible.

*Nature, perpetually resolving the whole into its parts,*
*Does not arrive at a minimum; and by no reasoning through art*
*(So says the common crowd) will a magnitude be found that lacks any parts.*
*Indeed, they judge the nature of things to be such*
*That in their own perception, they never cease adding*
*Number to numbers, zeal to zeal, reason to reason;*
*Voices are joined to voices, and words are woven with words.*
*And yet, any finite matter that we perceive*
*Consists entirely of finite parts,*
*Whenever reason and nature divide a single thing*
*And distribute the many parts, constrained by a fixed limit.*
*But by adding mass to mass, and number to number,*
*One will find no end at any point in time.*
<margin>10</margin> *Yet while subtracting number from number, and mass from mass,*
*The **monad** <term>monad: the numerical minimum or the "one"; the metaphysical unit</term> and the **atom** <term>atom: the physical minimum; the smallest possible particle of matter</term> will be encountered, beyond all doubt.*
*But a foul deception holds back the wretched mind*

<summary>Bruno argues that the study of the "minimum" (the atom) is the necessary foundation for all science and begins a poetic critique of the Aristotelian belief that matter can be divided infinitely. He asserts that while we can add numbers forever, in the physical world of division, we eventually hit the "monad" or "atom."</summary>

-># ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM, BOOK I<-

When one thinks the continuum can be cut into whole parts,
Counting sections in an endless excursion; nor does he
<margin>20</margin>See that by adding numbers he runs on without end,
Yet he cannot subtract a great thing from a great thing in the same way:
Since he hastens toward the end, however much he adds,
And it becomes long because the path to the end is open,
(Though many things may stand in the way), as often as the whole is under the name of number.
But if you take the "instant" of the present time,
Doubtless a duration without end enters in, by which
Wisdom <note>Sophia: the personification of Wisdom or philosophy, whom Bruno often invokes as his guide</note> moved me to write these dogmas truly:
Infinite ages will again follow, in which they will think
<margin>30</margin>That I have taught these things truly, for which that same thing
Is the beginning and the end: the single instant of the present time.
Therefore we hold that a beginning without end is given,
And finally a certain end stands without a beginning.
Therefore both beginning and end are found everywhere,
In any moment of time whatsoever, whether you think
Of the following parts, or wish to retrace the ages past,
The course remains, and the recourse without end.
So also, for one flying out from the region of his native Earth <note>Tellus: the personification of the Earth. Bruno uses this to describe an observer traveling into deep space</note>,
Space without limits will be encountered, and in that space,
<margin>40</margin>Bodies: for the void never follows without things <note>Bruno rejected the idea of a "pure" vacuum; he believed space was always filled with ether or matter</note>:
Yet this center is an end to that infinite space separately.
The whole is a middle, and a center without a margin.
But if you set down a finite species and mass,
Here it is certain there is a "greater," and so finally a **minimum** is certain;
Indeed, you will find it in the whole, always, everywhere.
For outside this is nothing, without this is nothing; just as without the **monad** <term>monas: the Greek term for 'unit' or 'one', which Bruno uses to describe the irreducible metaphysical atom and the source of all quantity</term>
A finite number will not be given, nor one without end,
Since the monad is the entire substance in number.

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 21. Having established that the "instant" is the minimum of time, Bruno now addresses the logical fallacy of infinite divisibility in physical matter, contrasting the way we count (adding up to infinity) with the way nature exists (dividing down to a fundamental unit or "monad").</meta>

# GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA

*The condition of reasoning is twofold: for one adding*
*Species to species already conceived, it will always be suitable*
<margin>50.</margin> *To ascend upward; but for one removing, he encounters the bottom.*
*But truly, if anyone should divide a mass, another*
*Will thrust forward the parts of its kind in this order: namely,*
*That by which reason always takes parts away from parts.*
*As often as a third part of one thing is sought, and then*
*A hundredth of that, of which you might again take one*
*From thousands, and again and again demand these back.*
*Do you foolishly think that nature and art can run on in this way,*
*As if by handling a mass of matter*
*One could never reach the ultimate goal?*

---

<margin>¶ 1.14.</margin> **I**t is a common opinion that for nature and art dividing the continuum <note>Continuum: something that is continuous and not broken into discrete parts, like a line or a flow of water</note>, they will never reach a point where parts occur that cannot be further split into other parts. It is as if the real potential in an actual object were such that one could proceed from parts to perceptible parts, just as in reasoning—having taken a thousandth part of one inch <note>original: "digiti" (a finger's breadth)</note>, we can still seek another thousandth of that one thousandth, and a hundredth of one of these, and a tenth of one of these hundredths, and a thousandth of one of these tenths, and so on without end, such that no limit to the process is ever established. It is as if there were no difference between adding multitude to multitude by counting, and subtracting magnitude from magnitude by dividing; as if it were the same thing to descend by cutting the continuous and to ascend by multiplying the discrete <note>Discrete: separate, distinct units, like the integers 1, 2, 3</note>; and as if there were one and the same logic for these opposite orders and for the different kinds of parts found in each.

<margin>¶ 42.</margin> Furthermore, we must establish that any finite mass of matter, however large, consists of finite, not infinite, parts. Just as one may run through the infinite by adding magnitude to it (as with a finite number of items), so, on the contrary, for one taking away parts and subdividing a finite magnitude, it is necessary that he eventually encounter the minimum <note>minimum: the smallest possible unit of a substance that cannot be divided further</note>, just as one encounters the monad <note>monas: the Greek term for "unit" or "one," which Bruno uses to describe the fundamental building block of all things</note> when subtracting numbers from a finite number.

<margin>¶ 26.32.</margin> Duration is absolutely infinite (I do not here define the eternity or time of this specific world); hence, whether those things which consist of indissoluble parts have always existed or not, or whether they will or will not exist perpetually, while even one thing remains, duration is eternal from the preceding part...

<summary>Bruno argues against the Aristotelian concept of infinite divisibility, asserting that while numbers can be added infinitely, physical matter must eventually be composed of indivisible "minima" or monads. He distinguishes between the mathematical potential to divide and the physical reality of fundamental units.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bruno’s critique of infinite divisibility. On the previous page, he argued that while we can mathematically divide numbers forever, physical nature must have a "bottom" or a fundamental unit. Here, he extends this logic to time and space, defining the "instant" and the "atom" as the building blocks of an infinite universe.</meta>

# ON THE LEAST KINDS OF EXISTENCE, BOOK I

<sig>B 4</sig>

...before this, and after this, and whatever instant of time (which you might grasp) stands in between; for truly, without any doubt, this instant, or the time in which I am writing, is an end. Thus, for me to be about to write today was true from this instant back to an immense past duration, and it was false that I had already written. Yet, for me to have written will be true from this time forward into a no less immense duration, and it will be false that I am about to write (as I once was). Therefore, at every point of duration—and every point of duration is—a beginning without an end, and an end without a beginning. Therefore, all duration is an infinite instant, simultaneously both beginning and end.

33. A similar judgment, in its own way, applies to every point of space and magnitude in the mass of the universe, if (as was proven in the book *On the Infinite*) it is immense: where the Earth <note>original: "Tellus"</note> is no more in the center than the moon, the sun, the pole, and all things. Those who were not permitted to perceive the Earth's motion around its own center <note>Bruno is referring to the rotation of the Earth, a Copernican idea he expanded upon to argue that there is no fixed center of the universe.</note> could not arrive at this contemplation.

The beginning and foundation of all errors, both in physics and in mathematics, is the resolution of the continuum into infinity <note>The "resolution of the continuum" refers to the idea that a line or a piece of matter can be divided into smaller and smaller pieces forever. Bruno argues this is a logical fallacy.</note>. To us, however, it is proven that the resolution of both nature and true art (which does not proceed outside of nature) descends from a finite magnitude and number into the **atom**. Then, by adding to nature and to the concept, no limit is established by things themselves, except for one looking at the nature of specific particular species. The **minimum**, therefore, is present everywhere and always; the **maximum**, however, is nowhere and never. Yet the maximum and the minimum can so converge into a single definition <note>original: "rationem"</note> that from this we recognize the maximum to be everywhere; for through what has been said, it is established that the maximum consists in the minimum, and the minimum in the maximum, just as the monad <term>Monad: the fundamental, indivisible unit of existence—the "one" from which all numbers and things flow.</term> is in the multitude, and the multitude in the monad. Although reason and nature can more easily set the minimum free from the maximum than the maximum from the minimum.

Therefore, the **immense** is nothing but a center which is everywhere. **Eternity** is nothing but the "always-instant." What is the one and remaining thing of an eternal reality is different from one thing to another in a certain succession and change of constant things. An immense body is an **atom**. An immense plane is a **point**. Immense space is the receptacle of the point or the atom; for other things are contained where the atom is contained, and not the atom where others are. Therefore, the individual <note>original: "individuum," meaning that which cannot be divided further.</note> is properly said to be everywhere; and because space is infinite, the center is said to be everywhere, and the atom is said to be all things. Truly, I understand the atom according to its different kinds, elsewhere distinguished.

<summary>Bruno argues that time and space are composed of indivisible units (the instant and the atom) and that in an infinite universe, every point is a center, making the smallest part (the minimum) identical in importance to the whole (the maximum).</summary>
