# CHAPTER VII.

## The same proposition is reinforced with several arguments.

*Therefore, minimums cannot exist without an end to their divisions* <note>original: "tomis," referring to the Greek "atomos" or uncuttable pieces</note>,
*Within a finite whole. Nor, in turn, can that boundary,*
*By which parts touch each other in potential or in act,*
*Exist as a number beyond counting; since one single boundary*
*Always belongs to both parts simultaneously,*
*Right here where they are joined to be fused into a whole.*
*Therefore, if you dare to believe the parts are finite,*
*I will consequently show that the boundaries are also finite.*
*Since, although a boundary is not itself a part, it is clearly*
<margin>10</margin> *established as something properly related to a part,*
*I would never believe it exceeds the number of parts compared;*
*For as the parts are many, so many boundaries result:*
*And these two follow one another mutually in reality and in reason.*
*Therefore, you should conceive of the parts as indefinite,*
*But not as being without an end; for these parts, too,*
*An indefinite boundary exists. For the substance of the Minimum*
*Does not vanish by being broken down into other parts, but remains*
*Constantly one (variable only in its function)* <note>Bruno means that the fundamental "atom" or Minimum doesn't change its essence, only its role or position within a larger structure.</note>.
*Therefore, the measurer errs who breaks down a fixed quantity*
<margin>20</margin> *without end, never surveying the footprints of nature,*
*Never reaching them, and by no means equaling them—*
*<term>(Whatever he handles)</term>* <note>The "measurer" here refers to abstract mathematicians. Bruno argues that by dividing numbers to infinity, they lose touch with the physical reality of the world.</note> *as often as he wanders through the void*
*Outside of nature's light, treating things more lightly than dreams—*
*A man to whom a sickly abundance is poured out from a mind without limit.*
*If the whole is divisible, what then is a divisible part to you?*
*How can it be equal to the infinite, or greater, or less, or the same?*

<summary>Bruno argues that because a finite object has a finite number of boundaries between its parts, it must also have a finite number of parts, proving that matter cannot be infinitely divided. He critiques mathematicians who ignore physical limits, suggesting their theories of infinite divisibility are mere fantasies detached from nature.</summary>

<keywords>Minimum, atomism, divisibility, geometry, boundaries, finite vs infinite, philosophy of nature, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno’s systematic attack on the Aristotelian concept of the "continuum"—the idea that matter or space can be divided infinitely. On the previous page, Bruno argued that boundaries and parts are logically linked. Here, he presents a "reductio ad absurdum": if a finite object were made of infinite parts, then adding a part would not make the object any larger, which contradicts physical reality.</meta>

->ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM<-

Since any small part, however modest it may be taken by you,
must match the parts of another, even a larger whole;
otherwise, the half would be held as equal to the whole, and to the entire thing,
<margin>30</margin> Indeed, when this part is added to that one,
it makes the whole larger. If, then, you see that the whole 
is finite, why do you assert that the parts are without end? 
Can you imagine such parts,
that when they are added together, the whole cannot grow?
Furthermore, if you truly want a "part" to be called by its name,
reaffirm that this part, when added to others, causes the whole to be larger.
If, then, the largest mass <note>original: "moles," referring to a physical body or bulk</note> is ultimately finite,
why do they dare to say the "minimums" <note>the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality</note> that compose it are without end?
<margin>40</margin> Therefore, turn the gaze of your poorly-directed mind here:
once you know that these parts establish a greater quantity
when they are added individually to the whole, and that the whole
is not infinite, but enclosed by a certain fixed boundary;
do not think that the substance of the thing, by an eternal 
process of division, can be split and cut again and again,
so that part can follow part without end.
Rather, it is a power of reason lacking both mind and measure
to invent numbers out of nothing by dissolving what is already dissolved,
granting at will an immense depth to the center 
of a continuum <note>a mathematical or physical entity where there are no gaps between parts; Bruno argues that if such a thing were infinitely divisible, its "depth" or complexity would be infinite even if its size were small</note>, even while its outer edge remains finite;
as if the whole were not increased by those very same parts being added.
Why would these things, which individually make a whole larger,
then integrate into a total that is, in the end, finite, if they themselves are infinite?
Are they not one and the same? Why are "upward" <note>the process of adding parts to make a larger whole</note>
<margin>50</margin> and "downward" <note>the process of dividing a whole into parts</note>, the ascent and the descent, treated so differently?
Therefore, come—since an infinite number of parts is not established—
let us consider how much of this mass should be referred to a certain fixed number.

<summary>Bruno argues that if a whole object is finite, it must be composed of a finite number of parts. He mocks the mathematical idea of infinite divisibility, noting that if parts were truly infinite, adding them together would never result in a larger, finite object.</summary>

### GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA

*From this, note how the powers everywhere and all bodies*
*provide a pattern of the sphere; the cycle is a pattern of the sphere, and these are patterns of the center.*
*For a circle is nothing except a visible center,*
*and an infinite globe is nothing but a center that is everywhere.*
*Therefore, here is simply the center, and the minimum, pouring*
*itself through the whole; the true, the one, always, in everything.*
*And every composite thing will be reduced to the minimum so that it may exist:*
*Since the minimum of nature encloses the powers of any*
*large sensible thing, and unfolds them from on high:*
<margin>10</margin> *For the underlying minimum <note>original: "minimum substans." This refers to the fundamental particle or "atom" that lies beneath the surface of visible matter.</note> excels wonderfully in strength*
*over any mass that has grown large through the assembly of parts.*
*Surely the power that increases the subject body,*
*strengthening it, and binding its parts into one,*
*preserving itself and all these things around itself,*
*is the minimum, or indeed something consisting of no physical bulk.*
*Finally, the single Monad <term>Monad: from the Greek "monas" (unity); for Bruno, this is the ultimate indivisible unit of reality, both physical and spiritual.</term> of monads is found,*
*which embraces the many and the great, and everything moving*
*into those things to be integrated. Giving being to entities, it is also God,*
*existing as the whole, the infinite, the true, the all, the good, the one.*
<margin>20</margin> *And those things which consist in the composite, the discrete, and the sensible,*
*and which dimension <note>physical measurement in space</note> fuses together separately,*
*are merely one thing after another, right down to the body of minimums.*
*These parts never endure one another in a single space,*
*here where matter fluctuates with outspread wings;*
*But in the True, the One, the Being, the Eternal, the Simple, and the Same,*
*they do not establish a number <note>meaning they are not "many" but are unified</note>; they are the Simple, Being, One, and the Same:*
*Hence, even contraries make their name proper to that One:*
*The Beginning, the End, the Middle, the Extreme, Nothing, and Everything.*
*To which the species of things are assimilated by certain steps.*
<margin>30</margin> *From which the Same is, in the infinite, the length, the width, and the depth;*

->## ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM##<-

*And the center is one in the whole, the whole itself, and throughout everything:*
*For this center is turned everywhere without end to such an extent on all sides,*
*and therefore the sphere of Xenophanes <note>Xenophanes of Colophon (c. 570 – c. 475 BC) was a Greek philosopher who described the divine "One" as a perfect, unmoving sphere.</note> was equal on every side.*
*And though a finite globe is not so vast in every direction,*
*it is nevertheless one; from its middle point a triple dimension <note>The three dimensions: length, width, and height/depth.</note> extends*
*Forward and upward, backward and downward,*
*and to the right and left. If you look at the rotation of the all-mother*
*Earth, by which she readies herself for the daylight,*
*any point whatever is both Sunset and Sunrise,*
<margin>40</margin>*as well as the pivot of midnight and of midday,*
*and any other point, whatever the distinction may be.*
*Furthermore, even opposites come together in one [point] by their positioning*
*here, where they do not consist of any separate depth,*
*in the circle and the globe: the convex and the concave form,*
*which hardly any mental effort or action can pull apart;*
*which in the underlying subject can, by no reasoning, exist or be taken*
*separately. Moreover, is it not the one*
*and the same point, when a line rises from a plane,*
<margin>50</margin>*simultaneously from one subject, that becomes both an obtuse and an acute*
*angle when unfolded? They are perhaps in no way separated,*
*since certainly that one point lies beneath both,*
*and they vanish into it together, just as they arise from it.*
*And how far apart are the concepts of the smallest arc and its chord,*
*if they know no difference—this greatest [the circle] and that [the line]?*
*How does its diameter <note>original: "dimetrus"</note> differ from its smallest circle?*
*How far apart are the greatest straight line, the arc, and the whole*
*circle, since the larger the arc is, the more it shares the nature of a straight line?*
<margin>60</margin>*Therefore, that which unfolds into an infinite mass and*
*the contracted minimum will be unable to vary their species <note>original: "species"; here meaning their essential forms or types.</note>.*
*Center (I say), circle, chord, area, diameter, arc—*

<meta>catchword: And re-</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that at the level of the "minimum" (the point), all geometric and physical opposites—such as sunrise and sunset, or concave and convex—coincide as one. He demonstrates that in both the infinitely large and the infinitely small, the distinctions between a straight line and a curve vanish.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bruno's exploration of the "Minimum" as the ultimate constituent of reality. Following the previous discussion on how points and angles converge, Bruno now moves into the metaphysical paradox where extremes meet: infinite speed is indistinguishable from rest, and the smallest unit (the Monad) is the source of all power and magnitude.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>
<meta>catchword: ¶ 16 God</meta>

*And the ray comes before us with no distinction,*
*whether all things are conceived as smallest or largest.*
*Conceive of the infinitely sluggish and slow: it is stationary.*
*That which is extremely swift, in a motion without end,*
*must also remain stationary. An orb driven by the greatest force*
*will be moved in a point, and moved at one instant;*
*it is both the end of motion and the point of contact.*
<margin>70</margin> *But if it turns itself in a cycle, it moves and stands still*
*at once; for it goes and returns simultaneously, and having returned, it rests.*
*The simply slow and that which remains forever are the same;*
*that which goes and returns at once is both motion and rest.*
*Therefore, rest and motion are most fully together in that*
*One, which is moved the least and yet the most, being the same,*
*traveling from end to end, yet utterly unmoving,*
*and remaining as a whole in all things at once, and above them.*
*Thus, there is nothing besides the monad <term>monad</term><note>original: "monaden"; from the Greek 'monas' (unity). For Bruno, it represents the irreducible "One" that is both the physical atom and the divine center of the universe.</note>: for bodies are nothing*
*without the atom; no plane should be thought of outside of points;*
*number is nothing besides the monad—nothing, in the end, exists*
*without the existence of the one. Therefore, no one*
<margin>80</margin> *who is now a Natural Philosopher <term>Physicus</term><note>A student of nature or physics.</note>, or who intends any study of Mathematics <term>Mathesis</term><note>Specifically the study of quantities, but often carrying astrological or mystical connotations in the Renaissance.</note>,*
*or who seeks the first heights of Wisdom <term>Sophia</term><note>Divine or foundational wisdom.</note>,*
*will contemplate these things without this principle. Indeed, the higher*
*he reaches and the more he directs his light from above,*
*the more it is worth his effort to collect himself and,*
*dispersing number, turn himself into the monad, and so*
*enter the monad, that he draws it into himself.*

**T**he minimum is the most powerful of all things, since it contains every momentum, number, magnitude, and power. It is its nature to compose, increase, and form, and finally to be the thing composed, formed, and great, even unto the maximum—which, as we have expressed more clearly elsewhere <note>Bruno refers to his doctrine of the "Coincidence of Opposites" (coincidentia oppositorum), where the infinitely small (the minimum) and the infinitely large (the maximum) are functionally identical in the divine unity.</note>, coincides with this same [minimum].

<summary>Bruno argues that at the level of the "monad" (the ultimate unit), opposites like motion and rest or the smallest and largest become identical. He asserts that all physical and mathematical study must begin with this irreducible "One" to reach true wisdom.</summary>

He argues that at the level of the "Minimum," all distinctions—like center and circumference, or beginning and end—vanish into a single unity.</meta>

16. God is the Monad of monads <term>monas</term><note>The fundamental, indivisible unit of existence. For Bruno, God is the "Monad of monads," the ultimate source from which all other units derive.</note>, namely the Entity of entities: wherefore even among common philosophers, Being and the One <note>original: "Ens & Vnum"</note> do not differ. Just as, therefore, through the monad all things are one, so also through the monad they exist; since that which is not one is nothing at all.

20. In the minimum, the simple, the Monad, all opposites are the same: even and odd, many and few, finite and infinite. Therefore, that which is the minimum is the same as the maximum, and whatever lies between these.

This is shown first in God, who is said to be everywhere and nowhere: grounding all things from beneath, governing all things from above; within all things but not enclosed, outside all things but not excluded; he is all things through his excellence and vastness, but nothing by way of a limited definition; the Beginning bringing forth all things, the End bounding all things. He is the Middle, binding and distinguishing all things; a center everywhere, the innermost of the interior. A boundary nowhere, because he measures and concludes all things, himself unmeasured and peerless; in him are all things, and he is in nothing—not even in himself, because he is indivisible and simplicity itself, but he *is* himself.

30. Second, in the universe, where length, width, and depth are indifferent <note>original: "indifferentia"; meaning they are indistinguishable or equivalent in an infinite space</note>: and which has a center everywhere. ¶ 34. Third, in the particular sphere or world in which, from a single center, the indifference of dimensions is perceived. ¶ 37. Fourth, in the eternal rotation, in which upon the universal surface of the earth, every point is simultaneously the point of the east, the west, the south, and any other of that kind.

42. Fifth, in the circle which is the boundary or periphery, in which neither reason nor nature subjectively distinguishes the concave from the convex.

## CHAPTER V.

### An excursion in which the relationship of this con-
### templation to practice is repeated.

*Therefore, seeking out the principles of measurement and shape,*
*and aiming by the same path toward various ends—*
*He who, as the same being, gives form while measuring and*
*measures while giving form—it is necessary that he first*
*contemplate the nature of the minimum, and grasp the solid element.*
*Every speculation conceives of this as a work added [to nature],*
<margin>10</margin> *nor is the present action void of deep reason:*
*For by the fathers of Wisdom, the perfection of the human mind*
*is chiefly sought; if practice <term>praxis</term><note>In this context, "praxis" refers to the practical application of knowledge, such as geometry or mechanics, as opposed to pure "theoria" or contemplation.</note> also joins as a witness to it,*
*the light is able to pour itself forth all the more beyond itself,*
*so that it might descend from the order of the mind to the senses—*
*just as the sense is accustomed to ascend to the high thresholds*
*via stairs, rising through the crossroads of middle reason.*
*Therefore, by the light of nature, we teach by working,*
*so that in turn those learning may be moved toward nature's*
<margin>20</margin> *sublime gaze, which shines in the darkness and, deeply*
*poured through the immense, presents itself to all according*
*to their capacity, and as much as each person has offered himself to it.*
*The practice of study is not of as much value as the reason and*
*the end, for which these things are subsequently made accurate.*
*After the dawn has put to flight the thick shadows,*
*the ethereal sun will thrust out its head with brilliant light;*
*Wherefore there is need of certain steps and a certain order:*
*and first, let the primary foundations of the false be removed,*
*which are accustomed to extinguish the light of nature* <note>original: "naturai" — Bruno uses this archaic Latin genitive ending, famously used by the Roman poet Lucretius, to signal his connection to ancient atomist philosophy.</note>
*and to break the clear course of reason.*

B 2

He emphasizes that one must first understand the "minimum" (the fundamental building block of reality) to clear away false philosophical foundations and allow the "light of nature" to shine.</summary>

<margin>1</margin> **F**rom the things said before, it is necessarily inferred that the contemplation of the minimum <term>minimi</term><note>For Bruno, the "minimum" is the smallest possible unit of existence, serving as the fundamental building block of the universe, whether in geometry (the point), physics (the atom), or metaphysics (the monad).</note> is both necessary and must be established first, before natural, mathematical, and metaphysical science. For many faculties, although by different methods, are concerned with magnitude, especially Geometry, which shapes by measuring and measures by shaping.

<margin>¶ 6</margin> Here, truly, although speculation is connected to practice, and practice to speculation: nonetheless, the principal thing proposed before the eyes of intention is the light of truth, which may sometimes be declared by the work and effect itself as a witness; truth alone remains immobile and invariable under the banner of eternity.

<margin>¶ 22</margin> But so that minds may be better disposed for the reception of truth, one must proceed from the demolition of the foundations of falsehood.

# CHAPTER VI.
## To those who accept that the continuum is divisible into infinity.

**N** *ature, perpetually resolving the whole into parts,*
*Does not arrive at a minimum; and by no method of art*
*(So the common crowd says) will a quantity be found without any parts.*
*Truly, it indicates the nature of things to be such*
*That in its own sense, it never ceases adding number to numbers,*
*Zeal to zeal, and reason to reason;*
*And voices are bound to voices, and words to words.*
*And yet, all matter received before us as finite*
*Consists entirely of finite parts,*
*Whenever reason and nature divide the unit,*
*And distribute the many, constrained by a fixed limit.*
*But by placing mass upon mass, and number upon number,*
*One will find no end at any time.*
<margin>10</margin> *While you take away number from number, and mass from mass,*
*The monad <term>monas</term><note>The monad represents the metaphysical aspect of the minimum—the "one" that is the source of all numbers and existence.</note> and the atom <term>atomus</term><note>The atom is the physical minimum, the smallest indivisible particle of matter.</note> will occur beyond any doubt.*
*But a foul deception holds back the wretched mind...*

## ON THE MINIMUM, EXISTENCE, AND LIBERTY

*When the mind thus considers that it can cut the whole continuum,*
*Counting its divisions without end as it moves through them: it does not*
<margin>20</margin> *See that by merely adding to a number it runs on without limit,*
*Yet one cannot in the same way take a great part from a great whole:*
*Since it hurries toward an end, no matter how much space it*
*Supposes; and it arrives at the long-sought end because there is a path*
*Where it must stop, whenever the "whole" is classified under a number.*
*But if you take the instant of the present time,*
*Without doubt a duration without end underlies it, through which men*
*Have known me, <note>Bruno is speaking in the first person here, asserting his place in history as a messenger of these truths.</note> under the guidance of Wisdom <note>original: "Sophia"</note>, to be about to write these true doctrines:*
*Infinite ages will follow in turn, in which they may think me*
*To have taught these things truly; for them, that same instant of*
<margin>30</margin> *Present time both begins and ends.*
*Therefore, we hold that a beginning without end is given,*
*And a certain end finally stands without a beginning.*
*Thus both the beginning and the end are found everywhere,*
*Whether you think of the moments of any age as following*
*In sequence, or if it pleases you to unravel the ages already passed,*
*The forward course and the backward return remain without end.*
*So also, as one flies from the ancestral region of the Earth <note>original: "Telluris"</note>,*
*Space without limits will be encountered, and within that space,*
*Bodies: for a void <note>Bruno rejected the idea of a "pure" vacuum, arguing that the infinite space is always filled with either air, "aether," or celestial bodies.</note> will never follow without things:*
<margin>40</margin> *Yet this center, in relation to space without end, is separately*
*An end. The whole is a medium, and a center without a margin.*
*But if you set down a finite form and mass,*
*Just as a "greatest" is certain, so at last a "minimum" <term>minimum</term><note>The smallest possible unit of matter or space that cannot be divided further.</note> is certain;*
*Indeed, you will find it in the whole, always, and everywhere.*
*For outside of this there is nothing, and without this nothing exists; just as without the monad <term>monade</term><note>From the Greek 'monas' (unit). For Bruno, this is the metaphysical equivalent of the physical atom—the fundamental "one" from which all numbers and things are built.</note>*
*A number will not be granted, whether finite or without end,*
*Since the monad is the entire substance of number,*

### *The dual condition of the discourse.*

*To the one who adds,*
*It will always be fitting for species to pass through conceived species from above;*
<margin>50.</margin> *But to the one who removes, the end meets him at the bottom.*
*But truly, if someone divides a mass, another*
*Will thrust in parts of a kind, in this order, naturally,*
*By which reason always takes parts from parts.*
*Whenever a third part of one is sought, and*
*A hundredth of that, of which you again take one*
*From thousands, and again you ask for these, and yet again.*
*Do you foolishly think that nature and art can run on in this way,*
*As if, when handling the mass of matter,*
*It were never able to reach the final goal?*

---

<margin>¶ 1. 14.</margin> **I**t is a commonly held view <note>Bruno is referring to the Aristotelian physics of his time, which taught that the "continuum"—any continuous physical object—could be divided infinitely.</note> that for nature and art as they divide the continuum, they will never arrive at parts that cannot be further split into other parts. It is as if the real potential within an object matches our reasoning: just as we can take a thousandth part of a single finger's width, and then seek another thousandth of that thousandth, and a hundredth of one of those, and a tenth of one of those hundredths, and a thousandth of one of those tenths, and so establish no limit to proceeding without end. It is as if there were no difference between adding multitude to multitude by counting, and subtracting magnitude from magnitude by dividing. They believe the process of descending by cutting the continuum is the same as ascending by multiplying discrete numbers, and that the logic of these opposite orders—and of the different kinds of parts involved—is one and the same.

<margin>¶ 42.</margin> Furthermore, we must establish that any finite matter under consideration does not consist of infinite parts. While one may run toward the infinite by adding magnitude to it—just as one does with a finite number by adding to the multitude—the opposite is true for one taking away and subdividing parts from a finite magnitude. Just as when subtracting a number from a finite number, one must eventually encounter the <term>monad</term> <note>The monad is Bruno’s term for the fundamental, indivisible unit of existence—the physical and metaphysical "atom" or "minimum."</note>.

<margin>¶ 26. 32.</margin> Duration is absolutely infinite (though I do not here define the eternity or time of this specific world). Hence, whether things that consist of indissoluble parts have always existed or not, or whether they will or will not exist forever, or even if only one thing remains, duration is eternal from the preceding part <note>original: "a parte anteriori", referring to the past duration of time.</note>.

<summary>Bruno argues against the infinite divisibility of matter, asserting that while numbers can be added infinitely, physical division must eventually reach a "monad," or an indivisible minimum. He distinguishes between the conceptual infinite of mathematics and the physical reality of the material world.</summary>

He moves from the previous discussion on the impossibility of infinite division to show how the smallest unit—the "instant" in time and the "atom" in space—is actually the key to understanding the infinite universe.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM, BOOK I</header>
<sig>B 4</sig>

...before this, and after this, and any instant of time (which you may choose): to which indeed, beyond all doubt, this very instant in which I write is the end. Thus, that I was "going to write" today was true from this instant back through an immense past duration, and that I "had already written" was false; but that I "have written" will be true from this time forward through an immense duration, and the claim that I am "about to write" (as I once was) will then be false. Therefore, at every point of duration—and every point of duration is a beginning without end, and an end without beginning—total duration is therefore an infinite instant, being the same beginning and end.

<margin>¶ 38.</margin> A similar judgment, in its own way, applies to every point of space and magnitude in the mass of the universe, if (as proved in the book *On the Infinite* <note>original: "libro de infinito"; Bruno refers to his own work, likely *De l'infinito, universo e mondi*, where he argued for an unbound universe without a central point.</note>) it is immense: where the Earth is no more in the center than the moon, the sun, the pole, and all other things. Those who were not permitted to perceive its <note>The Earth's</note> motion around its own center could not arrive at this contemplation.

¶ The beginning and foundation of all errors, both in physics and in mathematics, is the resolution of the continuum into infinity <note>term: "resolution of the continuum" — the process of dividing a physical object or a mathematical line into smaller and smaller parts.</note>. To us, however, it is proven—both by nature and by true art, which does not step outside of nature—that resolution descends from a finite magnitude and number into an **atom**. Furthermore, whether by adding to nature or to a concept, no limit has been established by things themselves, except when looking at the nature of certain particular species. The **minimum**, therefore, is present everywhere and always, while the **maximum** is nowhere and never. Yet, the maximum and minimum can coincide in such a single principle that we may understand the maximum also to be everywhere; since, through what has been said, it is clear that the maximum consists in the minimum, and the minimum in the maximum—just as the multitude exists in the unit <note>original: "monas", the Monad or the fundamental "One".</note>, and the unit in the multitude. Although reason and nature can more easily separate the minimum from the maximum than the maximum from the minimum.

Therefore, the infinite is nothing but a center everywhere. Eternity is nothing but the "always" of the instant. This is the one and remaining thing of the eternal being, while other things exist in a certain succession and change of constant states. The immense body is the atom. The immense plane is the point. Immense space is the receptacle of the point or the atom: for other things are contained where the atom is contained, and not the atom where others are; therefore, the individual <note>term: "indiuiduum" — literally "the indivisible," used here as a synonym for the atom.</note> is properly said to be everywhere, and because space is infinite, the center is said to be everywhere, and the atom is said to be all things. Truly, I understand the atom according to different kinds, distinguished in other ways.

<summary>Bruno argues that the universe has no fixed center because every point (the atom) and every moment (the instant) is effectively a center of the infinite. He rejects the idea that matter can be divided forever, asserting that the "minimum" is the fundamental building block of all reality.</summary>

## CHAPTER VII.

### The same thesis is further supported by several arguments.

*Therefore, the smallest units* <note>original: "minima"; Bruno's term for the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality—the atoms of space, time, and matter.</note> *and the atoms* <note>original: "tomi"; from the Greek "tomos" meaning a cut. Bruno uses this to refer to the parts created by division.</note> *cannot exist without limit*
*within a finite whole. Nor, in turn, can that boundary*
*where parts touch each other—whether in potential or in actuality—*
*be infinite in number; since always only one single boundary*
*belongs to both parts at the point where they*
*are joined together to be fused into a whole.*
*Consequently, if you dare to believe that the parts are finite,*
*I will grant that the boundaries following from them are also finite.*
*Since, although a boundary is not itself a part, it is established*
<margin>10</margin> *by its specific relationship to a part;*
*thus, I do not believe the number of boundaries exceeds the number of the parts compared.*
*For just as the parts are many, so the boundaries result in many;*
*they follow a mutual relationship between the two.*
*Therefore, you should conceive of the parts as indefinite,* <note>Bruno distinguishes between "indefinite" (uncounted or vast) and "infinite" (truly without end). He argues that while we might not count them, the parts of a finite object cannot be literally infinite.</note> *but not as endless;*
*and an indefinite boundary for them also exists.*
*For the substance of the Minimum does not vanish*
*by being broken down into other parts, but remains*
*constantly one (changing only in its function).*
*Thus the measurer* <note>original: "mensor"; referring to mathematicians and geometers who rely on abstract calculations rather than physical reality.</note> *errs when he resolves a certain quantity without end,*
<margin>20</margin> *never surveying the tracks of nature,*
*never reaching them, nor matching them with any firm points*
*(no matter what he handles), as often as he wanders through the void*
*outside of nature's light, dealing with things more flimsy than dreams—*
*a man to whom a sickly abundance is poured out from a mind without limit.*
*Since the whole is divisible, what indeed is the divisible part to you?*
*Why is the infinite then equal to, greater than, or less than itself?*

<meta>catchword: Since (Latin: Cum)</meta>

<summary>In this chapter, Bruno argues that a finite object cannot contain an infinite number of parts or boundaries. He critiques mathematicians who believe in infinite divisibility, asserting that they ignore the physical reality of the "Minimum" (the atom).</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter VII of Book I. Bruno is developing a geometric and logical argument against the Aristotelian view of the "infinite divisibility" of matter. He argues that if a finite object were made of infinite parts, each of those parts (no matter how small) would contribute to the size, eventually making the finite object infinite—a logical contradiction.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>

# ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM, BOOK I.

*Since a small amount, however it is taken by you, must*
*be equal to the parts of another, even if that other is larger;*
*then a half would be considered equal to the whole, and to the entire thing,*
<margin>30</margin>*because even when this part is added to that one,*
*it will make the whole larger. If, finally, you see that the whole*
*is finite, why then do you assert that its parts are without end?*
*Or can you devise such parts*
*that, when added together, the whole is unable to grow?*
*Furthermore, if you truly wish for a "part" to be called by its name,* <note>Bruno is arguing that for a "part" to exist, it must have a definitive size (a "minimum"). If a part had no size, adding it to another would not increase the total size.</note>
*confirm that this part, when added to others, causes an increase.*
*But if the greatest mass is, at long last, finite,*
*why do they dare to say the smallest integrating units are without end?*
*Turn the gaze of your ill-driven mind toward this point,*
<margin>40</margin>*so that you may know how much these parts establish a "greater" amount*
*when they are applied individually to the whole—and that whole is*
*not infinite, but enclosed by a certain boundary.*
*Do not suppose that because a subject can be split*
*by the perennial discourse of the mind,* <note>original: "perenni discursu"; Bruno distinguishes between what we can imagine (dividing a number forever) and what exists in physical reality.</note> *it can be divided thereafter;*
*as if parts could follow upon parts without end.*
*Rather, it is the power of a reason lacking mind and measure*
*to fashion numbers out of nothing by dissolving what is already dissolved,*
*attributing at will an immense depth to the center*
*of the continuum, while acknowledging its finite edge;*
*as if the whole were not increased by the addition of those very parts.*
*Why then would these infinite things—each of which*
*makes the whole larger—finally integrate into a finite whole?*
*Are they not the same? Why are "up" and "down,"*
<margin>50</margin>*and "ascent" and "descent," treated as unequal?* <note>Bruno uses "ascent" to mean building a whole from parts, and "descent" to mean dividing a whole into parts. He argues the logic must work both ways.</note>
*Therefore, come, since a magnitude is not constituted as infinite,*
*it must be referred to a specific number...*

<meta>catchword: (be thought / *putemus*)</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that a finite object cannot consist of an infinite number of parts, as adding infinite parts with any magnitude would logically result in an infinitely large object. He critiques the "limitless" nature of mathematical reasoning when it is applied to physical reality without the grounding of a "minimum" unit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter VII of Book I. Bruno is addressing the logical paradoxes that arise if one denies the existence of a physical "minimum" (an atom) and instead treats space or matter as infinitely divisible. He is arguing that in an infinite system, standard measurements like "inches" versus "miles" lose their meaning because infinity cannot be made larger or smaller.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

->GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA<-

*Nor should you try to escape by inventing "similar parts";* <note>original: "partes... adsimilares"; Bruno is likely referring to the Aristotelian or Anaxagorean idea of "homeomeries"—substances that can be divided infinitely into parts identical to the whole.</note>
*For you are sufficiently on our side if you admit these parts*
*are of such a kind that, when added, they make the whole larger.*
<margin>60</margin> *For otherwise, they would rashly rejoice in the name of a "part,"*
*and you would be grasping at something "divisible" based on false signs.*
*Furthermore, however many species of numbers or measures there are,*
*because they are repeated without end, each one of them*
*would clearly constitute an infinity—*
*no less than any others, and no less than all of them taken together.*
*Do you believe that more palms and fingers are required to comprise*
*immense space than paces, acres, and furrows?* <note>Bruno argues that if space is infinite, the unit used to measure it is irrelevant; you cannot have "more" miles than "inches" in an infinite span, as both sets are endless.</note>
*Would you, like a fool, seek differences of measurement*
*within the immeasurable, or distinctions of boundaries within the infinite?*
<margin>70</margin> *Perhaps the scale of numbers rests within the innumerable?*
*Therefore, desiring to proceed soberly so that you may*
*begin to know more from this point, conceive these secrets in your mind:*
*namely, that the "even" and the "odd" cannot exist among the innumerable.* <note>original: "nequit innumeris par esse vel impar"; Bruno posits that mathematical properties of finite numbers, like being even or odd, do not apply to the concept of infinity.</note>
*And let there be* <margin>(let there be</margin> *no more sets of twenty, or ten, or five,*
*or units <term>monads</term> (the fundamental, indivisible units of reality), than there are thousands,*
*when they are without end. Nor,*
*in the past ages (the world being eternal),*
*have more months elapsed, or more hours passed,*
*or more days or nights to come,*
*than there have been Winters, Summers, Autumns, or Springtimes;*
<margin>80</margin> *nor more <term>Lustra</term> (five-year periods), Ages, or high revolutions of Saturn.* <note>Saturn was the slowest known planet; Bruno uses its long orbital cycle to show that in eternity, even the longest "year" occurs as many times as a single hour.</note>
*Therefore, the same result is forged from these infinites*
*as from those others. For if one number were "less"*
*than another, that number would then have to be*
*considered finite. Furthermore, if you took one*
*finite thing from the whole—by whatever name it is called—*
*the entire number would then become finite because of that one.*

<meta>catchword: Qua-</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that in an infinite universe, all scales of measurement are equal, as infinity cannot be divided into "more" or "less" parts. He uses the example of an eternal world where the number of hours is equal to the number of years or planetary cycles.</summary>

He is arguing that in an infinite system, standard measurements like "inches" versus "miles" lose their meaning because infinity cannot be made larger or smaller.</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that infinite divisibility leads to the mathematical absurdity of "larger" and "smaller" infinities, using the example of a year having more "parts" than a month. He concludes that one must stop the infinite multiplication of parts and accept a fundamental "minimum" or atom.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bruno's critique of the Aristotelian (Peripatetic) view of infinite divisibility. On the previous page, he argued that treating space as infinitely divisible leads to mathematical absurdities where different sizes of infinity must exist within each other. Here, he turns his focus to the "minimum"—the fundamental building block—and why both nature and human reason require a stopping point in division.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
### GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA

*Go ahead then, you ridiculous counter, unhappy measurer,*
*And wanderer, enter into the progressions of reason*
*With countless numbers, in countless ways, and without ends or orders,*
<margin>120</margin> *An infinite multiplier without a beginning—and I wander.*
*Why does the "Stagyrite" <note>Aristotle, who was born in the city of Stagira.</note>, bleary-eyed of mind,*
*Not call his own much worse dogmas to the same mirror*
*Of examination when he attacks Anaxagoras? For [Anaxagoras]*
*Said that "homeomeries" <note>original: "omaomeras"; uniform parts that make up a whole, a concept from the pre-Socratic philosopher Anaxagoras.</note> are drawn out from a body without end,*
*And this perpetually, since "all things are in all things"?* <note>Anaxagoras’s famous principle that every substance contains seeds of every other substance, which Bruno uses to show that Aristotle’s own theories of infinite divisibility are just as paradoxical.</note>

---

<margin>1 & 19</margin> **I**t will be further established that in a finite whole there are no infinite parts, neither in act nor in potential, once the reasoning by which the Peripatetics <note>The followers of Aristotle, known for walking while they taught.</note> and philosophers of this kind approach the infinite becomes known. For they do not distinguish between a "boundary" <note>original: "terminum"</note>, which is not a part at all, and the "minimum," which is the first part. As far as the minimum is concerned, they must finally yield to reason.

First, on the part of nature when it dissolves things: in dividing a finite continuum, nature should not wander infinitely either in act or potential. Instead, nature must finally stop at that small thing beyond which it never divides, and in whose potential there is no further part. Nor should we believe that the active power of nature in dividing is greater than the passive power of nature—that is, the divisible matter itself—and vice versa. 

Likewise, they must yield on the part of the imagination, or mathematical analysis <note>original: "matheſis reſoluentis"</note>, by noticing how nature does not correspond to an infinite progression, nor does practical application support it. Whence even mathematicians, understanding more cautiously, will use the "infinite" to mean the "indefinite" (or "as much as you like"), but never for that which has no practical application and would be wrongly conceived. 

Now, therefore, they say indistinctly that a magnitude is not composed of minimums. From this view, it follows that magnitude is not made of parts, or is made of parts in such a way that they are not "first" parts. This is a prejudice against nature as a "composer," as if there were no first thing from which magnitudes could coalesce. Then it is also a prejudice against "art" <note>Here, "art" refers to human skill, technique, or applied science.</note>, since we can imagine nothing unless some first measuring part is assumed. 

It may be that what is taken as the first part in one task can be taken as the last in another. Nevertheless, however much one multiplies task upon task, one will never work without some first part being established, which is the "minimum" for that specific task. Therefore, because the operation of art is "indefinite," the minimum is also "indefinite." Likewise, because the operation is not infinite, there is no need for an infinite subordination where a smaller thing is perpetually placed under a smaller one. It is not the same in nature: for nature is determined toward a maximum and a minimum.

<meta>catchword: mum (minimum)</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues against Aristotle's theory of infinite divisibility, asserting that both nature and human mathematics require a "minimum" (a fundamental unit or atom) to function. He critiques the idea that matter can be divided forever without reaching a primary building block.</summary>
<keywords>Minimum, Aristotle, Anaxagoras, Homeomeries, infinite divisibility, Peripatetics, nature vs art, atomic theory</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno's argument from page 28, where he began to dismantle the Aristotelian idea that matter can be divided forever. He is moving from a critique of infinite parts to a positive definition of the "minimum"—the fundamental, indivisible building block of reality.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>ON THE MINIMUM, THE EXISTING, AND THE FREE</header>

[mini]mum <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of the smallest unit.</note> in all finite species. But for the Infinite (by the immortal God!), by what reasoning could there be a need for it?

¶ They say the <term>minimum</term> <note>original: "minimum"; in Bruno's philosophy, this is the indivisible "atom" or "monad" that forms the basis of all matter and geometry.</note> is no part. But I ask, what can they understand by the name "minimum" except the *first* part, before which there is no other? In every order of parts, must there not be a first? If the first did not exist, could anything of that order exist after it? How can you posit "prior" and "posterior" parts without a first? How can you say there are "greater" and "smaller" parts if there is no minimum? Is not the ratio of the minimum to the maximum (of the first part to the whole) established by nature just like that of the greater to the lesser? Just as the greater tends toward the maximum, does not the lesser strive toward the minimum?

¶ You say again that what [the mind] took as the first part, it can soon take as the last; or in another act of measurement, it can take it as a kind of "whole." So be it; I say that the mind thinks it descends by dividing magnitude from the finite toward the infinite, just as it ascends by multiplying numbers. In vain, surely, do you conclude that there is an "infinity in potential" <note>Aristotle argued that matter is not "actually" infinite, but "potentially" infinite because you can always keep dividing it.</note>, since you cannot assert any actual act that could worthily aspire to it. For what does it help to thrust forward that mathematical "potential" which is not bound to an act (as <term>Physics</term> <note>original: "Physica"; the study of the natural/material world as opposed to abstract mathematics.</note> is bound), into the infinite in vain, rather than reasonably into the indefinite? 

You say: "If a minimum touches [another], it touches with its whole self; and this will not make it larger, because one minimum cannot be outside another according to one part while being with it according to another part; for if it did, it would have 'part and part'—the part that touches and the part that makes it larger. Therefore, if it is a minimum, it does not make it larger. And just as it does not make one [larger thing], so many [minima] should not either. Therefore, the whole is not composed of minima. Therefore, by resolving a thing through the resolution of its parts, one never reaches a minimum." This is the ***Achilles*** <note>A reference to Zeno's "Achilles and the Tortoise" paradox; Bruno uses it here to mean a "strongest" argument that he intends to defeat.</note>. This, I say, is a confusion of terms, from which that universal ruin of logic collapses: the failure to distinguish the **minimum** from the **boundary** of the minimum, or the part from the boundary of the part.

¶ Let us speak more accurately. The *minimum* does not touch another minimum with its whole self, nor with a part of itself, but it can touch several minima with its **boundary**. Just as no body touches another with its whole self or a part of itself, but touches with its whole extremity or a part of its extremity. In a plane where a surface ends at a surface, or in a line where a part ends at a part, we do not understand that one part touches another by its own substance or by a part of itself, but through its <term>boundary</term> <note>original: "terminum"; the mathematical limit or edge that has no thickness or dimension of its own.</note>. Therefore, the boundary is that which is no part at all, and consequently, not even the smallest part. For it is contradictory to say that something is the "minimum of a thing" and yet "not a part of the thing." If you consider this, you will understand that a minimum, by a certain fixed number—not by one part or many parts, but by many boundaries—touches several things equal to itself. 

Thus, the minimum **triangle** touches three similar and equal ones; the **square** <note>original: "tetragonus"</note> four similar and equal ones; the **circle** six similar and equal ones <note>Bruno is describing the "packing" of shapes: a circle surrounded by six others forms a hexagonal pattern.</note>. The same judgment applies to **atoms** in bodies, each of which must necessarily touch several similar things with its extremity.

<summary>Bruno argues that the "minimum" (atom) must exist as the fundamental first part of any whole, refuting the Aristotelian idea of infinite divisibility. He resolves the paradox of how indivisible points can form a larger object by distinguishing between the "minimum" (the smallest part) and the "boundary" (the dimensionless limit where parts meet).</summary>
<keywords>minimum, atomism, Zeno's paradox, Achilles, geometry, boundaries, potential infinity, Aristotle, physical vs mathematical, circle packing</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical distinction Bruno is making between physical "parts" (atoms) and mathematical "boundaries" (limits). He is responding to the Aristotelian paradox that if things touch at a point, and a point has no size, then the whole world could be compressed into a single point.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>

->GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA<-

¶ You will say, quite stupidly, "Therefore something smaller than the minimum exists, since this point by which a minimum touches a minimum must be smaller than the minimum itself." Not at all, my friend; you are merely confusing yourself in your usual way. In this matter, there are two types of minimum: there is the minimum of that which touches (that is, the part), and the minimum of that by which the touch occurs (that is, the <term>limit</term> <note>original: "terminus"; in Bruno’s geometry, a "limit" is the boundary where two things meet, like a point or a line, which has no width of its own.</note>). You should speak this way: there is no part smaller than the minimum part, and there is no limit smaller than the minimum limit (which must necessarily exist within the minimum part). Larger parts touch each other with a larger limit, and smaller parts with a smaller one. Nor does every sphere touch another sphere at a "simply minimum point," unless it is an atom—which is the minimum sphere—whose point of contact is also the minimum within the category of limits. However, it is true that a sphere, no matter how large, touches an equal sphere with a part no larger or smaller than the way a minimum sphere touches its equal. Yet it does not follow from this that the large sphere touches at a "simply minimum point," because for it to be touched by such an extreme limit, it would have to be a minimum itself.

¶ Let the Sages <note>Referring to the "Sophi" or the established academic philosophers of his day, often with a hint of irony.</note> therefore say: a <term>limit</term> added to a limit does not make a thing larger. A limit is not a part. If a limit were to touch, it would touch with its whole self; therefore, magnitude is not constructed out of limits, nor out of points (which are limits), nor out of atoms, lines, or surfaces (which are limits). But it is quite right to say that a large surface has a line as its minimum part in terms of width—and simply a point—according to the meaning that signifies a "first part," but not according to the meaning of a "limit" by which one part touches another. Therefore, you should define the <term>minimum</term> as that which is a part in such a way that it has no parts itself, either in an absolute sense or within its own kind. Define the <term>limit</term> as that which has no part, and is not itself a part, but is the means by which an extreme is touched by an extreme, or by which part touches part, or whole touches whole. Thus, the limit varies according to the type of magnitude: there is one kind of limit for a line to a line, another for a surface to a surface, and another for a body to a body.

¶ It remains now to see whether it is necessary for limits to be infinite, since under the definition of limits, a point touching a point, a line a line, and a surface a surface, touch each other with their whole selves. On the contrary, I say that there is no "touching" of these things themselves; rather, they are the means by which the touch of other things occurs. They are not the things that stick together to create a quantity; they are the means by which other things, in touching each other, become contiguous or continuous. But just because they are not "parts" and do not increase size when added together—and consequently do not result in composition or integration—does it follow that they are infinite? Certainly not. For where parts are not infinite, either in actuality or in potential, how can the limits of those parts be infinite? Indeed, since the parts are finite, nothing belonging to them can be infinite. This is even more true where parts are continuous and joined to one another, where one limit is always shared by two parts. Therefore, in a body or in the depth of a continuum, there cannot be more limits than there are parts. The same applies to a surface, by which a body is always bounded in relation to another.

<summary>Bruno distinguishes between "parts" (the smallest physical units of matter) and "limits" (the mathematical points or boundaries where parts meet). He argues that while limits have no size, they are not infinite in number because they are tied to a finite number of physical parts.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, atomism, minimum, limit, terminus, sphere contact, continuity, finite vs infinite, points and lines</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bruno’s argument against the Aristotelian concept of infinite divisibility. Having established in the previous page that "limits" (mathematical points) are distinct from "parts" (physical atoms), he now argues that if physical matter were infinitely divisible, we would fall into logical absurdities where a finger is as large as a mile because both would contain an infinite number of parts.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM, BOOK [I]</header>

¶ 2. & 14. Therefore, the potential and the act of <term>limits</term> <note>original: "terminorum"; the boundaries or endpoints of a shape, like points on a line.</note> follow the potential and the act of the parts. And just as parts relate to even and odd, or many and few, so it is with limits—whose nature is entirely secondary—there is an indefinite analogy. Nor does the multitude of <term>accidents</term> <note>Properties that exist within a subject, such as color or size.</note> fail to follow the multitude of the subjects they belong to.

¶ 38. 42. If you define a "part" (as one ought) as that which, when added, makes a thing larger, and when subtracted, makes it smaller—because that which produces a quantity must necessarily be a quantity itself—then without a doubt, just as it was impossible to reach the infinite by adding parts together, it will also be impossible to reach it by dividing them. Why should that which is finite as a whole composition be infinite in the division of its parts?

¶ 59. 64. 95. What help is it to flee to "proportional parts"? <note>A mathematical concept where a whole is divided into parts that get smaller and smaller (like 1/2, 1/4, 1/8...), supposedly allowing for infinite division without ever reaching zero.</note> For however many they are, they must still be the kind of things that make a whole larger when added and smaller when subtracted. <term>Limits</term>, surely, are not themselves divided, but are multiplied as parts are divided; they are not "greater" or "smaller" things in themselves, but are simply the boundary of something larger, smaller, or the minimum. Therefore, in a situation where you can take any number of proportional parts forever: if you take these infinite parts from one place and add them to another, they would constitute no less a quantity than if you had added the same number of larger parts. This is because the "immense" does not consist of infinite "groups-of-five palms" in such a way that the same thing could consist of five times as many infinite "groups-of-five fingers." <note>Bruno is arguing that if you have an infinite number of parts, the size of the individual parts (a "palm" vs. a "finger") ceases to matter, which leads to a mathematical paradox.</note> Indeed, in the infinite, a palm, a finger, and a <term>mile</term> <note>original: "milliare"</note> do not differ; likewise, infinite minimum parts and infinite <term>ells</term> <note>original: "vlnae"; a traditional unit of measurement, roughly the length of a man’s arm.</note> do not differ; infinite units do not differ from infinite thousands. For these infinites cannot exist without those infinites, nor those without these. In the infinite, truly, if any single number is finite, every number must be; and if any one number were greater than another, the thing itself would not be infinite.

¶ 68. 104. Does this division into "always-divisibles" <note>The Aristotelian doctrine that matter can be divided infinitely.</note> not fall into the same trap with which **Aristotle** himself (where he deals with first principles) argued against **Anaxagoras**? <note>Anaxagoras was a pre-Socratic philosopher who believed everything contained a portion of everything else.</note> From Anaxagoras's opinion, one would conclude that within the infinite there are "infinitely infinite" things; and thus, in an infinite number, there would be numbers that are infinitely more numerous than infinity?

*will make no difference to our senses, and it may be thought*
*the same even when separated by many and various numbers.*
*As tiny living creatures* <term>animalcula; microscopic organisms</term> *sufficiently show by their own limbs—*
*limbs which can hardly be seen even when the body is whole.* <note>Bruno, writing in 1591, uses the concept of "animalcula" to explain that just because we cannot see the tiny parts of a microscopic creature, it does not mean those parts (and the atoms they are made of) do not exist.</note>
*Therefore, it is not permitted to think of things moved*
<margin>80</margin> *through a middle point between seven and eight, now slower*
*and now faster; they remain held back by the minimum.* <note>This refers back to the paradox on page 33. Bruno is saying that motion doesn't happen in "half-steps" of 7.5; it happens in discrete jumps from 7 to 8 minimums.</note>
*Add also that the sense by which you perceive the slow*
*and the fast, as well as the reason we mentioned just before,*
*does not come from a point that recedes by a thousand numbers.*
*And since this perception escapes the senses to such an extent,*
*it happens that whatever is divisible by many rules*
*may appear in both even and odd segments;*
*even though, in truth, there are a fixed number of monads* <note>original: "monades". Bruno uses "monad" to mean the fundamental, indivisible unit of existence.</note> *for every certain number.*

<meta>catchword: Con-</meta>

<summary>Bruno explains that the apparent "divisibility" of space and time is an illusion of human scale and perception. He uses the example of distant planets and microscopic organisms to argue that while we cannot see them, indivisible "minimums" or "monads" are the true, fixed units of all motion and matter.</summary>

when, however, the whole force of the matter should not be attributed

Furthermore, no small confusion is thrust upon the mind 
that does not distinguish the continuum, so that it is present in all things, 
and in various ways to various things; because although it is established 
by the senses that these things, in which part is adjacent to part, 
<margin>110</margin> run through in an uninterrupted <term>flux</term> <note>original: *fluxus*; a flow or continuous movement.</note>; nevertheless the 
continuum is not equal in all motions. But (my good man) I 
perceive many degrees. The faster a thing is moved, 
the more its flux must be thought of as "continuous," 
even if no mediating delay is perceived by the senses. 
This can be seen clearly in the intense flows of waters and 
likewise when they are slackened, and in drops falling from on high: 
which, just as they increase their motion, so they create a more 
continuous flux; and therefore it must be denied...

<meta>This page continues Bruno’s critique of the Aristotelian (Peripatetic) view of the continuum. On the previous page, Bruno argued that there must be a physical "minimum" (an atom) to satisfy both nature and logic, countering the idea that matter can be divided infinitely. Here, he explores the paradoxes of motion and speed that arise when one denies the existence of these indivisible points.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>
<meta>catchword: neris</meta>

### *The mobile continuum, motion, and place are subject*
### *To a single judgment: furthermore, remember that there are many reasons*
### *For the continuum, and diverse species.*

**T**here is a doubt standing on the firm bases before the eyes of the <term>Peripatetic school</term> <note>The followers of Aristotle, known for walking while they lectured.</note>, yet buried by a certain pretense of reverence for their leader <note>Aristotle, often called "the Philosopher" or "the Stagirite."</note>, which we desire to see revived. It concerns a rod whose length is divisible into infinite parts, and around which an inverse spiral line is understood to be drawn, proceeding from point C to the immediately following point D, and from D to E, and so proceeding further by the magnitude of its height and the multitude of its circles. Is it not possible, between the defined boundaries A and B, to comprehend an infinite line? There are then two points which carry the whole weight and all the "sinews" <note>original: *neruos*; meaning the core strengths or arguments.</note> upon which they lean.

¶ 19. The first concerns two things moved in equal time which consist of equal magnitude through an equal space, one being twice as fast as the other. Because as this one and that one move continuously to pass a point—which is <term>indivisible</term> <note>original: *indiuiduum*; something that cannot be cut further, like an atom of space.</note> (just as it is by the parts of divisibility, so it is by the times of its passage)—the one twice as fast would not have passed the whole of it while the slower one was delayed at its midpoint. Thus, just as in one point, so in all points, the difference would not occur by which the twice-as-fast object arrives at the end sooner.

¶ 32. It is as if the ratio of "slow" and "fast" were taken only from the perspective of the medium through which or over which the motion occurs, and not also from the <term>power of the impulse</term> <note>original: *virtute impetus*; the internal force or momentum driving the object.</note> by which the moving body is carried from boundary to boundary. It is also as if, in the continuation of motion, there were not certain degrees and differences by which something is called faster or slower; and as if the ratio were not taken from that, by which this object is carried twice as quickly. Therefore, while the ratio of the points to be passed remains fixed, if this object has advanced by one point, the other must have advanced by two points.

¶ But here is added what they argue in the second place. Because if the points were an odd number—for example, when the twice-as-fast object has passed seven points—at the same time the slower one must have passed either only three, or four; and then one would be more or less than twice as fast, which was not the original premise. Or it would have passed three and a half points, and then the point would be divisible, the contrary of which is held by both sides of the debate.

¶ 40. But to remove this difficulty (from which the whole engine of ignorance arises), one must look first to the distinction between the **minimum** <note>The smallest possible unit of a thing.</note> and the **boundary** <note>original: *terminum*; the limit or edge where one thing ends and another begins.</note>. Second, one must look to the various reasons and degrees of reasons by which the minimum possesses time, space (or place), body, and motion. Since the minimum of one kind is contained by the large of another kind, and of another
kind...

<summary>Bruno challenges the Aristotelian denial of atoms by presenting paradoxes of speed and motion, arguing that if space were infinitely divisible, we could not explain why one object moves faster than another over indivisible points.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bruno’s argument against the infinite divisibility of the continuum. On the previous page, he was discussing a geometrical paradox involving a spiral. This page begins by completing a sentence about the "kind of divisibility" and then moves to compare the scale of the Earth to the vastness of the stars, arguing that even the largest bodies are like "points" in a higher genus of measurement. He concludes by distinguishing between the "mathematical" or "sensory" minimum and the "natural" minimum (the atom), citing the Roman poet-philosopher Lucretius.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>OF THE BOOK ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM</header>

...kind <note>original: *neris*, the ending of *generis*, meaning 'of the kind'.</note> contains a great deal; just as the minimum in a body exists. The Earth <note>original: *Tellus*; the personified or physical Earth.</note>, compared to space—which in its greatest circumference can be seen to have consumed a dimension a thousand times the diameter of the earth—is vast. Whence even so ingens a globe of earth is but a point compared to the imaginable periphery among the fixed stars. And yet, here we do not step outside the same genus of the measurable. Now, what should be thought concerning the analogy of time or duration to motion? Of impulsive force to the impulse and the act of impelling? Do you wish for time not to exist outside of motion, so that you might eventually force me to introduce as many species of time as there are species of motion? For the single time of all things, which might subjectively exist in the first heaven <note>In the Aristotelian cosmos, the "First Heaven" (Primum Mobile) was the outermost sphere whose motion was thought to govern all time.</note>, has long since perished [as an idea], as we have indicated elsewhere and will clearly indicate. Since things are so, you will not judge the points of time and motion by the points of the subject [matter]; because a thing of one genus does not define a thing of another genus. The duration of one thing relates to the duration of another without a <term>univocal ratio</term> <note>A direct, one-to-one mathematical relationship.</note>. Just as the parts of one and the other are parts <term>equivocally</term> <note>Meaning they share a name but differ in nature or definition.</note>, and the minima of different definitions are minima by the condition of their own properties. Therefore, one is not measured by the other in this way, except perhaps as a matter of mechanical practice; for a skillful demonstration is done through homogeneous things, just as a measure is not of a different genus from the thing being measured.

¶ 4. Because of the lack of definition and the unreachability of the minimum, it happens that however much we divide, we do so not only according to diverse but even contrary differences of numbers, such as those proceeding from the even and the odd. If this diversity and opposition occurred not only from our own position [as observers] but also on the part of the subject matter itself, some would rightly judge that the <term>continuum</term> <note>A continuous extent, like a line or time, that can theoretically be divided.</note> is by its own nature simple.

<meta>This page continues Bruno’s exploration of the "minimum," the fundamental building block of reality. He transitions from discussing cosmic scales to the microscopic world, arguing that the true physical atom (the natural minimum) is far smaller than the smallest thing the human eye can perceive (the sensible minimum). He invokes Lucretian imagery of tiny organisms to illustrate this gap between perception and reality.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>
# GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA

*So far below our senses, and by so much smaller,*
*That, wondrously to our eyes, the smallest parts*
<margin>10</margin>*Are left behind in a long series. If indeed we see*
*Such tiny creatures <note>original: *animalcula*. Bruno is likely thinking of mites or small insects, following the Epicurean tradition of Lucretius.</note>, a third part of which could be noted*
*By no power of sight. How great then must we think their brain and belly?*
*How great their eyes? How great the heart? The nerves and organs*
*Which have obtained a certain shape from those other parts,*
*And likewise consist of various members on every side?*
*By how much these indeed stand apart from their own elements,*
*And how much art frugally presses its own minimum,*
*Is allowed to be seen by no argument.*

**M**inimum of nature, or the real minimum <term>minimum: the "atom" or fundamental particle that cannot be divided further</term>, is so wondrously contracted below the <term>sensible minimum: the smallest point a human eye can physically see</term> that it is not within the power of any art to define it. To this, we add: we know that the sensible minimum can be perceived by the most perfect sight, otherwise it would not be "sensible" at all. We distinguish the "sensible" in a general sense from the "sensible" in a specific sense. As for the first, nothing exists in a specific species that is not also affirmed in the general category: therefore, the "simply sensible" is defined from the general category of sensitive substance. As for the second, due to the diversity of species, the degrees of visibility and the power of seeing are diverse; hence, the sensible minimum stands at a great distance from the minimum of nature. For just as the former is defined by addition, so too must it consist of addition; otherwise, every minimum would be sensible <note>Bruno argues that if the atom were the same size as the smallest thing we can see, then everything would be visible, which is not the case.</note>.

But indeed, the minimum that can be tasted, touched, or perceived by the other senses must have obtained certain qualities through which it has an analogy to that sense; these qualities will only flow from a certain "composition" <note>i.e., a grouping of many atoms together.</note>. For indeed, according to the "first form" by which they are both minima and bodies, all things are held to be undifferentiated. The fact that subsequently some of these are sensible and others are not must occur through some addition; and it is not believable that any quality of this kind is founded immediately upon "prime matter" <note>original: *prima materia*; the formless, underlying substance of the universe.</note>.

Therefore, the material principles are distinct to some thinkers, with whose opinion we more closely agree: we do not believe that Light, the Moist Nature <note>original: *Naturam humentem*; likely referring to the elemental principle of water or fluidity.</note>, and Atoms (or the Dry) consist of a common matter. Furthermore, the vital power <note>original: *Vim animalem*; the soul or life-force.</note> is so indivisible that it is "whole in the whole and whole in each of the parts." By itself, and apart from its composition with matter, it suffers nothing from the conditions of place or time. Just as we do not understand it to be an "accident" <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a non-essential quality, like being "tall" or "musical," which cannot exist without a subject.</note>, such as a harmony or the act of another, we likewise conclude that it is a substance that is either non-material, or...

<summary>Bruno argues that the true physical atom (the natural minimum) is vastly smaller than the smallest thing humans can see. He posits that life-force is an indivisible substance rather than a mere byproduct of material arrangement.</summary>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the limits of human perception, Bruno now moves from biological "minima" (tiny organisms) to the physics of light and geometry. He explores whether the "point-source" of light can actually be seen by the eye, using this to further distinguish between mathematical points and physical reality.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>ON THE MINIMUM AND THE EXISTENT, BOOK 1</header>
C 4

...of that matter which does not fall into the same category as the three previously mentioned. <note>Bruno is likely referring to the traditional elements—earth, water, air—distinguishing light or fire as a unique fourth substance.</note> Zoroaster called this nature Light; Democritus and Empedocles called it fire. Now follows a judgment on the experts in optics and physics who use these propositions without making proper distinctions. If light (they say) were a point, it would be seen, or would be visible; the reason they give for this is that it is the nature of light to spread itself spherically, and thus it would be seen as a sphere. But this is the same as saying that point-like light <term>point-like light: *lux punctalis*, light conceived as a single mathematical point without extension</term> is seen indirectly and "by accident"—that is, through something else—just as we see a man or a plant through their external qualities, or a center through its surrounding sphere, or a living soul through the acts of life. Speaking more formally than the common people and the rhetoricians, we say that it is not point-like light that is seen (which we can define as a point neither by our senses nor by reason drawn from the senses), but rather the *diffusion* of light. 

For us, light exists in four ways: First, as a certain essence by which a composite or simple thing is formed. Second, as the "being" that is found within the composition itself or in a simple individual substance. Third, as the ray or splendor by which that outflow and propagation is perceived; in this way, the ray and splendor of the sun is light sent out from that light which remains incommunicably in the sun—that which is the formative power of this or that individual sun. This is different from the absolute light, a certain substance in itself, which Moses, <term>Hermes Trismegistus: a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a vast body of philosophical and occult literature</term>, and other Chaldeans and Egyptians called "First-born" in their accounts of Creation <note>original: *Genesis*</note>. Fourth, light exists through a gradual participation, as in the air, the Moon, the Earth, a mirror, and other things that use the name of "light" more loosely.

Given these facts, we say that point-like light (even if it were possible) can in no way be seen. I say it cannot be seen immediately or in itself because (to use the reasoning of the optics experts themselves) it does not form a pyramid <term>visual pyramid: in Renaissance optics, the cone of vision with its base on the object and its apex in the eye</term>. For whatever is seen is seen under the shape of a pyramid, the base of which is in the thing seen, and the apex in the eye. But from a point to a point there is no pyramid, only a most simple line. Nor is it seen indirectly, because from that spherical diffusion which makes light visible, we cannot conclude by sense or by reason that light is a point. Just as an equilateral triangle (even if it were truly equilateral, down to the point-like equality of its sides) cannot be judged so by the senses. For it is possible that it is seen, but not that it is seen *under the logic of equality*. Thus, it remains that point-like light is visible, but not under the logic or nature of "point-ness," but rather under the logic of diffusion.

*Nature is before our eyes, and in her bosom and her hand,*
*Whereby species are unable to cross a certain boundary.*

*Passes beyond it. This [extra division] we have said exists in vain;* <note>Bruno believes that while a mathematician can "cut" a line infinitely in his mind, in physical reality, you eventually hit an indivisible atom.</note>

He is building toward his central argument: that the universe is composed of indivisible atoms rather than infinitely divisible mathematical space.</meta>
