*Thus for various causes, and according to the varying*
*Order of matter, the sculptor, the painter, and the surveyor each accept*
*A different minimum, so that they may finally assign various parts*
*To the whole—some close by, and others far removed.* <note>Bruno explains that the "smallest unit" depends on who is looking: a surveyor’s smallest measurement is much larger than a painter's finest brushstroke.</note>
*Now the smallest circle is a center, and then that*
*Middle point which the sharp edge of sight is able to reach.*

**W**e distinguish the "minimum" in two ways, and not without cause: for there is the simple and absolute minimum, which must be of one single kind; and there is the minimum by hypothesis (or by supposition and relation), which is established differently according to the variety of subjects and goals. However, to establish such a variable minimum as a positive reality is not so much a property of Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to human technology, mathematics, or skill.</note> as it is consistent with Nature, which has defined for each individual species its own maximum and minimum. Just as there is a measure which a human's size has never exceeded, there is also a defined quantity of matter below which the form of a human cannot be preserved. <note>Bruno argues that there is a physical "limit" to how small a human (or any object) can be before it loses its essence and becomes something else.</note>

<margin>3</margin> ¶ 16. Here it is defined that a minimum is touched by a minimum, rather than one boundary by another. By these two meanings—distinguishing between the point and the atom—we are not confused. For a point which is a minimum, when added to another point which is also a minimum, constitutes a composite that can be dissolved back into two points. But a point which is a boundary, or the end of a magnitude, is never composed with another boundary as one part with another; rather, it is the means by which one part occasionally touches another. <note>original: "punctum" and "terminus". Bruno makes a vital distinction: an "atom" is a physical building block with size, while a "boundary" is just a mathematical limit with no size of its own.</note>

¶ 22. There are different kinds of minima: for here is the solid minimum, which is the <term>atom</term> <note>From the Greek 'atomos', meaning 'indivisible'.</note> and the primordial body; and there is the plane minimum, which is the point, by which (according to the manifold condition of the figure) one minimum body is touched by several minimum bodies. Just as any large circle on a plane is touched by exactly six equal circles, so too must the smallest circle be touched by only six minima. <note>Bruno is referencing the geometric fact that you can fit exactly six circles of equal size around a central circle.</note> Thus you shall judge regarding the atomic body, which must be touched by atomic bodies in its own way.

¶ 28. The minimum is also different for different things by a certain analogy and order. For what is large and composite to one person is not rashly accepted as the first and minimum by another: just as the first principles of natural things, or the beginnings of other disciplines, are primary for some but secondary for others. Thus, things which are derived and dependent for one person are the very sources for another. And not incorrectly, according to the variety of contemplation and method: for <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>Ancient Greek philosopher who believed numbers were the ultimate reality.</note>, the principles of things are the <term>monad</term> <note>The number one; the ultimate unit of reality.</note> and numbers; for <term>Plato</term> <note>Famous Greek philosopher; here Bruno references his 'Timaeus' where geometric shapes form the elements.</note>, they are atoms, lines, and surfaces; for <term>Empedocles</term> <note>A pre-Socratic philosopher who proposed the four elements.</note>, the four...

<summary>Bruno distinguishes between "absolute" physical atoms and "relative" mathematical boundaries, arguing that everything in nature has a minimum size below which it cannot exist. He cites ancient philosophers to show that the definition of a "first principle" or "minimum" varies depending on the field of study.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Bruno argues that while "minima" (the smallest possible units) are defined differently by different observers (like a surveyor or a painter), there is also a physical reality where nature limits how small a form can be before it ceases to exist as that thing.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO THE NOLAN</header>

...or simple bodies; for the Physician, these are the four humors <note>In ancient medicine, the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—were the smallest parts of the body that determined health.</note>. For those working with fire, they are the substances that are separated by the operation of fire. For the Surgeon or anatomist, they are flesh, bones, nerves, and cartilages. For the Painter, they are hairs, cheeks, ears, fingers, and eyes. It is not appropriate for a painter to look deeper into the principles of the external form. If a Surgeon tried to go beyond his boundaries to find the parts of these primary parts, he would be crossing into another category of study; likewise, a Physician would look ridiculous if, alongside the philosopher, he tried to abstract his study from a more specific subject. Empedocles <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 490–430 BC) who believed everything was made of four elements: earth, air, fire, and water.</note> begins his physics from the first things we can perceive; Plato from the first quantities; Pythagoras from the first things simply. Yet the Pythagorean monad <note>original: *monas*; the fundamental unit or "one" in Pythagorean thought.</note> exists before any monad situated in space. Matter is prior to bodies for Plato, just as qualified bodies are prior for Empedocles; the four simple elements are prior for Empedocles than the four primary complexions of those simples are for the physician. Thus, the rest follow in their own order. In the "scale of the knowable" <term>scale of things that can be known<gloss>Latin: *schala scibilium*</gloss></term>, some sciences take their beginning and first elements from lower levels, while others take them from higher ones. Therefore, in a certain analogy within the universality of contemplation, the "minimum" and the "principle" remain constant.

¶ 38. Therefore, returning now to the kind of measure: we consider that for a Sun-dweller <note>original: *Soliste*; those who place the sun at the center, or perhaps inhabitants of the sun.</note> with their assembly of planets, the sun is the center and a kind of minimum relative to the great world; just as any other smallest thing among the visible "minima" (along with its own circling planets) does not create a noticeable dimension when it is separated from our sight by a great distance. This Earth <note>original: *Tellus*</note> is a "minimum" compared to the eighth sphere <note>In the older model of the universe, the "eighth sphere" was the outer shell containing the fixed stars.</note> that people imagine. A circle drawn on a plane is a "minimum" compared to the Earth; a point in the middle is a "minimum" to the circle; and the very tip at its center is a "minimum" to the point.

### CHAPTER X.

#### There must be a minimum in every graded genus.

*AND according to the varying condition of subjects,*
*In the Divine, Logical, Physical, and Harmonic,*
*It is fitting to unfold the nature of things from the minimum;*
*I say that any genus, Species, Force, Order, Power,*
*Passion, sensible Form, Distance, Time,*
*Moment, Weight, Voice, Accent, Reason, Law—*
*All acknowledge their own minimum, which stands above every great thing.*
*Thus the minimum plane and the minimum solid are like the point;*
*And the body of the atom is not something that you, O learned one, can divide further.*
<margin>10</margin>*After this, we distinguish the "point" in two ways: let one be*
*A boundary, but the other be a final part. And these...*

<vocab>Giordano Bruno, Empedocles, Plato, Pythagoras, minimum, monad, atom, Earth, Sun-dweller, scale of the knowable</vocab>

<summary>Bruno explains that every discipline—from medicine to painting—has its own "smallest unit" or minimum, and argues that everything in the universe, from physical matter to abstract concepts like law and time, is built upon an indivisible foundation.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Bruno establishes that different disciplines (physicians, painters, philosophers) identify different "first parts" or minima according to their needs. He is now transitioning into a more abstract philosophical and mathematical defense of the "minimum" as a real, substantial part of the universe, rather than just a conceptual boundary.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header># ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM</header>

They agree because no part of either is ever granted;
But the **minimum** is the first thing, while no **boundary** is a part.
The crowds of the ignorant are carried along, never making this distinction.
Hence, one says that "nothing is composed of minima,"
Just as a boundary composes nothing, and is no part. <note>Bruno is critiquing the Aristotelian view that points (boundaries) cannot make up a line. He argues that while a "boundary" has no dimension, a "minimum" is a physical building block that does.</note>
But to say that a minimum is that of which not even one part is granted,
Because it is not itself a single part, clearly implies
Contradictions; for there is no doubt that the minimum exists
<margin>20</margin> In the order of greater and lesser things; it is the extreme in that order.
And finally, it is the first part, the single substance of the whole,
Through which whatever is called a "part" becomes a part.
The crowd of the ignorant is like a massive mountain-machine
Before our eyes: they cannot grasp this light of truth,
So that in physics they are worth nothing, and in the <term>mathematics of nothing</term> <gloss>original: *nihili mathesi*; likely referring to the study of points or infinitesimals that have no quantity</gloss>
They make no progress: since everything depends on this minimum,
Everything consists of it, is formed by it, and takes its shape from it.
A boundary is not a part, nor is it a "lesser" thing, or a "minimum";
For "minimum," "great," and "greater" are distinctions belonging to the part
<margin>30</margin> And the whole: above these sits the "greatest mass."
Therefore, just as the <term>monad</term> <gloss>the fundamental unit of number; "the one"</gloss> is a certain part of number, so the **point** is
The first part, and the substance of every plane: For by
The **point**, the **atom**, and the **monad**, there is the plane, the body, and number. <note>This is Bruno's "Triple Minimum": the Point for geometry, the Atom for physics, and the Monad for mathematics.</note>
And since it is not permitted to look into the infinite depth of the continuous,
It is permitted to cut it both equally and unequally,
And in various parts; it comes before us in various orders,
Yet it must, by its own nature, consist of certain numbers.
Between minima, there always exists a single boundary,
The beginning of that part, and the end of the one following.
<margin>40</margin> Hence it is that the ancients <note>Referring to the Pre-Socratic atomists like Leucippus and Democritus.</note> said that the **plenum** <gloss>fullness or solid matter</gloss> and the **vacuum** <gloss>empty space or void</gloss>
Are given in every continuum; and likewise of atoms...

<summary>Bruno distinguishes between a "boundary" (a conceptual limit with no size) and a "minimum" (the smallest actual building block of reality). He argues that points, atoms, and monads are the essential "first parts" that compose all geometry, matter, and numbers.</summary>
<keywords>minimum, boundary, atomism, monad, point, vacuum, plenum, mathematical physics, substance</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 43, Bruno further distinguishes the "minimum" as the fundamental, indivisible building block of reality. Having argued that the minimum is a physical part rather than a conceptual point, he now moves to describe how these minima interact, how they create space (vacuum), and why their primary shape must be spherical.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

> *When bodies are spherical in their meeting, they are always*
> *touched with a vacuum cast between them, and are destined to be dissolved.*
> *But to us it is clear that dry matter joins with a moist nature,*
> *and it is not the role of a vacuum to bind bodies together.* <note>Bruno is arguing that while the gaps (vacuums) between atoms allow for objects to be broken apart, the actual binding of matter is caused by "moisture" or a cohesive nature, not by the empty space itself.</note>

I There are as many kinds of minimums as there are things consisting in number, moment, and any magnitude: there is a minimum limit, a minimum plane, a minimum angle, body, ratio, science, sense, number, and others. There are also minimums under the same name that have different meanings, such as the point, the <term>atom</term><gloss>original: *atomus*; here referring to the physical minimum of a solid</gloss>, the line, and the plane—which sometimes signify the smallest part of the long, the wide, and the solid, and sometimes signify their boundaries.

¶ Since all things composed of parts consist of first parts: it follows that it is not by the nature of the thing, but by choice and convention, that the same quantity is divided equally or unequally; differently, I say, than a defined species of number is divided. <note>Bruno suggests that while we can mathematically divide things however we like, nature has fixed "first parts" (atoms) that cannot be divided.</note>

¶ 40. Minima, insofar as they are joinable, are also separable; they do not penetrate each other, nor are they mixed, but they only touch each other. Hence nothing is a solid body except for them, and therefore everything besides them is dissolved; their separation is no less possible than their union. If this is so, they do not touch at one common boundary, but at two distinct boundaries, between which two boundaries contact occurs; and hence, according to <term>Democritus</term> <gloss>An ancient Greek philosopher (c. 460–370 BCE) who first proposed that the universe is made of tiny, indivisible "atoms" and empty space</gloss>, a <term>vacuum</term> is cast between bodies. And because a minimum is touched by other minima not at all points, but at a certain defined number, it follows that between a sphere being touched and several spheres touching it, spaces of certain pyramidal shapes result—just as between six touching circles of equal size and one equal circle being touched, certain triangular voids are left behind. And by such spaces, both the bodies and regions of the smallest spheres and circles, as well as those of the globular <term>worlds</term> <gloss>original: *mundorum*; Bruno uses "worlds" to refer to stars and planets</gloss>, are contained by the intervening <term>ether</term> <gloss>original: *aethere*; the subtle fluid or "space" that fills the universe</gloss>. And such is the vacuum that Democritus and others understood to be outside the worlds—that is, cast between stars and stars. For by the name "worlds," they understood the stars (one of which is the <term>Earth</term> <gloss>original: *Tellus*</gloss>), and not that world encompassed by the imagined subject of universal revolution, as we shall demonstrate in its proper place. <note>Bruno rejects the Aristotelian idea that the stars are fixed to a single rotating sphere; he argues they are individual bodies floating in a vast, infinite space.</note>

*He will find composites with both straight and curved sides.*
*From this, he may know the simple <note>By "the simple," Bruno means the indivisible unit or atom.</note> within curves, and that the triangle is the first*

*And only this <term>void</term><gloss>original: "inane"; the empty space between particles</gloss> exists as something smaller than the minimum:*
*A triangle in a plane, and a pyramid throughout solid bodies.* <note>Bruno argues that while the atom is a sphere, the "empty gaps" between spheres take the shape of triangles or pyramids.</note>

*Namely, spherical matter and the void cast between all things.*

*Every resolution <note>The process of breaking a complex shape down into its simplest components.</note> acknowledges only the triangle,*
*Never reaching the circle; it comes to rest in the minimum triangle.*
*And although nature holds these parts in its own order*
*So that a void space smaller than the minimum body exists*
*Thrown between them; yet Art <note>Here, "Art" refers to the human discipline of geometry or measurement.</note> resolves the solid and the plane*
*Into parts bounded on all sides by straight lines, and allows*
*No further intervening void to be held in things*
<margin>50</margin>*Other than what is known to the senses, clinging and filling through the whole.*
*Plane belongs to plane, and solid to solid: and there comes*
*A minimum of the same form as the whole figure.*
*Just as when the parts of one side of a figure are found—*
*Whether it consists of equal sides on every part,*
*Or even if by some chance it is uneven or lopsided—*
*One can conclude from how many parts the whole consists,*
*When the number of these parts is multiplied by itself or by the rest.*

**1** The figure of the minimum in a plane is the circle; the figure of the minimum in a solid is the sphere. All plane figures differ from the circle, and all solid figures from the sphere, by the presence of an angle.

¶ **4** Every plane figure is resolved into triangles, and every solid figure into pyramids, as if into the very things from which they are forged.

¶ **11** That the minimum is indeed round is made manifest: First, by the senses, etc.

<meta>catchword: first by the sense, etc.</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that the sphere and circle are the true physical "minima" (atoms), while the triangle and pyramid are the smallest possible "voids" or geometric units. He distinguishes between the physical reality of round atoms (following Leucippus) and the geometric method of breaking shapes down into triangles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 46 regarding how our senses perceive the "minimum" or the smallest possible unit of matter. Having previously established that distance and motion tend to "round off" the appearance of objects, Bruno now moves into a formal geometric explanation of how circles, spheres, triangles, and pyramids serve as the fundamental building blocks of both geometry and physical reality.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>

# ON THE BOOK OF THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM

...sense and imagination. For any object that can be sensed or imagined as having angles can be perceived as smaller once those corners are removed. Secondly, this occurs by the thinning effect of nature itself; for we see that stones and even the hardest materials are worn down at their corners first. Thirdly, distance itself thins the apparent diameter of an object. To someone approaching closer and closer, objects take on the angles of their true shape and bulk—angles which appear blunted <note>The Latin *obtundunt* suggests a dulling or rounding effect caused by distance.</note> to those moving away.

¶ 31. In a plane, therefore, there are two primary figures that are both minimum and maximum: namely, the triangle and the circle. In a solid, there are two corresponding forms: the pyramid and the sphere. Between circles that touch one another, there are "curved-back" triangles; <note>Bruno is referring to the triangular, concave gaps created when circles are packed together.</note> between spheres that touch, there are pyramidal spaces with similarly curved faces. If you wish to divide a straight-lined <term>rectilinear</term><gloss>shapes made of straight lines</gloss> figure into a cycle and angles, you will find yourself with triangles composed of both curved and straight lines. From this, one can understand that the absolute minimums are the circle and the sphere, along with the curvilinear triangle and the pyramid of concave surfaces.

¶ 42. Furthermore, geometric practice does not need to carry out this resolution <note>The process of breaking a shape down into its smallest parts.</note> to such an extreme degree. Indeed, in the breaking down of straight-lined shapes—whether they are flat or solid—geometry might wrongly assume that empty spaces <term>voids</term> are mixed in. However, it is useful for the geometer to always assume that the "minimum" part is of the same shape as the whole. This is not possible with the circle and the sphere because, as will be explained a little later, their decomposition requires us to count a "minimum" of a twofold species. <note>Bruno suggests that while a square can be made of smaller squares, a circle cannot be perfectly composed of smaller circles without leaving gaps.</note> Now we have added the model <note>original: "archetypum"</note> for what has been said regarding flat and straight-lined shapes.

## THE FIELD OF LEUCIPPUS
<note>Named after Leucippus, the 5th-century BCE Greek philosopher who was the first to propose that the universe is made of indivisible atoms and void.</note>

->**(A grid representing the atomic division of space)**<-

| | | | |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 1 / \ 7 | 2 / \ 6 | 3 / \ 5 | 4 / \ 4 |
| 7 \ / 1 | 6 \ / 2 | 5 \ / 3 | 4 \ / 4 |
| 2 / \ 6 | 3 / \ 5 | 4 / \ 4 | 5 / \ 3 |
| 6 \ / 2 | 5 \ / 3 | 4 \ / 4 | 3 \ / 5 |

<note>This table represents the "Campus Leucippi" diagram. In the original manuscript, this is a 4x4 grid where each square is bisected by crossing diagonals, creating four triangles per square. Various integers (1 through 7) are placed within these triangles to demonstrate a mathematical or harmonic progression. The entire grid is surrounded by a decorative border featuring stars and circular symbols, representing the cosmic nature of these geometric laws.</note>

<summary>Bruno explains that while triangles and pyramids are the "minimum" units of straight-lined geometry, the circle and sphere are the true "minimums" of nature. He introduces the "Field of Leucippus" diagram to visualize how space is organized by these fundamental atomic units.</summary>

# CHAPTER XIII.

## The minimum and the limit are not quantities of the same kind.

**A**n edge is not any quantity itself, but the limit of a quantity;
> *Hence, the point possesses no dimension of this kind,* <note>original: "huius generis nulla est dimensio punctus." Bruno distinguishes between a mathematical point, which has no size, and his "minimum," which is a physical atom with actual dimension.</note>
> *But truly, the minimum is the first dimension of length*
> *And width; indeed, it is both of these together as a whole.*
> *The principle from which and out of which dimension arises is only a*
> *Limit. But the minimum is that from which a dimension is composed,*
> *Of which it is truly the first part, just as "one" is the part of a number.*
> *A plane encompassed on all sides receives two equal measures*
> *Toward the center; for it, width and length do not differ:*
<margin>10</margin> > *But a sphere, from its center, relates length, width, and depth*
> *Concentrically as parts, just as minimums are referred back to the One.* <note>Bruno suggests that all three dimensions of a sphere emerge from a single central unit.</note>
> *The circle is the greatest of planes, as the sphere is of solids;*
> *Here is the infinite plane, and here is the body without end,*
> *Which will then be endowed with such a condition on all sides,*
> *That the nature of the immense is closed by no margin,*
> *Just as a finite thing is known by only one point,*
> *It recognizes length, width, and depth as equal;*
> *Therefore it is equal on all sides, in every direction and everywhere.*
> *And it does not admit the difference of a finite sphere*
<margin>20</margin> > *Apart from that of the infinite, unless one seeks primary absurdities.*
> *But it is enough that the center is equal to everything around it:*
> *Whence it is necessary that the infinite be a sphere in its entirety.* <note>This reflects Bruno’s famous cosmological idea that the universe is an infinite sphere whose center is everywhere and whose circumference is nowhere.</note>
> *Therefore, any quantity whatsoever will have some figure,*
> *Like the sphere and the circle: the minimums and the quantities above all others.*

<summary>Bruno defines the "minimum" as the actual building block of space (like the number one), distinct from a "point" which is merely a boundary. He concludes that because all dimensions are equal from the center of the infinite, the universe itself must be considered an infinite sphere.</summary>
<keywords>minimum, limit, geometry of the infinite, atomism, infinite sphere, dimension, cosmological principle</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno's metaphysical geometry. On the previous page, he established that the "minimum" is a physical building block with actual dimension, whereas a "limit" (like a mathematical point) is merely where a shape ends. Here, he extends this logic to the infinite universe, arguing that the universe is an infinite sphere whose center is everywhere.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>ON THE EXISTENCE OF THE MINIMUM, BOOK I</header>

**E**verything that has dimension possesses some shape, and whatever you might believe to be shapeless is not the kind of quantity about which you can make any assertion. Since the differences of quantity are the finite and the infinite, it is not contrary to the reason of the "simply great" (which is the universe) to be infinite; indeed, it is rather more reasonable than it being bounded. This being the case, the universe should not be understood as having no shape, but rather a spherical one, which alone is suitable for the infinite. The spherical nature of the infinite differs from that of the finite because the undifferentiation and equality of dimensions <note>In a sphere, the radius is the same in every direction, making the dimensions "indifferent" or equal.</note> which the finite has from a single point, the infinite has from every point. Therefore, the infinite is "simply" and "wholly" itself, whereas the finite is only so in a certain respect, in some part, and through something external. For in the infinite alone, the center—which is the logic of the sphere—is in every part and in every boundary you might take. In a boundary <term>terminus|the mathematical limit or edge of a shape</term>, there is no dimension. In the <term>minimum|the smallest possible physical unit of matter, the atom</term>, dimension is originally undifferentiated. In another circle, two dimensions are actually undifferentiated. In a finite sphere, three dimensions compared from one point to another—at the edge and at the middle—do not differ. In an infinite sphere, this is true from every point and toward every point. What we say of the sphere in the solid, we understand the same of the circle in the plane. Therefore, we attribute the same shape to the greatest and to the minimum. The boundary is the principle of the dimensioned as "from where" or "concerning what"; but the minimum is the principle "out of which" <note>Bruno distinguishes between the "limit" (an abstract starting point) and the "minimum" (the actual material component).</note>. ¶ To Aristotle, it seems that being infinite contradicts the definition of a sphere and of everything shaped—indeed, even of a body. This definition he surely drew from the archive of the "finitists" <note>original: "finitariorum." Bruno's derisive term for philosophers who believe the universe is finite.</note>; therefore, he assumes the very thing he ought to have proven. There, he likewise marvels at the stupidity of Xenophanes <note>Xenophanes (c. 570–475 BCE) was a Pre-Socratic philosopher who described God/the Universe as an infinite, unchanging sphere.</note> for asserting infinity alongside sphericity; in this, Aristotle—the most stupid of all who go by the name of "leading philosophers"—fails, in his usual way, to reach the depth of the meaning.

# CHAPTER XIV.
## Again, that the minimum is evident in the great and the greatest.

**I**t *is not, therefore, for no reason that this wisdom found*
*The minimum, even though it stands so far from the senses,*
*Since I shall clearly show it in the first parts of the great,*
*And I will teach how to truly grasp it,*
*And how the moments of the minimum are revealed in anything great.*
*For indeed, we see that the minimum plane is a circle,*
*And we have revealed that the minimum solid is a sphere.*

<sig>D</sig>

<summary>Bruno argues that the infinite universe is a sphere because a sphere's dimensions are equal in all directions; unlike a finite sphere with one center, an infinite sphere's center is in every point. He mocks Aristotle for dismissing the idea of an infinite sphere and asserts that the circle and sphere are the fundamental shapes of both the smallest atoms and the vast universe.</summary>
<keywords>infinite sphere, Xenophanes, Aristotle, minimum, dimension, geometry, atomism, finitists, center everywhere</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 49, where Bruno discussed the "minimum" as a physical atom and the infinite sphere of the universe. <page-num>50</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

*Thus, therefore, to the first minimum, other minima are added*
*In a specific number to form a solid, just as in a plane figure*
<margin>10</margin>
*A circle admits no more than six equal circles,*
*Which fill the space around it and represent the first figure*
*Enlarged by the addition of such a gnomon* <note>In geometry, a gnomon is a shape that, when added to a figure, results in a new figure similar to the original. Bruno is using this to explain how atoms cluster to expand matter.</note>
*As is found in a circle increased by doubled parts,*
*In which the forms were constant with a straight boundary.*
*Indeed, here also you will find three meeting around one,*
*Where a figure divided through the middle*

*From this, you may see not only dissimilar parts*
*Which you cannot say are as large as the first parts;*
<margin>20</margin>
*But also how the cycle rises at every margin*
*As it does at the center, or stands apart with a certain distinction*
*On the remaining table, with a gathering number of equal sections* <term>tomorum|From the Greek 'tomos', meaning sections or slices of a whole</term>.
*Furthermore, if the first part receives a plane figure,*

### THE AREA OF DEMOCRITUS

<note>original: "AREA DEMOCRITI" — Named after Democritus of Abdera, the ancient Greek philosopher who first proposed that the universe is made of indivisible "atoms." Bruno uses this diagram to show how a central atom is surrounded by six others, a fundamental pattern in his theory of matter.</note>

*There is no need to suppose a double species of quantity* <note>Bruno argues against the prevailing view of his time that "mathematical" points (which have no size) are different from "physical" atoms. To him, they are the same.</note>
*For the practice of the surveyor: for this distinction*
*Will not hold up in this order when the physicist examines these things.*
*Therefore, let the archetype of the minimum be set before you,*
*So that as multiple minima gather around, it expands*
*And completes a figure of the same kind, growing larger,*
<margin>30</margin> *In the Area of Democritus, as it is called by its own name.* <note>original: "Area Democriti" — Named for the father of atomism, this refers to a diagram where a central circle is surrounded by six others, forming a hexagonal "atom" of space.</note>
*There is no need to foolishly devise another doctrine*

*No use follows from it, unless by chance*
*It is adapted to a solid.*
*Confusion grows with a manifold heap of errors*
*For those guides who, with always empty zeal,*
*Have increased the rules of the chord, the arc, and the sagitta,* <term>sagitta|Latin for "arrow." In geometry, it refers to the distance from the center of an arc to the center of its base (the chord).</term>
<margin>40</margin> *From there they fill various calculation tables and boards with rhythms,*
*So that, wretched, they teach how to go mad with great labor.*
*Meanwhile, they think they are performing a service*
*For Euclidean defects; the province boasts itself*
*The mother of such a great author. But the measure of the heavens and the globe,*
*And the ratio of the minimum, have been found from the straight and the curved,*
*And they satisfy the Elements of Euclid in every way:*
*Indeed, even more than is perhaps necessary for us;*
*Since so many parts of principles have been expressed*
<margin>50</margin> *Which, when reduced to a small number, powerfully allow*
*Whatever Euclid strives for in vain through two hundred means*
*To be grasped by just one,*
*As the doctrine of the minimum makes the way of discovery clear.*

*With a remarkable method, without which no Art of Measurement* <note>original: "Metria." Bruno uses this term for his specific system of geometry based on the "minimum."</note>
*will represent its true form: although I do not for that reason*
*offer any less gratitude to the ancients because they did not*
*bring the power of their principles to a better realization,*
*(For what mortal could have been able then*

*With these men as guides, it was possible to grasp a greater light,*
*and to lift the mind, formed deep within, to other things.*
*But we complain about the fools, who have dared to defile*
*ancient discoveries with new ones, and who labor*
*to multiply methods, by which that simple and single*
*appearance of the truth is confused, and hidden by much ambiguity.*

The **Minimum**, although it is imperceptible to the senses, can nonetheless be an object of contemplation. Indeed, the senses derive a certainty about the minimum from something large, when it is joined with the kind of reasoning we will reveal in the following sections. This is easily done through a single type of learning: namely, by understanding how and in what numbers bodies of the same shape and size come together to complete a larger body rising into that same shape.

¶ 14. Every rectilinear figure <note>A shape with straight sides, like a square or triangle.</note> indeed grows first by the addition of three equal parts, then five, then seven, and thus always by a sequence of successive odd numbers; just as, in its own way, a semicircle does also. <note>Bruno is describing the "gnomon" property: adding odd numbers of units to a square creates a larger square (e.g., 1+3=4, 4+5=9). This demonstrates how complex forms are built from simple units.</note>

¶ 19. And from this we will very easily grasp the instruction for understanding not only proportional parts, but also (by measuring more truly) equal parts.

¶ 23. This work differs, however, in the addition of spheres to spheres, and planes to planes: since in the former, it is necessary to consider the parts according to a double aspect of the figure, but in the latter, a single aspect. For to those who posit round atoms, there occurs by inescapable necessity a **vacuum** <note>original: "vacuum." A void or empty space.</note>, sometimes interspersed within the solid, and sometimes always outside the surface of the globes—as if diffused outside the body of the Earth—in order to draw a single continuous surface over a large sphere. <note>Bruno argues that because spheres cannot pack together perfectly without leaving gaps, the "void" is a physical necessity in an atomic universe.</note>

¶ 30. In a plane, the arrangement of this matter is demonstrated by the **Area of Democritus** <note>original: "Democriti area." A diagram representing how circles (atoms) pack together. Democritus was the ancient Greek philosopher who first proposed the existence of atoms.</note> where it rises into a hexagonal figure as a suitable number is added upon number: just as the minimum grows together in a continuous order with the sides increasing by one part each; where if you should wish the additional pow...

<sig>D 3</sig>

...that the void at the periphery is made equal to the solid parts. The subsequent 
triangular parts of the <term>void<note>In Bruno’s atomic theory, "void" (vacuum) refers to the inevitable gaps left between spherical atoms when they are packed together.</note></term> do not follow the first parts of the void as equals, just 
as circles do not, nor do they preserve the same shape. This 
would undoubtedly not happen in a spherical body, nor upon a 
concave surface when the parts are multiplied. Certain men 
wrongly and foolishly *pursue* the doctrine of triangles and 
other parts of a sphere on a flat plane; for we can no more 
define and precisely designate the proportions of a sphere on a 
plane than we can the proportions of a plane on a solid—namely, 
on a globular surface. 

Add to this that the very thing which is sought (though it is never 
to be found) would, once finally discovered, perform no useful 
service. *But to us* it is clear that the ratio of a spherical triangle 
on a sphere is altogether one and the same as that of a 
rectilinear triangle on a plane. Therefore, there was no need for 
anything to be added to the <term>Euclidean principles<note>Referring to the fundamental geometric rules established by Euclid (c. 300 BCE).</note></term>, 
but only that they should be better understood and perceived. 
And Euclid did not forget that every matter has its own reason, 
for he revealed certain rules of the triangle before the propositions 
of the circle, and others after the propositions of the circle. 

One should not take the multiplication of propositions and 
axioms as a sign of greater knowledge, but rather as a sign 
of greater poverty, stupidity, and ignorance. For a craftsman, 
the more perfect he is, the fewer means and instruments he 
requires to work. We shall offer an example of this matter in 
this proposal: with a far smaller number of *Theorems*, 
using only the circle and the radius (things which others 
have attempted in vain), we will demonstrate everything 
with ease once the method is mastered. It is true that 
*Euclid* called that method of his "royal"; he was able to 
compare it not with a better method hidden from him—which 
he certainly would have seized—but with clearly inferior 
methods, which he also despised with a more clever diligence.

<summary>Bruno critiques mathematicians who complicate geometry by treating spherical and plane shapes as fundamentally different. He argues that his "minimum" method simplifies these concepts, returning to a "royal road" of geometry using fewer axioms and tools.</summary>

# GIORDANO BRU-
# NO OF NOLA, THE BOOK OF 
# CONTEMPLATIONS ON 
# THE MINIMUM.

## CHAPTER I.

->From the light of a single truth, the light of manifold truth arises, just as from one absurdity, many consequences follow.<-

*Just as flowing fire, once ignited, flashes from a single*
*body; soon it propagates swift flames through a thousand*
*seeds with ease, and, acting as a ravager, shakes off the*
*sluggish darkness: in no different way, if the sober*
*mind has shone forth from a single point, suddenly the entire*
*horizon of reason conceives radiant lights from its bosom. Therefore,*
*when the power of the principle of the <term>minimum<note>In Bruno’s philosophy, the "minimum" is the indivisible building block of reality, similar to an atom, from which all larger structures in nature and thought are built.</note></term> has been made clear,*
*as well as the order by which it connects itself—the weaver of those*
*things which nature produces with abundant care, and art represents:*
<margin>10</margin> *thousands of things soon rush to be grasped by the keen*
*intellect. They are laid open as if by a single sight: just as*
*before <term>Hesperus<note>original: "Hesperus". The personification of the evening star (Venus), which appears in the sky before the other stars become visible.</note></term> summons the driven cohorts of countless stars.*

<summary>Bruno introduces the "Minimum" as the fundamental starting point of all knowledge, using the metaphor of a single flame or the evening star to show how one primary truth illuminates countless others. He argues that once the smallest unit of reality is understood, the complexities of both nature and art become clear to the mind.</summary>

<keywords>Giordano Bruno, the Minimum, atomism, logic, Hesperus, illumination, philosophy of nature, light of truth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument introduced on page 54, moving from the metaphorical "single point" of truth to a structured metaphysical explanation of how the "One" or "Minimum" acts as the source of all existence and knowledge. It transitions from a poetic address to a formal exposition of Matter, Form, and Agency.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>CONTEMPLATIONS FROM THE BOOK ON THE MINIMUM</header>

# *Therefore, seize the most important moments from all*
## *(O Distinguished Leader) in the very order in which they*
### *will present themselves to me to be recounted. And this is the first of all.*

**ONE** <term>Matter<note>The substance or "stuff" of the universe.</note></term>, one <term>Form<note>The internal essence or design of a thing.</note></term>, one <term>Efficient Cause<note>The agent or force that brings something into being.</note></term>. In every series, <term>scale<note>original: "Schala". Referring to the logical or cosmic hierarchy of being.</note></term>, and analogy, the multitude proceeds from the One, consists in the One, and is referred back to the One: which must be considered the first subject, the first exemplar, and the first agent. In this category (where we now are), what is despicable in size, modest, and minimal, is in power the Greatest, the Large, and the Whole: just as a spark of burning fire, if matter is supplied and its operation is not disturbed, is able to propagate itself into infinity, with nothing (whatever it may be in actuality) hindering its potential.

¶ Just as some bodies are very easily illuminated, penetrated, or set on fire, while others are moved with difficulty, and some indeed with the greatest difficulty; so too among the senses, the talents, and the intellects, some more readily grasp the light of truth and imbue a quality that is, as it were, kindred to the soul. Many are turned away from their natural impulse by various and multiple things, and remain hindered by disturbances. But very many, having obtained a kind of resistant power, are first of all most unfit to be wise. Secondly, fleeing the light with a certain aversion, they throw up a shield before their eyes. Thirdly, they are armed, incited, and hurled against the divine grace of that sun by the sting of a certain "zealous" <term>impudence<note>Bruno often used the term "Zelotae" (Zealots) to describe dogmatic religious opponents who fought against the "sun" of reason and natural philosophy.</note></term> and insolence. Hence, truly, the One is most wise; a few are wise in something; but the number of fools is infinite. For more fortunate intellects, the light then arises suddenly, is immediately recognized, and is most joyfully grasped. Then it is retained with primary diligence, defended with solicitous care, and adorned with more intense study. Then, consequently, it is increased through its own moments, propagated through its own numbers, and, having been enlarged and propagated through its innate divinity, it is cherished.

¶ After the setting of the sun itself (which the soul encounters through the <term>companionship<note>original: "contubernium". A term suggesting a shared dwelling or "tent-bonding," referring here to the soul's temporary residence within the physical body.</note></term> of this body), the soul proceeds and returns in a defined order from the minimum and the <term>monad<note>The "monad" is Bruno's term for the indivisible unit that is simultaneously a mathematical point, a physical atom, and a metaphysical soul-unit.</note></term> to the grasping of the large and the many, and from there to the innumerable and the immense. Just as from <term>Hesperus<note>The evening star.</note></term> seen alone one travels toward the multitude [of stars], so that from that multitude, by repeating the journey through the innumerability of the stars, one is able to reach through a reflected act <term>Phosphorus<note>The morning star, or light-bringer.</note></term>, seen lastly as a single point preceding the rising Sun; so that by this very path, being least submerged in the deeper shadows with the multitude of unhappy spirits, and having been moved forward to the sight of the monad of the most longed-for day, the soul may at last be drenched in the fuller light of the beatific sun.

<sig>D 4</sig>

<vocab>Matter, Form, Efficient Cause, Monad, Phosphorus (Morning Star), Hesperus (Evening Star), Zealots, Companionship, Scale</vocab>

<summary>Bruno explains that all reality stems from a single "Minimum" or "One," which contains infinite potential like a single spark. He also describes the soul's journey from the darkness of the body back toward the light of truth, moving from the singular to the many and back to the singular.</summary>

# CHAPTER II.
## That a true circle cannot be perceived by the senses.

*No matter the divine vigor of the sense,*
*It lacks the power to summon the circle to its own tribunal,*
*So that its truth might ever be revealed by its examination:*
*For the sense does not receive a variable subject in such a way*
*That the true [circle] is defined by anything but the mind as the indicator.*
*Since if in a circle a single point deviates,*
*It is not at all a circle. For it does not consist of parts*
*Equally distant from the center in every direction, as its*
*Nature requires. Under a similar condition I shall describe*
<margin>10</margin> *The remaining figures, because neither nature nor the*
*Ingenuity of art brings them forth from the womb of matter*
*Ready-made, so that they might be seen in the field and face of things.*
*Wherefore, just as a point recedes many thousands of degrees*
*Below the sharp edge of the sense, so does the orbit of a true circle.*
*And if a true one were presented before us, for that very reason,*
*It is not known to the sense, because it does not occur entirely*
*Under the indicator of sight in all those parts where order exists.*

The sense does not apprehend a true circle, for if even one of the innumerable points deviates, it is no longer a circle. And if a true circle were to be placed before the sense, the apprehension would be entirely <term>confused<note>In this context, "confused" means "fused together" or "undifferentiated," rather than "muddled."</note></term>, rather than distinct. Indeed, even when the appearance of a circle or any other figure comes to be apprehended by the <term>common sense<note>The internal faculty that coordinates and interprets data from the five physical senses.</note></term>, there is no faculty present that can distinguish the true form from the mere appearance. Therefore, to truly see a true circle placed before us as an object, there is a requirement for a manifest, proper, and equalized disposition—both of a single point and of the innumerable points, and of every point individually in relation to all others and to the center. I speak of those things which are commonly understood as infinite, but which we grasp as <term>indefinite<note>Bruno makes a distinction here: "infinite" usually implies something without end, while "indefinite" refers to things that are too numerous or small for the human mind to count or resolve.</note></term>. ¶ According to the <term>Peripatetics<note>The followers of Aristotle.</note></term>, there is one power that perceives the circle, and another power that doubts and defines its truth, or judges that it sees a circle. That second facul-

<meta>The text cuts off mid-word "facul-tas" (faculty) which will continue on page 57.</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that true geometric circles cannot be perceived by physical sight because the senses cannot resolve the infinite/indefinite precision of points required for a perfect form. Consequently, perfect geometry is a matter for the mind and intellect rather than sensory observation.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, circle, perception, common sense, Peripatetics, points, infinite vs indefinite, sensory limits</keywords>

...which they call the internal sense, it does not add or intensify light <note>original: "-cem," likely a continuation of "lucem" (light) from the previous page</note>; but it performs some sort of comparison or reflection upon it, specifically according to a certain action. Every faculty that follows after sight is commonly signified by the name of the **mind’s indicator**.

## CHAPTER III.

### It belongs first to the sensitive and external power to apprehend a circle.

**T**he mind alone defines the circle. Furthermore, its external
shape is suited to the senses only in so far as
it can be adapted; therefore it should be regarded as
their primary object. For after that, it is grasped through the shadow
of the *Phantasy* <term>Phantasy<note>In Renaissance psychology, the Phantasy (Phantasia) was the faculty of the soul that stored and manipulated images received from the senses.</note></term>, and through dense shadows.
The image of a distant parent is not as clearly
met by me as was the actual presence <term>hypostasis<note>original: "hypostasis" – here meaning the physical substance or "being-there" of a person's face</note></term> of their face when they were here.
Therefore, the phantasy lightly grazes the shadow of the image,
which dreams convey more effectively, since
<margin>10</margin>
the rested, neighboring faculty <note>Referring to the internal senses when the body is asleep and no longer distracted by external stimuli</note> does not allow the appearance
to be disturbed; nor is the sense distracted by various
external forces, but the interior sense turns itself
entirely toward a single object: and it beholds nothing in its own sphere
except what it has received from the external shores [the world outside],
whether that be whole and pure; or confused, and mixed in a thousand
ways and joined together, constructed from parts that were seen,
with countless arrangements converging there in various ways.
It is not granted to a sensible object to elevate itself into the true mental form;
just as the sense <margin>20</margin> cannot grasp the pure conception of the mind.
Different powers are suited to different things;
for the ear does not perceive light and colors,
just as the eyes do not perceive rumors <note>original: "rumores" – used here to mean general noises or sounds</note>, clatter, or song.

<summary>Bruno explains that while the mind defines perfect forms, the senses provide the raw material which the "Phantasy" or imagination then processes, often more clearly in dreams when external distractions are absent.</summary>
<keywords>Phantasy, internal sense, perception, mental form, dreams, sensory limits</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter III of Giordano Bruno’s "On the Triple Minimum and Measure." Following the previous page’s discussion on how the "phantasy" or internal mind processes images from the physical world, Bruno now addresses the reliability of sensory data.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>

->IORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA<-

*Though they cling within the embraces of a single genus,*
*The object of the senses wanders much less, therefore,*
*Outside the boundaries of its own kind, so that it might reach*
*The inner chambers of the mind <note>original: "triclinia mentis" – literally "the dining couches of the mind," where the soul "feasts" on or processes perceptions</note> as something pure. The definition*
*And nature of a true circle elude all senses; and the thing itself,*
*Which stands before our eyes, is measured according to the matter it is made of.*
*Furthermore, this same thing is truly known by no one, even if*
*It is truly present before us. Let us see soon whether this is so.*

**T**he Peripatetics <term>Peripatetics<note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy, who famously taught while walking in the Lyceum in Athens.</note></term> bring forward many arguments (as is their custom) to prove that the senses are deceived, in opposition to the ancient philosophers who maintained that the sense can neither be deceived nor deceive. Many difficulties of this kind are brought up, of which it is enough to note these: that which is reported back as a present and whole image may actually be from a visible thing that is corrupted or distant. From the immense size of the sun, the eye conceives the likeness of a disc one foot across; in the depths of water, it judges visible things to be larger than they are; a sound that is delightful to one person’s hearing is unpleasant to another’s. What one eye excels at seeing, the other fails to perceive. Food which a little while ago was sweet to a hungry man produces nausea once his hunger is satisfied. To the taste of a donkey, thistles are smooth, but to a human they are very rough; hemlock is the most pleasant food for a goat, but deadly to a human; to a hyena, nothing is more desirable than human dung, and humans are primarily delighted by dung and the putrid glands <note>original: "putridorum testium" – Bruno refers here to the pungent secretions of animals like the civet or musk deer, which were highly prized in Renaissance perfumery despite their "foul" origin.</note> of certain animals. A monkey is most beautiful to a monkey, but to a human, both are of a comical ugliness. Melancholics <note>Those suffering from an excess of "black bile," one of the four humors, thought to cause hallucinations or deep sadness.</note>, because of their physical disposition, seem to see things for themselves which are not visible to others. And a sick man could not judge the things that affect him poorly any differently if he is unable to compare his present state with a healthy one; instead, he would make assertions about things based on common judgment just as those blind from birth, not knowing what they are saying, will dispute about colors out of a habit of hearing about them.

¶ All these things depend on the thinness of a single thread, and are removed by its simple cutting. Elsewhere we have discussed this more extensively and at greater length than might seem necessary. But the truth of the matter is opened to those who have "eyes of the mind" through a certain simple and fitting indication, the principle of which may be drawn from a correction in our way of speaking. For those who speak more carefully will not say, "this smells good," "this tastes good," "this sounds good," or "this has a beautiful appearance," but will add: **to me, now, at this time.** He will likewise consider that good and evil, pleasant and troublesome, beautiful and ugly, are nothing simply and absolutely; rather, some of these things, when compared to various species and individuals (even when the objects themselves remain the same), receive contrary names from the senses, just as they are in reality <meta>catchword: contraries.</meta>

<summary>Bruno addresses the skeptics' view that the senses are inherently deceptive by arguing that sensory perception is not absolute, but relative to the observer, their species, and their current state.</summary>
<keywords>sensory perception, relativity, Peripatetics, subjectivity, epistemology, optics, aesthetics, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter III of Giordano Bruno’s "On the Triple Minimum and Measure." Bruno is expanding his argument that sensory perception is relative to the observer and the context, moving from physical size (the sun and moon) to moral and ontological truths.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>

->CONTEMPLATIONS FROM THE BOOK ON THE MINIMUM, 59<-

<margin>The text on the left margin is partially obscured by the gutter/binding.</margin>

...they induce various affects; the sun and the moon appear larger to our eyes than other stars, and yet from that location <note>The location of the stars themselves.</note> they are perceived as small. Wherefore all these and other names, which are reduced to these and similar categories, are **relative** according to their expression. According to the thing itself, however, all things that come to be signified by these same names must be considered relative. Nor, since there are many dispositions of the sensory organs, can it be said that some are simply in a good state and others in a bad one, or that they are honorable or dishonorable. For to copulate openly, or even to carry around erect genitals <note>original: "vitilia" – likely a transcription error for "virilia," meaning the male organs</note> with a naked body, is not indecorous, foul, or dishonorable for all species of living beings, nor even for the whole human race; rather, such things are made so for some by nature, and for others by custom. 

As other opinions arise and succeed to the status of being the "best" opinions, the very same things were seen among the <term>Druids<note>Ancient Celtic priests and judges known for their nature-based rituals.</note></term> and the <term>Magi<note>Ancient Persian or Chaldean sages; Bruno often uses them to represent ancient, pre-Aristotelian wisdom.</note></term> in the form of sacrifices to be celebrated before the people, and as decorous and pleasant, which now, even if committed in secret, are condemned as execrable and most foul crimes; such things must certainly be considered according to the path of the laws and the condition of the present state. 

But since **Philosophy** has learned to abstract from particulars and to lead the nature and condition of things toward an absolute judgment (as much as is possible), it must define "useful" and "good" in one way when speaking simply <note>meaning absolutely or in general</note>, and in another way when restricted to the human species. Hence, among the number of philosophers, one may see and hear those who have been driven by this principle to the point that they comprehend nothing as absolutely good or absolutely true in nature; rather, if anything of this sort exists, it must be sought above and outside of nature. 

Others have declared that whatever is said, even contradictions themselves, are true. Others (by clearly the same judgment) held that nothing is true, because through their intellect and contemplation they wished to transcend the limits of nature, and meanwhile they contemplated nature itself in a certain absolute unity and property; which nature, while it is diffused in particulars through opposition, is also diffused through a certain primary unity and many other subsequent unities. 

At will, it was permitted to the former (on the basis that there is nothing which is not good and present to someone) to place all things in the category of the good and the true, and consequently to hold that no sense is deceived. To the latter, however (on the basis that there is nothing which is not bad and hidden to someone), all things were thought to be referred to the category of the false and the unsuitable. 

But to us, who do not step beyond or outside the scope of the senses and <term>sensible things<note>Objects that can be perceived by the five senses.</note></term>, it seems necessary to define that nothing exists absolutely which can be more truly referred to one or the other side of a contradiction. And therefore, the same sensible thing, since it is referred equally to contrary subjects and powers—for these things which corrupt one, generate another; those which preserve one, destroy another—therefore, things are good and bad, pleasant and sad, for different individuals. Furthermore, to some more and to others less; to some always, and to others sometimes. From the side of the objects, therefore, there is no definite good and definite evil, no definite truth and definite fal[sehood]...

<summary>Bruno argues that truth, morality, and sensory perception are relative to the observer, the species, and the cultural context. He suggests that while nature contains contradictions in its particulars, philosophy seeks a higher, absolute unity beyond these sensory differences.</summary>
<keywords>relativity, perception, Druids, Magi, ethics, absolute truth, contradiction, nature, sensory organs, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno's argument from page 59 regarding the relativity of moral and physical judgments. He is moving from the idea of "decency" and "custom" to a broader epistemological claim: that "good" and "evil," as well as "truth" and "falsehood," are dependent on the specific faculty and measure being applied.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>

->GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA<-

...so that with a single intuition you may, for one side of a contradiction, call all things good; for the other side of the contradiction, all things evil; for another, nothing good or evil, so that neither side of the contradiction is true; and for yet another, all things both good and evil, so that both sides of the contradiction are accepted as true. On the side of the <term>powers<note>The "faculties" or "potencies" of the soul, such as the senses, imagination, and intellect.</note></term>, however—whether simply or according to time, or genus, or species, or defined individuals—good and evil, sweet and bitter, useful and useless are defined, and truth and falsehood are established according to the affirmation and negation of these things. 

When these matters are rightly perceived, it will be very easy to conclude that there is no sense that deceives or is deceived: for a sense always judges its own proper object according to its own capacity, which is its own true and unique measure. Indeed, even <term>sensible things<note>original: "sensibilia" – things that can be perceived by the five senses.</note></term> are not compared to the senses, nor senses to sensible things, according to some other "better" or "worse" kind of truth. Neither reason, nor intellect, nor the mind can know or define anything about these sensory objects, no matter how much those most annoying **sophists** <note>In Bruno’s time, "sophists" usually referred to the Scholastic philosophers of the universities who relied on rigid Aristotelian logic.</note> chatter with their own ridiculous little distinctions. For reason can judge colors even less than hearing can: just as the former is recognized in one kind of cognition, the latter is in another species of that same kind. It is the discourse of a stupid person to wish to reduce sensible things to the same condition of cognition in which **things of reason** and **intelligible things** <note>original: "ratiocinabilia & intelligibilia" – objects of the higher mind and logic rather than the physical senses.</note> are discerned. 

For sensible things are true not according to some common and universal measure, but according to a homogeneous, particular, proper, mutable, and variable measure. Therefore, to wish to define sensible things universally, insofar as they are sensible, is the same as trying to define intellectual things by the way of the senses. Likewise, concerning the act of the external sense, there can be perfection or defect according to its degrees. However, truth and falsehood—which consist in the comparison of the subject to an affection—simply do not exist there; for expressible truth and falsehood are found by necessity in <term>three terms<note>Bruno refers to the structure of a logical proposition: Subject, Predicate, and the Copula (the word "is"). Truth requires a judgment connecting these, which the eye alone cannot do.</note></term> (whether taken from the first or second adjacent). 

With the eye, we see light, color, and motion, but we cannot see "truth" with the eye. For the power is not located in the eye by which we might judge this to be true color and true light, and distinguish them from appearances of that kind. For the person who affirms that "man is an animal," it is necessary to know "man," and "animal," and that "animal" is present in "man," and other things which, as means and circumstances, concur in this simple and complex cognition. But it belongs to the external sense only to apprehend one simple image of a single object. Indeed, to contemplate the name, the truth, and the difference from other objects of a shaped and colored subject—based on its color and shape—belongs entirely to another, more deeply inherent power. We shall demonstrate in its proper order how this distinction of powers was investigated more laudably by the **Peripatetics** <note>The followers of Aristotle; Bruno acknowledges their work here while often disagreeing with their conclusions.</note> than it was successfully discovered.

¶ + Objects for that sense to which they are first and properly attributed are most...

<meta>catchword: ...most (maxime)</meta>
<meta>signature: ¶ +</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that the senses are never "wrong" because they operate by their own specific, particular measures; truth and falsehood only arise later when the intellect attempts to combine sensory data into logical propositions.</summary>

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>CONTEMPLATIONS FROM THE BOOKS ON THE MINIMUM</header>

...are present: and the acts that follow regarding the same object, along with the subsequent <term>powers<note>The "faculties" of the soul, such as imagination and memory, which follow after the initial sensory "power."</note></term>, serve by virtue of the first and preceding one. For there is no one who would dare deny that a deaf man can neither imagine nor dream of voices which he has never heard, nor a blind man of shapes and colors which he has never seen. Nor is it possible for these <term>species<note>original: "species" – mental images or representations of things perceived by the senses.</note></term> to be more effective in the internal sense than they previously existed in the external senses, either according to the whole and the same order, or according to parts and other orders.

¶ Nature intensifies one power with its own act all the more as another is relaxed or hindered. But the mind does not define a circle except after sensory information; nor will it be the task of the sensory power to define, just as it is impossible for it to apprehend the <term>absolute minimum<note>The smallest possible unit of matter or space; Bruno argues that because our senses have a limit to what they can see, they cannot perceive the "true" smallest particle.</note></term>. Therefore, even a true circle (if one were to be found in the nature of things) is not for any of those powers with which we are endowed to comprehend. Nor for that reason do we fail to grant the definition of a circle according to logic, or any other kind of abstract technical specification, through what would otherwise be idle reasoning.

### CHAPTER IV.
#### That a true finite circle is not possible in nature.

*Nature in the <term>forest of matter<note>original: "sylua" – literally "forest," but here Bruno uses it as a Latin translation of the Greek "hyle," meaning primordial matter.</note></term>, because nowhere shall we find*
*one part entirely similar to another part of the Great Mother.*
*(Just as atoms are like atoms) for the first principles*
*are perhaps endowed with this, if any are perhaps endowed with this.*
*Wherefore a circle consisting of parts equal*
*in all respects, and equally related to the center,*
*is nothing in nature; it is nothing crafted by art.*
*You see how, with straight and curved, the work*
*of the all-parenting nature unfolds every shape into a mixture;*
*how the straight is placed against the curved, and the void against the full:*
<margin>10</margin>
*For as much as flat surfaces cling together by their large faces,*
*so do flat and spherical things touch one another but slightly;*
*they meet at a point, being of the smallest consistent mass.*

<summary>Bruno argues that human imagination is limited by sensory experience and that perfect geometric shapes, like a true circle, do not exist in the physical world of matter and atoms.</summary>

*Bodies exist because there is a certain smallest thing below the mass of **atoms**—*
*A boundary, just as a single thing is composed of equal parts around*
*Several others, as they come together from various directions in turn.*
*For although it is necessary for a thing to be touched by many others, so that they may be*
*Enriched by a heap of first principles, and so that the **bodies** of things*
*May be increased; just as they take up the forces <note>original: "momenta" – here referring to the physical weight or moving force of the particles.</note> of various kinds.*
<margin>20</margin> *Therefore, because they consist of flat and curved surfaces by oblique contact,*
*Coming together more or less closely, upon this depends*
*The **rare** and the **dense** <note>The "rarum" and "spissum"; early scientific terms for how tightly packed matter is.</note> in things, to a greater or lesser degree;*
*But whatever things you see compacted from **flat figures***
*To be greater, these also consist of a firm body, just as that*
*Which may be clearly seen in the case of the **indomitable diamond*** <note>In the Renaissance, diamonds were considered the ultimate example of solid, indestructible matter composed of "flat" atomic facets.</note>
*Compacted from flat parts, as if into the parts themselves*
*Perpetually cut: because the diamond itself is not able to be seen*
*In a rounded form that would deceive the senses to such an extent,*
*Nor could it split the underlying body from any given*
<margin>30</margin> *Point, prevailing everywhere with its hidden tip,*
*Even though a circle is implied in every body,*
*Just as every figure is implied everywhere as no less than nothing,*
*Here where there is a finite flat body and space.*
*Since indeed nothing is pure, equal, similar, or one*
*Either to itself or to the whole remainder, or even in part.*
*Look, whether anything occurs to the **senses** surveying the halls of nature,*
*Any moist **rainbow** <note>original: "iri" – the rainbow, often used as an example of an optical illusion in nature.</note> more exact in its arc,*
*Or a test of art that does not shrink away; but it is itself*
*A kind of phantom, and a reflected image of light*
<margin>40</margin> *Toward the sun from the body of an opposite cloud, sending*
*To our sight an appearance which always deceives the*
*Attentive observer, for it is always thought to be seen from a center:*
*However, it is no small difficulty to understand how as one*

## ON PERCEPTION AND THE ILLUSION OF THE CIRCLE

<unclear>A</unclear> center can exist for those placed in various stations, 
No less to the right than to the left; for to those standing 
Many paces apart, there seems to be 
The <unclear>ve</unclear>ry same and identical center. This is because the image of light 
Migrates to the eye in a spherical <term>spectrum</term> <note>original: "spectro" – here referring to the visual image or appearance of an object as it travels through a medium.</note>, just as 
The <term>horizon</term> <note>original: "horizon" – the limit of one's vision, which Bruno argues is relative to the observer.</note> appears equal to one looking out 
Equally through the whole, whatever it may rotate with it; likewise, an image 
Sent from a flashing cloud <margin>50</margin> 
Under the dewy air, just as 
A figure might come forth from the bright splendor of a mirror, 
Which comes to meet whoever is present in that same appearance; 
Although it is not seen from the same point, 
It is as if it were seen from the same point. 
So, when the sun strikes the back of a spectator on a slope, 
<unclear>An</unclear>d when a stone falls headlong into quiet waves, 
The waves are pushed into a circle by the weight of the falling object, 
In a continuous order, so that the same <term>cycle</term> <note>original: "cyclus" – used here to describe the expanding ripples or "circles" in water.</note> is believed <margin>60</margin> 
To grow together. Furthermore, it is always new, and 
Continually in one place after another, so that the blow 
Seems to be poured out from the middle to the furthest edge, 
<unclear>An</unclear>d is thought to have grown into a great circle. 
In this way, a perfect circle will be reckoned everywhere 
To be unfolded; yet this appearance must not be considered true, 
Unless the shape of the falling stone were made exactly so, 
No wind were to breathe, and the liquid <term>water</term> <note>original: "lympha" – a poetic term for pure water.</note> 
Yielded everywhere in an equal manner and at an equal time. 
But since one part cannot be so completely equal to another, <margin>70</margin> 
For there are no distinctions in the <term>temperament of matter</term> <note>original: "materiei temperies" – the specific mixture or consistency of physical substance.</note> 
Throughout the whole—even though the eyes are deceived by the 
Continuous flow and the easy pulse of the movement. 
Voice and sound have a similar cause, though the medium varies.

<summary>Bruno explains that geometric "perfection," such as a perfect circle or a fixed center, is an optical illusion created by the limitations of our senses. He uses the example of ripples in water to show that what we perceive as a single, growing circle is actually a series of distinct, imperfect movements in a constantly changing medium.</summary>
<keywords>optics, perception, geometry, ripples, horizon, atoms, medium, illusion, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

Here, he explains the physical reasons for these imperfections, citing the inconsistency of the medium (air or water) and the continuous flow of atoms, leading to his conclusion that geometric perfection exists only in the infinite or the infinitesimal.</meta>

*And though they may be captured diffusely by the empty orb,*
*Yet their progress is not governed by an equal law on all sides.*
*For the permeable air does not yield equally on all sides*
*To continuous motion, even though the wave of the sea*
*Is tranquil or stands constant under the ultimate law of rest*
<margin>80</margin> *Which nature allows; nevertheless, all its parts*
*Flow, and they receive distinctions of the thick and the thin* <note>original: "crassi, & discrimina tenuis" — referring to variations in density within a medium like air or water.</note>,
*Which you cannot perceive with your eyes. For this reason,*
*The parts turn themselves unevenly, they let themselves in, and are carried along.*
*And as often as the wide horizon captures the cast of sight,*
*Restoring the appearance of a perfect circle and placing*
*The form of a sphere before the eyes, it is so reckoned; but all this*
*Image is deceptive: for the air is not clear in every part*
*With an equal balance, so that the great harvest of the visual ray*
*Is poured out equally from the arc on all sides.*
<margin>90</margin> *Therefore, you will nowhere find a true circle* <note>original: "cyclus" — Bruno argues that because the physical medium is always changing and uneven, a mathematically perfect circle cannot exist in the material world.</note>
*Visibly present in the action of nature, or by the power of art,*
*Since the object and the power of perception flow continuously,*
*And cannot coincide with themselves or others at an equal pace.*
*Thus light and heat are cast forth from a central fire;*
*Thus the fragrant thing gives off scent, with <term>minima</term> <note>original: "minimis" — the smallest possible particles or atoms that Bruno believes make up all matter.</note> cast around on all sides*
*In a continuous outflow, which fly out from things carried through the world,*
*And are likewise flowing back into them from the world.*
*But they do not succeed continuously in equal parts,*
*At equal times, in equal spaces, with equal power or in an equal place;*
<margin>100</margin> *Although they are always poured out on all sides in a spherical*
*Approach, they thus decrease and thus fail.*
*Wherefore you might say that the true infinite is the one*
*Sphere and circle, because it is simply the minimum*
*And the great above all, in which nothing adheres unevenly.*

<summary>Bruno argues that perfect geometric shapes like circles and spheres are optical illusions because the physical mediums of air and water are always in flux and uneven in density. He concludes that true perfection and symmetry only exist in the "Infinite" and the "Minimum," where all such irregularities vanish.</summary>
