--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno’s argument against the Aristotelian (Peripatetic) view of the cosmos. Having argued on the previous page that perfect geometric shapes do not exist in the physical world due to the unevenness of mediums like air and water, he now expands this to a universal principle: everything in nature is in a constant state of flux and change, governed by the movement of atoms.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>CONTEMPLATIONS FROM THE BOOKS ON THE MINIMUM</header>

**A**ll natural things (though some more so, others less; some more slowly, others more swiftly; some more clearly, others imperceptibly) are continually altered, shaken, moved, and agitated. For this reason, although a single form may appear fixed for some time for our use, standing according to a single rule, we nevertheless judge it simply impossible, by the very nature of the thing, for it to remain truly constant. This fact is not so hidden from the common people that they do not also admit that a true circle <note>original: "verum circulum"</note> is not to be found in matter exposed to the senses. Furthermore, the sublime deities of the Peripatetic <term>Peripatetic: a term for the followers of Aristotle, who walked while teaching</term> minds, just as they created spheres, orbits, and the entire heaven with its "movers" out of nothing—that is, out of a fifth essence <note>original: "quinta essentia" — the celestial aether, which Aristotle believed was a perfect, unchanging substance distinct from the four earthly elements</note>—so they could very easily co-create that kind of philosophy. This philosophy, dealing with immobile and invariable causes (consisting of an invariable matter, or no matter at all), would understand those circles and globes that a geometer could imagine as most perfect to be caused within the heavens.

But we (who are not accustomed to grazing upon the mere heard word to satisfy the soul's hunger, but rather demand the bread of better senses and more universal reasons) have a place where we may contemplate and admire the power and possibility of the impartible and all-powerful God in nature, not stripped of its own action. Let us bring this forth without the support of those light-headed dreams. Now we see—not where, according to the predictions of the holy prophets, such a sun, moon, stars, orbits, and movers are rolled back into their own vanishing nothingness—but rather, with a clearer mind and the senses as our guide, let that one immense etheric space be beheld (which is the same as it was before the aforementioned ages of the heavens). In that space, all things are new: those stars, which are said to consist of that dreamed-up fifth substance, may fall, rush down, and be driven into nothingness; and in their place, a most venerable and fertile species of worlds (reducible to the same body as this one which we inhabit) may be seen to have most happily succeeded. Likewise, a New Earth rises, which in the face of the Moon, Venus, and Jupiter, emerges from the opacity, darkness, and dregs of seed-bearing bodies.

->*The convex of heaven, and the wandering orbits with their intertwined*<-
->*Orders, leaning upon the twin poles of the axis—*<-
->*A single day will give this celebrated world to destruction,*<-
->*Whereby a better species will be spread open from the high sky.*<-

We hold that whatever is discerned by the eyes pertains to these same elements and is equally subject to the perpetual order of variability and change. And we comprehend that, except for atoms <note>original: "atomos" — Bruno revived ancient Greek atomism, believing that while the universe is infinite and changing, it is built of tiny, indivisible, eternal units</note> (which always remain the same in their own proper substance), all compounds—and things consisting of a certain physical composition—cannot remain the same for even a single moment. Each of these things is continually altered in all its various parts by the outflow and influx of innumerable atoms.

¶ 8. Hence, nothing is simply straight, and nothing in its composition is simply circular; besides **atoms** <term>atomos: in Bruno's philosophy, these are the indivisible, fundamental units of both matter and spirit</term>, nothing is simply full, and nothing is simply a vacuum <note>original: "vacuum"</note> except for the space that lies between three atoms meeting on a plane, or four atoms meeting in a solid. Consequently, nothing is simply continuous and one, except for the atom, the universal space, and the substance that exists simply between bodies and those things whose existence is to be contemplated around bodies.

¶ 22. Nothing seems more composed of parts arranged in a flat plane than a diamond itself. Nothing seems more like the arc of a semicircle than a rainbow, nothing more like a circle than the horizon <note>original: "finitore" — the "finisher" of the sight</note>, nothing more like a hemispherical cavity than the night sky. Yet, in these and other things—whether natural or, even more so, artificial—a most obvious inequality will appear to those looking closely, both on the part of the object itself and on the part of the senses and the surrounding circumstances.

¶ 100. Therefore, we judge that only the one infinite <note>Bruno believed the universe was infinite and that only this totality could be considered a perfect, equal whole</note> is truly round, spherical, and equal in every direction.

# CHAPTER V.

## It is impossible to show two figures or lines in matter that are entirely equal, or to repeat the same thing twice.

*They deny that matter can ever unfold true figures,*
*Yet they do not cease to seek them within it;*
*And with reason as their guide, even by the metric ruler* <note>original: "Metrica amussi" — a "metrical level" or carpenter's rule used for precision</note>
*A certain sought-after polygonal shape always results.*
*But as we discern the true standard by the light of nature,*
*We do not presume so highly in the things that are made.*
*We know how trembling the first operation of measurement* <term>metria: from the Greek for "measurement," referring here to geometry</term> *is,*
*Which is the foundation for the rest that must be done in turn:*
*How can anyone truly divide a straight line into equal parts,*
<margin>10</margin> *Or make lines truly parallel? Or let the angle itself be*
*Exactly what one desires; or let the perpendicular fall truly?*
*Neither the hand nor the sense perceives equal parts twice;*
*You will not find a man who is the same weight*
*Twice, except when tested on a crude scale;*

<summary>Bruno argues that perfect geometric shapes like circles and straight lines do not exist in the physical world, as all matter is composed of discrete atoms. He asserts that due to the constant flux of nature and the limits of human measurement, no two objects or dimensions are ever truly identical.</summary>

<keywords>atoms, geometry, vacuum, infinite universe, measurement, inequality of matter, Giordano Bruno, polygons</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 66 regarding the physical impossibility of perfect geometric shapes. Here, Bruno focuses on the failure of physical instruments—like compasses and rules—and human senses to achieve true precision, because the material world is in a state of constant, inconsistent change.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>CONCERNING THE SMALLEST THINGS FROM THE BOOKS ON MEASUREMENT</header>

The instruments used to handle these matters do not remain constant, neither in nature nor in the mind, if one seeks to work with perfect precision <note>original: "amussi" — literally a mason’s level or rule, used here to mean "with absolute exactness"</note>; for it is not possible to touch the true center.
One will not be permitted to draw a straight line equal to a previous one,
Nor to touch that same line again in its entirety.
Indeed, one can never retrace the exact same path completely.
<margin>20</margin>For each individual moment brings forth different counts and quantities of things,
Individual parts which would not unite by any swift force.
Who, then, could be thought to have provided exact instruments,
Here, where the measurement of the flow <term>fluxus: the continuous change or motion inherent in all physical matter, meaning no object is ever "still" or "identical" to itself from one moment to the next</term> is never the same, so that a boundary might stand at an equal distance from another, not even once?
For one flow is thicker than another, just as weight, the medium, the subject, and a thousand other concurring factors are all variable.
Nor will the compass <term>circinus: a pair of dividers or a compass used for drawing circles</term> fail to deceive you, if its point is always changing,
Now one way, now another, as it moves along more contracted,
<margin>30</margin>Or more extended; since even a straighter edge tracing a plane does not touch the same number of points as one that is more oblique. The traces of the mark vary.
One who attempts to correct <note>original: "rectificator" — a person or tool intended to straighten or align</note> does so in a single space as if the error begins there, simply because that is where it is first noticed;
Yet he does not see that the error persists at every single point,
And therefore he merely transfers the discovered error,
Attempting to correct a single instance from a vast accumulation of many.
Nor can the means by which he repairs things be any less uncertain or empty: the senses, reason, order, power, application, time, and the rule <term>norma: a carpenter's square or a standard used for checking right angles</term> in which he must place his trust.
<margin>50</margin>But individually, these are no less false than the whole;
For if they were true, they would still be rendered false by their association with falsehood,
Or if even a single firm application is not achieved, then they can never be reduced to a single, consistent rule.

<vocab>Instruments, center, flow, dimension, compass, reason, order, power, rule</vocab>

<summary>Bruno argues that human instruments and reason cannot achieve geometric perfection because matter is in a state of constant flux. He contends that every physical measurement contains inherent errors that are merely shifted rather than corrected.</summary>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno’s metaphysical argument that true, perfect equality only exists in the divine "Monad" (the ultimate, simple unity of all things). In the physical world of matter and motion, no two things are ever truly identical or equal, because everything is in a constant state of flux and differentiation.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO THE NOLAN</header>

*Where Unity and equality are a single simple essence,*
*There you might find something that is perfectly and simply equal,*
*But in all other things, as they follow their fixed sequences, they fall short,*
*And are excluded from that standard. There are no two weights, no two lengths,*
*No two voices, harmonies, or numbers that are perfectly equal in every respect;*
<margin>50</margin>*Neither are there two motions, nor even parts of a motion, that are identical*
*In every way. For this reason, those who wished to define*
<margin>(all</margin>*Motion as "unequal in kind" <note>original: "specie... iniqui" — here "iniqui" carries the sense of being uneven, unbalanced, or lacking the 'equity' of perfect symmetry</note> seem to have judged well.*
*For that which is perfect, whole, and possesses all—*
*This itself is Equality; but motion is always unequal,*
*Both to itself and to others, even if you try to capture just two moments.*
*You have found no kind of thing to which you could assign a particular*
*Double or twin that you could conceive to match in every*
*Part, even when examined by our dull senses <note>original: "crassi examine sensus" — the "coarse" or "gross" examination of the physical senses, which fail to perceive the microscopic differences between things</note>.*
*Indeed, one thing never becomes two except through the inherent difference*
<margin>60</margin>*Between all things, which allows one to be distinguished from the rest.*
*A "particular thing," they say, is that whose unique set of properties*
*Cannot be identical to the properties of any other.*
*Since these things descend from the general form to specific numbers, they must*
<margin>(you must</margin>*Descend away from Unity <term>monas: the Monad or the primary unit of existence; for Bruno, this is the divine simplicity where all contradictions and differences are resolved</term>. They must take on the distinctions*
*Of matter, and fall under every name as distinct things.*
*And do not think that simply because we observe the "greater" and the "lesser,"*
*A perfect "middle ground" of equality has been found between them.*
*For although numbers exist to be observed in their simplicity,*
*One kind of number is never equal to another kind,*
<margin>70</margin>*Because a number is always becoming either larger or smaller.*
*What will you say when so many factors converge—the types of numbers,*
*The forms, the differences in matter,*
*In time, in place, in the efficient force <term>vis efficientis: the "efficient cause" or the active power that brings a thing into being</term>, and the actual state of being?*
*To say that ten trees are equal to ten other trees...*

<meta>Catchword: As</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that perfect equality and symmetry are divine attributes of the Monad, while the physical world is defined by constant variation and inequality. He asserts that no two physical objects, motions, or even moments in time are ever truly identical.</summary>
<keywords>Monad, equality, inequality of motion, particularity, matter, efficient force, physical difference, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bruno’s metaphysical argument that while human logic and mathematics can conceive of perfect equalities and abstract measures, these do not exist in the physical world. He argues that because all physical objects are "composite" (made of parts) and in a state of constant change, no two things can ever be truly identical, nor can a physical object even be identical to itself from one moment to the next.</meta>

# CONTEMP. EX MIN. LIB.
## E 3

*...you have become accustomed, surely by logical reasoning,*
*And not by reality; for indeed individual things are one, and all things are unequal <note>original: "iniquè" — meaning uneven or lacking the perfect symmetry of the divine Monad</note>.*
*Even if the measure and the thing measured are taken as a single*
*subject, they will be conceived of neither as equal, nor as one;*
*but they are distinct, as the logical is from the physical, for which*
*a comparison of the same kind is not at all suitable.*
<margin>80</margin>
*Art does not match nature, nor does the image of reason match the things made;*
*nowhere does nature match nature, nor art match art:*
*Although it is necessary for all things arising in various kinds*
*to pass through a certain confused middle state, before*
*they reach their own proper goal <term>metam: the goal-post or finishing line of a race; here, the final form of a thing</term>: it does not follow from this*
*that they possess the specific nature of simpler things.*
*Now, just as the reasoning of number, or a line growing*
*by fixed limits, do not make equal any smaller thing,*
*when they rise in order toward other limits*
<margin>90</margin>
*in this pursuit, so that they may finally touch the stationary goal <term>metam</term>,*
*so too a composite thing will never be equal to another composite thing.*
*Thus, where reason and mathematics <term>mathesis: the formal discipline of mathematical learning</term> conceive*
*the length of this thing simply, as if it were that one; you may take*
*any equal part you like from every mass, just as you take*
*a smaller part in a larger one. But you will never be able*
*to precisely match a mass with a number (because a man is not equal to a man,*
*nor one composite to another composite) at any time.*
*For that very thing does not remain the same in those same parts*
*once a single moment of time has elapsed,*
<margin>100</margin>
*Because it consists of certain limits, for which a wide*
*boundary—not an atom <term>atomus: here, the smallest possible unit of measurement</term>—is prescribed; but also its parts*
*are carried along in a continuous flow and outflow <note>original: "fluxu effluxuq;" — the Heraclitean concept that matter is constantly entering and leaving a body</note>.*
*A circle is not reached by those same numbers*
*By which it was once touched, just as neither the same...*

<summary>Bruno argues that mathematical abstractions like "equality" do not apply to the physical world because all matter is in a state of constant flux. He asserts that no two physical things are ever truly identical, and even a single object changes from one moment to the next.</summary>

-># GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA<-

*You will not return to the spring twice as one and the same person,*
*Just as you will not be able to see the same flame of a lamp twice;* <note>original: "Bis repetes fontem..." — Bruno is referencing the famous fragment of Heraclitus: "No man ever steps in the same river twice, for it's not the same river and he's not the same man."</note>
*For certainly it flows faster than any sense can perceive,*
*And having been rapidly dissolved, it flies to be scattered through the air,*
*Converted into smoke, although it is believed to consist in a single*
<margin>110</margin> *Appearance, and sometimes to remain fixed.*
*But [nature] gives to each instant of time another and yet another [form];*
*For truly, as new matter is always supplied,*
*So the rapid fire always rapidly drives out something new,*
*Which does not remain the same in number <note>i.e., numerically identical over time</note> as itself,*
*Any more than the matter does, without which nothing is produced,*
*Or, as the common people think, it conceives it to be of a*
*Foreign body; but it only recedes after being scattered and dissolved,*
*As the reasoning of the method urges us to show in its proper place.*
*Therefore, since the substance of the body makes you a*
<margin>120</margin> *Composite thing, clearly visible in certain numbers,*
*You will never be able to be called "one and the same" twice.*
*Add to this that, just as in physical things, the mind does not find*
*An actual simple minimum <term>minimum: the smallest possible unit of matter, or an atom</term> any more than nature actually resolves;*
*For Logic and Mathematics <term>Mathesis: the formal discipline of mathematical learning</term> teach that there is*
*A minimum within a certain class <note>original: "certo in genere"</note>, but not a minimum in the abstract;*
*If this is so, I add: Neither can anyone ever define*
*A simple maximum, nor any simple middle point*
*Equally related to the extremes;*
*For nothing truly exists, if it does not become one with eternal things.*

**T**he figure, which they <note>referring to mathematicians or traditional philosophers</note> admit is not true in matter, they nevertheless think they find according to the canons of reason even to the most exact degree; meanwhile, the outcome convinces them that they labor under a double misfortune. We, however, while in our work we neither urge anything nor presume: since [we assign] those operations and numbers to nature itself, individually and according to its own more common rea-
<margin>sons</margin>

He asserts that while mathematics can conceive of perfect "minimums" and "maximums," these do not exist in the physical world of composite, changing bodies.</summary>

->## CONTEMPLATIONS FROM THE BOOK ON THE MINIMUM<-

<note>original: "CONTEMP. EX MIN. LIB." — abbreviation for *Contemplationes ex Minimum Libros*</note>

...we recognize that [physical] things are not geometrically regular; for you will never observe, whether you look at particular things or at the species <note>The "form" or "type" of a thing</note> existing in nature, a thing end at the very same minimum <term>minimum: the smallest possible unit or "atom" of a thing</term> from which it began. For although there is simply one magnitude for a minimum, yet for the order of each species and for each individual within those species, various minima are supposed to exist, just as the various species to be produced are subjected to change. One species is the beginning of another, just as an approach is given from the form of the embryo to the form of the animal or the human without a total dissolution <note>i.e., the embryo becomes the animal through gradual change, not by being destroyed and rebuilt</note>.

16. For us, the **minimum** is also distinguished by different turns and actions even in the same subject, so that it is not possible to designate the middle point of the same line twice, nor to take two parts of a circle's edge that are entirely equal. Likewise, one cannot trace the same footprint twice with a pair of compasses, nor press the two feet of the compasses down again in a way that is entirely equal to the previous markings to designate equal intervals.

20. Indeed, nothing concurs in an effect—whether materially, instrumentally, circumstantially, efficiently, or formally—that does not change at every single instant.

33. That zeal of the **rectifiers** <note>Scholars who tried to "correct" physical observations to fit perfect mathematical models</note> proceeded from a hollow and stupid spirit; they judge there to be an error only where it is first perceived by the senses, and they believe the error begins there and is specific to that point.

¶ 44. Out of many things concurring toward a "rectification," if one part stumbles, all things are thrown into confusion. This is just as we understand the many motions by which the earth is revolved, or as they themselves understand the motion of that star-bearing **firmament** <note>The "firmamentum stelliferum," believed to be the outer sphere of the universe</note>: if even one motion is irregular (as certainly must be the case, such as the one understood to come from the change in the center of gravity), it follows (as we show in its proper place) that all others must be irregular. Nor is it possible to find in any species two individuals that are entirely concordant or equal; I mean to say that two days, two orbits, or two years cannot be reported as equal and similar, not only in relation to other things, but even absolutely in their **physical power** <term>physica potentia: the actual material reality and energy of a thing, as opposed to its abstract measurement</term>. Therefore, all those who strive to define the magnitude of a year with exact precision are pointlessly worried.

¶ 83. If the height of two men or plants were like the length of two **rational lines** <note>Lines that can be measured in whole numbers or exact fractions</note>, there could undoubtedly be a specific instant in which each one, according to its length, is equal to the other—whether it is so now, will be, or was at some time. For all of us humans will arrive from a minimum stature, through the middle stages, to an adult height that is perfect according to each person’s own characteristics. From this, it must follow that everyone, at a certain instant, reached the same linear length. However, this is to compare a **mathematical line** to a line at that instant, but not to compare one physical man to another. For you must recognize that his height consists of innumerable lines, and I say that it is not possible for those lines, in their specific types and sizes, to be entirely concordant with the innumerable other lines [of another person].

<sig>E 4</sig>

<summary>Bruno argues that perfect mathematical equality and "rectification" are impossible in the physical world because nature is in a state of constant, infinitesimal flux. He asserts that no two physical objects, time periods (like years), or measurements (like a person's height) can ever be truly identical due to the underlying irregularity of matter.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, minimum, physics vs mathematics, measurement, change, human growth, celestial motion, center of gravity</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno's critique of abstract mathematics when applied to the physical world. While the previous page discussed how physical objects never occupy the same space or "minimum" twice due to constant change, this page expands that logic to the nature of numbers themselves, arguing that human counting systems are arbitrary and do not reflect the complex "numbers" of Nature or the divine.</meta>

# GIORDANO BRUNO THE NOLAN

¶ 51. Equality exists only in those things that remain forever constant. **Mutable** <note>subject to change</note> things, however, are always unequal: both to themselves at any two moments, and to others in all instances.

¶ 66. In numbers, although one may be greater or smaller than another according to its kind, a specific type of number is never found to be truly equal to another. Consequently, just as the genera and species of things are distinguished by such degrees, individuals within each species are also found to vary by degree. Thus, within the human species, the characteristics of all animal species are clearly reflected; we must assume this is no different in all other species (though it remains more hidden). For example, in the species of the horse, there is also a likeness of the human, the ox, the dog, the donkey, the ape, and the sheep; in plants, this similarity is even more concealed. In one place there is more, in another less; here it is simple, there composite—just as must occur through the numbers and degrees of various **complexions** <note>The specific physical makeup or "temperament" of a body</note>, which never entirely coincide with the degrees of another.

And it is truly the mark of a most senseless mind to think that the nature of numbers possesses differences in the same way we do: namely, by proceeding through odd and even, unevenly even <term>unevenly even: an ancient mathematical category (Latin: pariter par) for an even number that, when divided, eventually yields an odd number</term>, or by counting from tens to tens and hundreds to hundreds, or by other such methods of reasoning. Furthermore, to assert that gods, demons, and any rational beings define the numbers and measures of objects by this same series of reasoning, or that they operate under the same conditions of justice and injustice, equity and inequality—this is surely the claim of a "breast without a head" <note>An idiom meaning a person who possesses spirit or heart but lacks any intelligence</note>.

For the wise, it ought to be a most certain truth that both numbers and the logic of counting are as diverse as the fingers and heads of those who count; their intentions are not the same. Therefore, those things that belong to the numbers of all-producing Nature can never coincide with our own numbers. Furthermore, the differences between equality and inequality—which to us seem few, slight, external, or even invisible—can have almost nothing in common with those incomprehensible numbers of Nature.

¶ 74. Thus, ten men and ten horses, which are defined by the same specific number in arithmetic, clearly possess the same "equality" and "number" only under an **equivocal** name <note>A term that sounds the same but refers to different things; e.g., "bank" of a river vs. "bank" for money</note> in the realm of Physics; they do not possess it under the same **univocal** <note>Having a single, identical meaning in all contexts</note> logic.

¶ 98. Nothing variable and composite remains exactly the same in its parts or the order of its parts across two moments of time, since the outflow and inflow of **atoms** <term>atoms: For Bruno, these are the fundamental, indivisible "minima" that make up the universe; he believed they were in constant motion</term> is continuous in all such things. Therefore, regarding the ratio of the primary integrating parts, you cannot name the same thing twice in such a way that the name signifies exactly one and the same reality both times.

¶ 122. If, in a given quantity, it is impossible to define a maximum, a minimum, or a central point, how can any specific "how much" <note>original: "quotum" — a definite quantity or portion</note> of that magnitude be defined? Moreover, if any one of these remains indefinite, how can the whole—or any other part in relation to the whole—be determined?

<meta>catchword: CAP. (Chapter)</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that human mathematics (like "ten") is a simplified human construct that fails to capture the infinite complexity and constant atomic flux of Nature. He asserts that every individual and species is unique, and because matter is always changing, a thing is never truly identical to itself from one moment to the next.</summary>
<keywords>atomic flux, theory of numbers, species variation, physical equality, equivocal naming, the minimum, infinite nature, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

# CHAPTER VI.
## A Physical Excursion to Contemplate the Nature of the Soul.

**T** *herefore you might say that nature is only atomic* <note>original: *atomam*; referring to the "indivisible" or the "minimum" which is the building block of all reality</note>
*Forever, which has either no shape, or one specific own shape.*
*Therefore the nature of the mind* <note>original: *animi*</note> *is found to be divine,*
*Which no otherness, nor any passion* <note>In the sense of being acted upon or suffering change from the outside</note> *destroys,*
*And which, though it is subject to fate, and enters*
*Into a part of a composite thing, scarcely remains for a single moment*
*Affected by a like lot; yet it persists in one order of number.*
*Retaining this with a vigorous mind, and bringing it back*
*To the lofty gaze of the intellect, you will be shaken by nothing*
<margin>10</margin>
*That might make you think there is anything great in human affairs.*
*Fortune dwells in the mind's deaf assemblies;*
*Never will any pain, or any pleasure,*
*Provide anything that tears the man from his station.*
*For however many things come to be, are changed, fall and rush*
*Continually toward one thing after another, you will believe them to be non-beings.*
*But by meditating on nature in its perennial form,*
*To which you ought to conform and keep yourself*
*Similar, you will find yourself a sharer with the gods*
*In life: and you will say, "This is our substance."*
<margin>20</margin>
*Nothing that motion, time, and fate bring back [is our substance],*
*Things which, while they exist, do not exist; which you might consider*
*Lofty only for this futile reason: because they stand above you while you remain below.*
*But by speculating further on the minimum; let us supply*
*Matter to the mind, which may raise itself to the greatest things.*

**T**he indivisible <note>original: *individua*; meaning that which cannot be divided further, i.e., the atom or monad</note> substance alone is being <term>being: *ens* in Latin, referring to the fundamental reality of a thing</term>, while the rest are truly in being—accidents <term>accidents: properties that can change without destroying the thing itself, like color or position</term>, assistants, and composites surrounding and arising from being—not otherwise...

<summary>In this poetic and philosophical passage, Bruno argues that the mind (or soul) is an indivisible, divine substance. While physical objects are in constant flux and "non-being," the soul remains constant, allowing the philosopher to transcend human suffering and fortune by aligning with the eternal laws of nature.</summary>

<keywords>soul, mind, atomic nature, minimum, substance, being, accidents, divinity, fortune, indivisible</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the "minimum" or the indivisible, moving from the mathematical concept of the monad to the physical and spiritual reality of the soul. It explores how certain things—like sounds or spirits—exist as whole entities that cannot be broken into parts, even when they occupy large spaces or multiple bodies.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
-> GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA <-
<meta>catchword: co, tum.</meta>

...which we have called the monad, the substance of numbers. First, we say that **atomic nature** <note>original: *atoma natura*; meaning the "indivisible" nature of things that cannot be split further</note> is twofold: namely, **negative** and **privative**.

In the **negative** sense, atomic nature is twofold: accidental and substantial. The first is accidental, such as a voice, a sound, or a visible appearance <note>original: *species*; the visual form of an object as it travels through the air to the eye</note>. These things, unfolding themselves in a complete sphere, are whole wherever they are. Indeed, every eye standing around a thing receives its one shape in its entirety; every ear receives one voice completely. For it is one thing to be received more intensely by some and more weakly by others, but quite another to be received as a whole by some and only as a part by others. <note>Bruno is explaining that you don't hear "half" a word just because you are further away; you hear the whole word less loudly. The "atom" of the sound is not physically divided.</note>

The second negative type is substantial, such as a **demon** <note>In the Renaissance sense, a *daemon* is a presiding spirit or intermediary being, not necessarily an evil entity</note> or the **soul**. The soul is whole in the whole body, or even within the entire life-horizon of **Earth** <note>original: *Telluris*; Bruno views the Earth as a living, ensouled being</note>, by whose life we live and in whose being we exist. This occurs first by a certain common act by which we live within the whole sphere; then by a proper act by which we live in a specific horizon or hemisphere. From this, we find that magical rites and contacts are performed, which reach out to those far away, and even to the deceased and those recently dead (provided their corpses have not been cremated). For the soul recognizes its own bodily matter everywhere, a fact we shall reveal most clearly in the book on the **Physics of Magic**. 

Furthermore, the soul acts by a more specific power by which we live in this organized body; even if a part of it is torn away, it still pertains to us and remains under the guidance of our soul. This is manifest from the fact that wicked sorcerers inflict intended injuries upon the body and mind by using a person's "superfluities"—excrement, nails, and hair. It is seen most clearly in this: when a man dies, a nose that had been fashioned for another person from that dead man's flesh will rot away. <note>This refers to a famous 16th-century belief regarding early plastic surgery; it was thought that a skin graft from a donor would decay the moment the donor died, due to a lingering spiritual connection.</note> Finally, the soul acts through that most intimate condition by which we, as minds, enter and exit the "ocean of spiritual substance" through the gate of the heart.

Beyond this is the individual substantial nature: the soul that exists in the greater horizon, such as the **Soul of the Earth** in the "great assembly," which we consider the second form of the world. Higher still is the **Soul of the entire assembly**, which exists in the universe. The supreme is the **Mind of minds, God**, the one spirit filling all things entirely. He is the ordainer above and outside every order, and therefore he is to be magnified above all things by the voices and praises of the gods. He cannot be named, spoken of, or fully grasped by any of the gods, worlds, or minds. He is not, I say, comprehensible, except by his own unique and most simple infinity; yet he is equal to all.

¶ In the **privative** sense, however, atomic nature (which I call "privative" not because it is capable of being divided, since it is an atom, but because it is the principle and substance of magnitude) is also twofold. First is the first part of the "discrete," which is the mathematical unity of arithmetic...

<summary>Bruno defines two types of indivisibility: things that cannot be divided because they are non-physical (like sound and the soul) and things that are the basic building blocks of matter and math. He describes a hierarchy of souls, from the individual to the Earth, the Universe, and finally God.</summary>
<keywords>monad, atom, soul, Earth, magic, God, indivisibility, Neoplatonism, sympathy</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of the "minimum" or "atom" begun on the previous page. While the previous page discussed atoms in terms of spirits and sensory perceptions (like sound and sight), this page expands the definition to include logic, geometry, and time, eventually leading into a discussion on the immortality of the soul and God.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>CONTEMPLATIONS ON THE MINIMUM</header>

...[conceived] <note>completing the word from the previous page</note> then as what is universally called the **logical atom**: just as for the Grammarian the atom is the syllable, for the Dialectician <note>a scholar of logic and formal argument</note> it is the word, and for the Poet it is the foot <note>the smallest unit of poetic rhythm</note>. Secondly, there is the primary unit of the continuous; and thus, according to the many types of the continuous, the atom is manifold. There is also the **moment** <note>original: *momentum*; the smallest unit of change</note> in active, passive, and neutral qualities. For here we find the minimum pain, the minimum sweetness, the minimum color, and the minimum light; there, the minimum triangle, the minimum circle, the minimum straight line, and the minimum curve. In duration, the atom is the **instant**; in place, it is the minimum space; in length and width, it is the point; and in a physical body, it is the first body itself.

With so many types and primary species of atoms existing, it follows that in no category or species is an atom capable of being corruptible, or mortal, or created from other things. Rather, some of these, by their primary property and meaning, belong to those things which are specific **accidents** <note>original: *accidentia*; qualities that exist only within a subject, like the color of an apple or the sound of a voice</note>. When they exist, they do not exist through internal composition; and when they cease to be, it is by no means through dissolution or division (since they do not exist within a subject as a part), such as a voice or a visible appearance <note>original: *species visibilis*; the visual form of an object as it travels through the air to the eye</note>. Thus, it is said of them, according to an accidental mode, that they now "are" and now "are not"—simply because they exist at one moment and not at another. It would be most improper to say they are "generated" or "corrupted," just as it would be to say they are "born," "die," "walk," "run," or "spin." Yet others are, by their proper and intrinsic nature, eternal, immortal, incomposite, and indissoluble: **souls**, **the Gods**, and **God**. We shall define these more accurately in our *Metaphysics* and in those works concerning the soul, God granting us the time. Nevertheless, for now, let us wander a little into the uncertainties of the future—to awaken meditation in deeper minds.

# CHAPTER VII.

## Plato called the circle a Polygon, a
## whole angle composed of the
## straight and the curved.

->*Since the circle consists of points, it is a certain polygon,*<-
->*For the minima from which it is made are not joined*<-
->*at every boundary. Therefore it is spread by the mouth of Plato,*<-
->*Not without the scale of judgment and a high-seeking mind,*<-
->*That it is composed of the straight and the curved, and found to be a whole.*<-

Bruno concludes by referencing Plato's theory that a circle is actually a polygon made of infinite tiny straight lines.</summary>

<meta>This page follows the classification of the "minimum" or atom from page 75. While the previous page defined atoms in logic, time, and perception, this page applies the concept of the "minimum" to geometry—specifically to the problem of "squaring the circle" (quadrature). It transitions from the general definition of a physical atom to the specific geometric theories of the philosopher Antiphon.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

*An angle, and entirely straight, and entirely curved;*
*Which seems to rise as a whole and incline as a whole,*
*As the wise Antiphon <note>Antiphon of Athens (5th century BCE), a philosopher who suggested that if you keep doubling the sides of a polygon inside a circle, the sides will eventually become so small that they match the circle's curve.</note> had clearly noted long ago,*
*With arcs drawn out, and cut again by straight lines.*

## ANTIPHON’S SQUARING OF THE CIRCLE <note>original: *Tetragonismus*; the mathematical challenge of constructing a square with the same area as a given circle using only a compass and straightedge.</note>

Because (according to what was shown in the Hall of Democritus <note>Democritus (c. 460–370 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who pioneered the theory that the universe is made of indivisible "atoms." Bruno uses "Hall of Democritus" as a metaphor for the school of atomist thought.</note>) a point is joined to another point not by its whole self, nor by a part of itself, but by its boundary, there must be a mediating point of void between point and point, and between line and line; thus, nothing is truly continuous <note>In Bruno’s physics, "continuous" things like a smooth line are actually made of tiny, discrete "minimums" or atoms separated by tiny gaps of void.</note>, whether it be a plane or a composite solid. Leucippus, Democritus, Epicurus, and many others testify to this. In a circle, therefore, where point is so adjacent to point that they meet to form a curved and recurved line, an angle is understood to exist wherever there is a point of contact. For on either side of that point, a pair of arc-like lines is understood to incline into that point; and indeed, one may take that point anywhere the circle touches a plane or another circle. 

However, insofar as the inclination is seen to be uniform everywhere and reflected back into itself, the entire circle is called a "single angle." Insofar as reason understands the shortest line of matter <term>matter: original *pleni*, referring to the "fullness" of an atom as opposed to the "void" of empty space</term> between point and point (as was just stated), it can be understood that the circle touches a plane along such a line of such a size between such boundaries, and touches a similar circle at two such points. Therefore, Plato’s circle <note>A reference to the Platonic idea that geometric shapes in the physical world are composed of more basic, straight-edged elements.</note> is conceived as being composed of the straight and the curved. Insofar as it is equally distant from the center in every direction, the ascent coincides... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the catchword "cum" on the original Latin page indicates the next word is "with" or "when."</note>

<summary>Bruno uses atomist philosophy to explain Antiphon's method of squaring the circle, arguing that circles are actually polygons made of infinitely small straight lines (atoms). This bridges the gap between the "straight" world of the square and the "curved" world of the circle by reducing both to their smallest units.</summary>

<keywords>Antiphon, Squaring the Circle, Quadrature, Atomism, Democritus, Epicurus, Geometry, Minimum, Void, Discrete Mathematics, Philosophy of Space</keywords>

<page-num>77</page-num>

->CONTEMPORARY EXCERPTS FROM THE BOOK ON THE MINIMUM<-
<note>original: *CONTEMP. EX MIN. LIB.*; likely referring to Bruno's work *De triplici minimo et mensura* (On the Triple Minimum and Measure).</note>

...both by descent, and by the same diameter it defines a length and a height <note>The OCR 'titudinem' is likely *altitudinem* (height).</note> that are indistinguishable from it, just as in a sphere the three finite dimensions are indistinguishable from the center. Indeed, in an infinite circle or sphere, this is true in every direction: though we would call these "dimensions" only in an improper sense. To the extent that, finally, Antiphon’s <term>chord</term><note>A straight line segment whose endpoints both lie on a circular arc.</note> and <term>sagitta</term><note>Latin for "arrow." In geometry, it is the vertical distance from the center of an arc to the center of its chord.</note> support the arc in a continuous order—the arc, then a part of the arc, and then a tiny particle of that part—until they reach that very **minimum** in which the arc, the chord, and the sagitta coincide into the same individual point. It happens that by dividing and supporting more and more, the circle is resolved into a straight line equal to itself. It appears composed of both ascent and descent. For to whatever curve so bounded by a straight line that you may perceive a path indistinguishable from it, you will be able to assign a sagitta connecting its midpoint to the midpoint of the other. Likewise, one can assign two external straight lines always ascending in succession; from these, a progression is made to others further and further from the center in a series, while meanwhile the boundaries of each and every line remain equidistant from the center. Pay attention here so that you may conclude this: if, in reality or reason, in actuality or in any potential way, the process of bisection and drawing chords has no end, then one could accept infinite lines that are further and further away in that order. Consequently (at least rationally and potentially), the distance between the two ends of any sagitta would be infinite.

## CHAPTER VIII.
### That a polygon does not grow by a minimum, nor does a circle.

> *And the Circle must be composed of finite parts*
> *(As it is a thing that grows together in a certain order):*
> *Since a shape does not emerge larger from only one*
> *Added part, but instead proceeds by six parts at a time.*
<margin>10</margin>
> *Do you see how, if one part is added to the radius,*
> *The circle does not increase by less than six of its parts?*
> *So also, a polygon is not increased by a single minimum alone,*
> *But every side seeks its share, however much is added*
> *To one of the rest: therefore, as every figured shape grows*
> *By its own <term>gnomon</term>, it takes its increase by its own number.*
<note>A gnomon is a geometric figure that, when added to an existing shape, creates a new shape similar to the original. Bruno is arguing that shapes must grow proportionally across all their parts to maintain their form.</note>
> *Tell me then, how will you be able to make such various figures equal?*

<summary>Bruno argues that at the level of the "minimum" (the mathematical atom), the distinction between a curve and a straight line vanishes. He also asserts that geometric shapes like circles and polygons must grow by specific proportional units (gnomons) rather than by adding a single atom to one side.</summary>

*How can it be that from equal and the same number of parts*
*There exists both a circle and a triangle? Or why do you labor to square the circle?* <note>original: *quadrare*; "Squaring the circle" is the ancient geometric challenge of constructing a square with the same area as a given circle using only a compass and straightedge.</note>

*Whose distinct forms you are unable to identify?*
*For what else do you believe can be composed of parts*
*Equally great and numerous, unless there are conceived*

*On this side—so that the shape remains as it remained before—*
*It is necessary that all sides grow equally; and we see*
*The same thing occurring in all other many-sided shapes.*
<margin>40</margin> *Since the series is the same. Indeed, they grow*

*And whether you suppose it, or no minimum, it is necessary* <margin>90</margin>
*That there be a judgment of equals, and the ratio be equal. Gaze upon*
*The Archetype <note>original: "Archetypum." In Bruno's thought, the ideal or divine pattern that underlies all physical reality.</note> whose parts, both in reverse and*

**N**ow, since a minimum is defined in nature, a square is not equal to a circle <note>original: "circulo quadratum." This refers to the "squaring of the circle," the ancient mathematical challenge of constructing a square with the same area as a given circle. Bruno argues this is impossible because the "atoms" of space cannot be rearranged that way.</note> either in actuality or by reason. Indeed, neither is a square equal to a pentagon, nor a triangle to a square, nor finally is it possible to equate any species of figure with a figure of another species; for a different number of sides requires a different order and number of parts. And truly, since figures in this regard are like numbers, and the species of one number can be equal to the species of another number neither formally nor fundamentally, we shall never make an equilateral figure equal to another equilateral figure from the first parts <note>The "first parts" are the indivisible minimums or atoms.</note>. For even if the parts of one were equal in number to the parts of another, yet the parts will never be made equal in magnitude—namely, by the number of first and similar parts. For just as a triangle, which I constitute from three minimums, differs from a quadrangle of the first parts, which I compose from four minimums, so no triangle can ever be equal to any quadrangle or other figure from those that follow. For although flat and angular figures may grow by an equal number of parts with a gnomon <note>original: "gnomone." In geometry, a gnomon is the shape that must be added to a figure to increase its size while maintaining its original shape. For squares, the gnomons are the sequence of odd numbers.</note> placed around them in the order of odd numbers, yet the magnitude of the parts of one will never be equal to the magnitude of the parts of the other, whether taken individually or collectively. But if a rectilinear figure is not equal to another rectilinear one, how can a rectilinear figure ever be equal to a circular one, which assumes homogeneous gnomons in the order of even numbers, just as the former do in odd?

¶ 33. Add to this that it is not possible to increase a square by a square, nor a triangle by a triangle, nor a circle by a circle, by a single part. For tell me, where will you add that part? For if it is added to one of the sides, it will no longer preserve the square, nor a quadrangle of the same species as it was, but a trapezium <note>A four-sided shape where not all sides are equal; adding a single atom to one side of a square "breaks" its perfect symmetry.</note>. Therefore, for the equality of the sides, it is necessary to have added parts through each of the sides, so that what you added to one, you also added to the rest. Nor will there yet be an end to your labor; since the perimeter, having been made larger, has ceased to be the boundary of the same or equal field; therefore the parts of the area must also be multiplied, which [are] four...

# JORDAN BRUNO OF NOLA

If the addition of those points to the ends of the sides can be equated according to its own law, then it will be clear once you have learned the definite order by which—when a <term>gnomon</term> <note>original: "gnomone." In geometry, a gnomon is the shape that, when added to a figure, results in a new figure similar to the original. For example, adding an L-shaped border to a square to make a larger square.</note> is added—the figure is increased simultaneously and equally in both its perimeter and its area. Indeed, just as a circle grows by the addition of defined equal and even parts, so too does a polygon grow through defined odd and equal parts. Therefore, the ratio of the parts to be added (which constitute a similar figure) is easily derived from the ratio of those parts to which the addition is being made. For a square whose outermost gnomon is composed of seven parts, a gnomon of nine similar and equal parts must be added immediately next. It is appropriate for any other polygonal series to be increased in this same way. See, then, whether you are able to increase any figure of the same kind by any given ratio of parts. See also whether you can inscribe or circumscribe a pentagon within any circle by a method other than by a radius increasing at a certain ratio through subtraction from a <term>sextant</term> <note>original: "sextante." A mathematical instrument or a sixth part of a circle (60 degrees).</note>; from this, the division of the circle will appear more exact—not merely by logic, but to the senses. To this exactness, the periphery of the fourth gnomon—namely, of twenty-four parts—leads, where the whole consists of sixty-one similar parts, with sixty recurved curvilinear triangles <note>original: "triangulis curvilineis recuruis." Bruno is likely describing the small, curved gaps created when trying to fit straight-edged units into a circular area.</note> individually receiving all but a single middle part.

¶ 16. It is necessary to refer those things which are to be judged as equal to a common and similar part of the same magnitude and shape, whether we resolve to work according to the order of nature or according to the law and order of reason.

¶ 22. We do not hinder that unskillful and non-geometric transformation by which a cube, a pyramid, a sphere, or any other equilateral shape is made from the same wax or lead. Indeed, by reason of various locations and boundaries, the matter variously thins and thickens, is compressed and extended—just as the differences of excess and deficiency are hidden in sensible matter. Truly, by the compression of physical dimensions, we make a pyramid or a cube out of a sphere. Although nothing of the solid parts may have been added or subtracted, the size of the voids and pores must have been altered; for he who fashions a sphere from a square, or a square from a sphere, compresses and pours the same material in different ways. It is therefore a mark of an unreflective or rather dull intellect to take arguments for equality or other reasoning from the successes of this kind of practice.

¶ 45. But there is a more pressing motive from what we see the craftsmen of this kind do: they change every triangle into a rectangle, and from that, they constitute a <term>parallelogram</term> <note>original: "parallelogrammo." A four-sided figure with opposite sides parallel.</note> equal to it, which they may extend or contract at will between the same parallels according to the rule of any angle, and finally from the parallelogram...

<summary>Bruno discusses the geometric laws of growth (using gnomons) and argues that physical reshaping of materials like wax does not prove mathematical equality between different shapes, as the internal "pores" and "minima" are merely rearranged.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bruno’s discussion of geometric proportions. On the previous page, he described how polygons and circles grow through specific ratios, suggesting that while they appear to change smoothly, they are actually governed by discrete numerical laws.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>CONTEMPLATIONS FROM THE BOOKS ON THE MINIMUM</header>

...make it square <note>original: "dratum faciant," completing "quadratum" from the previous page.</note>: and we have noted similarly in the book *On the Principles of Measurement and Figure*, that you may resolve every figure into a triangle; so that many triangles, circles, and whatever other figures may merge into one circle, triangle, or any other figure. Likewise, we pose many other things according to the way and custom of the common people <note>original: "vulgi via," referring to the standard mathematical practices of his time.</note>, which seem to oppose these positions of ours. But we grant that all those things are done mathematically and quite conveniently for the senses, but never according to the <term>reason of nature</term> <note>original: "rationé naturæ." Bruno distinguishes between "mathematical" truth (useful abstractions) and "natural" truth (the actual atomic structure of the universe).</note>. Indeed, that those transformations and reductions are distant from the truth by many steps can be manifest a thousand times over from the arguments we have brought forward a hundred times. The reason why it is convenient for the senses is that by resolving and transforming the figures themselves, we approach those <term>insensible differences</term> <note>Differences so small they cannot be seen by the eye.</note> which you are able to perceive (as we have shown) not by sight, but by reason or a certain analogy. For in the indistinction of those parts which approach more closely to the <term>minima</term> <note>The "minima" are Bruno’s "atoms"—the smallest possible units of matter and space.</note>, the difference between those magnitudes lies hidden. Furthermore, it is not difficult in our way to reason about those things through parts proportional to the minima, based on first, indemonstrable, and most proper principles.

¶ 89. Most foolishly, some think they can extricate themselves from all difficulties by the "division of the continuum into infinity" <note>Bruno is mocking the Aristotelian idea that space can be divided forever. He believes space is made of finite, indivisible points.</note> using whatever and however many invented parts they like. However, when one wishes to show the equality of one thing with another, it is necessary to take a fundamental reason from a certain defined minimum, which serves as the measure in that kind or subject—whatever is to be thought concerning the possible or impossible division of the subject matter into infinity, a process which no reason, nor any action of ours or of nature, can actually follow through. Therefore, those transformations <note>Such as "squaring the circle" or turning a triangle into a square.</note> stand, but they never stand upon proven and evident principles. We ourselves include the assertion of these transformations among our theorems for the sake of common and practical sense, and they are confirmed through the induction of the greatest magnitudes; for otherwise, it was not possible to obtain any better practice, or a reason more conveniently ordered for it. It stands, therefore, that in that ordering and application of principles, such things are provided—both in practical work and in demonstrating from things visible to the senses—so that we may avoid the deception of matter, which, so to speak, insults and accuses the eye. However, we do not do this so that we might reach the absolute truth in its own precise numbers (as we do in these philosophical speculations). For the things of <term>praxis</term> <note>Practical application or craft.</note> are entirely one thing, and those of <term>contemplation</term> <note>Theoretical or philosophical study.</note> are another; and of these, some principles belong to the condition of nature, while others belong to the condition of our own minds.

<sig>F 2</sig>

<summary>Bruno argues that while mathematicians can "transform" one shape into another (like a circle into a square) for practical purposes, these are only sensory approximations. True reality consists of indivisible "minima" (atoms), which make such perfect transformations impossible in the natural world.</summary>

He is challenging the Euclidean notion that a circle touches a line at a single, dimensionless point, arguing instead for a universe composed of discrete, indivisible atoms.</meta>

## CHAPTER IX.

### A body touches another body or a plane neither by itself nor by a part of itself.

**I**t is an idea far too common that a circle touches [a line], or a
sphere a globe, or a cycle, at a single point; but the masters <note>The "masters" refers to traditional mathematicians and Aristotelian philosophers whose geometric theories Bruno is refuting.</note>
have not at all distinguished the condition and the manner [of this contact].
It has been shown to many of us that the <term>limit</term> <note>original: "terminus." In Bruno's physics, a limit is the outer edge of a thing, which he argues cannot be a dimensionless mathematical point.</note> itself,
being simply a point, is no part [of a body], nor is it able to be.
Any contact, however minimal, is found to exist across 
multiple points. Nor, I say, can a thing itself be 
touched with any certainty, if no part of it is touched.
Since, when a surface is widely embraced by 
<margin>10</margin> an adjacent one, you should by no means say that the thing
is touched throughout all its parts—rather, not even one—
if the outermost surface is not a part. Nor when 
those parts come together and unite with others, 
does a part touch a part by means of a "part," but rather by one
or many points of the surface, which is itself not a part. 
Therefore, if part touches part, just as the whole 
is adjacent to the whole, the extremity of one is touched
by the extremity of the other. But the thing itself is not touched,
but rather something belonging to it; nor is a part touched, but 
<margin>20</margin> rather this boundary of a part. Wherefore there is no mixing 
of anything, so that it is established that <term>first bodies</term> <note>original: "corpora prima." These are the atoms, the fundamental building blocks of Bruno’s universe.</note> exist,
which are perhaps not penetrable by anything. 
A part can touch a part, or a whole a whole, 
if you look at the surface, but not at the bodies of things; 
unless perhaps you speak with the improper and indirect voice 
of the common people <note>original: "cum vulgo." Bruno frequently distinguishes between the "common" understanding of the senses and the "rational" understanding of the philosopher.</note>, when one part of the extremity is seen...

<meta>catchword: Of that [Istius]</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues against the mathematical abstraction of "points" in physical contact, asserting that if objects touch, they must do so via physical boundaries (atoms) rather than dimensionless geometric locations. This supports his theory of atomism, suggesting that matter is composed of "first bodies" that cannot be further divided or penetrated.</summary>

<keywords>atomism, geometry, physical contact, points, limits, first bodies, surfaces, mathematical abstraction</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 84, where Bruno refutes the geometric concept of dimensionless contact. He transitions from discussing the "limit" of a body to the physical reality of how atoms—the smallest possible units of matter—interact with one another without actually merging.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>CONTEMPLATIONS ON THE MINIMUM</header>
<sig>F 3</sig>

*The end of this thing and that thing meet in one,*
*Which is neither a part of this face, nor of that*
*Face, but rather belongs only to the empty space between;*
<margin>30</margin> *So atoms, just as an atom stands as the limit of this and that.* <note>original: "Sic atoma, vt atomus stat terminus huius & eius." Bruno is playing with the idea that the "limit" or boundary between two atoms is itself an indivisible unit, similar to the atoms it separates.</note>

**A**n atom is touched by an atom, and every body is touched by another body, not by the whole nor by a part, but by the <term>limit</term> <note>original: "termino." In Brunian physics, the "limit" is the outermost edge of a physical body. He argues that things touch at these edges, not through their internal substance.</note> or extremity of the whole or the part. I say that the things themselves are not touched, but rather something belonging to them is touched. Indeed, the extremity has its own parts, just as the body itself does; and the <term>minimum body</term> <note>The "minimum" is Bruno's term for the atom, the fundamental, indivisible building block of the universe.</note> has a minimum part of its limit by which it may touch another minimum. It can be touched by neither a larger nor a smaller part: because in the limits where contact occurs, the thing touched and the thing touching are equal. Furthermore, if a smaller thing were touching it, the larger touching thing would be, at least in theory, divisible or penetrable. Since atoms are unchangeable and impenetrable, there is nothing that we can understand as truly and properly "mixable" <note>Bruno is rejecting the Aristotelian theory of "mixtio," where elements combine to form a new substance. For Bruno, atoms only cluster together (coacervation) while remaining distinct individuals.</note>; yet when certain bodies are heaped together according to their finer parts, they seem to pass into a certain third type of appearance. But this truth does not extend beyond the senses.

## CHAPTER X.
### That contact exists in the minimum, and the difference 
### between that which touches and that by which it touches.

**I**n the minimum, contact is perhaps proved in more than one way, 
*Whether it be a part of a plane or a solid;*
*For a point touching a point, or an atom an atom,*
*Occurs when two things touch each other by their own nature. But a line*
*Never touches a point by its own nature; if it ever touches through*
*Its own extremity, it clings to its whole kind.*
*And so a curved line is never able to touch a straight one,*
*Except for a certain part of the one and a certain part of the other; hence*
<margin>10</margin> *The limit itself is not one single thing touching both at the same time,*
*But is rather that in which these things touch each other in turn.*
*Since a thing is said to touch a thing by its extremity:*

<summary>Bruno argues that atoms touch at their boundaries (limits) rather than through their entire mass, and that because atoms are indivisible, they cannot "mix" in the chemical sense but only aggregate. He further distinguishes between the "minimum" as a physical object and the "limit" as the point of contact between objects.</summary>
<keywords>atom, limit, contact, minimum body, impenetrability, mixture, geometry vs physics, extremity</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno’s refutation of Euclidean geometry, which posits that lines are made of dimensionless points. Bruno argues that physical reality consists of "atoms" or "minima," and that contact between these atoms occurs through their boundaries rather than through a mathematical point of zero size. He continues the transition into Chapter 11, focusing on the contact between spheres.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

> *If you truly began to speak of the edge of an edge,*
> *You would never learn to halt your infinite race;*
> *For there are ever more edges to an edge,*
> *Touching an edge that touches the same in turn.*
<note>original: "Extremi verè extremum..." Bruno is warning against the "infinite regress" of geometry. If you assume an edge can always be divided into a smaller edge, you will never reach the fundamental building block of matter.</note>

**S**trictly speaking, we shall not say that a line touches a point. For whenever the extremity of one line is said to touch the extremity of another line or a plane, you would more safely say that it reaches the other line by the extremity of the line. Here, an atom does not touch an atom by itself, but (as was said in the previous sections) by a mediating boundary between the point of one and the other. This happens <term>per accidens</term> <note>Latin: "by accident" or "incidentally." In philosophy, this refers to a property that is not essential to the thing's nature, but happens to be true in a specific context.</note> or through something else. Neither does an end touch an end, nor a limit touch a limit, if it is not in the nature of a limit *to be* a thing, but rather the means *by which* contact is made. 

Thus a line (which is the <term>minimum</term> <note>Bruno defines the line as the "minimum part of width," meaning the thinnest possible unit of a surface.</note> part of width) is joined by its own limit to the limit of another line, since no part of width is conceived to mediate between the one and the other. Therefore, it is a contact of point with point—certainly not a contact *of* a limit, but *by* a limit. This limit is twofold: both that by which each shareable thing is finished, and that in which the meeting of the limits is achieved. Indeed, since the limits of two things touching each other are not a single continuous thing, it follows that an indivisible space mediates between both surfaces. <term>Democritus</term> <note>Ancient Greek philosopher (c. 460–370 BCE) who first proposed that the universe is made of tiny, indivisible "atoms" and "the void."</note> called this the <term>void</term> <note>original: "inane." The empty space between physical objects.</note> cast between bodies. This void must clearly mediate between any atoms whatsoever, no matter how tightly they rush together, just as the extremity of one is distinct from the extremity of the other. Beyond the <term>indivisible</term> <note>original: "insectile." A literal translation of the Greek "atomos," meaning that which cannot be cut.</note> itself, which has no parts, you can understand nothing to be truly continuous.

¶ 12. But as for those things by which certain objects touch each other—if someone still wishes to say *what* it is that touches, or *by what means* they are touched—one or two answers will not easily suffice for the seeker. For if he wishes them to touch by something else that in turn touches, there will be no end to the number of things that touch and the tangibles by which they reach each other. <note>This is another warning against infinite divisibility. If contact requires a "third thing" to facilitate the touch, and that thing requires another, you fall into an infinite loop.</note>

## CHAPTER XI.

->How a sphere touches a sphere, and a plane,<-
->at a point, the common crowd does not understand.<-

> **T**he confused voice of the common crowd rings out from their mouths,
> *As it is accustomed to bring forth truth with a false meaning;*
> *But do not let yourself be deceived, blind, by that saying,*
> *Know that the rule for the contact of spheres varies,*
> *Just as the measure of those sticking to a plane is not one.*

### CONTEMPLATIONS FROM THE BOOK ON THE MINIMUM

*Thus there is a grade in points, a fixed order and proportion,*
*And part is related to part, and center to center,*
*Just as the proportion of a diverse cycle is presented:*
*For the whole Earth is scarcely called a <term>minimum</term> dimension* <note>The "minimum" is the smallest possible unit of a substance or space. Here, Bruno uses the Earth as a relative minimum compared to the vastness of the heavens.</note>
*Relative to the sky, whose diameter is thought to be bounded*
<margin>10</margin>
*By the bodies of two stars on either side,*
*Which twinkle for us from afar with a tiny light.*
*Therefore, the Earth is not believed to be any significant part*
*Of such a vast body, nor could it touch a globe*
*Of comparable mass at any boundary.*
*Therefore, wherever a globe touches a plane or another globe*
*At a point, lest by chance you take up the task with a confused mind,* <note>original: "confuso Marte," literally "with a confused Mars," a Latin idiom for a muddled or struggling effort.</note>
*Recall first what we have said: that a larger circle*
*Is driven more into the appearance of a straight line;*
*And therefore the point of contact owed to it is*
<margin>20</margin>
*Larger: just as the ratio of the minimum does not remain the same*
*In all things, when viewed through the inner nature of the object.*

**T**here is an order by which the center is occupied by the size of the circle and the sphere. Since (as is found in the <term>field of Democritus</term>) <note>Democritus was an ancient Greek philosopher who first proposed that the universe is made of "atoms." His "field" refers to the geometric arrangement of these atoms.</note> it is manifest that one minimum—whether large, or however spherical it may be—is touched by no more than six other equal points. Between the angles of these six meeting points, six others immediately intervene in a second layer and are placed around it, acknowledging the same center. From this, the most skillful antiquity referred every power of <term>influence</term> <note>Latin: "influxus." In Bruno's time, this referred to physical or spiritual forces flowing from the stars to the Earth.</note> to twelve spaces only; if a more exact distribution of these could be found, we would obtain a far fuller judgment for prognosticating.

From this, it is clear that only six equal spaces, defined by six lines, send a pure influence toward the center, while six others send one that is more weakened and, as it were, composite. Thus it is clear how the center is regularly occupied both physically and geometrically: for after six are placed around one, and then twelve equal units are placed around those six, the whole complex consists of nineteen parts with their eighteen spaces. <note>Bruno is describing "circle packing." 1 center + 6 in the first ring + 12 in the second ring = 19 units total.</note> This whole complex is then taken as a center around which six equal units are placed in the same order. And so one may proceed into the infinite or the indefinite (if you like) in reasoning, but one should not expect the power of the influence to persist. For although the <term>continuum</term> <note>The continuous stretch of space or matter.</note> may be infinite, action cannot be infinite, as has been shown elsewhere.

<summary>Bruno describes the geometric arrangement of atoms (minima), explaining that a central sphere is surrounded by six others, which in turn are surrounded by twelve. <page-num>88</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

We have shown this extensively: one of the primary reasons for this is that because the center varies according to the relationship of individual things, the whole cannot be contracted from every side into a single center of influence. Thus, in the infinite and toward the infinite, we can perceive the center as being both nowhere and everywhere. <note>This is a famous Brunian paradox: in an infinite universe, every point can be considered the center, or there is no center at all.</note>

¶ But returning to the intention of this heading, we say that a circle and another sphere equal to it touch at a point. And yet, one must conceive a certain analogy of the point and its contact to another point and its contact, just as it is established that there is a proportion between the magnitude of one circle or globe and another.

# CHAPTER XII.

## Why, with contact remaining at a single point, a larger circle 
## moves faster over the same plane 
## than a smaller one.

**H**ence you know how, over one and the same plane,
*One larger circle and another smaller one in various places*
*Do not run their course in the same time*
*When they continue their motion in this way,*
*Neither between the former nor the latter are there differences in points,*
*Or in the number of points. Since they recognize infinite points*
<margin>in both</margin>
*In both: therefore they are distant by no difference.*
*But for us, the larger globe (due to the differences of the point)*
*Will complete its course more quickly, if it is driven by one*
*And equal pulse, than that which is smaller than it.*
<margin>10</margin>
*Since I believe it touches either by another point, or by more than one,*
*Composed of both the straight and the curved, or by a curvature*
*Approaching more closely to the rule of a straight line;*
*Just as the greatest curvature seems to belong to the smallest thing.* <note>Bruno argues that as a circle gets larger, its arc becomes flatter (more like a straight line), meaning its "point" of contact is actually qualitatively different or "larger" than that of a small, highly curved circle.</note>

Therefore, although the larger and smaller circle are moved with equal velocity, and all other things are equal: besides the fact that the <term>footprint</term> <note>original: "vestigium," meaning the mark or track left by the circle as it rolls.</note> of the larger circle is greater, it will necessarily happen that a greater space is traversed by the larger circle in the same time; for indeed, as was said just above, the center of every circle is not equal, but by reason of the greater extreme parts to be bounded, the center of the larger must be greater. Likewise, the contact of every circle on a plane, or with a circle comparable to it, is not equal—

# CHAPTER XIII.
## A line falling obliquely upon a plane does not touch it at a single point.

*Set aside that contact at a point is always understood by these thinkers*
*In such a way that much ignorance follows thereafter,*
*When one straight line falls upon another straight line*
*Obliquely, they likewise reckon that these two*
*Touch at a single point. Nay, if one touches the other entirely,*
*As soon as it is separated by one point or part,*
*A section follows which stands upon a single point;*
*And thus from innumerable points a progression into a single*
*Continuum is made, although it also continuously*
<margin>10</margin>
*Traverses parts with all other parts: and this*
*Is made manifest by a certain continuous succession,*
*So that after a part, as a designated part, one limit*
*May indicate it, and the fall of the inclining line <term>clinantis</term> <note>The "clinans" refers to a line leaning or tilting toward a surface.</note> stops in that,*
*Which whether you seek by physical sense, or by the reason of Mathematics*
*And Logic, could never deceive.*

Minima and any equal parts of the same magnitude and regularly arranged with equal intervals—such as those which belong to the side <note>original: *costa*, the side of a geometric figure</note>—individually touch those parts which are on the diagonal <term>diametro</term> <note>Bruno uses *diametrus* here to mean the diagonal of a square.</note>. For when they proceed and are ordered continuously through the diagonal, they do not follow the same law of progression and continuity as they do through the side. For in the square **ABCD**, the atoms touch each other continuously from **A** toward **B** and **D**, but not from **A** toward **C**. Also, the squares which are from **A** toward **B**, and **D** touch each other laterally and are joined, but from **A** toward **C**, and from **B** toward **D**, they touch only at their angles. On the contrary, however, in the triangle **EFG**.

<summary>Bruno argues that the way geometric shapes and lines touch depends on their orientation (oblique vs. straight) and that at the "atomic" level, diagonal contact differs from lateral contact. He challenges the traditional Euclidean idea that an oblique line touches a plane at only one infinitesimal point.</summary>
