<note>This represents an isosceles triangle formed by the arrangement of atoms. This specific shape, similar to the Pythagorean tetractys, was used to demonstrate how discrete units can form larger geometric figures.</note>

¶ In the square, therefore, there is no continuation of homogeneous parts, nor of the absolutely smallest parts—namely, the circles <note>Bruno posits that the "minimum" or atom is spherical or circular in shape</note>—along the diameter, but rather along the side <term>costam</term> <note>original: *costam*; literally "rib," used here to mean the side of a geometric figure</note>. From this it is clear what must be said to those who, against our opinion, wish to argue—based on the points of line AB, all of which flow individually and immediately into line CD by intersecting the diameter AC, and from the fact that nothing comes between a point and the point immediately following it that meets with that same immediacy—that the points of line AB are so equal to the points of line CD that they cannot be more numerous than the points of line AC.

<note>Bruno is addressing a famous mathematical paradox: if you draw lines from every "point" on the side of a square to the opposite side through a diagonal, it might seem that the side and the (longer) diagonal have the same number of points. Bruno argues that because lines are made of finite atoms (minimums) with actual size, not zero-dimensional points, this "infinite" division is impossible.</note>

<summary>Bruno explains that geometric figures like squares and triangles are composed of discrete, circular "minimums" (atoms) and argues that continuity exists along the sides of a shape rather than through its diagonal, refuting traditional geometric paradoxes regarding infinite points.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the geometric and philosophical investigation of the "minimum" (the smallest indivisible unit of matter or space), specifically addressing the relationship between the side and diameter of a square in an atomic framework.</meta>

A C, from which it follows that the parts of the diameter and the sides <term>costae</term> <note>original: *costae*; literally "ribs," referring to the sides of the square</note> are equal, and no ratio remains by which the diameter exceeds the side of the square <note>In Bruno’s "atomic" geometry, if lines are made of a finite number of discrete points, the diagonal of a square must contain a specific number of atoms, potentially challenging the classical Greek proof of the irrationality of the square root of 2</note>. It is also clear what must be said regarding the other difficulty they raise concerning this same distribution in two straight lines: those which, emerging from a center, continually encounter a greater distance from one another and cannot pass through the parts of the larger circle as immediately adjacent as those of the smaller concentric circle. It is certain to us from what has been said that not all parts reach the center, nor do all lines simply emerge from the center to every point of the circumference in the first place. Furthermore, among others, there always exist the final six composed of two parts, which emerge from the second-to-last circle, and likewise as many which simply reach the middle center throughout, as is evident in the expanded **Field of Democritus**.

¶ I will not use the proposed rule in the discovery of the minimum where I judge the quantity of the base from the angle of an isosceles triangle, since the lines do not proceed according to that specific directness. For there, we are not investigating equal parts of a figure, but the proportional parts of one line and another.

¶ We have mentioned more extensively elsewhere that a line intersecting another line obliquely does so not according to a point, but according to a length <note>Bruno argues that because points/atoms have a physical size, an angled line must "touch" more than one unit as it crosses another</note>. Nothing more foolish could be imagined than what they say: that for those living under the pole, an entire **semicircle** <note>half-circle</note> rises in an instant of time, and that the diameter—because it lay entirely upon the plane of the horizon—is suddenly moved to the intersection of that same horizon at a point. And yet, these are the very same people who contend that motion does not exist in an instant; here, they describe in words a progression of this kind (which is impossible for the mind to grasp) from one extreme to the other in an instant, and they defend it by most stubbornly babbling on.

# CHAPTER XIV.

## How a straight line equal to a circle is marked out from contact at a point, and the successive transition of infinite points in finite time.

*Add also that if a sphere touches a continuous*
*Plane at a point, and is rolled straight through it,*
*A curved length follows through that which is drawn.*

Making the straight line clear. I say that this revolving figure,
Turning itself across a plane with a continuing motion,
Marks out through its own traces a continuous straight line
Equal to itself upon the plane, the endpoint of which is that same
Point where it happens to touch the underlying parts
Of the plane again, which it first held. Wherefore, as both 
<margin>10</margin> Consist of innumerable parts <note>While Bruno often argues for a finite number of atoms, here he uses "innumerable" to describe the density of points in a continuum as perceived by the senses or traditional geometry</note>, and they 
Touch each other continually in a single point—namely, always in one—
It is not fitting for more points of the straight line to touch the circle, 
Nor for more points of the circle to touch the straight line.
At last, the conclusion stands firm and inviolate: 
That this motion may have traversed infinity itself, 
Whether you take it physically, or perhaps by any method of **<term>Mathematics</term>** <note>original: *Mathesis*; for Bruno, this refers to a blend of geometry, logic, and the study of universal structures</note>. 
It is enough if the resolution was continuous and there was 
No intermission until the two ceased to touch and 
Be touched by each other. But we think that a contact without end 
<margin>20</margin> Along with number is nowhere truly possible or within our power. 
But only a contact distinct from the tangent—the curved 
From the straight—will never make it so that the contact 
In a point is not continuous, nor that the points of contact 
Are always individual <note>Bruno is arguing that even though a circle and a line touch at a single point, the "flow" of motion creates a continuous sequence of these individual contacts</note>. Indeed, the underlying parts will deny 
That many points can be reached by a single overlying point. 
For who could devise a reason why he should consider the line 
Being touched to be less continuous than the line which is touching? 
Therefore, it is better that the dogma be held by the senses, 
Lest you be submerged in a rushing river of errors: 
<margin>30</margin> That the parts are finite, as are the boundaries which join them, 
And the points of contact are finite, unless there is one 
Continuous thing which requires coming to the examination 
Of the mind and the grasp of the senses, not by number, but by its mass <note>original: *mole*; referring to the physical magnitude or "bulk" of the object as a whole, rather than its division into abstract numbers</note>.

*And truly this is more fitting: because mass and number*
*Cannot perpetually be of one kind or similar.*

*For as that remains one and the same,*

<margin>50</margin>
*And the **<term>Monad</term>** <note>The fundamental, indivisible unit of reality; for Bruno, it represents the physical atom, the mathematical point, and the metaphysical soul</note>, the essence and being of all things.*
*Therefore it is foolish to think that things grasped by the eyes*
*(In whatever ratio they occur, and suitable to the senses)*
*Are not composed of certain numbers, and a certain limit of parts.*

---

**I**f contact occurs at a point and is continuous, and the tangent and the thing touched are equally continuous, and the single point of a curve does not rest upon more than one point of a plane: then it is surely necessary that all the parts, starting from the smallest units—or however you wish to define them—touch each other in such a continuous succession that we may conclude (not merely mechanically, but rationally from the laws of nature) that a straight line is equal to a curve when the circle CDEF is rolled over the straight line AB. For it touches the starting point A at point C; and finally, by rotating continuously, with points succeeding one another immediately and individually, and with the curve and the straight line touching each other point-by-point in turn, that same starting point C arrives at the contact of point B. From this, there can be no room for escape or excuse: those wretched **<term>infinitarians</term>** <note>original: *infinitarii*; Bruno's derogatory term for philosophers like Aristotle who argued that space and matter are infinitely divisible</note> must admit that infinite parts of the straight line were passed by infinite parts of the curve, just as the points become contiguous to one another point-by-point, continuously and individually.

**<term>Monad</term>** <note>The ultimate unit</note>, **<term>Continuum</term>** <note>The continuous whole</note>, **<term>Infinitarians</term>** <note>Believers in infinite divisibility</note>, **<term>Essence</term>** <note>The nature of a thing</note>, **<term>Parts</term>** <note>original: *Tomorum*; atoms or sections</note>, **<term>Point</term>** <note>A geometric minimum</note>

<summary>Bruno argues that the physical world is composed of finite, discrete atoms (monads) rather than being infinitely divisible. He uses the geometric example of a circle rolling on a line to prove that both must be composed of a specific, finite number of points.</summary>
<keywords>Monad, atomism, geometry, infinitarians, continuum, discrete mathematics, tangency</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the physical world is composed of finite, indivisible units, moving from the geometric logic of the previous page to the psychological reasons why people struggle to accept this truth.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
->GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA<-

¶ 29. It is therefore better (and indeed necessary) to think that parts, and the **<term>boundaries</term>** <note>original: *terminos*; the points or limits where one part ends and another begins</note> (by which parts are joined to other parts), are finite. Likewise, the potential of these boundaries and parts following one another is not infinite by nature; rather, it is **<term>indefinite</term>** <note>Bruno distinguishes between "infinite" (unbounded) and "indefinite" (appearing without limit only because we cannot see the end)</note> due to convention, chance, or even the way nature is structured. Indeed, for our practical purposes, boundaries and parts are indefinite to such an extent because our senses and our manual work fall so far short of the true **<term>minimum</term>** <note>The smallest possible physical unit; an atom</note>. Consequently, in practice, our reason divides and composes things vaguely, even though in reality, everything consists of distinct differences in specific numbers.

## CHAPTER XV.

### Conclusion: how the power of the habit of believing false things even disturbs the senses themselves.

*Here, no less, the sense imbues the mad with a false*
*Foundation of faith, through habit from their earliest years,*
*Than that holy man who professes under the word of God.*
*This man rejoices no less, as if for light attained,*
*Than the other gives thanks for darkness cast aside.*
*Hence, although manifest truths present themselves persistently*
*Through every sense, and knock at the doors of reason with a friendly*
*Touch, and though nature and its voice cry out against them;*
*He does not understand for that reason: because the first foundations*
<margin>10</margin>*Stand in the way, sticking by their roots deep within his*
*Internal organs. As if brightened by a higher light,*
*The unhappy man has learned to deny both the light and his own eyes;*
*Believing that nature and wisdom are beneath his feet;*
*From this degenerate class of men, anyone easily comes forward*
->voluntarily<-
*As a credulous supporter of an unlearned doctrine, so that for him*
*The voices of nature create miracles, and no more...*

<summary>Bruno argues that while we perceive things as infinitely divisible in practice, they are actually composed of finite parts; he blames "habit" and "false faith" for blinding people to this natural truth.</summary>
<keywords>finite parts, boundaries, the minimum, psychological habit, sensory deception, critique of dogma, nature vs. doctrine</keywords>

### GIORDANO BRUNO THE NOLAN

*(However immense the circle is based on its edge)*
<margin>30</margin> *Although it is always closer and closer to a point;*
*And as it grows, the arc of a larger circle more and more imitates*
*the appearance of a plane (the ignorant crowd bellows <note>original: "boat inscia turba"; Bruno often uses "bellowing" to describe the loud but unthinking adherence of scholars to old traditions</note>)*
*That angle <note>This refers to the "angle of contact" or horn angle between a curve and a tangent line</note> will never be violated by a touch:*
*For Euclid says these meet at a single point.*
*But how far this wretched crowd of Euclid recedes*
*from sense, nowhere making distinctions between a point*
*and a touch: and if it does not discern the level of the minimum*
*both in the appearance of things and in the measurement of the mind and eyes,*
*you will gather from those things we spoke of a little while ago.*
*And from there you will conclude that as all things are constrained by a limit*           
<margin>40</margin> *they are at last a minimum and a nothingness encompassed under the orb,*
*reduced from the immense to the appearance of an atom and a point.*
*Hence only one thing is simply great, without restriction;*
*Other things are great or small by a certain relation:*
*Nothing is ever touched by the simply minimum:*
*For one single form does not contain two things simply,*
*nor will you imagine a touch by right reason through both of these.*

**M**aster teachers say this is a marvel: that whether a circle is the smallest, small, medium, large, or however much larger it grows, it touches the plane or line **AB** not ever as a line, but as a point; that is, it touches the line or plane at the point **C**. And the larger the circle is, the lines **DA** or **DB** intersect at points further from the boundary **D** and closer to the boundaries **A** and **B**. Yet (they say) it is not suitable or possible for a circle to ever be found that would come into contact with the outer angles **A** and **B**. Why? Because contact is always at a point. 

But I, for my part, marvel much more at their persistent stupidity, just like that of the man <note>This likely refers to Apollonius of Perga or a similar geometer discussing asymptotes—lines that approach each other but never meet</note> who concludes that two lines extended into infinity (from the same kind of principles) can never meet, even though they approach closer and closer. Soon, being "exceedingly learned," he prepared a rich subject-matter of an excellent volume on the character of that cloudy imagination for those with too much leisure and wasted talents.

<meta>catchword: GIORDANO</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues against the Euclidean idea that a circle and a line only ever touch at a single dimensionless point, asserting instead that in a physical reality, large circles eventually merge with planes. He mocks mathematicians who focus on abstract infinitudes while ignoring the physical reality of atoms or "minima."</summary>

JUST SO <note>Latin: *SIC*. Bruno is drawing a parallel between the myth of Caeneus and the state of philosophy.</note> have the sophists, long before our time, [crushed] the simplicity of inviolable philosophy through the multiplication of books and commentaries, through various superstitious excursions and questions, and also through the novelty of superfluous demonstrations, all for the display of their own cleverness. After them, down to our own times, followed a deluge of presumptuous and most arrogant grammarians <note>In the Renaissance, "grammarians" often referred to humanists who focused on linguistics and philology rather than deep philosophical truth.</note>. These people—having recited Greek texts from their source, along with summaries, interrogations <term>erotematibus</term> <note>original: "erotematibus"; refers to a question-and-answer format used in teaching.</note>, handbooks, collections of excerpts, and "treasures" <note>referring to large encyclopedic dictionaries or *thesauri*</note>—offered various interpretations according to the exact rule <term>amussim</term> <note>original: "amussim"; a carpenter's rule or plumb line, used here to mean strict, pedantic imitation.</note> of Cicero and true Latinity. They did this while falsifying originals so they might find something to forge anew as a literary specimen. They gathered readings as if to bring together new disputes about the "legitimate" text added alongside [the original], acting as the creators and chief-abbots <term>archimandritæ</term> <note>original: "archimandritæ"; a high-ranking official in the Eastern Church, used here mockingly by Bruno.</note>—the fathers of asses. With innumerable tables, lexicons, and also introductions <term>isagogis</term> <note>original: "isagogis"; introductory texts, such as Porphyry's *Isagoge* to Aristotle.</note> (that is, "Introductions," or if you prefer to call them, "Exclusions"), they have led us into extreme confusion: just as the invulnerable Caeneus lay oppressed by the heavy mass of innumerable rocks and forests piled up by the half-wild Centaurs.

# ON THE DISCOVERY OF THE MINIMUM, BOOK [I]

It pleased them to cast this from the center. Then, every truer form of measurement, utterly extinguished, vanished; and it left its surveyors with a lying name, so that there is no distance between their measuring and their lying. <note>Bruno creates a wordplay here between the Latin *metiri* (to measure) and *mentiri* (to lie), suggesting that without a true understanding of the "minimum" unit, all measurement is essentially a falsehood.</note> Yet the substance of quantity will remain; the elements of Measurement will stand eternal in the fields of Nature and the observant mind.
<margin>10</margin> We know how, when a gnomon <note>In classical geometry, a gnomon is the shape that remains when a smaller similar parallelogram is removed from a larger one. Bruno uses it here to describe how figures "grow" by adding units.</note> is applied around a single minimum, within planes contained by a straight boundary of such size, by keeping a similar figure in the field of Leucippus <note>Leucippus (5th century BCE) was the earliest Greek philosopher to propose that the universe is made of indivisible units called atoms. We know how, around a spherical atom, only six equal atoms will first come together to fill the space; <note>Bruno is describing the geometry of "close-packing." In a two-dimensional plane, exactly six circles of equal size can fit perfectly around a central seventh circle of the same size, forming a hexagonal pattern.</note> then they always come in increasing numbers by that same ratio, so that the circle keeps one rule of the odd through the full space, and preserves the pattern of the even in the void: which takes the differences of number by alternating turns—differences which the point, the plane, the angle, and the mark define.
<margin>20</margin> The hexagonal form in the plane will demonstrate what is proposed, for the contemplation of the cycle becomes more proper in the solid, which dares only to be considered the boundary of a sphere: clearly, a circle described in a plane is nothing but a ring—the boundary of a slice of a sphere—merely a part of a spherical body, with one or more minimums marking the depth.
From which it is easily evident how, after the first part is found or given in the composite, you may solidly conclude how many units are in the whole body through the geometry <term>metria</term> <note>original: *metriam*. Bruno uses the suffix "-metria" (from the Greek for measurement) to denote the science of calculating dimensions through straight lines and arcs based on fundamental units.</note> of lines and arcs. From there it arises as if from a radius, with one part growing...

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page follows the discussion of how geometric shapes are constructed from "minima" (indivisible units or atoms). Bruno is transitioning from the technical description of atomic circles to a philosophical defense of his system against traditional Euclidean mathematicians who believe in infinite divisibility.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>

->GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA<-

For those to whom the principles of the sciences have been poorly foreseen and explored, they are gradually pushed into the thickest darkness, as if from a single source of error, so that at last they most firmly defend things openly contrary to the senses and to reason. Thus, one may see two kinds of Geometry, which are separated as much as truth from falsehood, knowledge from ignorance, and light from darkness. One of these proceeds from a posited minimum <note>The "minimum" is Bruno's fundamental building block of reality—an indivisible atom. He argues that geometry must be based on these finite units rather than infinitely small points.</note> as a necessary foundation; the other, however, proceeds from a minimum that is ignored. One is thrust into horrible obscurities, confusions, and confessed myriads of irregularities; the other, lifted into the light of all things, shines. 

On behalf of that former side, a certain one of the "correctors," <note>Bruno is likely referring to contemporary commentators or editors of Euclid's *Elements*, such as Christoph Clavius, who defended traditional geometry against atomic theories.</note> in a certain preface on Euclid addressed to the "candid reader," cries out thus: 

"But here, where Democritus and Leucippus grant those atoms of theirs and indivisible corpuscles, and Xenocrates grants certain impartible magnitudes, the foundations of Geometry are openly attacked and utterly overturned. With these destroyed, I indeed see nothing else remaining than that the most magnificent theaters of the mathematicians should suddenly collapse. Will there lie ruined then (if it please the gods!) so many famous theorems of the Geometers concerning asymmetric and irrational <term>alogis: irrational or incommensurable</term> magnitudes? For what reason would you give why an indivisible line might measure this one, but cannot measure that one? Since whatever minimum is found in each kind, that is accustomed to be the common measure of all." <note>The critic argues that if atoms exist, then every line must be made of a whole number of atoms. This would make it impossible for things like the diagonal of a square to be "irrational" (incommensurable) with its side, a cornerstone of Euclidean math.</note>

Against him, I respond—not with such beautiful Latin as with the goodness of sense: 

Will they lie ruined then (I say), if it please the gods, O master candidate, the true lights of philosophy? And having been buried together, will they not eventually rise again? Is it just because ignorance has grown with such great numbers, titles, and privileges? Should I think, O most honorable master, that we ought to weep for the death of irrationality <note>original: "alogia." Bruno is punning on "alogia" meaning both mathematical irrationality and lack of reason/logic.</note> and incommensurability, rather than rejoice for the rebirth of rationality and measure?

¶ Consider therefore first how, without a minimum of its own kind, there is no measure in any kind; and what follows from the consideration of the maximum and minimum of a similar figure.

¶ In what order the minimum is placed around the minimum in a circle and sphere, and likewise in rectilinears and plane-faced regular bodies, so that they increase the magnitude of that same shape.

¶ 32. That spheres must necessarily be composed of orbs (of which the circle is the boundary) into which they also ought to be resolved; these consist of the thickness of one part or minimum, with a certain number filling the width or area according to the condition of the periphery. From this, a judgment concerning the composition of other bodies and planes easily follows. For it is easy to see by what ratio a finite circle rises from the boundaries of six parts, by whatever divisibles those parts are built.

¶ 40. Nor does it hinder, nor is it hindered, that a perfectly spherical and circular body—or even a body of any shape—(if more deeply...

<summary>Bruno defends his atomic geometry against Euclidean critics, arguing that the existence of a "minimum" unit provides a rational foundation for measurement, whereas traditional geometry leads to the "darkness" of irrational numbers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Giordano Bruno’s argument for a geometry based on physical atoms (minima) rather than abstract, infinitely divisible points. Having previously criticized traditional mathematicians, he now introduces the specific geometric configurations of these atoms, heavily utilizing a hexagonal packing structure where six units surround a central one.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>

### ON THE DISCOVERY OF THE MINIMUM, BOOK [II]

...if you were to consider it) through practice, it cannot be comprehended at all: because globosity and circularity are found, properly and by themselves, to most truly correspond to the one Infinite that is such throughout its whole.

¶ 59. Contemplate the field of Leucippus and the area of Democritus <note>Leucippus and Democritus were the foundational Greek philosophers of Atomism, the theory that the universe is composed of indivisible units.</note>; so that they might advance you toward every measurement through the consideration of the minimum <note>The "minimum" is Bruno's term for the atom or the fundamental unit of space/matter.</note>.

¶ 60. See there in what order the lines and radii are multiplied around a center, and how the orders of several circles succeed one another in their series with an increasing number of parts. Likewise, see how in the surface toward the indivisible pole, and in the depth toward the indivisible center, they decrease in number in various circles succeeding one another in continuous order, right down to the monad <note>The "monad" is the single unit or the number one, representing the central atom.</note> itself. Where also we define the magnitude of the multitude of circles with a triangular space, and of spheres with recurved pyramids, by a similar number.

¶ 61. It is not the case that as many lines as there are parts of the periphery are extended all the way to the center; instead, perpetually only six touch the central minimum. There are six points without a line by which the external circle grows upon the immediately subject internal one. There are six straight lines from the periphery toward the center consisting of two points, six consisting of three points, and so on, up to those six greatest lines which define the number of the radius reaching the center. <note>Bruno is describing a hexagonal packing of atoms. In this system, only six "atoms" can physically touch a central atom. This contradicts Euclidean geometry, where an infinite number of lines can technically converge at a point.</note>

¶ And what does it profit to define magnitude and number through proportional parts—which you can take to be as many and as large as you like—in a thousand different and indefinite positions, which prefer to be investigated by a single position of a defined minimum, or by a most certain order of dividing and sub-defining?

¶ 83. They [traditional mathematicians] also lack a system for proportional parts, which we gather with all ease as depending on equal minima. Thus, by however many sextuplets <note>original: *senariis*. Bruno bases his geometry on the number six, the number of units that surround a center in a flat plane.</note> of parts the circumference consists, the radius must consist of just as many of its own parts, of which one is the base (namely the middle) and the common terminus for the six emerging radii; therefore, where a circle takes twelve parts, the radius is of two; where the circle is composed of eighteen minima, the radius is of three; where that one is twenty-four, this one is four. Hence, just as the parts of the circumference are multiplied by the progression of the sextuplet, so for the investigation of the minimum, when we proceed to new divisions of the arc, we also assume a new ratio of the parts.

¶ 90. It must be recalled to memory that in a sphere, the greatest circle is that which is bounded by the diameter through the center, where, with the number of subordinated parts consisting of a sextuplet: it is manifest how many parts the area contains. For if anyone inspects a sphere regularly composed of globules <note>Bruno visualizes the "area" or "volume" of a shape simply by counting the number of physical atoms/globules that fit within its boundary.</note>, if the circuit of the greatest area is first set at twenty-four parts, the total area will be sixty-one parts. If the circumference consists of eighteen parts, the area will be thirty-seven parts. With a circumference of twelve parts, it encompasses an area of nineteen parts. <note>Bruno’s math: Area = 1 + (sum of the rings). Ring 1 = 6; Ring 2 = 12; Ring 3 = 18; Ring 4 = 24. A circle with a circumference of 12 has 2 rings (1+6+12=19). A circle with a circumference of 18 has 3 rings (19+18=37). A circle with a circumference of 24 has 4 rings (37+24=61).</note>

<summary>Bruno describes a discrete, atomic geometry where circles and spheres are built from "globules" in a hexagonal pattern, arguing that the number six is the fundamental key to relating a circumference to its radius and area.</summary>

That [outer circle] consists of six parts, while this [entire arrangement] consists of seven. Finally, one part remains, which encompasses itself or a homogeneous nothingness. Therefore, whoever wishes to calculate the number of the whole sphere or circle must first understand the ratio of the diameter. From this it is manifest that, by dividing the circle into constituent <note>original: "subiectis"</note> rather than imaginary parts, it is not possible to rationally propose any other parts for it (parts which are reached through demonstration and measured more truly) than those which arise from the **senary progression** <term>senary progression: a numbering system based on the number six, reflecting Bruno’s hexagonal packing of atoms</term>: **Six, twelve**, and beyond according to the same series.

### CHAPTER III.
### That even for Euclid an angle is not divided into more than two parts.

*Here I call upon you, Euclid: look back at the laws of fate,*
*Look back at those authors of the principles*
*Who have so learnedly multiplied the sub-rules.*
*Once, an angle was split into twin parts by you,*
<note>Bruno is addressing Euclid directly, critiquing the fundamental geometric definitions found in Euclid's "Elements."</note>
*As the two lines that meet are far or near to their divisions,*
*Just as when they are joined at the cusp*
*Which reaches the center; and from there an angle is defined*
*By the meeting of two straight lines, of which one*
*Comes inclined toward the other. Then you considered this:*
<margin>10</margin>
*That the minimum point where they touch cannot then*
*Be touched by more than one line produced between them;*
<note>Bruno argues that if a point has a physical size (a "minimum"), there is no room to fit a third line between two lines that meet at that point.</note>
*For however large a space an angle is given,*
*That one angle cannot itself pass into more than two*
*Parts; for if you conceive of a single*
*Marked minimum upon a straight line, it stands adjacent*
*Only to two parts. Therefore, from a single point,*
*A finite straight line—like a horizon—migrates into no more*
*Than three parts upon the supine axis.*
*Now, what will happen when the power of the point is narrowed,*
<margin>20</margin>
*And the fold of the sinus is contracted by the inclination of two lines?*

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>ON THE DISCOVERY OF MINIMA, BOOK 1 <note>original: "DE INVENT. MINIM. LIB. 1"</note></header>

*Therefore, a newly bisected angle returns; never is that same angle*
*Split into more than twin parts.* <note>Bruno argues that an angle is formed by two lines meeting at a point; thus, it naturally consists of two sides and can only be halved, not infinitely subdivided at the source.</note>
*Nor does a single boundary encompass many parts—*
*That boundary which you know has no part of its own.*
*The Minimum does not bound more similar parts*
*Than those we have named.* <note>Referring to his theory that only six atoms can surround a central one.</note> *And just as*
*An angle is more acute (because it inclines more),*
*So its very limit comes further from the minimum;*
*And the dimension of the long void grows beneath it.* <note>This refers to the "empty" space that opens up between two lines as they extend away from their shared vertex point.</note>
*Furthermore, I shall perhaps note these things more fully in another place.*

**I**t is a marvel how, without any limit, order, or reason, the most stupid of all recent men—operating under the name of **Geometers**—undertake to divide an angle into innumerable parts, just as they think they can accept a perimeter placed above it. They hold the division of the one and the other to be almost the same thing, so that what is everywhere divisible no longer takes the character of the <term>indivisible</term> <note>original: "indiuidui"</note> and the <term>boundary</term> <note>original: "termini"</note>, but rather the character of something highly dimensioned. 

"An angle exists by virtue of the angle," one of the most clever defenders will say, "because in that greater or lesser boundary of inclination, the cause of the width of its **base** (which it subtends) is seen." 

But (I pray), will a single angle therefore be accepted as divisible into more than two parts by any reasoning? Could that single point be the boundary of all the parts that are multiplied by the division of the field? <note>Bruno is mocking the idea that a single, dimensionless point could serve as the vertex for an infinite number of sub-angles.</note>

<header>GIORDANO BRUNO THE NOLAN <note>original: "IORD. BRVNI NOL."</note></header>

¶ Here I establish for you not just an angle, but the whole curve <note>original: "sinum"</note> of the diameter AB itself, so that from point C you may revive as many angles as you can by the termination of the parts. Tell me, does it not seem to you that there is place for scarcely three points touching outside the line, since it cannot be touched by more than six equal <term>minima</term> <note>The "minimum" is Bruno's version of an atom—the smallest possible physical unit of space or matter.</note> from the whole circle? Now, therefore, if the straight line EC falls upon 그 point, I ask whether—regarding the largest angle I give you (and which I wish to call as such), angle BCA—after it has been divided into two parts, angle BCE and angle ECA, it happens that you can conveniently subdivide the same or two other angles so that they look toward the division of the same angle: BCD, DCE, ECF, FCA? And then, wishing to proceed to a further division, can you establish that same point C as the boundary?

If it is possible to do this (as is clear enough), what prevents you from understanding thereafter that there is no place for a new division of other angles, unless perhaps you wish to understand the whole area by the name of a single angle? Now if you do not agree (because you have not read or perhaps not understood what was said before about the multiplication of the minimum and the boundary, and their mutual consequence), I refer you again to those things to be inspected, upon which the whole reason for these matters depends. This, however, my good man <note>original: "ô bone"</note>, should be sufficient for you: whether or not a simple minimum is granted, no matter how small a part you take or conceive of that is placed in the middle between equal parts, that part (without doubt) as is visible in the <term>space of Democritus</term> <note>Democritus was the ancient Greek philosopher who first proposed an atomic theory. Bruno uses "Democritus’s area" to refer to a space governed by atomic/discrete geometry rather than infinite Euclidean lines.</note>, cannot be a part common to more than three diameters, just as it is not touched by more than six rays. And remember that the same reasoning applies to the largest, the larger, and the smallest things.

# CHAPTER IV.
## The center is not the boundary of all lines from the circumference.

> *Meanwhile, see the source <note>original: "protoplasten", literally the first-formed or primary maker</note> of the foolishness of that perception;*
> *Then see the center, which has no divisible part,*
> *Yet it terminates all the flows from the circle into one.*
> *No matter how far the edge of the field extends into the immense.*
> *But why, by descending through dissimilar parts—*
> *Of which the smaller ones make an end to the larger—*
> *Has it finally come to this: that here it terminates parts without end in number,*
> *Yet is not the boundary for a single part,*
> *If you look at the minimum which is so distant from it?*

This challenges the Euclidean notion of a point as a dimensionless location that can host infinite intersections.</summary>

*Let them say how a boundary could belong to so many*
<margin>10</margin>
*parts as the circumference of a great circle,*
*for just as many do not fit a <term>minimum</term> <note>The "minimum" is the smallest possible physical unit, which Bruno argues must have a finite number of sides or contact points, unlike a mathematical point which is theorized to have infinite lines passing through it.</note>, or at least not equal ones,*

*so that you may avoid a vacuum. Therefore, the center*

*Therefore, the center itself will be a quantity consisting of as many parts*

*and it consists of its own parts—however many—*

*Wherefore, you should think that for the measurer, the minimum*
*is a part composed only of what is nearest, whatever that may be.*
*Therefore, the mass of the Earth <note>original: "Terrai," an archaic Latin genitive.</note> cannot be a minimum,*
*nor can it be a boundary to the infinite; nor can any boundary*
<margin>30</margin>
*exist, as if the universe were enclosed by no margin.*
*Nay, because this atom is neither a part [of the infinite] nor a final boundary,*
*there is no reason for you to demand only a whole without end,*
*since even the common folk know that the mass of the Earth,*
*though appearing small beneath the stars set high above, is yet present;*
*and for such a mass, a larger "minimum" can be considered.*
*Therefore, if it happens that a measurer apprehends*
*a minimum as a point, toward which every portion of the circle*
*sends its influence: you will also hold this in mind...*

<summary>Bruno argues that a central point cannot be touched by an infinite number of lines, as the point itself would then require an infinite number of sides. Instead, he applies his theory of "minimums" to explain that the center grows in proportion to the circumference it serves, challenging the foundations of Euclidean geometry.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

*So that one minimum cannot be called greater than another minimum,*
*And a minimum is bounded by another minimum at certain points.*

From this same chapter, one may examine whether the center of all the parts contained within a circumference can be one and the same <term>boundary</term> <note>original: "terminus." In Bruno's geometry, a boundary is the limit where one thing ends and another begins; he argues it must have some physical dimension.</note>. Here it is necessary to recall and understand what was said above regarding the definition and distinction of both the <term>minimum</term> <note>The "minimum" is the smallest possible physical unit or atom of space.</note> and the boundary—which should be more than sufficient for a mind that is not entirely dull. For one who thinks otherwise must establish a single part that can be touched by more equal parts than it has sides or angles. Let him show that a minimum acting as a boundary can limit more parts than a minimum that is simply a part itself. Let him show that the "minimum of minimums"—the archetype of which is the circle, into which every angled shape is resolved—can be touched by more than six others around it <note>Bruno is referencing hexagonal packing: in a plane, a circle can only be touched by six other circles of the same size. He uses this to prove that a center point cannot be touched by "infinite" lines.</note>. If he can, then we will concede that this same thing (be it a minimum part or no part at all) can be the common boundary of all lines descending from the periphery; otherwise, it is the boundary of only six radii, and the common part of only three diameters. All these things are beyond all controversy if what we said in the first book has been correctly understood. Furthermore, whether you posit the contact of a minimum or a boundary, it is undoubtedly necessary that it be touched by some part of itself; hence, whether you call it a part or a boundary of parts, it must be touched from diverse and opposite directions. Such a thing cannot be indivisible and a boundary unless it is also divisible according to those diverse and opposite boundaries, no less than the entire area of a circle or the whole dimension of a sphere.

¶ 30. Furthermore, to one who considers the truth of the matter more carefully, it is manifest how, through a certain series, order, and analogy, the center or middle must always be understood as larger and larger as a larger and larger circumference is assumed. This continues to the point that even what is in itself a certain sphere coalescing from many parts—when compared to a far-removed surroundings—eventually becomes not just a part, but not even a boundary for the parts being compared. This has even been commonly conceded by the pressing light of truth itself, although the principle and cause of this fact has hitherto not been manifest to them due to a lack of foundational doctrine.

From this it will also be established how equal parts grow together, and by what orders of intervals it is permitted to both reach and discern certain and defined parts.

# CHAPTER VI.

## That ignorance of the minimum is the mother of the doctrine of irrational and incommensurable magnitudes.

<margin>10</margin>
*Since they lack a minimum, they are forced to say how great the **irrational** <term>aloga</term> <note>original: "Aloga." Derived from the Greek for "without ratio" or "unreasonable." In mathematics, these are irrational numbers that cannot be expressed as a simple fraction.</note> is. For this reason, how can these people be called measurers, when they are mindless, lacking a certain fixed minimum? (Which they themselves surely intended to call a measure). Why do they produce so few ratios outside the use of the **monad** <term>monadis</term> <note>original: "monadis." The fundamental unit of number; for Bruno, this is the mathematical equivalent of the physical atom.</note>?*
*Consequently, they do not measure from within the thing itself, but they impose a number themselves, just as the "continuous even" is now accepted as known, and the "odd" at other times. Furthermore, this free power is granted to the **divider** <note>original: "sectori." Literally "the cutter," referring to a mathematician who divides lines or shapes into infinite parts.</note> to choose a blind number of minimums; but the nature of the underlying matter certainly does not permit this at all.*
*But truly, when something is divided into equal and properly matched parts, if either type of cut occurs at will, nature (to which he is not truly subject) does not support him. In such cases, it is often impossible to find a part that serves as a common measure for one who is considered a measurer without a defined minimum.*
*You will see that such a man can define very few types: just as if a musician <note>original: "Rhythmicus." One who studies rhythm, meter, or musical proportion.</note> were unable to use the monad, how could he discern many things?*
<margin>20</margin>
*For a common part is not to be found everywhere in number unless both values are **commensurable** <note>original: "connumerabilis." Quantities that can be measured by the exact same unit.</note>. And the nearest number must be sought by a similar chance, through which they also approached a measure of a measure.*
*In this way, the measurer becomes like a mere counter; both are as close to the true art of number and measure as darkness is to light, or as falsehood is joined to truth.*

<meta>catchword: Every (original Latin "Omnis")</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that the concept of irrational numbers (aloga) and incommensurable magnitudes (asymmetra) only exists because mathematicians fail to recognize the "minimum" or fundamental unit of reality. He claims that without a fixed minimum, measurement becomes arbitrary and loses its connection to the true nature of matter.</summary>

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>OF THE DISCOVERY OF MINIMUMS, BOOK III</header>

The whole <term>treatment</term> <note>original: "tractatio"</note> of <term>irrational</term> <note>original: "alogis." Derived from the Greek for "without ratio" or "unreasonable."</note> and <term>incommensurable</term> <note>original: "asymmetris." Referring to magnitudes that cannot be measured by the same unit, like the side and diagonal of a square.</note> magnitudes has proceeded from ignorance, both from failing to capture the <term>minimum</term> <note>original: "minimi." The smallest possible unit of matter or space.</note>, and also from the fact that any magnitude is divisible on the part of the underlying object in the same way that it is divided according to the intention of the divider.
But we have said above that these continuous magnitudes are divided practically and mechanically, now into even parts and now into odd: because, on account of their imperceptibility, the countless primary parts are blurred together under a kind of appearance of indifference. But that difference, which in coarser works one can neither perceive nor see—as when a measure of grain or a pole <note>original: "pertica." A Roman unit of length, similar to a rod or perch.</note> can be divided into equal measures of a diverse and contrary kind—occurs because the distribution is not made by the number of minimums and indivisibles. In more refined works, the common crowd of <term>geometers</term> <note>original: "geometrarum"</note> sees [the difference], but for lack of a principle, they are unable to grasp it; yet they pursue measures only through the nearest approximations. But how we both grasp this well-perceived difference and contrast it according to the condition of the matter will be made manifest in what follows.

## CHAPTER VII.

-> I will examine any part, however great it may be of the whole, <-
-> and I will cast away the tables of sines. <-
<note>original: "tabulas sinuum." Bruno is rejecting the standard trigonometric tables of his era, which he believed were based on the false assumption of infinite divisibility.</note>

> *So that the <term>minimum</term>, hidden from the senses, may be encountered, and from there*

**TO GATHER** how great any part of the whole is as shown on a straight line, which is set as a total of sixty or twelve parts. <note>Bruno is referencing the two most common base systems for measurement in the Renaissance: the sexagesimal (base 60) used in astronomy and the duodecimal (base 12) often used in commerce and local measurements.</note> As an immediate principle, from which the demonstration of the subsequent operation is gathered, it must be noted that any part of a first part relates to the whole first part just as the first part relates to the whole. Likewise, any part of a second part (which is the third <note>By "third," Bruno likely means the third level of division, such as "seconds" in time or "thirds" in ancient sexagesimal geometry.</note>) relates to the whole second part, just as the whole second part relates to the first, and the first to the whole. The same ratio holds for fourths, fifths, sixths, and other successive parts of parts. Whence it is clear that in a line of twelve parts, one part occurs twelve times: its half twenty-four times: its third thirty-six times, and so on. Therefore, if indefinitely any part of one first [part] is taken,

...thus single or (if need be) doubled or tripled, which should be extended twelve times through the length of the whole line, so that part **B C** is divided upon the straight line **A B**, whose first parts are **PA**, **AR**, **RT**, etc. <note>Bruno is explaining how to take a tiny measurement and "magnify" it by repeating it twelve times along a scale.</note> If it is the third part of one [unit], then that part extended twelve times will fill four first parts; but these are called four **second parts**: If, for example, it is a little more or less than a third part of one, then (for the examination of the **third parts**) let that remainder which is beyond, or short of it, be taken and extended twelve times itself; or (if it should be smaller than can be taken) take another larger part, so that you may inspect the other parts to be added in alternating order right to the end.

This extension is done in practice upon the same single line in which the first parts are marked; which we show here on subordinate straight lines for the sake of explanation and distinction.

These parts, according to the progression from firsts to seconds, and from seconds to thirds, take up less the more they are multiplied; therefore, in the same order that the numbers increase, the volume designated by those numbers is conversely diminished, as is evident in the underlying triangle **ABC**.

**T**he second mode, easier and better, is designated here where: 1 is the straight line AB. 2 is the Angle ABC. 3 is one part added, BC. 4 is the triangle ABC. 5 is the Right angle at point B. 6 is the end of the base, C. 7 is the third side CA. 8 refers to triangles EBD and GBF, which are proportional to triangle ABC. 9 is the midpoint of the base, D. 10 is the "cut line" into which that point flows, AB. 11 is the "flow" <note>Bruno uses "fluxus" to mean the projection or drawing of a line</note> or line parallel to AC itself, which is CE. 12 is the midpoint of AB at point E. 13 is any other point, such as from point F. 14 is the parallel line FG [parallel] to the remainder, that is, to AC itself. 15 is at the cut line AB. 16 "there" means at point G. 17 it indicates the expressed parts (new parts) with a remainder, which must be examined from the base BC—either by itself or through another larger remainder—just like the others. 

The demonstration of this operation, as if from an immediate principle, is from that which is the second and fourth theorem of the sixth book of Euclid: *If a line is drawn parallel to one side of a triangle, it cuts the sides proportionally.* And: *The sides of equiangular triangles are proportional.*

The fourth mode is the best of all, and to it neither a better nor any other similar method can be added. It is formed by an equilateral triangle, which—since it is equal to itself on every side—will meet every side and every angle with similar parallels, distances, and parts. In this figure, it is the easiest of all to perceive parallels relative to every side. And we hold this figure to be as the sun among mathematical tables: since it alone manifests both itself and all other things.

<meta>catchword: When-</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that plane and spherical geometry are fundamentally different and warns that trying to measure a curved surface using the rules of flat surfaces leads to inherent errors. He specifically points out that the midpoint of a straight chord and a curved arc do not coincide, making simple proportional translations impossible.</summary>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>ON THE DISCOVERY OF THE MINIMUM, BOOK [III]</header>

*When will a greater distinction and clearer use not arise in this way?*
*But the parts labor by being thus confused,*
*not so that a right rule and exact reasoning might become known*
*from tiny parts: but rather so that error might be hidden,*
*and the confusion become greater by so large a number.*
*Hence let the idle <term>sine tables<gloss>Mathematical tables used in trigonometry to calculate angles and distances, which Bruno viewed as inaccurate approximations</gloss></term> be cast far away;*
*then you will know that the quadrant of a circle is always unequal*
*compared to the radius, whether you conceive of the whole*
*or all the parts of the straight and curved together,*
<margin>50</margin>
*where each part individually follows an unequal path,*
*so that any whole consists of these parts of its own,*
*taking from there a fresh measure,*
*whenever a progression is made to another part's*
*<term>momentum<gloss>The smallest unit of motion or change; here, the infinitesimal step in a geometric progression</gloss></term>. So that the midpoint of a midpoint*
*will never be accepted as the quadrant, and the third of a quadrant*
*could not be accepted as a third by following a certain order*
*in the curve from the straight, and in the straight from the parts of the curve.*
*For just as the arc is stretched obliquely from the radius,*
*so for any part, a corresponding part must be placed beneath it;*
<margin>60</margin>
*nor does it help for the countless parts of the unequal to be doubled,*
*for they will find the unequal no less because of it.*
*Therefore, just as no certain proportion ever comes*
*from parts to parts, there will also be no measurement here.*

---

->The proper subject of the circle, in which its <term>minima<gloss>The fundamental, indivisible units of geometry in Bruno's philosophy—mathematical atoms</gloss></term> are seen demonstratively (just as they are seen on the flat <note>original: "Charitum plana cythara." The "Lute of the Graces" is a metaphor for a perfectly harmonious and clear mathematical demonstration.</note> lute of the Graces), is the sphere. Therefore, both practice and theory are perfected in an equilateral triangle formed upon a globe-shaped body: because there, the <term>analogy<gloss>In this context, a consistent ratio or proportional relationship</gloss></term> of parts to parts is always preserved. On a plane, however, that proportional section and increase cannot be shaped or governed by any art: because new measurements occur for one subdividing an arc on a plane. For a compass, even with its legs opened twice as wide, does not capture an arc on a plane that is twice as large, just as it does not capture a chord that is twice as large...<-

<summary>Bruno argues that traditional trigonometry (sine tables) is flawed because straight-line measurements cannot perfectly map onto curved arcs. He asserts that true geometric harmony is only found on a sphere, where the "minimum" units of geometry remain consistent.</summary>

# CHAPTER X.
## I will delineate any polygon, and I will divide an arc or circle by a given ratio.

*A circle and an arc are then cut*
*by a certain ratio: as when you have divided the orb*
*with the radius into six parts (since this measure*
*is perennial, firm, and persistent, accessible to the inner parts,*
*observing a single rule), let there be the limit of the arc of a triangle,*
*namely the simple and single base of a twin [triangle]: but for one,*
*the center will be the point. But if it should flow down from there,*
*it will be seen to have bisected the arc into the point of another.*
*Then a single radius is divided into as many parts*
<margin>10</margin> *as the proposed arc desires to be divided.*
*Then, as far as the limit of one part stands distant from the center,*
*let the <term>radius of rotation<gloss>original: "gyriradius"; the radius used to define circular paths or orbits within a diagram</gloss></term> and the sixth part of a smaller*
*arc be spread out; a line connecting the middle of this*
*to the center and the angle of the other triangle defines it, so that*
*the half of the arc, taken twice, replaces the part in the rotation*
*at the edge, so that, led through the arc twice as many times,*
*or doubled in the margin of the outermost rotation, it may mark it out.*

**H**ow arc HF may be divided in a given ratio. For example, into three parts, using that rectilinear isosceles <note>A triangle with two equal sides.</note> triangle CHF placed beneath it, whose side is divided into three parts, and whose plane is bisected by the straight line CE; then, from the boundary of one part **I** moving around, the arc **I K** is obtained. This arc, when more accurately taken as a median in the two parts **I G** and **G K**, and drawn twice through the outermost periphery, will define one part. You will perform this no less safely through the isosceles triangle G H F, handled in the same manner.

¶ 19. ### Third Method.

*Or after a given part has been traced through the whole circle,*
*Yet does not close the circuit with an exact boundary,*
*But exceeds it by an uncertain length, or is exceeded by it.*
*Once the parts are marked, let the <term>plectrum<gloss>The "striker" or movable arm of the geometric instrument</gloss></term> of the Arachne be fashioned for you,*
*Whose side should not extend beyond the radius of the circle; and here,*
*Join a part parallel to the first ones at the tip,*
<margin>40</margin> *Thence let the clarified minutes and seconds come to you,*
*With the rest, just as the Arachne’s striker searches them out.*

**J**udging what portion of the whole a part of the circle is—not by a given ratio, but simply—happens in three ways. This applies to parts that are definitely and absolutely defined, such as the semicircle (half), the whole quadrant (quarter), the sextant (sixth) <note>original: "semicirculus seu medietas, totus quadrans, sextans"</note>, and so forth, which are understood not by human convention, but from the intrinsic nature of the thing. The first way occurs with no underlying measure, but by the simple deduction of the part.

<meta>catchword: Second-</meta>

<summary>Bruno describes the 'Third Method' for dividing a circle into precise minutes and seconds using a geometric instrument called the Arachne. He emphasizes that certain divisions, like the semicircle or sextant, are natural properties of geometry rather than arbitrary human inventions.</summary>

<keywords>Arachne instrument, plectrum, circle division, minutes and seconds, geometric measurement, sextant, quadrant, intrinsic geometry</keywords>

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>ON THE DISCOVERY OF MINIMA, BOOK [I]</header>

Secondly, from the parts defined within the circle itself. Thirdly, from the remainder left by drawing a given part around the circuit. It is possible, however, to divide by any given ratio according to the smallest parts perceivable to the senses <note>original: "minima sensus"</note>, though not according to the actual nature of the thing, in cases where a ratio cannot be given from the root of six <note>original: "radicem senarii"; Bruno believed the number six was fundamental to the geometry of the physical world</note>.

# CHAPTER XII.
## I shall find a common
## measure.

*As it is permitted to descend into the first minimum part,*
*A common measure must also be discovered by you;*
*A certain ability is granted for all things proposed,*
*The irrational part (which is called <term>Aloga<gloss>From the Greek 'alogos', meaning irrational or lacking a fixed ratio</gloss></term>) at the tip of the Arachne's <term>plectrum<gloss>The movable arm or pointer of the geometric instrument described on the previous page</gloss></term>,*
*When it comes as an addition and is distributed through all the parts*
*Where there is need of pairs, for which there is one rule of the whole,*
*It makes the division known, and renowned in the great [work],*
*Called a <term>scale<gloss>original: "schala"</gloss></term> by its graduated name in the divine work,*
*Which the <term>rhythmicist<gloss>One who works with proportions and the "rhythm" of numbers</gloss></term> soon resolves into a simple form by his own art.*
*It does not come into practice because it is not to be grasped by itself*
*When it occurs. Furthermore, it is a great task to make these things similar,*
*Then finally it must be observed by you through a similar [measure].*
*Let no "Aloga" [irrational] doctrine assist you; and being without*
*A meter, may you be a measurer; let not rules be multiplied*
*In vain; so that you do not perpetually follow things never to be found,*
*And let a "near" <note>An approximate or "neighboring" measure</note> measure satisfy the measurer.*

<margin>16</margin>

## The common whole and common part
## of the measure.

*For the diameter supports the middle of the circuit, and*
*The side [of a square] is able to be completed four times [around it],*
*And by a radius of six: and you will see in a certain series*
*How the measure, the circle, and the straight line correspond in turn.*

<note>Vocabulary: measure, measurer, Aloga (irrational), circuit/turn, diameter, scale, of six/senary, circle.</note>

<summary>Bruno argues that while infinite mathematical division is possible in theory, a practical "common measure" must be found using instruments like the Arachne. He suggests that an approximate measure ("mensura propinqua") is often more useful for the practitioner than chasing irrational numbers.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from page 127, Bruno transitions from the theoretical existence of a "common measure" and the "minimum" to the practical application of these concepts in geometry, specifically citing the work of the mathematician Maurice Bressieu and introducing his own twelve-fold division of the circle.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

*As the radius is to the rotation of the circle, so too is every*
*Straight line contained under an arc in certain turns,*
*As Bressius <note>Maurice Bressieu (c. 1546–1617), a French mathematician and royal professor whom Bruno met in Paris</note> shows at times, as does the angle of the <term>Arachne<gloss>Bruno’s specialized geometric instrument, often described as a "spider-web" of intersecting lines and arcs</gloss></term>.*
*But the proper measure of the same kind through all things is*
*The spherical within the sphere, and the three-sided plane triangle*
*Within the plane, of which a similar part is from the vertex.*

**AS** it is permitted to find the minimum and the first part, so also from the <term>indifference<gloss>the point at which two things become indistinguishable</gloss></term> of the assumed minimum arc and the minimum chord, there is a path to a possible <term>quadrature of the circle<gloss>The ancient challenge of constructing a square with the exact same area as a given circle</gloss></term>—namely, by means of external positions (since it is not possible from within, as we shall show) and only to be understood through the perimeter. Likewise, to find a common measure is very easy—to find it, I say, to see it, and to name it; although in given magnitudes we cannot fully convey the exact smallness of that "minimum" or first and proper part. The common and <term>heterogeneous<gloss>consisting of different kinds; here, measuring a straight line by a curve</gloss></term> measure of two straight lines is the circle, through which they are examined in the Bressian work whenever they are contained within the same circle whose radius or diameter is larger. But the minor chord is whatever it may be. The <term>homogeneous<gloss>consisting of the same kind; measuring a curve by a curve or a line by a line</gloss></term> measure is the spherical triangle for arcs, and the rectilinear triangle (namely, the "Harp of the Graces" <note>original: "Charitum cythara"; a poetic name Bruno uses for a specific triangular geometric configuration</note>) for straight lines. Likewise, our common measure is twofold: namely, the **Whole** of diverse parts (and this is indirect), and the **Part** of diverse wholes (and this is direct). In the first way, the number twelve <note>original: "duodenarium"</note> is the common measure of all things contained within it, such as eleven, which contains the whole [twelve] along with an eleventh part of itself, or ten, which contains the whole along with a fifth part of itself, etc. In the second way, the number three <note>original: "Ternarium"</note> is the common measure of six, nine, and twelve. The first common measure is one, namely the "minimum," and this is twofold: simply (as it exists in nature) and in its genus (which is further divided into two: namely, according to the senses, and according to reason). By these ways, we are permitted to investigate and to find.

# GIORDANO BRU-
# NO OF NOLA, ON THE PRIN-
# CIPLES OF MEASURE AND
## FIGURE, BOOK ONE.

### CHAPTER I.

->Progression from the monad to the few, thence to the many, even to the innumerable and the infinite.<-

> ***A**nd once Pythagoras of Samos, foster-son of the Italian land* <note>original: "Latiæ Telluris alumnus" — referring to Pythagoras's school in Croton, Southern Italy</note>*, exalted the monad* <term>monad: from the Greek 'monas', meaning unity or the fundamental 'one'</term> *within the Tetrad* <term>Tetrad: the number four, which Pythagoreans believed contained the essence of the universe (1+2+3+4=10)</term>*, and the Tetrad within the monad;*
> *He who taught how the Monad migrates into the Dyad* <note>original: "Diadem" — the number two, representing the first step toward multiplicity</note>*, the Dyad into the Triad, and the Triad*
> *Into the Tetrad. This, finally, he said possessed the end,*
> *the number, and the determining moments of all things.*
> *For in itself it is first the minimum* <note>In Bruno’s system, the 'minimum' is the atom of geometry and physics</note>*, which then, as it flows,*
> *is followed by the first dyad: because a straight line,*
> *enclosed by two ends, is prior in order to the slanted circuit* <note>meaning the circle or curved line</note>*.*
> *Which is produced when one end*
<margin>10</margin>
> *circles around a fixed point until it has returned to the same,*
> *and has joined the straight tracks of its continuous flow;*
> *presently it brings back the inverted plane, the center remaining constant.*

<summary>Bruno introduces his work on geometry by invoking Pythagoras, describing how all complexity in the universe arises from the "monad" (a single point) expanding into lines, surfaces, and solids.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Monad, Tetrad, Dyad, Triad, geometry, minimum, straight line, circle, cosmology</keywords>

<page-num>131</page-num>

...the bodily appearance of a sphere <note>original: "sphaerae" — here Bruno is completing a sentence from the previous page describing the creation of a three-dimensional body</note>. Nor is it granted thereafter

Add that the monad, dyad, triad, and tetrad themselves
Represent every principle. The monad <term>monad: the "One" or unit</term> is indeed for all things that which

While the triad <term>triad: the number three</term> is the first odd number; and then follows (at line 20)

Which closes all numbers, the number of numbers.

By the name of which the first dimension of quantity is marked.
But first, it is worth the effort to go and salute
the inner sanctuaries of the sacred fountain <note>original: "sacri penetralia fontis" — a metaphorical reference to the Monad or the divine source of all things</note>, and to pluck the first offerings,
<margin>30</margin> Since nature will first allow us to recall to certain forms
the infinite species <term>species: here meaning the varied outward appearances or types of things</term> through which we wander by art—
those forms which the kindred mind encompasses in a few,
all the way to the first Mind of number, which possesses all
powers and action at once in the monad <term>monad: the "One" or the fundamental unit from which all numbers and things arise</term>. Thus, in a certain order,
number propagates itself (hanging from the fountain of the monad).
Thus nature gave to art the first foundations
upon which it may fashion similar species. And soon, from these elements,
it may run into an innumerable count, with the elements placed in various order.
Therefore, the closer a power is to the Mind,
<margin>40</margin> the more it recedes from the confused circle of number;
so that at last a simple series leads back to the One.
But reason, even with all its effort, is not of such great power
that the simple might first follow the composite <note>original: "ut se composta simplex primum adsequutetur" — Bruno suggests that the human mind usually perceives complex things first and must work backward to find the simple "One" underlying them</note>.
But sometimes disentangling the chaos, reason approaches number,
and follows the monad, perceiving it in a single,
double, or triple order: because the simple, unless it be released,
cannot become easily visible to our eyes.

---

The universe to Xenophanes <note>Xenophanes of Colophon (c. 570 – c. 475 BC), a Greek philosopher who emphasized that "All is One"</note> the metaphysician is one, unique, and absolute. To Parmenides <note>Parmenides of Elea, a student of the Eleatic school who argued that reality is a single, unchanging Being</note>, his disciple—who descends from divinity to nature, and from nature to natural things, and again strives from natural things through nature back into divine speculation—the universe is defined as threefold: in **Effect**, **Type**, and **Archetype** <term>Archetype, Type, and Effect: the divine idea (archetype), the formal blueprint (type), and the physical result (effect)</term>. Or it is defined Materially, Formally, and Efficiently. Or Explicitly, implicitly, and summarily. Namely, in **Number**, in **Order**, and in the **Monad**; that is, through individuals, through all things, and through the Whole. Where, in the first way, the universe is **Subsistently**, in the second **Consistently**, and in the third **Existentially**. Likewise, first **Movably**, second **Stably**, and third **Immovably**.

¶ 30. According to the ratio of the first, we look upon anything as distinct in its incommunicable property; it is one separately and in itself.

<summary>Bruno explains that while nature and art produce infinite variety, they all originate from the "Monad" or the One. He cites the ancient philosophers Xenophanes and Parmenides to illustrate how the universe can be viewed as a single, absolute entity or a threefold progression from divine ideas to physical effects.</summary>
<keywords>Monad, Xenophanes, Parmenides, Neoplatonism, unity of nature, archetypes, number theory, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical transition from the geometric construction of the previous pages to the metaphysical origins of number and form. Having established the Monad as the source, Bruno now explains how the human mind ascends from complex, material things back to the simple unity of the One, and how nature generates the many from the few.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>
->### ON THE PRINCIPLES OF MEASURE AND FIGURE, BOOK...<-

According to the logic of the second [the Dyad], we turn toward all things that flow from that same principle, persist in that same preserver, and breathe together toward that same end. According to the more absolute contemplation of the third [the Triad], we experience how all particular things ascend to certain species, then all species to subordinate genera, which finally ascend to one most common and most general being—existence itself—and from there to the most absolute entity, Truth, the <term>Monad</term> <note>Monad: from the Greek "monas," meaning unity or the number one. For Bruno, it represents the indivisible source of all existence</note>. There, the unity that is the source of all numbers embraces all multitude and variety in a way that cannot be multiplied or changed, existing both incommunicably and invariably. Thus, from the light found in shadows and composite things, we ascend to the simple and absolute light; so that, having known that light, we may descend to the judgment, examination, and governance of the other.

¶ None of the things that exist differ so much that they do not, in some way—indeed even in most and principal ways—agree with that from which they differ and to which they are opposed. For we see that nothing in the elements of nature acts as an opponent unless it agrees with the other in the desire for the same subject, or at least in the desire for its own self-preservation. Hence, it is manifest even to the common crowd of <gloss>philosophers</gloss> <note>original: "philosophantium" — those who practice philosophy</note> that all opposites consist in the same genus because of the matter they both share. Likewise, nothing in the universe is so small that it does not contribute to the integrity and perfection of the excellent whole. Similarly, nothing is "evil" to certain things or in one place that is not also "good" and "optimal" to others and elsewhere. Therefore, to one looking at the universe, nothing foul, evil, or incongruous will occur; for variety and contrariety do not prevent all things from being the best, specifically as they are governed by nature. Nature, like a <note>original: "phonascus" — a voice teacher or choir director in antiquity who ensured harmony among different singers</note>, directs and leads contrary voices—the highest, the lowest, and those in the middle—into one single symphony, the best that we can imagine. But we have defined these things more extensively in the book *On the Principle and the One*, where we more expressly revealed the <term>coincidence of all opposites</term> <note>original: "oppositorum coincidentiam" — the mystical and philosophical idea that at the level of the infinite, opposites like "greatest" and "smallest" become the same</note> and restored the best principle of a philosophy once dead and now to be recovered.

¶ Human reason can by no means arrive at a precise grasp of that indifference of all opposites, which lies hidden through the contemplation of the greatest in the smallest, and the smallest in the greatest. We strive in this kind of work toward a speculation of those things which are in and around nature through the way of the "coincidence of dimensions." We observe, likewise, how nature proceeds to the production of multitude by the unfolding of the <term>Monad</term> into the <term>Dyad</term> <note>Dyad: the principle of "two" or duality</note>. And soon, by the combination of the <term>Dyad</term> and the <term>Monad</term> into the <term>Triad</term> <note>Triad: the principle of "three" or the first stable composite</note>; and again, with the <term>Monad</term> composed with the <term>Triad</term>, and the <term>Triad</term> with the <term>Dyad</term>, and these and others with the rest, nature moves forward perpetually to the production of innumerable things through various and manifold types of union—which it does now in numbers and measures (mathematically), at other times in the elements of things (physically), and again in the realm of ideas.

<sig>I 3</sig>

<summary>Bruno discusses how all plurality and opposition in the universe eventually resolve into the unity of the Monad, arguing that "evil" or "discord" are merely matters of perspective within a larger natural harmony. He introduces the "coincidence of opposites" as the key to understanding how nature generates infinite variety from the basic building blocks of the Monad, Dyad, and Triad.</summary>
<keywords>Monad, Dyad, Triad, Coincidence of Opposites, Universal Harmony, Nature as Conductor, Metaphysics of Number, Giordano Bruno</keywords>
