<meta>This page continues Bruno's metaphysical exploration of how the "One" becomes the "Many." Having previously discussed the Monad as the absolute source, he now uses geometry—the movement of a point into a line, then into a plane and a solid—as a map for how the divine mind generates the physical universe. This transition from "Metaphysics" to "Mathematics" is a hallmark of his philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

...and we contemplate metaphysically by a certain prolific light above mundane things.

¶ 6 Applying ourselves, therefore, to the purpose of the present contemplation, we strive from our <term>Monad</term> <note>The Monad is the "unit" or the "One." For Bruno, it is the indivisible foundation of both numbers and reality.</note>—which is the **point**—into the propagation of multitude in its own order. Where the Monad has moved out from absolute being to be situated somewhere as a Monad, here it is an atomic body, and there it is a point. This point, flowing toward another established end or boundary, generates the image of the <term>dyad</term> <note>original: "diadis" — representing the number two and the principle of duality or division.</note>: the **line**. This line, limited by two boundaries or wandering vaguely, would conclude, fix, or figure nothing; unless it applies itself to a third common boundary, and then the first **triangular** figure follows, the leader in one order. Or, both ends of the line are turned and reflected back upon themselves around the other, and then there is the first figure of another order [the circle]. And this is the first <term>triad</term> <note>original: "trias" — the number three, representing the first stable form.</note> produced from the image of the Monad, the point: the **Straight Line**, the **Triangle**, and the **Circle**, which are the principles of all characters, figures, and images. Immediately following from the particulars of this first triad are the nearest principles of all things, where the straight line meets another straight line in an angle in three ways to bring forth the three types of triangles, which are found both as a measure and as something measurable within a triple order of circles. Thus from Mathematics we aspire to the deeper speculation of nature and the contemplation of divine things.

¶ 13. The first thing from a flowing point is the **straight line**; if this is moved with one end fixed until it reflects back on itself, it produces a **plane**. If, with the center remaining constant, that plane is turned from semicircle to semicircle in a merging track, it produces a **sphere**. Or, where the flow of the point gave length, the flow of length gave width, and the flow of width gave depth. There is no access to a fourth type of magnitude, because in the same point G, no more than three diameters—AB in length, CD in height, and EF in depth—will intersect each other.

¶ 14. Therefore, just as the order of nature requires, we must progress from the contemplation of the <term>minimum</term> <note>Bruno's "minimum" is the basic unit of reality—the atom in physics, the point in geometry, and the monad in metaphysics.</note> to the contemplation of the line. But for the sake of both the line itself and the other things that must subsequently be held by <term>apodictic</term> <note>original: "apodictica" — meaning certain, proven, or demonstrative reasoning.</note> reasoning, let us first assert the archetypes and elements of all sensible forms.

¶ The lower the intellect, the more it consists of a greater multitude; for it degenerates from the first monad—the supreme mind—toward the region of matter. Nature, the <gloss>craftswoman</gloss> <note>original: "natura opifex" — nature as a creative builder or artist.</note>, informed by one or another or at least very few objects of ideas, explains the many kinds of things we see, both through the various composition of those species and the various disposition of matter (unless you understand one of these to be better than the other). Then, who can define how many things **Art**—from such a great number of natural forms—is able to represent, as well as fashion into and against their likeness, by composing and dividing, and likewise by perceiving and abstracting? Yet it is also a property of art that from...

<summary>Bruno explains how the three-dimensional world arises from the "point" or Monad, moving through the line and the triangle to create the basic archetypes of all physical form. He argues that all complexity in nature and art stems from these simple, divine geometric origins.</summary>

...it is advanced from certain and definite principles to indefinite consequences. Therefore, in the likeness of Nature, from a few definite elements—which are at once the most fertile and all-yielding—it prepares itself for the logic of numerous works.

# CHAPTER II.
## On the enfolding of all things, and the unfolding
## of the three figures into all things.

> *There are three primary <term>archetypes</term> <note>original: "archetypi" — the original patterns or models from which all things are made.</note> in whose appearance everything*
> *is a rule of momentum, a measure, and a figure.*
> *I give the first to **Phoebus** <note>The sun god Apollo, representing the unity of the Monad and the light of the mind.</note>, and the second squares with **Minerva** <note>The goddess of wisdom, here associated with the square and the structured order of the intellect.</note>,*
> *and the third belongs to **Venus** <note>The goddess of beauty, representing the harmony and "attraction" found in circles and triangles.</note>. Indeed, they recognize*
> *their own seal in the face of these, and secrets in the deep.*
> *Where the fourfold cycle comes together for a triple lot,*
> *if either one center, or one rule, enfolds them all—*
> *The sensible cycle and the triangular figure*
> *fitly constitute those things which are, in turn, the measure.*
> *For these two are the smallest, and they are the largest in turn.*
> *The largest [figure] which embraces the cycle is a single triangle,*
> *and the smallest drawn within a cycle is a single triangle;*
> *that same figure alone carries an inscribed cycle.*
> *But the circle alone contains all things inscribed within it,*
> *and the inscribed circle alone remains the same in all things.*
> *Therefore, whether simple or under that triple appearance,*
> *it is the measure for measures, and the figure for figures;*
> *then with clear light, and with a very easy stroke,*
> *it indicates and brings about whatever is sought by the ray.*
> *Therefore, we desire to distinguish all things from the whole,*
> *and to provide from the whole the cause for all the parts,*
> *as the appearance of the thing itself was first discovered and received.*

<sig>I 4</sig>

<vocab>archetype, measure, figure, triangle, cycle (circle), Phoebus, Minerva, Venus, enfolding (implication), unfolding (explication)</vocab>

<summary>Bruno introduces three primary archetypal shapes—the circle, the square, and the triangle—and assigns them to the deities Phoebus, Minerva, and Venus to represent different aspects of cosmic order. He argues that these fundamental geometric forms are the "measure of all measures" from which the entire complexity of nature is unfolded.</summary>

While the previous page focused on the three "archetypes" (Sun/Apollo, Minerva, Venus), this page moves into the methodology of "unfolding": how one must study the whole before the parts to avoid losing the essence of the subject, using the anatomical metaphor of a body.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO THE NOLAN</header>
<meta>catchword: IN</meta>

*In the order of nature, as the appearance of the*
*composite whole is thereafter revealed, through individual members and sections;*
*therefore, look upon the faces of the book by which you may behold*
*all things before you; then you must read each thing again.*
*For one wishing to recall the parts of man to a number,*
<margin>30</margin> *let the order of the <term>hypostasis</term> <note>original: "hypostasis" — the underlying fundamental reality or essential substance of a thing.</note> be first entire before the eyes,*
*then, from there, let the smallest parts into which the viscera are finally*
*dissolved come forth in their own steps, unlocked with their own figures,*
*measures, signs, reasons, and functions:*
*even if all things that are to be are not perpetually present,*
*nevertheless, grasp them from this greater part, and the principal ones;*
*thus a sufficient appearance of the figure is presented to you,*
*so that there is no need for words for a demonstration to be made.*
*Thus you will not recognize **Hippolytus** <note>In Greek mythology, Hippolytus was dragged by his horses and torn into pieces. Bruno uses this as a metaphor: if you study a body only as scattered parts, you lose the "person."</note> in torn and limbs scattered*
*everywhere; nor will you truly understand those to be his parts:*
<margin>40</margin> *but as if driven further away, however much individual things*
*are grasped by the eyes on their own, they are more confused;*
*because they do not reflect the <term>formal condition</term> <note>The "form" or organizing principle that makes a thing what it is, rather than just a pile of matter.</note> of the part,*
*nor the certain act of the whole.*
*For comparison and order indicate the part and the whole*
*reflexively. Therefore, it is our purpose to show the parts*
*within their own whole; so that all things may then be easily proven,*
*as if from internal objects, causes, proper and immediate, to the highest cognition.*
*The same certain order which nature noted,*
*surely in an exalted condition for those things coming together,*
*so that individual things of the formed may be explained to you in all things,*
<margin>50</margin> *and all things individually and properly in those things*
*with which they consist, coming together in a certain series.*
*And here, finally, is the goal of the art and the sacred **seal**. <note>original: "sigillum" — for Bruno, a "seal" is a complex symbolic image that captures a universal truth or mnemonic structure.</note>*

<summary>Bruno argues that to understand any complex system (like a human body or a mathematical figure), one must first grasp the "whole" or its "formal condition" before analyzing the individual parts. He warns that studying parts in isolation—like the scattered limbs of the mythical Hippolytus—leads to confusion rather than true knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Hippolytus, holism, anatomy, hypostasis, formal condition, seal, order of nature, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

<header>ON THE PRINCIPLE, MEASURE, AND FIGURE OF THE BOOK</header>

### REGARDING CHAPTERS II, III, IV, V, VI

The fourfold set of circles <note>original: "circulorum quaternarium"</note> will fruitfully join together in no other way than by these three differences which were just proposed: in these, all **cosmometric figures** <note>original: "cosmometricæ figuræ" — shapes used for measuring and mapping the structure of the universe</note> will be found, not only implicitly, but also explicitly; either in one of them, or in two, or in three; either individually, or in a combination of two or three. We shall very easily grasp their admirable excellence through their application toward investigating, finding, and judging, as anyone endowed with even moderate judgment has found out for himself.

$\P$ 20. Such is the order of knowing: from the whole, and as if from a confused state, to the parts distinctly; and from the parts distinctly perceived, at last to a distinct conception of the whole.

$\P$ 34. If anyone seeks the most exact and briefest demonstration for the body of a figure with the application of principles to be referred back, let him look in turn: for the whole is proved by the **all-ness** <note>original: "omnitate" — a philosophical term for the state of being a complete "all" or containing all parts</note> of the parts, and all and individual parts are proved by the totality of the entire whole.

## CHAPTER IV.
### The Construction of the Court of Pallas.
<note>Pallas refers to Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom. In Bruno's system, her "court" represents a specific structure for organizing intellectual or scientific data.</note>

*And now the second sublime temples of Pallas follow in this place,*
*Here where the wealthy Amalthea <note>The goat-nymph who nursed the infant Jupiter; in memory systems, she often represents abundance or the source of ideas.</note> brings forth the first nourishments of Jove;*
*And Bacchis <note>A follower of Bacchus or a priestess; here acting as a guide through the memory structure.</note>, the holy priestess, flows around this place*
*By the rule of an extended ray, and having surveyed the circle,*
*She finds six painted shrines:*
*The first, indeed, for herself; and a second built for Ceres <note>Goddess of agriculture.</note>.*
*Then, placed in order and separated by an equal distance,*
*Are Deiopeia, Euphrosyne, Flora, and Galatea <note>These figures (a nymph, a Grace, a flower goddess, and a sea nymph) serve as specific "containers" for information in the circular diagram.</note>.*
*From there, one path exists joining the sacred things of Ceres and Galatea,*
<margin>10</margin>
*And another path connects Flora to Deiopeia.*
*Bacchus would go straight across these to reach Euphrosyne;*
*And here, where the path of Ceres is divided, Hebe <note>Goddess of youth.</note> is seen,*
*And where the path of Flora is split, Io is glimpsed.*
*Bacchis will move around Hebe, and Euphrosyne will go around Io.*

<summary>This page concludes the spatial arrangement of the Court of Venus and introduces the Court of Pallas, a mnemonic structure featuring six shrines dedicated to mythological figures like Ceres and Flora. These figures act as specific "loci" or places where a practitioner of the Art of Memory would store information.</summary>

*Know that at the extreme end of this lies the desert*

## CHAPTER V.

### The structure of the Atrium of Venus.

**B**ehold the holy worship and venerable
->*temple of Venus;*<-
*In the center of which is Love, the father of geniuses <note>original: "Amor ingeniorum" — here Love is presented as the source of all creative and intellectual talent.</note>.*
*A fourfold secret of concentric circles surrounds him,*
*Which a single radius had distinguished with equal intervals,*
*A radius which was first released from the center into the orb,*
*Which the same radius then divided into six boundaries.*
*Through these, Love pierces the outermost faces with six arrows,*
*And divides the penetrated circles into just as many parts;*
*Commanding that all the inner chambers take up their own presiding power*
<margin>10</margin> *Each one individually. From here, a fourfold order counts out*
*Twelve parts; in this series they are placed there to be seen.*
*The first is the Appearance of the Good. Holy Concord*
*has obtained the second* ->(and<-
*place; following her is Affection, and Emphasis,*
*Enchantment, Burning Fury, and Sweet Grace.*
*The following harmonic order first presents Honor,*
*And mild Indulgence in the sacred image of Fire.* ->(and<-
*Then Dance, most pleasing to the choirs of Nymphs and Dryads,*
*Followed by playful Lustfulness, soft Music,*
*And the Novelty of Nature. But the third order has*
<margin>20</margin> *Ornament placed first, and Peace from the fountains of Porus <note>In Greek mythology and Plato's philosophy, Porus is the personification of Resource or Plenty, and the father of Love.</note>.*
*Following these is Complaint joined with snatched rest,*
*Hope burning with rigor, and Trepidation of the mind.*
*The fourth order then claims twelve groves,*
*Which take their name individually from the neighboring deity.*
*And thus you may approach the other Circles by their general name.*

Each compartment is assigned a personified quality—such as Concord, Honor, or Music—to act as a visual hook for the memory.</summary>

## THE ATRIUM OF APOLLO.

*For all things, there is a return to the center by a shorter path,*
*And for all, access to all others follows the same order.* <note>Bruno is describing the efficiency of this mnemonic "map." In this system, every point of information is interconnected, allowing the mind to jump from a specific detail back to the central concept (Apollo/The Sun) or across to a related idea with mathematical precision.</note>

## CHAPTER VI.

### Concerning every figure, whether explicit or implicit, 
### found herein.

<note>The diagram accompanying this text, titled "Atrium Apollinis," is a masterclass in Renaissance "Sacred Geometry." It features a central "Flower of Life" pattern—overlapping circles that create a hexagonal grid. This is encased in a square frame with four crescent moons and stars in the corners, symbolizing the celestial influence on terrestrial mathematics. The letters (P, C, F, E, etc.) act as coordinates for the memory artist to place specific facts or concepts, turning a complex geometric shape into a functional filing cabinet for the mind.</note>

# GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA, 
# ON THE PRINCIPLES OF MEASURE 
# AND FIGURE, BOOK I.

### C H A P T E R  I.

#### The progression from the monad to the few, then to the many, up to the innumerable and the immense.

*Pythagoras of Samos, foster-son of the Italian land,* <note>Pythagoras (c. 570–495 BC) moved from Greece to Croton in Southern Italy, which explains the "Italian land" reference. He is the central figure for Bruno's mathematical mysticism.</note>
*Once extolled the monad in the Tetrad, and the Tetrad in the monad;* <note>original: "monadem in Tetrade" — The <term>Monad</term> (the One) and the <term>Tetrad</term> (the Four). In Pythagorean thought, 1+2+3+4 = 10 (the Decad), meaning the source of all numbers is contained within the first four.</note>
*He who taught the Monad passing into the Dyad, the Dyad into the Triad,*
*And the Triad*
*Into the Tetrad. This, finally, he called the limit,*
*And said that the movements of things possessed this number.*
*For in its own right, first there is the <term>minimum</term>, from which, then flowing,* <note>original: "minimum" — For Bruno, the "minimum" is the fundamental building block of the universe, equivalent to a geometric point or a physical atom.</note>
*The first dyad follows: because a straight line, enclosed by two*
*Boundaries, is prior in order to the oblique circuit.* <note>Bruno argues that the straight line (defined by two points/dyad) must logically exist before a circle (the oblique circuit) can be drawn.</note>
*Which is generated when one end circles around the other*
<margin>10</margin> *Constant point until it is revolved back to that same point,*
*And has joined the straight tracks of its continuous flow;* <note>This describes the geometric construction of a circle using a compass: one point remains fixed while the other travels a "straight track" (the radius) around it.</note>
*Soon it brings back an inverted plane, with the center remaining constant.*

<summary>This opening page introduces the Pythagorean foundation of the work, describing how all reality and geometry flow from the 'Monad' (the One) through the 'Tetrad' (the Four). It explains the generation of the first dimension (the line) and the second dimension (the circle/plane) from these primary numerical principles.</summary>

*By the name which marks the first dimension of quantity.*
*But first, it is the task to go and greet the inner sanctuary*
*Of the sacred spring, and to pluck the first offerings,*
<margin>30</margin> *When it will be permitted to first recall the infinite species—*
*Through which we wander by art—to the certain principles*
*Of nature, which the kindred mind embraces in a few things,*
*All the way to the first mind, [which holds] the virtues and the act*
*Of every number in the <term>monad</term> <note>original: "monade" — the fundamental unit or "One" that Bruno considers the source of all existence.</note> at once. Thus, holding <gloss>having</gloss>*
*Itself in a certain order, number propagates (hanging from*
*The fountain of the monad). Thus he gave the first foundations*
*To the art of nature, so that she might fashion similar species.*
*And soon, in these elements, an innumerable number*
*May run forth in a varied order. Therefore, the closer*
*The power is to the mind, the further it recedes from*
<margin>40</margin> *The confused orb of number; until at last a simple series*
*Leads back to the one. But reason, with all its effort,*
*Is not of such great power that it might first reach*
*The simple from the composite. But sometimes, unwinding*
*The chaos, it approaches number, and follows the monad,*
*Perceiving it in a single, double, or triple order;*
*Because the simple, unless it is loosened, cannot*
*Be made easily accessible to our eyes.*

The universe to **Xenophanes** <note>Xenophanes of Colophon (c. 570 – c. 475 BC) was a Greek philosopher known for his early monotheism and the idea that "All is One."</note> the metaphysician is one, unique, and absolute. To **Parmenides** <note>Parmenides of Elea (early 5th century BC) was a student of the school of Xenophanes who argued that reality is a single, unchanging Being.</note> (the disciple of the former), who descends from divinity to nature and from nature to natural things—and again, who contends from natural things through nature back into divine speculation—the universe is defined in a triple way: in **Effect**, in **Type**, and in **Archetype**. Or **Materially**, **Formally**, and **Efficiently**. Or **Unfolded**, **Enfolded**, and **In Summary** <note>original: "Explicatim, complicatim, summatim" — standard Neoplatonic terms describing how the One "unfolds" into many and "enfolds" back into unity.</note>. Namely, in **Number**, in **Order**, and in the **Monad**—that is, through **Individuals**, through **All Things**, and through the **Whole**. Where in the first mode, the universe is **Subsistently**; in the second, **Consistently**; in the third, **Existently**. Likewise, in the first, it is **Movably**; in the second, **Stably**; in the third, **Immovably**.

¶ 30. In accordance with the logic of the first [the Monad], we look upon anything as distinct in its incommunicable property; it is separate and one in itself.

<meta>catchword: In accordance (Iuxta)</meta>

<summary>Bruno describes the metaphysical descent from the divine "Monad" into the plurality of the physical world, citing Xenophanes and Parmenides to explain how the universe exists simultaneously as a single unit and a complex numerical order.</summary>
<keywords>Monad, Xenophanes, Parmenides, metaphysics, unity, Neoplatonism, number theory, archetypes</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 132, Bruno deepens his metaphysical exploration of how the "Monad" (the One) unfolds into the diversity of the physical world. He moves from discussing the philosophical unity of the universe to the harmony found between opposing forces.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>

->ON THE PRINCIPLES OF MEASURES AND FIGURES, BOOKS.<-

According to the logic of the second [level of reasoning], we turn toward all things that flow from the same principle, persist in that same conserving force, and breathe together toward the same end. According to the more absolute contemplation of the third [level], we experience how all particular things ascend to certain species, and then all species to subordinate genera; these finally ascend to one most common and most general **being**, to **existence**, and from there to the most absolute entity: **Truth**, the **Monad** <note>original: "Monadem" — the ultimate unit or "One" which Bruno identifies with God and the source of all reality.</note>. There, Unity—the source of all numbers—embraces all multitude and variety in a way that cannot be multiplied or changed, existing incommunicably and invariably. Thus, from the light found in shadows and composite things, we ascend to the simple and absolute light; so that, once that light is known, we may descend to the judgment, examination, and governance of the former.

¶ None of the things that exist differ so much that they do not also agree in some way—indeed, even in many and principal ways—with that from which they differ and to which they are opposed. For we see no opposition in the elements of nature except that which agrees with another in the desire of the same subject, or at least in the desire for its own self-preservation. Hence, it is manifest even to the common crowd of philosophers that all opposites, because of the matter they both share, exist within the same category. Likewise, nothing in the universe is so small that it does not contribute to the integrity and perfection of the excellent whole. Similarly, nothing is "evil" for some people or in some place, which is not also "good" and "best" for others or elsewhere. Hence, to one looking at the universe as a whole, nothing shameful, evil, or incongruous will be found. For variety and contrariety do not prevent all things from being the "best," insofar as they are governed by nature. Nature acts like a <term>conductor</term> <note>original: "phonascus" — an ancient term for a voice trainer or master of music who harmonizes different vocal ranges.</note> who directs and leads the extreme and middle voices—though they are opposites—into the one best symphony that we can possibly imagine. But we have defined these things more extensively in our book *On the Principle and the One*, where we more clearly demonstrated the <term>coincidence of opposites</term> <note>original: "oppositorum coincidentiam" — the philosophical principle that at the highest level of reality, contrary things (like the greatest and the smallest) are actually the same.</note> and restored the best principle of philosophy, which was once dead and is now to be recovered.

¶ Human reason is least able to reach that point of grasping the "indifference" of all opposites—that state where they are no longer different—which lies hidden through the contemplation of the **greatest in the smallest**, and the **smallest in the greatest**. In this work, we strive through the way of the "coincidence of dimensions" toward the contemplation of those things which are in nature and around nature. We observe how nature proceeds toward the production of a multitude of things by the **unfolding** of the **Monad** into the **Dyad** <note>The number two, representing the first step away from unity into duality.</note>. Soon, by the combination of the Dyad and Monad, it proceeds into the **Triad** <note>The number three.</note>. Again, with the Monad composed with the Triad, and the Triad with the Dyad, and these and others with the rest, nature moves forward perpetually to the production of innumerable things by a varied and manifold kind of union. This she performs now indeed in numbers and measures, <term>mathematically</term>; at other times in the elements of things, <term>physically</term>; and again in the realm of <term>ideas</term>.

I 3

<summary>Bruno explains how all diversity in the universe stems from a single "Monad" and how even opposing forces are harmonized by nature like a musical symphony. He introduces the "coincidence of opposites," arguing that at the highest level of reality, the greatest and the smallest are one.</summary>
<keywords>Monad, Coincidence of Opposites, Unity, Dyad, Triad, Metaphysics, Universal Harmony, Philosophy of Nature</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 133, Bruno continues to explain the descent from the absolute Monad (the One) into the geometric and physical world of plurality.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
->GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA<-
<meta>catchword: prin-</meta>

...and we speculate metaphysically above through a certain prolific, mundane light.

¶ 6 Therefore, applying ourselves to the purpose of the present contemplation, we strive to move from our <term>Monad</term> <note>original: "Monas." The fundamental unit, representing both the mathematical point and the divine "One" from which all existence flows.</note>—which is a point—into the propagation of multitude in its own order. When the Monad escapes from absolute existence to be situated somewhere, it is here an <term>atomic body</term> <note>original: "atomum corpus." For Bruno, the atom is the physical manifestation of the mathematical point.</note>, and there a point. Now this [point], flowing toward another established end or boundary, gives birth to the likeness of the <term>dyad</term> <note>original: "diadis." The "dyad" or "twoness" represents the first step away from unity into duality and extension.</note>, the line. This line, bounded by two endpoints or wandering aimlessly, would conclude, fix, or figure nothing; unless it applies itself to a third common boundary, and then the first triangular figure follows—the leader in one order—or else each endpoint is turned back upon itself and reflected around the other, and then it is the first figure of another order [the circle]. And this is the first <term>triad</term> <note>original: "trias." A group of three.</note> produced from the image of the Monad, the point: the **Straight line, Triangle, Circle**, which are the principles of all characters, figures, and images. Immediately after, from the particulars of this first triad, the nearest principles of all things follow, where the straight line meets the straight line in three ways at an angle to lead out three types of triangles, which is found both as a measure and as something measurable along with the three orders of circles. Thus from Mathematics we aspire to the deeper speculation of natural things and the contemplation of the divine.

¶ 13. First, from the flowing point comes the straight line: which, if one end is fixed and the other moved until it reflects back, produces a plane; but if it is turned with the center remaining constant, from a semicircle into a semicircle flowing together in its wake, it produces a sphere. Or, where the flow of the point gives length, the flow of length gives width, and the flow of width gives depth, there is no entry to a fourth kind of magnitude, because in the same point **G**, no more than three diameters—**A B** in length, **C D** in height, and **E F** in depth—will intersect one another.

¶ 14. Just as the order of nature requires, we must proceed in contemplation from the minimum to the line; but to account for both the minimum itself and the other things that must subsequently be held, we shall first bring forward the archetypes and elements of all sensible forms by <term>apodictic reason</term> <note>original: "apodictica ratione." Reasoning that is clearly demonstrable or logically certain.</note>.

¶ The lower the intellect, the more it consists in a greater multitude; for it degenerates from the first Monad—the supreme Mind—toward the region of matter. <term>Nature the craftsman</term> <note>original: "natura opifex." The personified creative force of the natural world.</note>, informed by one or another or at least very few objects of ideas, unfolds the many kinds of things we see, partly through the varied composition of those species, and partly through the varied disposition of matter (unless you understand one of these to be better than the other). Who then can define how many things **Art** is able to represent, or to shape toward and against the likeness of these, from such a great number of natural forms by composing, dividing, observing, and abstracting? However, it is also a task of Art that from...

<summary>Bruno explains how the Monad (the One) unfolds geometrically from a point into lines, triangles, and circles, which form the basis of all physical reality. He argues that three dimensions (length, height, depth) are the limit of physical magnitude and describes how Nature and Art manifest from these primary forms.</summary>
<keywords>Monad, point, atom, triad, geometry, three dimensions, nature opifex, intellect, metaphysics</keywords>

...one is carried forward from certain and defined principles to indefinite consequences. Therefore, in the likeness of Nature, from a few defined elements—which are at once most fertile and universally available—one girds oneself for the reasoning of numerous works.

# CHAPTER II.

->On the enfolding of all things, and the unfolding<-
->into all things of the three figures.<-

**T** *here are three archetypes <note>original: "archetypi." The primary models or patterns from which all things are derived.</note> in the beginning, in whose appearance everything*
*is the standard of weight, measure, and figure.*
*I give the first to Phoebus <note>original: "Phœbo." The sun-god Apollo, representing the Monad or the Circle.</note>, and the second squares for Minerva <note>original: "Minervæ." The goddess of wisdom, here associated with the stability of the square.</note>,*
*and the third belongs to Venus <note>original: "Veneris." The goddess of love, associated here with the triangle as a symbol of generation.</note>. For indeed they recognize their own seal*
*in the face of these, and the secrets in their depth.*
*Where the fourfold cycle <note>original: "cyclus." Usually refers to a circle or a recurring period.</note> will combine for a triple lot,*
*if either one center or one rule enfolds them all—*
<margin>10</margin> *which the sensible circle and the triangular figure*
*aptly constitute, which are in turn the measure.*
*For these two are the minimum, and these in turn are the maximum.*
*The greatest is the single triangle which encloses the circle,*
*and the smallest is the single triangle described within the circle;*
*only the same figure carries the circle inscribed within it.*
*But the circle only takes in all those inscribed,*
*and the inscribed circle remains only the same in all.*
*Wherefore, whether simple or under that triple appearance,*
*it is the measure of measures and the figure of figures;*
*it indicates with clear light and by a very easy path,*
*and accomplishes whatever is sought by the ray <note>original: "radio." Referring to a geometric radius or a beam of light.</note>.*
<margin>20</margin> *Therefore, we desire to distinguish all things from the whole,*
*and to give from the whole the cause of all parts,*
*as first its species was found and received...*

<sig>I 4</sig>

<vocab>CHAPTER, Phoebus, Minerva, Venus, cycle/circle, triangle, measure, figure, archetypes</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the three fundamental geometric archetypes—the Circle, Square, and Triangle—linking them to the gods Phoebus, Minerva, and Venus. Bruno argues that these shapes serve as the primary "measures" and "seals" through which all complex natural forms are unfolded and understood.</summary>

*In the order of nature, how the appearance of the composite*
*whole is then revealed, through individual members and sections;*
*therefore I behold the faces of the book in which you may look*
*upon all things before you; then individual things must be read again by you.*
*To one wishing to recall the parts of a man to a specific number,*
*let the whole order of the substance <term>hypostasis (the underlying reality or essential substance of a thing)</term> be first before the eyes;*
*then, by its own steps, the smallest organs into which they are finally*
<margin>30</margin> *resolved, let them come forth unlocked by their own figures,*
*by measures, signs, reasons, and duties:*
*Thus, even if all future things will not be perpetually at hand,*
*nevertheless take these for the most part, and the principal ones;*
*thus the appearance of the figure presented to you is sufficient,*
*lest there be need for words by which the demonstration may be made.*
*Thus you will not recognize Hippolytus <note>In Greek mythology, Hippolytus was dragged to death by his horses and his body was torn into pieces. Bruno uses this as a metaphor for a system or body of knowledge that has been fragmented until it is no longer recognizable as a whole.</note> in mangled members*
*scattered everywhere; nor will you truly understand those to be*
*parts of him: but as if driven further away,*
*however much the individual parts may be perceived by the eyes on their own,*
<margin>40</margin> *they are more confused; because they do not reflect the formal*
*condition of the part, nor the certain act of the whole.*
*For comparison and order indicate the part and the whole reflexively.*
*Therefore it is our purpose to show the parts within their own whole;*
*so that thereafter all things may be easily proved—*
*as if to the knowledge from internal objects,*
*causes, both proper and immediate.*
*The same certain order which nature noted,*
*surely by the lofty condition of things coming together,*
*so that for you the individual things among all the formed things, and*
<margin>50</margin> *all things one by one and properly, may be unfolded in those*
*with which they consist, coming together in a certain series.*
*And this is finally the goal of the art, and the sacred seal <note>original: "sigillum." For Bruno, a "seal" is a complex mnemonic diagram or symbolic image that captures the essence of a philosophical system.</note>.*

<meta>Catchword: IN</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that knowledge must be grasped as a unified whole (a "seal") rather than through isolated parts, using the myth of the dismembered Hippolytus to illustrate the confusion of fragmented study. # CONCERNING CHAPTERS II, III, IIII, V, VI.

The set of four circles will combine fruitfully in no more than the three ways <note>original: "differentiis," referring here to three specific geometric configurations or variations</note> just proposed: in these, all <term>cosmometric (universal measurements or geometry applied to the cosmos)</term> figures are found—not only those that are implied, but also those that are explicitly unfolded. They appear either in one of these, or in two or three of them, whether individually or in a combination of two or three. We will very easily perceive their admirable excellence through application—for the sake of investigating, finding, and judging—as anyone endowed with even moderate judgment has discovered for himself.

¶ 20. Thus is the order of knowing: from the whole and the <term>confused (an undifferentiated or "global" view where details are not yet distinct)</term> to the parts seen distinctly; and from the parts seen distinctly, finally to a distinct conception of the whole.

¶ 34. If anyone seeks the most exact and shortest demonstration of the body of a figure with the application of the principles to be referenced, let him look in turn at how the whole is confirmed by the "all-ness" of the parts, and how all the parts, individually and together, are confirmed by the totality of the whole.

## The Construction of the Atrium of Apollo.

> *Behold, as if you could raise yourself into the likeness*

*And where the Floral way is split, Io is seen.*
*The priestess of Bacchus shall go around Hebe, and Euphrosyne around Io.*

### THE ATRIUM OF MINERVA.

<note>This section describes a complex memory diagram titled the "Atrium of Minerva" (the Roman goddess of wisdom). The woodcut mentioned in the source illustrates these intersections with letters (R, Z, Q, etc.) that correspond to different mental categories or concepts.</note>

*And Pales <note>An ancient Roman deity of shepherds and livestock</note> sits halfway between the regions of Maia and the Naiad;*

*She who is no bird, but stands firm at last with rooted feet.*
<margin>30</margin>

*Toward the furthest points on either side;*

*And by a path extended by the same number of steps,*

<margin>40</margin>

*Then she ordains that there be a straight path for Vesta all the way to Chaos,*
*And that it should flow from Chaos to the face of freezing Zamolxis.*
*Let her leave these behind in her desire to reach the gates of Quiet,*
*So that the goddess Vesta may at last enjoy her own dwelling.*
*Hope and Nemesis reach Euphrosyne <note>One of the three Graces, representing Joy</note> and Bacchus by straight*

*Leading to the four ways by which the entrances of Minerva <note>Goddess of wisdom</note> are enclosed.*
<margin>50</margin>
*Breaking through the circle, let the entire interior path align itself;*
*At the end of this path, know that there is a desert*
*Belonging to a neighboring divinity, which approaches from the edges of the circle.*

### CHAPTER VI.
### Every figure, whether explicit or implicit, found within these.

<note>In this section, Bruno describes the "Atrium of Apollo" (Atrium Apollinis), a mnemonic tool based on sacred geometry. In his system of "shadows," Apollo represents the light of the intellect or the central Monad. The diagram mentioned (and visible in the original woodcut) is a complex arrangement of overlapping circles—often called the "Flower of Life"—which provides a grid for organizing concepts, images, and information in the memory.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the "Atrium of Apollo," a geometric mnemonic structure characterized by a sixfold path and radial symmetry, where every point relates back to a central core. It marks the transition to Chapter VI, which discusses how all possible geometric and conceptual figures are contained within this singular design.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic description of the mnemonic diagrams (the Atrium of Apollo) from page 143. Bruno transitions from the geometric mechanics of the "sixfold path" to the philosophical and "archetypal" significance of these shapes, before opening Chapter VII which establishes the foundational definitions for his system of Wisdom (Sophia).</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

*NOt any principle, nor measure, nor figure exists,*
*Which does not arise from one of that triple order <note>Likely referring to the three fundamental principles of Bruno's philosophy: the Monad (One), the Dyad (Two), and the Trinity/All.</note>,*
*Or perhaps from two, or maybe from them all:*
*For sometimes a single path is taken toward various ends,*
*Just as not only one method leads back to a single goal.*
*Believe, therefore, that these figures are fertile,*
*Which not only encompass every kind of geometric invention,*
*But also reflect the archetypes <term>archetypos: the original patterns or ideal forms of all things</term> and seals <term>sigilla: symbolic imprints or magical characters used in memory</term> of things*
*In expressed forms—to be remembered elsewhere.*
<margin>10</margin> *The figure presented is similar through all its parts,*
*So that a clear demonstration may be made to you from its own properties;*
*Nor is there need to seek foreign forms, when*
*Here the members are placed in turn, compared with one another,*
*So that each is explained by the other and noted through itself—*
*The whole by its internal parts, and the parts by the whole.*
*Therefore, just as the sun is seen perfectly by its own light*
*So that thereafter the individual things may follow, as if*
*Placed in the light poured out through the whole:*
*Not otherwise do all forms shine in this image,*
<margin>20</margin> *As parts of one thing, as one substance. Nor are they*
*To be clarified by external things: unless perhaps darkness*
*Can contribute to bringing forth the light itself.*

## CHAPTER VII.
### DEFINITIONS.

*PRinciple known through itself and definition, by which*
*Attribute <note>original: "passio" — in Scholastic philosophy, this refers to a property or characteristic that "suffers" or follows from the essence of a thing.</note> and object are known by nature and name,*
*Must be known beforehand; and at the very threshold of Wisdom <term>Sophia: the personification of divine or philosophical wisdom</term>*
*As if before the entrance, they must be held by sense and mind.*

<meta>catchword: Whence</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that his geometric figures are not mere drawings but archetypal "seals" that contain the essence of things, illuminated by their own internal logic like the sun. He then begins Chapter VII, asserting that fundamental principles and definitions must be grasped by both the mind and the senses before one can enter the "Threshold of Wisdom."</summary>

<keywords>archetypes, seals, Sophia, principles, definitions, geometry, mnemonic figures, internal light, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the symbolic and archetypal significance of geometric figures into the formal definitions of Bruno's "geometry of the minimum." Having established that all figures arise from a triple order, he now begins to define the basic units—the point, line, and atom—both as physical building blocks (minima) and as mathematical boundaries (termini).</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>ON THE PRINCIPLES OF MEASURE, FIGURE, AND THE BOOK</header>
<sig>K</sig>

*From there, as if from a center, a thousand paths*
*Stretch out toward various riches; they bring back*
*The sign of things to each, returning to a certain center.*
*Therefore, through these general principles of the art, observe what they are.*

### What the MINIMUM and the BOUNDARY are.
*The* MINIMUM <term>minimum: the smallest possible unit of a thing that cannot be further divided</term> *is that which has no part, because it is itself the first part.*
<margin>10</margin>
*The* BOUNDARY <term>terminus: the outer limit or mathematical edge of a thing</term> *is an end which has no part, and which is not a part itself.* <note>Bruno distinguishes between the "Minimum," which is a real physical component, and the "Boundary," which is a conceptual limit that marks where a thing ends.</note>

### What the POINT is, as a Minimum.
*I call that the* POINT *which has no part but is the first part*
*In a plane—provided you understand it as the very form of a minimum.*

### What the POINT is, as a Boundary.
*Whether in a specific position, or simply in itself, that which is not a part and has no part—*
*Let the* POINT *be the boundary and end of every quantity for you.*

### What the LINE is, as a Minimum.
*The* LINE *is the continuation of a point along a length;*
*It is the immediate part of a plane, having parts only in respect to its length.*

### What the LINE is, as a Boundary.
*Then it also has no part <note>meaning it has no width or depth</note>, being such a continuation*
*Of a point that it serves as the boundary for any solid or face.*

### What a SURFACE is.
*The* SURFACE <term>superficies: a two-dimensional area or face</term> *is merely the boundary of a solid,*
<margin>20</margin>
*And is assigned parts only according to length and width.*

### What the ATOM is.
*The* ATOM <term>atomus: the indivisible physical unit of matter</term> *is the smallest length, width, and depth*
*Of a body; it is capable of being both a part and a boundary itself.*

### What the THREAD is.
*I name that a* THREAD <term>filum: a one-dimensional physical string or fiber</term> *which is a simple length composed of atoms;*
*It too can be both a part and a boundary of a body.*

<summary>This page defines the fundamental geometric and physical building blocks of Bruno’s system, distinguishing between "Minima" (the smallest physical parts) and "Termini" (the mathematical limits or boundaries).</summary>
<keywords>Minimum, Boundary, Point, Line, Surface, Atom, Thread, geometry, physical units, mathematical limits</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno’s systematic definitions of geometric and physical principles. Following the definitions of points and lines on the previous page, he now moves into three-dimensional bodies (orbs and plates), the relationship between parts and wholes, and the fundamental nature of measurement and angles.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

### What the ORB and the PLATE are.
The **Orb** is like the sphere, and its dimension is pyramidal <note>In Bruno’s cosmology, the sphere is often understood as being composed of pyramidal or conical sections radiating from a central point.</note>; 
the **Plate** <term>lamina: a flat sheet or surface</term> is a body that can be divided by its length and width.

### What a FORMED BODY is.
Whatever is called a **Body** under a specific name is considered, in these teachings, to be the actualization of a body <note>Bruno distinguishes between "prime matter" (abstract body) and a "formed body," which is matter given a specific shape or boundary.</note>.

### What the WHOLE and the PART are.
<margin>30</margin>A **Part** is whatever you take within a whole that is less than the whole itself; 
the **Whole** is the composite which exists by virtue of all its parts.

### What MEASURE is.
**Measure** is that which, when applied and reapplied, shows how great the whole is; the minimum generally defines the limit of the measure itself <note>For Bruno, measurement is not an abstract number but the process of counting the "minima" (the smallest units) that constitute an object.</note>.

### What GREATER, LESSER, EQUAL, and UNEQUAL are.
If one exceeds another, it is **Greater**, and it will also be **Unequal**; 
that is **Equal** which will be found to be neither greater nor lesser.

### What STRAIGHT, FLAT, Curved, and Convex are.
**Straight** is whatever is the shortest distance between two boundaries; 
**Flat** is that which is bounded on all sides by straight markers <note>original: *signis*. Here referring to the lines that define the edges of a plane.</note>.

### What PARALLEL Straight lines, Circular lines, the Orb, and Flat Parallels are.
That which neither meets, nor would meet afterward even if extended, 
I call **Parallel**, being equally distant from the center.

### What an ANGLE of a point and a line is.
An **Angle** is the meeting of threads <note>original: *filorum*. A poetic term for lines or edges.</note> or of the faces 
<margin>40</margin>of a body or a plane, according to how they are inclined toward each other.

<meta>Catchword: What</meta>

<summary>This page provides concise, verse-based definitions for fundamental geometric and physical concepts, including the orb, the whole, measure, and the nature of parallel lines and angles. It emphasizes that measurement is based on the "minimum" and defines bodies through their boundaries and parts.</summary>
<keywords>Orb, Plate, Formed Body, Whole and Part, Measure, Equality, Straight and Flat, Parallel Lines, Angle, Geometry, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric definitions from page 146, moving from the basic concepts of measure and surface to the specific properties of angles and three-dimensional figures. Bruno continues to use verse to encapsulate these definitions.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>
<header>ON THE PRINCIPLES OF MEASUREMENT AND FIGURES</header>
<sig>K 2</sig>

### WHAT A RIGHT ANGLE AND A PERPENDICULAR LINE ARE, ETC.

*An angle is **right** if a straight line is suspended equally above a similar curved or flat surface.* <note>original: "Angulus est rectus si supra consimilare Curuum vel planum, rectum suspenditur aqué." Bruno defines the right angle through the concept of equilibrium or "equal suspension," much like a plumb line hanging perfectly straight relative to the ground.</note>

### WHAT OBTUSE AND ACUTE ANGLES ARE.

*That angle which the leaning perpendicular line moves away from is called **obtuse**; the one toward which it inclines is called **acute**.*

### WHAT A FIGURE IS.

*A **figure** is the definite boundary of a body or a plane, made visible either by a curve or by more than two straight lines.* <note>In Euclidean geometry, at least three lines are required to enclose a plane figure (a triangle); Bruno here emphasizes that a figure is the "comprehension" or the limit that defines an object's shape.</note>

### WHAT A GNOMON IS.

*Let a **gnomon**, in a general sense, be that which, when added to a figure, increases it into a similar form, or when taken away, leaves the form the same.* <term>gnomon: an L-shaped tool or the part of a parallelogram remaining after a similar smaller one is removed from a corner</term>

### WHAT A CIRCLE IS.

*A **circle** is a plane whose edge is equal on all sides to the radii coming from the center and measuring through the center.*

<margin>50</margin>
### WHAT AN ORB IS.

*The **orb** is a body with two faces, and it is equally deep through all its connected and hollow parts.* <note>Bruno often distinguishes the "orb" (like a ring or a thick shell) from the "sphere" (a solid ball). Here he describes a hollowed body with "twin faces"—an inner and outer surface.</note>

### WHAT A SPHERE IS.

*A **sphere** is that in which the length is equal to the width and the depth; a circle also touches and surrounds its outermost limit.*

### WHAT A PYRAMID IS.

*A **pyramid** is a body that, by diminishing several planes from one base plane, gathers them together into a single point.*

### WHAT THE OTHER FIGURES ARE.

*Those things which consist of these shall be defined by them; and let particular things be given as finite to the wandering sense.* <note>Bruno suggests that all other complex shapes are derivatives of these primary forms, though our senses might perceive them as "wandering" or less precise than the mathematical ideal.</note>

<note>Technical vocabulary used on this page: right angle, perpendicular line, obtuse angle, acute angle, figure, gnomon, circle, orb, sphere, pyramid.</note>

<summary>This page provides versified definitions for fundamental geometric shapes and angles, distinguishing between the hollow 'orb' and the solid 'sphere.' It establishes the gnomon as a principle of proportional growth and defines the pyramid as a convergence of planes.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, right angle, gnomon, circle, sphere, pyramid, orb, mathematical definitions, spatial limits</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific geometric definitions of the previous page (angles, circles, orbs) to the broader logical and philosophical foundations of Bruno's system. He moves from "what things are" (definitions) to the self-evident truths (axioms) that allow us to reason about them.</meta>

<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

**Simple knowledge** <note>original: "Cognitio incomplexa." In medieval logic, this refers to understanding a single term (like "dog") without making a statement about it.</note> precedes **complex knowledge**; that is, the understanding of simple terms comes before the understanding of a proposition. Simple terms consist of the **subject** and the **attribute** <term>attribute: "passio" in Latin, referring here to a property or characteristic predicated of a subject</term>. Regarding both of these, one must first know "what it is" and "that it is," or what is being posited or supposed. Therefore, definitions must be set forth before those **axioms** <term>axioms: "dignitates," self-evident principles that require no proof</term> which become clear once the terms themselves are understood.

# CHAPTER VIII.
## Axioms of the art in themselves, and of terms with manifest meaning.

*Before the doors of Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom and weaving; Bruno often uses her as a symbol for the organized structure of knowledge</note> the order of principles is renowned,*
*By which nature creates the whole and the mind perceives individual things;*
*From whose number, if anyone should subtract even one,*
*He will multiply his delusions alongside the common crowd.*
*Distracted too much by the senses, he will seek first principles in many places,*
*Which occur to me through the act of a simple medium,*
*And will prove themselves to be shown by their own numbers.*
*By these first things, various arts demonstrate all things—*
*Whether numbers, or measures, or weights, or mo-*
<margin>10</margin> *ments by which one thing is carried more, less, or equal to another.*
*These we provide not only as suited to the matters at hand, but freely*
*In every genus, every species, and every order;*
*In this series, therefore, I report them just as they are written there.*

->ORESTES<-
<note>Orestes and Pylades were legendary friends in Greek mythology. Bruno uses them here as personified "voices" to deliver his mathematical and logical rules.</note>
*To one thing there is one reason; to the like there is the like; to the equal, the equal;*
*To the opposite there are opposites; to the inverse there are inverses, says Orestes.*

->PYLADES<-
*The similar and the equal is that with which everything agrees*
*In quality and quantity. Pylades noted this in his writing.*

->AMYNTAS<-
*From a pair, if an equal is taken away or added, they will remain...*

These axioms—such as "the whole is greater than the part"—form the "greatest propositions" upon which all scientific demonstration is built.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>ON THE PRINCIPLES, MEASURE, AND FIGURE, BOOK [X] <note>original: "LIB." for Liber (Book). This is likely Book I or II of the treatise.</note></header>

*As Amyntas said, whether they were equal or unequal.* <note>Amyntas is a common name in pastoral poetry; here he represents a witness to the comparative state of things.</note>

### H E R M E S. <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage to whom Bruno attributed the origins of Egyptian wisdom.</note>

*If an equal amount is taken from an equal amount, the whole will be equal to the whole*
<margin>20</margin>
*Or similar, and the remainder conforms to the whole, says Hermes.* <note>This is a variation of Euclid’s axiom: "If equals be subtracted from equals, the remainders are equal."</note>

### P O L Y T E S.

*That which can contain the whole will be considered greater than it;*
*Just as that which is a part is less; Polytes indicated this within the circle.* <note>The axiom that the whole is greater than any of its parts.</note>

### P E R I C L E S.

*If two things are alike, such, or so great as a third:*
*They are alike, such, and so great among themselves, says Pericles.* <note>The principle of transitivity: things that are equal to the same thing are equal to each other.</note>

### E M I C L A S.

*The proportion that existed when things were separate is the same when they are placed together,*
*Provided the species <note>original: "species"; here meaning the form or essential nature</note> has not changed, Emiclas testifies.*

### A R C A S.

*If a thing varies in its outward appearance, yet the whole consists of the same parts*
*In the form of a single genus, they are equal, says Arcas.*

### H O R V S.

<margin>30</margin>
*As the whole relates to the whole in a similar kind, Horus says,*
*So also relate the similar parts and whatever belongs to them.*

### I A S O N.

*If the distance of both from a fixed limit is found to be*
*Known relative to each other, Jason considered it to be so in reverse as well.*

### Z O R O A S T E R.

*Whatever certain proportion arranges things into a certain series,*
*These things are known from pairs through Zoroaster.*

### A R I O N.

*Where a continuous order consists of a similar ratio,*
*They are those whose distance is equal, wrote Arion.*

**A**xioms <term>axioms: "maximae," literally "the greatest [propositions]"; self-evident truths that serve as the foundation for reasoning</term> are propositions that are manifest in themselves or from the very meaning of their terms. And these are the first things from which demonstrations <note>The text cuts off here and continues on the following page.</note>

...we demonstrate <note>original: "stramus", completing "demonstramus" from the previous page</note>. In this intended class, the means of argumentation are taken, first, from unity and identity, or similarity, proportion, and the reasons of equality, opposition, and congruence. Second, from the act of addition and subtraction. Third, from the whole and the part. Fourth, from the relationship to a third <term>term<gloss>the middle ground used to connect two ideas in a logical argument</gloss></term> or from a third to the others, or from the others. Fifth, from the consideration of the composite based on the nature of the simple parts, and vice versa. Sixth, from the identity and homogeneity of the parts or the concurrent elements. Seventh, from the degrees of distance and proximity. Eighth, from coordination and analogy.

### III.

*An equal diameter <note>original: "dimetiens", a term Bruno uses for the diameter of a circle</note> [divides] figures with equal radii;*
*The diameter divides any figure through the middle,*
CHREMETES taught. Now receive the words of DIAULUS:
*The larger [chord] is nearer to the center, the smaller is further away,*
*And equal ones are at the same distance; the largest is that drawn through the center.*

### IIII. <note>4</note>

*From the center, both the circle and the straight line are reached by three equal radii;*
*But from any part by two,*
ERICYX expressed. But do you wish to hear FULIGNUM?
*You will touch the circle at two points equally distant from all sides,*
*And the sphere with innumerable circles equally distant from all sides.*

*Preserves the same rule in all things.*

LEMARCHUS speaks. Soon MELAMPUS discusses these things:

### VIII.

*An area is always determined by its height and its base*

# CHAPTER X.
## Demonstration of the Theorems.

### OF ASTYANAX. <note>Astyanax is likely used here as a mnemonic persona for a specific geometric rule regarding the circle.</note>

**T** *he line by which Bacchus is distant from bright Apollo* <note>In Bruno’s geometric system, Apollo often represents the center or the sun, while Bacchus represents a point on the circumference. This line is the radius.</note>
*Measures the circle, and the figure properly inscribed within it.*
*The arc by which the Graces* <note>The Graces (Charites) usually number three, representing an arc of a specific division of the circle.</note> *stand apart from the threshold of Bacchus*
*Is the measure for a figure inscribed with twice-three points,* <note>original: "Cuspide bis terna." Referring to a hexagon, which has six points and is inscribed using the length of the radius.</note>
*But closer to the thresholds of the circle returning to Bacchus.*

### THE PART.

*If the straight line and the arc are taken as a primary part,*
*The arc does not suffer any difference in its curvature.* <note>Bruno is arguing that at the level of the "Minimum" (the smallest possible part), the distinction between a curve and a straight line disappears.</note>
*But the straight and the curved have one and the same limit here and there.*
*It is not so if both are taken as divisible into parts:*
<margin>10</margin> *For a part of one will never correspond to a part of the other;*
*The principles of the Minimum and the definition of the part demonstrate this.*

### OF BERASTUS.

*The path that hastens from the citadel of Diana toward the Graces* <note>Diana often represents the moon or a specific angle in this mnemonic system.</note>
*Is equal to that through which Erigone* <note>Erigone is the constellation Virgo; Ganymede is Aquarius. These represent specific points or sectors on a circular diagram.</note> *comes to Ganymede.*
*Since they are distant from the part of the diameter* <note>original: "dimetientis"</note> *by a similar*
*Radius, from there the arc will be revealed and an equal angle.*
*Hermes and Amyntas demonstrate this with fit signs,*
*As do the understanding of Pylades and the watchful Pericles.*

### OF CHREMES.

*The things that showed Berastus show Chremes.*

### OF DIAULUS.

*Compare the path where it is joined to the Grace, with that*
<margin>20</margin> *Where Erigone runs down for the charming Ganymede;*
*Then, those things which showed Chremes and Beristus*
*Will very easily be able to confirm the sense of Diaulus.*

## OF HERIERICUS. <note>original: "ERICIS." Combined with the catchword "HERI-", this refers to a mnemonic persona, possibly "Heriericus."</note>

*A circle is not touched by more than twin equal lines from Jove,* <note>Jove represents the center of the circle. Bruno uses "waters" (aquis) as a metaphor for radii or lines of extension.</note>
*So that any point whatever is seen to be a center to none.*
*Show this with the words of Pylades and the mouth of Jason.*

<margin>1</margin> ### THE PART.

*And the path by which it abundantly joins Neptune to the Ocean*
*Is touched only by twin equal lines extending from the center.*
*To Fortune, Bacchus, Erigone, Hermes, and Ganymede.*
*Which either Pylades or Jason clearly confirms for you.*

### FULIGNUS. <note>Fulignus is a mnemonic persona used to store a specific geometric rule.</note>

<margin>30</margin> *If the point placed before you becomes your center, so that from there*
*You may extend radii, you will be able to touch the sphere of the point*
*(Which is the smallest possible circuit) with a shorter pull,*
*Either as a plane with twin points, or as a globe with*
*Innumerable cycles. The definition and action of the radius shows this.* <note>term: <term>Radius</term> (Latin: "radius" - a staff or spoke). In geometry, the line from the center to the edge. Bruno treats it as the "action" that creates the circle.</note>

### THE GORGONIANS.

*This is the path by which the Graces* <note>The Graces usually represent the number three; here they likely represent three radii forming a specific figure.</note> *descend to the face of Jove.*
*So it stands suspended above the track of the Ocean and of fierce Mars;*
*In such a way that all parts that stand away from Jove’s altar*
*Are removed by an equal distance.*
*With a long, similar, and equal point on both sides,*
<margin>40</margin> *It will not wait, inclining more toward one part*
*Than it is declining from the rest.* ---

### THE PART.

--- *But the side standing above,*

*Into twin parts. Therefore, this will be an equal point*
*To two of the three: which Bacchus, Apollo, and Diana*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;mark;

<page-num>159</page-num>

# THE BOOK ON 
## MEASUREMENT, 
### BY GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA

### CHAPTER I.

->*The Archetype of Truth. I shall examine the shortest line,*<-
->*which is the same as the straight line.*<-

*How great is the power of the true and the straight,* <note>original: "veri & recti". Bruno uses "rectus" here as a pun: in geometry it means a "straight" line, but in ethics and logic it means "right" or "correct."</note>
*That nothing rises more quickly, nothing emerges*
*More swiftly, so that it binds the beginnings to its own end.*
*Thus, a mark between two points is shorter by exactly as much*
*As it approaches the straight. From that path, curves are innumerable;*
*Indeed, they come with infinite variety,*
*Advancing from a single point toward the goal.*
*Nor can any number be assigned to those paths*
*Which strive in vain toward the proposed mark,*
*Because they eventually drift away toward opposite sides.*
<margin>10</margin>
*I test the straight line to see if it lies hidden entirely*
*Under a single point—namely, beneath the very tip—and when* <note>Bruno describes a perspective trick: if you look directly down the end of a perfectly straight line, the entire length of the line is hidden behind the single point of its starting position.</note>
*Bound by two points, it is the shortest path. So too the True, the Holy, and the Good*
*Conspire from all directions toward a single goal*
*Perpetually; and the meaning of that one thing is itself one.*

<summary>This page introduces Chapter I of Bruno's treatise on measurement, using a poem to argue that the "straight line" is the geometric archetype of Truth, Goodness, and Holiness because of its directness and singularity.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, geometry, straight line, metaphysics, truth, measurement, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening poem of Chapter I from the previous page, further developing the metaphor of the straight line as a singular path of truth versus the infinite deviations of falsehood. It then transitions into a systematic classification of "Measurement" into metaphysical, physical, and mathematical categories.</meta>

<page-num>160</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

*It aligns under a single path of the extreme target.*
*But the forms and figures of the false are innumerable;*
*Just as an oblique track takes up numbers without end.*
*If certain things are not grasped at all in a single order,*
20 *They will strive in vain, as if driven along a single path,*
*To contend from various ends toward a single goal.*
*Since individual things do not, in the end, consist of*
*A divided form, or are "one" only in their own parts.*
*If they attend to their own method, and the numbers of their traveling.* <note>The original Latin "meandi" refers to the way things move or pass through space.</note>
*Wherefore, that which is dispersed in numbers and in matter,*
*Anaximenes* <note>Anaximenes of Miletus (c. 585–528 BCE) was a Pre-Socratic philosopher who proposed that "Air" was the primary substance of the universe.</note> *called vain and a nothingness; to Melissus* <note>Melissus of Samos (5th century BCE) was an Eleatic philosopher who argued that reality is one, indivisible, and infinite.</note>
*And to Parmenides* <note>Parmenides of Elea (c. 515 BCE) famously argued that "All is One" and that change is an illusion.</note> *it is pleasing to call only the One "true."*

**The Triple Measure.** Above and before the thing. In and with the thing. After and outside the thing. <note>Bruno uses the classic Scholastic distinction of universals: *Ante rem* (the Idea in the mind of God), *In re* (the physical object), and *Post rem* (the concept derived by the human mind).</note> Measurement **above the thing** is triple: The One, the Mind, the Idea. Measurement **in the thing** is triple: Magnitude, Weight, and Moment. <note>original: "Momentum". In this context, it refers to the "turning point" or the defining force of a thing's nature.</note> **Magnitude** is triple: of one, two, and three dimensions. <note>Length, width, and height.</note> **Weight** is triple: Outside of its place, toward its place, and in or around its place. **Moment** is triple: of Substance, of Quality, and of Respect; or rather, the Absolute, the Concrete, and the Relative. Measurement **after the thing** is triple: Efficient, Formal, and Instrumental. The **Efficient** <note>The "agent" or cause that makes something happen.</note> is triple: Spirit, <note>original: "Dæmon". In Bruno's philosophy, these are intermediate beings or intelligences, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense.</note> Human, and Brute. The **Formal** is triple: Sense, Reason, and Intellect. The **Instrumental** is triple: First, Middle, and Immediate. The **First instrumental** is triple: Finger, Palm, and Cubit. <note>These are ancient units of measure based on the human body.</note> The **Middle** is triple: The Compasses, the Square, and the Plumb line. The **Immediate** is triple: The Point, the Straight Line, and the Circle. Of these species of measure, some are Metaphysical, others Physical, others Mathematical. Of the Mathematical measures regarding the first instrumental, we have spoken in the closely preceding books. It follows that we should contemplate the middle and the immediate—or that which concerns the middle and the immediate—under the scale <note>original: "skala". Bruno often uses the image of a ladder or scale to show the ascent from matter to the divine.</note> and analogy of many reasons of measurement.

¶ The shortest line between two points and the straight line prove each other in turn; likewise for that which is straighter and shorter. Between two points, infinite curves can be produced. Thus, the truth of apprehension is one, simple, and the shortest path; but falsehood is manifold, winding, and difficult—just as we see "sophisticated" Geometry to be, along with other parts of philosophy. <note>Bruno is critiquing mathematicians and philosophers who make reality more complex than it needs to be by ignoring the simple, "straight" atomic truths of nature.</note>

# CHAPTER <note>The text cuts off here at the start of the next section heading.</note>

<summary>Bruno categorizes "Measurement" into a hierarchy of three levels (metaphysical, physical, and conceptual) and argues that truth, like a straight line, is the simplest and shortest path between points.</summary>
<keywords>Triple Measure, Metaphysics, Pre-Socratic philosophy, Anaximenes, Parmenides, Geometry, Measurement tools, Efficient Cause</keywords>

<header>BOOK ON MEASUREMENT</header>
<sig>L</sig>

# BOOK ON MEASUREMENT.
## CHAPTER II.

### OF EQUALITY. From a given point or in whatever way, I take an equal to a given straight line.

**Y**ou will not find equality in all respects in the nature of things.
*This measurement does not exactly repeat that which had been previously established.* <note>original: "subactum," meaning something already worked over or subdued by the mind.</note>
*Hence, neither is a compound nor a certain combination able*
    *to be restored entirely anew to a single mark:*
*Since indeed parts concur in a countless order*
*With dissimilar nature and place, just as a portion*
*Of matter is not the same in its species, fortune, and position;*
*All things consist of varied series, both outside and within:*
*For just as, through countless members pressing through all things,*
<margin>10</margin>*There is a departure from all; nor can a similar or equal*
*Concession then arise from the same or such great things:*
*Since likewise there cannot be such a concordant discord* <note>The phrase "discordia concors" refers to the philosophical idea that the universe maintains harmony through the balancing of opposite forces.</note>
*From things everywhere diverse, varied, and placed in opposition:*
*Thus, in its immense course, this wheel of time snatches things away so that*
*In a single moment there are thousands upon thousands of divisions,* <note>original: "tomorum," from the Greek *tomos*, a slice or section.</note>
*And varied numbers of fortunes, although these parts*
*(The substance of things) escape the senses.*
*So also those things which appear together under the evidence of sense,*
*Nature truly separates into many moments;*
<margin>20</margin>*Just as our "minimum" is distinguished from its "minimum."* <note>Bruno's "minimum" is the smallest indivisible unit of existence—the atom in physics, the point in geometry, and the monad in metaphysics.</note>
*Nevertheless, reason promises this to itself, and provides it*
*Furthermore; and measurement delights in comparing things subjected*
*To the senses, so that it happens that many things fall under the rule*
*Of a single radius, and are encompassed by the same circuit.*

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Measurement** | **Equality** |
| **The Nature of Things** | **Moments** |
| **Reason** | **Concordant Discord** |
| **Divisions** | **Substance** |

<summary>In this chapter opening, Bruno argues that while geometric "equality" is a concept of the mind, in the physical world of constant change and "wheel of time," no two things are ever perfectly identical. He introduces the concept of the "minimum" as the basis for measurement across different scales of reality.</summary>
<keywords>equality, nature of things, minimum, atomism, concordant discord, geometry, time, substance</keywords>

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of equality. While the previous page established that perfect equality is impossible in the physical world of matter and change, this page critiques traditional geometric proofs of equality (specifically Euclid’s) and applies the concept of "balance" to both physics and medicine.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

*They will come suitably either by an arc or by equal marks:*
*But what need is there for a triangle,* <note>original: "triquetro," referring to a three-cornered figure or a specific astronomical instrument used for measuring altitudes.</note> *when simpler things are*
*the basis of reason for me compared to compounds; those things are not so in turn to them,*
*except for the physicist, and by a posterior discourse;* <note>Bruno is distinguishing between "prior" reasoning (pure mathematical/rational principles) and "posterior" reasoning (working backward from physical effects to causes).</note>
*This kind of wisdom usually adopts that method voluntarily because of*
*the dim light of nature and the depth of things.*
<margin>30</margin> *Therefore, you will produce nothing better by this reasoning,*
*by which, since certain limits are established for you,*
*this is more extended toward them, and that more contracted: and it*
*is not worth the effort of the Circle so that you may see things nearer to the center,*
*and hence more remote, which touch closest to the center,*
*those which remain held by the ring, those which recede when raised up:*
*And just as one seeks the light of the sun through the stars.* <note>Bruno suggests that trying to find perfect equality in physical objects is like trying to find the full light of the sun by looking at the dim reflections of the stars.</note>

**A**ristotle wisely—yet not, as can be seen, from his own workshop—defined "the equal" as receiving "the more" and "the less": for indeed, a greater or lesser approach to equality is found by practitioners, but reaching it is not possible at all.
¶ 3. What we say of one thing must be understood of all things whose being consists in a certain latitude, number, and degree. Just as, truly, one cannot find a healthy person in a state of most exact <term>eucrasia</term> <note>From the Greek *eukrasia*, meaning a "well-tempered" or perfect balance of the four bodily humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile). In Renaissance medicine, this perfect balance was a theoretical ideal, never perfectly achieved in a living body.</note> even for a moment, since the subject is composed of countless and diverse parts.
¶ 27. Equality of lines is wrongly proved by Euclid through an equilateral triangle: for it is not proper to demonstrate from things that come after, especially at the very beginning. <note>Bruno is critiquing Euclid's *Elements*, Book 1, Proposition 1. Euclid uses two intersecting circles to create an equilateral triangle to prove lines are equal; Bruno argues that the concept of "equal radii" in a circle is more fundamental and should come first.</note> The same would happen to us if, without a predefined circle, we proceeded from the equality of its radii or comparable ones.

# CHAPTER III.
### OF TEMPERANCE AND MEASURE: I cut off from the greater that which is in excess.

**T**he radius of justice is the norm and rule, by which the
line does not pass beyond the end that the line shows.

<meta>catchword: Majesty</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that perfect equality is a rational ideal that can never be fully realized in physical matter, critiquing Euclid for using complex shapes (triangles) to prove the fundamental concept of equal lines. He uses the medical concept of balanced humors to illustrate that in nature, everything is in a constant state of "more" or "less" rather than "exactly equal."</summary>
