--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general principle of equality (discussed on page 162) to the practical application of measurement and the abstraction of dimensions. Bruno continues his critique of traditional Aristotelian and Euclidean geometry, moving into Chapter 4 which focuses on "The Distinguisher" (Distinctor)—the mental or visual capacity to isolate one dimension from others.</meta>

->OF THE BOOK ON MEASUREMENT<-

*Whatever remains of the larger, once it equals the smaller,*
*Is cut away and cast off according to the rule of the measuring rod.* <note>original: "radio," a staff or rod used by geometricians for measuring.</note>
*If one end of the second is set as a center,*
*And takes its law of distance from the other;*
*So that soon, if this center is placed upon that center,*
*The remainder of the first is cut off by the rotation of the other's end,*
*And what remains appears clearly aligned by a straight-edge.* <note>original: "amussi," a tool used by masons to ensure a surface is perfectly flat or a line is perfectly straight.</note>
*Therefore, from one or a similar center, you will complete the tests* <margin>10</margin>
*Of the unequal; thus, the ends approach the first in equal measure.*

**W**e focus on bringing the greater back to equality with the lesser by the same principle mentioned immediately above, and the demonstration is from an axiom <note>An "axiom" is a self-evident truth that requires no proof.</note> as if from a first principle, and from a theorem as if from a proximate principle.

# CHAPTER IV.
## THE DISTINGUISHER. THE SIGHT LINE: I perceive length without width by sight.

*It is a common report, a reason, and a wisdom generally accepted,*
*That Mathematics* <note>original: "Mathesin."</note> *is not at all deceitful when it abstracts,*
*When it considers one thing without another, rather than conceiving them*
*As joined into one, just as it conceives one thing separated from another;*
*Yet sometimes they try to resolve by reason*
*What Physics resolves, and which the senses receive*
*More purely and better once resolved; when the duty*
*And zeal of reason fail, if by chance it applies itself to the same things.*
*Can there not be just one sensible dimension* <margin>10</margin>
*Entirely distinct from the other two?* <note>Bruno is arguing against the idea that "length" is merely a mental abstraction; he suggests that we can physically perceive a single dimension (like a line) independently of width and depth.</note>
*For the common folk think that this itself cannot be seen,*
*(While they see it all the while, because it is right before their eyes)*

<meta>This page continues the poetic and prose argument of Chapter 4, "The Distinguisher." ->GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA<-

*And for the internal eyes to gaze upon the sight, by whose aid*
*Reason abstracts from the rest. Nor does Sextus, the follower*
*Of Pyrrho,* <note>Sextus Empiricus was an ancient philosopher who represented the Skeptical school, which doubted the possibility of certain knowledge through the senses or reason.</note> *believe this very thing; his thought does not have*
*Such power that—captured by no embrace—it might*
*Perceive the appearance of length, which is called a line.*
*Furthermore, however much the "minimum length" might escape you,*
*Yet that length which is the boundary itself will not escape you,*
<margin>20</margin> *Namely, that which is the distinction between black and white.*
*Indeed, I would even dare to firmly add this truth:*
*Just as the thought is less able to bear the gazing of the senses*
*Upon the extreme limit of light; so clearly*
*The thought of beauty does not surpass, nor equal, nor even*
*Come close to the present appearance of beauty.*
*By nature, the sensible light of the sun stands better before*
*The sight itself as judge; and the secondary* <note>original: "vicaria," meaning a substitute or representative power, referring to the mind's eye compared to the physical eye.</note> *power will receive it less,*
*Just as the shadow of night does not carry colors.*

**I**t is a common report that "those who abstract do not lie." <note>original: "Abstrahentium non esse mendacium." A famous scholastic principle derived from Aristotle, meaning that focusing on one aspect of an object (like its shape) while ignoring others (like its material) is a valid mental tool, not a deception about reality.</note> For various sciences and arts are occupied with one and the same subject, which are distinguished not by the thing considered, but by the very mode, form, and purpose of considering it. Meanwhile, while things that are subjectively one and the same are grasped by other intentions by themselves and separately, there is clearly no departure from the nature of the thing. For it is permitted to consider the color of a rose separately, omitting those things which serve to explain its nature and its other properties. Likewise, there is one science that considers sight, and another that considers the eye; and yet, just because they are joined in their actual existence, they should not be understood as inseparable in our consideration, as if things were then considered differently than they are in the nature of things. But it is the mark of the most foolish cattle <note>Bruno often uses "cattle" or "beasts" to describe thinkers who cannot grasp subtle logical distinctions.</note> not to know how to distinguish between "considering this without that" and "this being without that."

¶ 18 The difference between two colors on a surface indicates a line, which is a boundary. But that line which is a <term>minimum</term> <note>In Bruno's geometry, the "minimum" is the smallest possible unit or "atom" of a dimension that cannot be further divided.</note> we can comprehend by no sense.

## CHAPTER V.

### DIVISION. BALANCING. *I bisect the length, and find the middle point within it.*

**A**nd you divide a single straight line into two parts,
When the radius of a fixed compass <note>original: "radii... manentis," referring to the stationary leg of a compass used in geometry.</note> comes to be placed
And its one extreme point flows around the other,
As much as the length of the radius is captured by the arc
On this side and that; so that at last the other limit
Falls into the remainder: just as the path where high Apollo <note>Apollo represents the Sun.</note>
Tends toward the Ocean is split by the ebb and flow of the Earth
At a point; when the point of Erigone <note>Erigone refers to the constellation Virgo.</note> toward Ganymede <note>Ganymede refers to the constellation Aquarius.</note>
Running a straight course has flowed down, seeing as from the very
<margin>10</margin>
Ocean of Erigone, Ganymede, and high Apollo
Are distant by an equal radius; and for that reason,
If the other [line] flows down from both sides into the opposite
Point, and the threads will come cut through the middle in turn,
Horus and Jason <note>Bruno likely uses Horus (the Egyptian deity) and Jason (the seeker of the Golden Fleece) as symbols of ancient wisdom or seekers of the "center" or "balance."</note> affirm these things with a Periclean head. <note>original: "Pericleo capite," likely referring to the wisdom or the helmeted iconography of the statesman Pericles, symbolizing intellectual protection or authority.</note>
Does not the nurturing parent <note>Nature.</note> wisely grant divisions to things
By a similar lot, she who has separated and composed
All opposites in equal parts with such numbers,
So that whatever has receded returns as a whole?
The circle is found to be their most certain standard,
Which is able to divide times into fixed successions.
<margin>20</margin>
So where the fixed center stands—Reason, and the power
Of Truth—opposing dogmas arise toward different ends,
Which, meeting in number,
->dissolve<-
the monad. <note>term: "monad" — the ultimate, indivisible unit of being or a singular point of unity. Bruno suggests that conflicting human opinions break this divine unity into "number" or multiplicity.</note>
Therefore, let that which any compass does not grasp
More than once come bisected for you, if—with equal parts
Taken here and there—you leave a graspable space in the middle.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a major new section in the work, titled "Book on Measure." While the previous pages focused on specific mnemonic figures and geometric diagrams involving mythological characters (like Loton and Jason), this page introduces the metaphysical and foundational principles of measurement, specifically the relationship between truth and the "straight line."</meta>

# GIORDANO BRUNO THE NOLAN, BOOK ON MEASURE.

## CHAPTER I.

### The Archetype of Truth. I shall test the shortest line, which is also a straight one. <note>Bruno often used "The Nolan" as a title, referring to his birthplace, Nola. Here, he begins a philosophical exploration of geometry, where "the straight line" is not just a mathematical concept, but a symbol for moral and intellectual truth.</note>

*How great is the power of truth and, oh, of the right!*
*That nothing rises more quickly, nothing emerges*
*Swifter, so that it may bind the beginnings to its own end.*
*Thus a mark* <note>original: "litura," meaning a stroke of the pen or a line.</note> *between two points is shorter by so much*
*The closer it approaches the straight. Furthermore, there are countless—*
*Indeed, curves come with an infinite difference—*
*Which advance from one point to the end.*
*Nor is any number able to be assigned to those*
*Striving in vain toward the proposed goal,*
*Wandering at last into opposite directions.*
*I test the straight line to see if the whole, under a single point—*
*Namely, if it lies hidden under the endpoint—and grasped by two points,*
*It is shorter.* <note>Bruno is describing the geometric definition of a straight line as the shortest distance between two points, while also suggesting that if viewed from the end, a straight line appears as a single point.</note> *Thus too Truth, and the Holy, and the Good,*
*From all parts conspire toward a single goal*
*Perpetually; and the meaning of that one thing is one.*

<summary>This page introduces the "Book on Measure," establishing a philosophical link between geometric straightness and the nature of Truth, asserting that while truth is singular and direct, error (represented by curves) is infinite.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, geometry, truth, straight line, measure, metaphysics, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory poem from the previous page, further developing the metaphor of the straight line as truth. It then transitions into a systematic prose categorization of the concept of "Measure," which serves as the foundation for the "Book on Measure."</meta>

# GIORDANO BRUNO THE NOLAN

*And beneath a single path, it aligns the extremes of the goal.*
*But the forms and figures of the false are countless;*
*Just as a crooked path takes up numbers without end.*
*If certain things are not at all grasped in a single order,*
<margin>20</margin> *They will try in vain, as if driven on one path,*
*To strive from various limits toward a single end.*
*Since finally individual forms do not consist of divided parts,*
*Or are one only in their own parts.*
*If they attend to their own method and the numbers of measuring—* <note>original: "metundi" or "metiendi." The OCR shows "me-" at the end of a line; in the context of Bruno's work on geometry, this refers to the act of measurement.</note>
*Wherefore Anaximenes called what is dispersed in numbers and matter*
*Vain and void; to Melissus*
*And Parmenides, it is pleasing to say that only the One is true.* <note>Anaximenes, Melissus, and Parmenides were Pre-Socratic philosophers. Bruno highlights the Parmenidean view that ultimate reality is a single, unchanging "One," contrasting it with the "vain" multiplicity of the material world.</note>

**M**easure is threefold: Above and before the thing. Within and with the thing. After and outside the thing. <note>This "threefold" distinction is a classic Scholastic and Neoplatonic framework (ante rem, in re, post rem) used to describe how concepts like "beauty" or "measure" exist: as an idea in the mind of God, as a physical property in an object, and as a concept in the human mind.</note> Measure above the thing is threefold: The One, the Mind, and the Idea. Measure within the thing is threefold: Magnitude, Weight, and Moment <note>original: "Momentum," which can refer to movement, importance, or a tipping point of force.</note>. Magnitude is threefold: of one, two, and three dimensions. Weight is threefold: outside of a place, toward a place, and in or around a place. Moment is threefold: of Substance, of Quality, and of Respect; or of the Absolute, the Concrete, and the Relative. Measure after the thing is threefold: Efficient, Formal, and Instrumental. The Efficient [measure] is threefold: Spirit <note>original: "Daemon." In Bruno’s cosmology, daemons are not necessarily "demons" but intermediary spirits or intelligences that act upon the world.</note>, Man, and Brute. The Formal [measure] is threefold: Sense, Reason, and Intellect. The Instrumental [measure] is threefold: Primary, Intermediate, and Immediate. The Primary instrumental is threefold: the Finger, the Palm, and the Cubit. The Intermediate is threefold: the Compass, the Square, and the Plumb-line. The Immediate is threefold: the Point, the Straight Line, and the Circle. Of these species of measure, some are Metaphysical, some Physical, and some Mathematical. We have spoken of the primary instrumental mathematical measures in the immediately preceding books. It follows that we should contemplate the intermediate and the immediate—or concerning those things around the intermediate and immediate—under the scale and analogy of many ratios of measure.

¶ A very short line between two points and a straight line prove each other in turn. Likewise, that which is straighter is also shorter. Between two points, infinite curves can be produced. Thus, truth is the one, simple, and shortest path of apprehension, while falsehood is manifold, long-winded, and difficult—such as we see "sophistical geometry" to be, along with other parts of philosophy.

### CHAPTER I.

<summary>Bruno defines "Measure" through a Neoplatonic hierarchy, categorizing it into metaphysical, physical, and mathematical realms. He argues that truth is represented by the simplicity of a straight line, while falsehood is found in the infinite complexity of curves and "sophistical" reasoning.</summary>

<keywords>Measure, Metaphysics, Geometry, Truth vs. Falsehood, Parmenides, Threefold Measure, Magnitude, Instrumental Measure</keywords>

# BOOK ON MEASURE.
## CHAPTER II.

### ON EQUALITY. From a given point, or in whatever way, I take a line equal to a given straight line. <note>This title is a variation on the language used in Euclid’s *Elements* (Book I, Proposition 2), which deals with the geometric construction of equal lines. Bruno is framing his philosophical poem within the structure of a geometric treatise.</note>

**Y**ou will not find anything equal in every respect in the nature of things.
*This measure does not repeat that which had once been mastered.* <note>original: "subactum." This refers to the object or matter that has been subjected to measurement or "tilled" by the mind.</note>
*Hence, it is not possible for a compound, or any complex thing,*
*To be brought back again to exactly one and the same mark:*
*For indeed, parts concur in a countless order*
*With dissimilar nature and position; just as a portion*
*Of matter is not the same in its species, fortune, or site,*
*Since all things consist of a varied series within and without.*
*For just as a departure from all things is caused by*
<margin>10</margin>
*The countless members pressing through all things, so it is not possible*
*For a grant so great or so identical to arise from things so great and identical:*
*Since likewise there cannot be such a **harmonious discord** <note>original: "discordia concors." A favorite Renaissance concept where the universe is held together by the balance of opposing forces.</note>*
*From things everywhere diverse, various, and placed in opposition.*
*Thus, in its immense stretch, this **wheel of time** <note>original: "rota temporis."</note> snatches things up so that*
*In a single moment, there are thousands upon thousands of divisions,*
*And all things are varied by the numbers of their fortunes, although*
*These parts—the very substance of things—escape our senses.*
*So also, those things which appear together under the indicator of sense,*
*Nature truly separates into many moments;*
<margin>20</margin>
*Just as our **minimum** <note>original: "minimum." In Bruno’s philosophy, the "minimum" is the fundamental building block of reality—the atom in physics, the point in geometry, and the monad in metaphysics.</note> is distinguished from nature's own minimum.*
*Nevertheless, reason promises this to itself, and spontaneously*
*Provides it; and the measure rejoices to compare*
*The subjects to the sense, so that it happens that many things*
*Fall under the rule of a single ray; and are comprised in the same circle.*

<vocab>measure, equality, harmonious discord, wheel of time, substance, reason</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the concept of equality, arguing that while geometry assumes perfect equality, it does not exist in the physical world due to the constant flux of matter and the "wheel of time." Bruno posits that "reason" allows us to impose order and measure on this diversity, even though nature is composed of "minima" that escape our direct senses.</summary>

<keywords>equality, geometry, atomism, minimum, flux, harmonious discord, wheel of time, nature of matter</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into measurement and equality. Having established on the previous page that perfect equality is impossible in the material world due to the constant flux of matter, Bruno now critiques the methods of Aristotle and Euclid, arguing that human geometry is an approximation of a deeper, hidden reality.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

*Either by an arc, or by equal markers, suitable things will come:*
*But what is the need for a triquetrum <note>A "triquetrum" was a medieval astronomical instrument used to measure the altitudes of stars. Bruno uses it here to represent complex, indirect measurement.</note>, when simpler principles*
*Are the reason for composite things to me? Those composites do not, in turn,*
*Serve as the reason for the simple, except for the physicist,*
*And by a posterior discourse <note>original: "discursu posteriore." In scholastic logic, this refers to reasoning backward from effects to their causes, rather than from principles to effects.</note>;*
*Which kind of wisdom usually adopts such methods of its own accord*
*Because of the thin light of nature and the profound depth of things.*
<margin>30</margin> *Therefore, you will produce nothing better by this reasoning,*
*By which, since certain limits are established for you,*
*One thing is more extended, another more contracted:*
*And it is not the task of the Circle that you see things nearer the middle,*
*And hence more remote, which touch the very near center,*
*Which remain grasped by the circuit, yet recede when raised:*
*It is as if one sought the light of the sun through the stars.*

---

**A**ristotle wisely indeed—though not (as can be seen) from his own workshop <note>Bruno implies Aristotle borrowed this idea or did not fully originate the proof himself.</note>—defined "equal" by "more" and "less": for in practice, a greater or lesser approach to equality is found, but reaching it exactly is not possible at all.

¶ 3. What we say of one thing must be understood of all things whose being consists in a certain breadth, number, and degree. Just as, in truth, a healthy person cannot be found in a state of most exact **balance of humors** <term>eucrasia: the perfect internal balance of the four bodily humors—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile—which was thought to constitute perfect health</term> even for a moment, because the subject is composed of countless and diverse parts.

¶ 27 The equality of lines is wrongly proven by Euclid through an equilateral triangle: for it is not proper to demonstrate a thing from those that follow after it <note>original: "ex posterioribus." Euclid's first proposition in the *Elements* uses the construction of an equilateral triangle to prove lines are equal; Bruno argues this is logically backward for a foundational principle.</note>, especially at the very beginning. The same would happen to us if, without first defining the circle, we proceeded from the equality of its radii.

# CHAPTER III.

## OF MODERATION AND THE MEASURE: I cut away the surplus from the greater part.

**T**he ray of Justice is the norm and the rule, by which
No line oversteps the limit which the line itself points out.

<meta>catchword: Maie-</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that perfect equality is a theoretical ideal unattainable in the physical world, criticizing Aristotle's definitions and Euclid's geometric proofs as being logically "backward." He introduces the concept of "eucrasia" (perfect balance) as something impossible to maintain in a complex body.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Euclid, geometry, triquetrum, eucrasia, equality, measurement, physics, logic, balance of humors</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the practical application of making things equal—through the physical removal of excess—to a philosophical discussion on the nature of mathematical abstraction. It specifically introduces the concept of dimensions, arguing that the mind and the senses can indeed perceive a single dimension (length) apart from width, despite common skepticism.</meta>

<page-num>161</page-num>
<header>ON MEASUREMENT, BOOK [III]</header>

*Whatever remains of the larger, making it equal to the smaller,*
*Is cut away and discarded according to the rule of the rod.*
*If one end of the first is established as the center,*
*And the distance takes its law from the other;*
*So that presently, if this center is joined to that center,*
*The remainder may clearly be seen to be cut back*
*As is proper by the straightedge <note>original: "amussi." A tool used by masons or carpenters to ensure a surface is perfectly flat or straight; here used as a metaphor for precision.</note>, with the end of the latter running in a circle.*
*Therefore, from a single or similar center, you will complete*
*The examinations of the unequal; likewise, the ends approach the first.*

<margin>10</margin>
**W**e observe the bringing of the greater into equality with the smaller by the same principle as just above; the demonstration is derived from an axiom as if from a first principle, and from a theorem as if from a proximate <note>In scholastic logic, a "proximate principle" is a secondary rule derived directly from a primary axiom.</note> principle.

## CHAPTER IIII.

### THE DISTINGUISHER. I perceive Length without Width by sight.

**B**oth rumor and reason are accepted by the common folk and by wisdom,
That Mathematics is least of all a liar when it abstracts <note>Abstraction in this context refers to the mental process of considering a property (like length) in isolation from the object it belongs to.</note>,
When it considers one thing without conceiving of the rest as one,
Rather than conceiving of one as joined to the other;
Yet sometimes they attempt to resolve by reason
What Nature <note>original: "Physicé."</note> resolves; the senses receive the resolved parts
More purely and better; while the duty and zeal of reason fails,
If by chance it applies itself to these same things.
Can there not be only one sensible dimension
<margin>10</margin>
Entirely distinct from the other two?
For the common folk think this itself cannot be seen,
(While they see it meanwhile, because it is plainly before their eyes).

<sig>L 2</sig>

<summary>Bruno discusses the physical method of achieving equality by trimming excess and introduces a defense of mathematical abstraction, specifically the ability to perceive a single dimension like length independently of width.</summary>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the validity of mathematical abstraction, specifically defending the concept of a "line" (length without width) against the skepticism of Sextus Empiricus. It moves from poetic defense into a logical explanation of how different sciences can study different aspects of the same object without falsifying reality.</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

*And for the internal eyes to look toward sight, by the aid of which*
*Reason abstracts from the rest. Nor does that follower of Pyrrho,*
*Sextus <note>Sextus Empiricus (c. 160–210 AD), a Skeptic philosopher who argued that since our senses can be deceived, we cannot have certain knowledge of the true nature of things. Bruno is refuting his claim that a line without width is an impossible fiction.</note>, believe this very thing; to him, thought is not*
*of such great power that, without being seized by any breadth,*
*it perceives the form of length, which is called a line.*
*Furthermore, however much the smallest length might escape you,*
*yet the "long" which is the boundary itself shall not escape,*
<margin>20</margin> *namely, that which is the distinction between black and white.*
*Nay, I would even dare to firmly add this truth:*
*The less thought can endure the gaze of the extreme light of sense,*
*so clearly does the thought of beauty not surpass,*
*nor equal, nor come nearly so close to the present form of the beautiful.*
*By nature, the sensible light of the sun stands as a better indicator*
*than sight itself; and the deputy power <note>The mental faculty or imagination that "stands in" for the senses.</note> will receive this light less,*
*just as the shadow of the night does not carry colors.*

---

**I**t is commonly said that those who abstract <note>original: "abtrahentium." In philosophy, abstraction is the mental act of focusing on one quality of an object (like its shape) while ignoring others (like its material), which Bruno argues is a valid way to find truth.</note> do not tell a lie. For various sciences and arts deal with one and the same subject, which are distinguished not by the thing considered, but by the very mode, form, and end of the consideration: meanwhile, as long as things which are subjectively one are taken by other intentions <term>intentions: mental representations or specific ways the mind "aims" at an object to understand a particular aspect of it</term> through themselves and separately, one does not depart entirely from the nature of the thing. For it is permitted to consider the color of a rose separately, setting aside those things which serve to explain its nature and other properties; likewise, there is one science that considers sight, and another that considers the eye; and yet, because they are joined according to their existence, they ought not also be understood as inseparable according to consideration, as if things were then considered otherwise than they are in the nature of things. But indeed, it belongs to the most foolish cattle <note>original: "ineptissimi pecoris." A characteristic Brunian insult for those who lack the intellectual capacity to distinguish between physical reality and mental concepts.</note> not to know how to distinguish between *considering this without that*, and *this existing without that*.

¶ 18 The difference between two colors on a plane indicates the line which is the boundary. Yet we can comprehend that which is the minimum by no sense.

<meta>Catchword: CHAPTER</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that mathematical abstraction (like defining a line as length without width) is not a lie, but a valid scientific method of considering properties separately. He uses the boundary between two colors as physical proof that a "line" exists as a distinct limit, even if it has no width.</summary>

## CHAPTER V.

**DIVISION <note>original: "Schisma." While usually meaning a religious or political split, Bruno uses it here in its literal Greek sense of a "cleaving" or "division" of a geometric line.</note>. BALANCING <note>original: "Libratio." This refers to the act of finding a balance point or leveling.</note>. I bisect the length, and find the middle point within it.**

**A**nd you divide one straight line into two equal parts, 
When it lies spread beneath the place of a fixed radius <note>In this context, Bruno is describing the use of a compass (the "radius") to find the center of a line.</note>,
And let one of its endpoints flow around it,
As far as it is gripped by the arc of the radius
From this side and from that; so that finally the other limit
Flows down into the remainder: just as the path by which high Apollo <note>Apollo represents the Sun.</note>
Stretches into the Ocean is cleaved by the ebb of the Earth 
At a point; when the point of Erigone <note>Erigone is the constellation Virgo.</note> toward Ganymede <note>Ganymede is the constellation Aquarius. Bruno is using these celestial markers to describe points on a circle or arc.</note>
Driving a straight course, shall have flowed down, seeing that from the very
<margin>10</margin> Ocean, Erigone, Ganymede, and high Apollo
Are distant by an equal radius: and for that reason,
If the other [point] flows down from both sides into the opposite tip,
And the threads <note>Bruno likely refers to the lines drawn between the intersections of the arcs.</note> come cleaved in turn through the middle,
Horus and Jason <note>Figures Bruno frequently uses to represent the seeker of wisdom and the light of the intellect.</note> affirm these things with a Periclean head <note>Likely meaning with the wisdom or eloquence of the Athenian statesman Pericles.</note>.
Does not the nourishing Mother [Nature] also wisely grant
Divisions to things by chance, she who has thus separated 
All contrary things into equal parts and composed them 
In such numbers, so that whatever has receded returns to the whole?
A circle is the most certain norm discovered for these things,
<margin>20</margin> Which is able to distribute the times in certain successions.
Thus where the fixed center stands—the reason and the power
Of truth—opposite dogmas arise toward different ends,
Which, when they run together into the number of the monad <note>The "Monad" is the ultimate, indivisible unit or "One" in Bruno’s philosophy, representing the source of all being and the point where opposites coincide.</note>, 
->they dissolve.<-
Therefore, let that which no compass can grasp 
All at once come to you bisected, if—with equal parts 
Taken from here and from there—you leave a comprehensible space in the middle.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and philosophical argument from page 165. Having discussed bisection (dividing in two), Bruno now addresses the "minimum" (the smallest possible unit) and moves into Chapter VI, which explains the "multisection" of lines—dividing them into many parts using geometric constructions named after various virtues and states of being.</meta>

<page-num>166</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

Assuming the existence of a **minimum** <note>In Bruno’s philosophy, the "minimum" is the physical and mathematical atom—the smallest possible unit that cannot be divided further.</note>—namely, a defined first part (as must also be the case from the perspective of matter)—not every line is divisible into two equal parts; this remains true even if that minimum is conceived indistinctly or confusedly <note>If a line consists of an odd number of "minima" or atoms, it cannot be split into two equal whole-number halves.</note>. It was stated above whence that capacity for dividing into even and odd parts arises. However, if divisibility continues to infinity, then there are no "first" parts, nor "middle" parts, nor can there be "proximate" and defined parts (measurable by reason); rather, parts would be indefinite according to their position and choice, following infinite degrees of difference.

### CHAPTER VI.

#### THE DISTRIBUTOR. I divide a given line into many parts by a certain ratio.

***J**ust as one part was cut for you, a line offered to be cut*
*Will very easily pass into just as many parts, provided that*
*The middle point of that cut line is first taken*
*As a center, and let it encircle one whole endpoint.*
*Thence, from the opposite pole <note>original: "cardine." Literally a hinge or pivot point, here referring to the endpoint of a diameter or line.</note> of the given line, let one limit*
*And the other, by a straight flow of any length,*
*Fall into the circle itself in an equal path, so that as if one*
*Conceives several parts, let the remaining part take up*
*Just as many similar ones; then let one be bound to its own boundaries,*
<margin>10</margin> *And the other to the boundaries of the second, if you desire the*
*Divided middle to be longer by more than one part*
*Equal to the rest. Just as **Playfulness** <note>original: "Lasciuia."</note>, the **Muse**,*
***Ornament**, and **Honor**, coming into one circle,*
*Show by what force the path—which descends as a **Habit** from the high*
*Pole to the opposite—comes divided through those*
*Equal parts, by which **Music** is bound here to **Ornament**,*
*And through her, **Playfulness** is joined to swelling **Honor**.*
*These wonders **Solimus** <note>Possibly a reference to Solomon, often used by Bruno as a figure of wisdom.</note> has the power to show to **Horus** and **Pericles**;*
*If it pleases you, by the admitted voice of the constant **Berastus** <note>Berastus is a recurring character or "witness" in Bruno's mnemonic and philosophical works.</note>.*

<summary>Bruno argues that geometry must be based on a finite "minimum" (atom) rather than infinite divisibility, then presents a poetic-geometric method for dividing lines into multiple proportional segments using allegorical figures.</summary>

## ARACHNE’S WEB FROM
### the Court of Venus
<note>In Greek mythology, Arachne was a weaver of such skill that she challenged Minerva. Bruno uses this name for a complex geometric diagram that resembles a web. The "Court of Venus" suggests a realm of harmony, beauty, and perfect proportions.</note>

->The Principal Distributor, likewise accomplishing this via a triangle.<-

*You will accomplish this even more briefly and even more successfully on your own,*
<margin>20</margin>
*if the straight base you have made to be cut [1]* <note>The line you wish to divide.</note>
*rises into a triangle [2], from whose vertex [3]* <note>The top point or "cuspis" of the triangle.</note>
*you take as many parts [4] as you wish, with a straight length stretched out [5].*
*Then a smaller, similar line [6], which consists of the same number of parts,*
*is joined to the ends [7] of both straight lines; for there is*
*a triangle here [8] of the same ratio; from which, if the angle [10]* <note>The angle or "ray" originating from the vertex.</note>
*flows down through those divided boundaries [9]*
<margin>30</margin>

*divided by the same ratio. Likewise, without end,*
*you can cut innumerable lines, both larger and smaller,*
*into the same number of equal parts on that same underlying base,*
*if they happen to fit the back of this triangle.*

# CHAPTER VIII.
## FIRST APPLICATION.

->From a given point above a given straight line, I draw a parallel,<-
->or I apply parallels as I please<-
->by means of a circle.<-

> *Parallel acts are the archetypes of the flatterer, and of every*
> *companion of Fortune, who follows the pattern of another’s life,*
> *either by shaping his own vow or by kindling his own;*
> *With the holy man he will be holy, with the perverse he will go crooked:* <note>This is a paraphrase of Psalm 18:25–26, using the geometric concept of "parallelism" as a metaphor for moral imitation or social climbing.</note>

> *by which, each one retaining his own method in his proper order,*
> *he will never disturb, and never be disturbed himself;*
> *but he will preserve the beautiful face of things and the specimen*
> *of nourishing Nature through the laws of skillful art.*

## THE INDICATING OF A STRAIGHT LINE FROM
### a sought point.

<margin>40</margin>

*Will you not prove a straight line through the points shown,*
*The circle, and are these not parts of the Cycle in turn;*
*And are they not proved just as we clarified a little before?*

¶ 14. 1. The shown point G. 2. Along the circuit <note>original: "gyrum"</note> G C B E. 3. The given point H. 4. The underlying line receiving the fall <note>referring to the perpendicular "falling" onto the base line</note> G C. 5. The middle point between G and C. 6. That sought line H B. 7. That which looks toward both G and C. 8. The sides <note>original: "costae"; literally "ribs," referring to the sides of the geometric figure</note> on both sides C H, G H. 9. ¶ 40. In a compass <term>circinus</term> <note>a mathematical tool used for drawing circles and measuring distances</note> (which, when opened, defines a certain straight line), the straight line tests the circle when one end stands still while the other is moved; and the circle tests the straight line as in the current proposition, but not without the straight line. Therefore, the straight is the indicator <note>original: "index"</note> of itself and of the slanted; but the slanted is an indicator of the straight only by means of the straight, as seen in points A B C D.

<summary>This page provides a keyed legend for a geometric diagram, explaining how to identify a straight line relative to a specific point using a compass. Bruno concludes with a philosophical-geometric axiom stating that while a straight line can define a circle, the slanted or curved can only be measured in relation to the straight.</summary>

# SECTION II ON THE ANGLE.
## CHAPTER I.
### The distinction of the angle.

*As the triad arises from the monad and dyad,*
*the Angle first comes into being, appearing in a threefold distinction:*
*the Right, the Greater than right, and the less than right.*
*By these species, the four meanings common to the wise*
*are further distinguished, at least in a plane;*
*(since an angle also exists in a solid,*
*which defines the face of a cone at a single point, and*
*into which multiple planes meet with straight lines,*
*here where it terminates at least three thinned faces <note>original: "hadras"; likely from the Greek "hedra," referring to the seats or faces of a geometric solid</note>).*
<margin>10</margin> *For it is either a point which is only a boundary, and*
*is not any part; Or it is the smallest part, connecting the ends*
*of this and that line—straight or curved, or even just one;*
*of which one is straight and the other joined is curved;*
*Or it is the minimum distance between two points, which are*
*neighbors at the edge with nothing in between. Nor is there*
*only one measure for this; but obtuse, right, or acute*
*it occurs according to various ratios. Or it will signify*
*the measure at the minimum, which names the first triangle*
*in a triangle <term>triquetrum</term> <note>original: "triquetrum," a three-cornered or triangular figure</note>: whose base is the first*
<margin>20</margin> *placed under the whole, and likewise to be divided.*
*Truly, as a parallel line is stretched beneath it and driven home,*
*cutting the triangle by a similar ratio, just as*
*was naturally evident when I was teaching how to explain the minimum.*

<summary>Giordano Bruno defines the nature of the angle, deriving it from the progression of number (monad to triad) and categorizing it into four distinct geometric meanings involving points, boundaries, and the "minimum" measure.</summary>
<keywords>Angle, Triad, Monad, Dyad, Right Angle, Obtuse Angle, Acute Angle, Solid Geometry, Triquetrum, Minimum, Geometry, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

->ON THE BOOK OF MEASURE<-
<sig>M</sig>

**F**rom one, nothing third is generated without a medium: nor is there any other medium without a third: just as a point does not flow into a line without another endpoint <note>original: "termino puncto"</note>: nor does a point reach another endpoint without a line.

¶ 10. The angle "runs" <note>functions or proceeds</note> with a fourfold meaning. First, as a point-boundary. Second, as the minimum connecting the ends of two lines. Third, as the minimum distance between those two points, which is defined by the differences of obtuse, right, and acute. Fourth, by a meaning peculiar to us, for the side of a triangle from one of the primary parts, parallel to the larger divided base, and cutting the <term>triquetrum</term> <note>a three-cornered or triangular figure</note> proportionally; its use is singular in the discovery of the minimum.

**I**n Euclid, the equilateral triangle is proved by the equality of the radii of the same circle, with the light of our first axiom added.

*Now indeed, so that a right angle may be aptly formed*
*From any point, let it be done in the curve of a circle;*
*Any point here, which at the faces of the twin poles* <note>original: "cardinis ora." Bruno often uses "poles" or "hinges" to refer to the primary axes or cardinal points of a geometric figure.</note>
*May flow from there, by which the diameter becomes tangent to the orb.*
*The Temple of Venus shows this, along with Fear and Complaint,* <note>In Bruno’s geometric-memory system, these are specific locations on a diagram. "Fear" (Timor) and "Complaint" (Querela) represent specific points or lines within the "Temple of Venus" construction.</note>

10 *Point in the arc which they touch simultaneously above and below.*
*Ramus <note>original: "Rauclius." This refers to Peter Ramus (Pierre de la Ramée), a famous 16th-century humanist and mathematician whose works Bruno frequently commented upon.</note> finished all this briefly and aptly.*

In order to establish a right angle at any given point—because it becomes the boundary of an equilateral triangle inscribed in a circle (which is considered among the easiest things to perform)—I thus also make a line tangent to the circle. Thus I also restore the circumscribed triangle with primary ease. Thus we execute other things which either follow from these, or are required to be made according to their standard.

## I shall raise a right angle at the point of contact of a circle, and a tangent line.

*This way, which connects the Good to Love in the middle,* <note>original: "Bonum medio connectit amori." In Bruno’s mnemonic geometry, "Bonum" (The Good) and "Amor" (Love) represent specific points or principles in his diagrams.</note>
*Desires to touch the very circle of the Good at a point*

# CHAPTER VII.
## THE WEDGE.
### I describe the minimum and maximum angle.

*Simply the smallest is said to be that which mediates*
*Between the twin Circle where they touch on either side;* <note>This refers to the "angle of contingency" or "horn-like angle" formed between two touching circles or a circle and a tangent.</note>
*But it takes on degrees, for when the circle is increased more,*
*It seeks out the conditions of a straight line all the more.*
*But the angle which comes from a straight and a curved line, and which*
*The diameter supports at its own endpoint, is held to be*
*The smallest of all, where a straight line will be added to it*
*On the other side. And a larger one adheres to any right angle—*
*Indeed the largest—when a comparison of these is made.*

<meta>To the [following]</meta>

<summary>Bruno describes the construction of a tangent and a right angle using his mnemonic system of points (Good, Favor, Nature), and begins a theoretical discussion on the "Wedge" (the angle of contingency), arguing that as a circle grows larger, its arc behaves more like a straight line.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from page 180, where Bruno discussed the "angle of contingency" (the tiny space between a circle's curve and a tangent line). Here, he further explores how curvature affects the size of an angle and begins Chapter VIII, "The Balance," which provides a mnemonic method for constructing equal angles using his unique system of naming geometric points after mythological figures.</meta>

<page-num>181</page-num>
<header>ON MEASUREMENT, BOOK [III]</header>
<sig>M 3</sig>

*For those angles raised from both straight and curved lines: let them be considered common things,* <note>original: "Vulgaria habentor." Bruno suggests that standard geometric rules often fail when mixing straight and curved lines.</note>
*For there is no rational proportion where the straight and the curved meet as one,*
*And you shall provide twin curves in various order.*

**W**e define the minimum and maximum angle in the ratio of a given positioned circle in this way. Simply put, we define the minimum and maximum angle by the magnitude of the curvature. Furthermore, the greatest curvature is to be seen in the smallest circle, and the smallest curvature in the largest; and curvature is greater in a circle closer to the <term>minimum circle</term><note>The smallest possible circle in Bruno's atomist geometry.</note> than in one closer to the maximum. This we have fully shown in the preceding sections.

# CHAPTER VIII.
## THE BALANCE.

-> I provide an angle equal to an angle. <-

**I**f each sector of a circle <note>original: "Cycli sector." A "pie slice" of a circle.</note> is rendered equal,
It is suggested clearly enough that the angle is equal:
Namely, where one angle and the other become the center of the Circle
And they embrace an arc that is both similar and equal thereafter;
Indeed, you see that the angle which **Oceanus** makes at **Apollo**
With **Ganymede** is similar to the one which **Mars** and **Hermes**
Give at **Bacchus**. And if by chance one side should adjust itself
In part of the margin, and the remaining part which stands out further
Should come below it, succeeding from the same point.
<margin>10</margin> For where **Mars** and **Hermes** come from the point of **Fortune**,
An angle similar to **Erigone’s** will be fashioned; from which
**Oceanus** will have flowed closer to the seized **Ganymede**.
Or also, let one leg flow down from the center as a radius, and
Let the rest be joined with the margin at both ends,
As the radius of **Bacchus** is to **Fortune**, and the straight <term>chord</term><note>A straight line segment whose endpoints both lie on a circular arc.</note>
From that point to **Hermes** is to the radius which **Apollo** sends
All the way to **Erigone**; and the chord of this, by which to **Ganymede**...

*A similar angle is joined. Every equal boundary too*
*Either occupies an equal circle standing upon it,*

*All things which an equal circle finally defines:*

**W**e grant an angle equal to another angle in four ways. 1. First, with neither side <term>costa</term><note>literally "rib," here meaning the side of an angle.</note> positioned at the periphery. Second, with both sides positioned at the periphery. Third, with only one of them there. Fourth, with the angle itself touching the periphery.
¶ Thus you shall also take an equal to an internal angle from the extreme parts, with both sides produced further and the center received in the angle itself; from a circle of any size whatever, which—by rays flowing back into the center from the ends of the base designated on the periphery—will preserve the removed angle.

### CHAPTER IX.
#### THE DISCUNEATOR.
<note>Latin: "Discuneator," a word Bruno coined meaning "the wedge-splitter," referring to an instrument or method for bisecting angles.</note>

-> I bisect the angle. <-

**A**n *angle is easily split into two equal parts,*
*Passing into the form of a triangle from equal legs.*
*Therefore, that angle which the way of **Hermes** gives to the **Graces**,* <note>In Bruno’s geometric code, Hermes and the Graces (Charites) represent specific points or lines. Likely, the Graces are the vertices and Hermes is a connecting line.</note>
*From which **Apollo** is found joined by a straight path,*
*If it be gifted with a base by which it returns equal legs,*
*Certainly it may either grow where **Apollo** extends himself*
*To the shores of **Ganymede** or (if you like) **Hermes**,*
*Let it remain in **Bacchus** with signs equalized with **Apollo**:*
*Thence the bisected base is able to rise from the middle point*
<margin>10</margin> *To the **Grace** from the points of **Jove** and **Diana**.*

**A**n angle, according to the first and second definition, Euclid defined as indivisible <note>original: "insecabilem." This refers to Bruno's atomist view that at a certain "minimum" scale, geometric entities cannot be further divided.</note> without controversy; yet according to the third definition, he recognized it as bisectable, just as we do.

Truly, we take the angle in its fourth meaning to be proportionally divisible into many parts, as is the side <term>latus</term> <note>the line or "rib" subtending the angle</note> lying beneath it; and there is no one who can reasonably deny that the perfection of all measurement is singularly located in this.

## CHAPTER X.
### THE FIXOR.
<note>Latin: *Fixor*. In Bruno’s system, this likely refers to a "stabilizer" or a specific mnemonic figure/instrument used to define fixed points in a geometric construction.</note>

->*I see an angle that cannot be divided into many parts.*<-

**A**nd just as a line is divisible into many parts,
Then let the underlying angle also be divisible for you
When the flows <note>original: *fluxus*. Bruno views lines as the "flow" of a point.</note> lean back, looking toward a single point.
But let the angle now occur in its third meaning,
Namely as the boundary where two flows meet;
Or some minimum thing that bounds both flows—
Or finally, one of the first parts assumed [of the flows].
Of these, the one that is a sign <note>original: *signum*. Bruno uses this to mean a geometric point or mark.</note> can neither be touched
By you, nor divided. And immediately thereafter,
<margin>10</margin>A contact of equals in a certain number will be able
To be present, perhaps positioned as an indivisible thing.
But truly, granted in its final meaning,
Such as we raise up for multiple and great use,
It will clearly come out divided just like the first line.
How rashly the art of cutting an angle will be added to Euclid,
Given the common meaning <note>original: *significato vulgari*</note> of an angle,
Has been made manifest in part from things said elsewhere,
Since a right angle is accepted as indivisible through any parts
After it has migrated into those two parts 
<margin>20</margin>By a return-flow coming in the middle. For a sub-section
Is truly a progression begun toward something smaller than the minimum;
Yet the flow does not run down to the same point.

**Vocabulary:** Angle, Measure, Euclid, The Fixor, Flow, Divisible/Sectionable.

<summary>Bruno explores the limits of geometric divisibility, arguing that while common geometry (like Euclid's) assumes angles can be infinitely divided, there exists a "minimum" point or "sign" that is indivisible. He categorizes the different ways an angle can be defined, moving from abstract points to measurable, divisible lines.</summary>

*That which is well understood is what is well held in the mind.*

*Whatever we clarified at the start, so that the minimum* <term>minimum</term> <note>For Bruno, the "minimum" is the basic, indivisible building block of existence—the atom in physics, the point in geometry, and the monad in metaphysics.</note> *is similar*

*And not at all does every meeting come to the same point;*
*Whereby the absurdity is more clearly revealed, when a single angle*
*Composed of both a straight and a curved line dares to be divided* <note>original: *scindier*. This refers to the "horn angle" (angulus corniculatus), a classic problem in geometry regarding the space between a circle's circumference and a tangent line.</note>
<margin>40</margin> *Into equal parts; where no choice of a part*
*Can be introduced with reason: for it is not permitted that this be split*
*Into two equal parts, because the curved line*
*Is a boundary from that side of a part lacking proportion* <note>original: *ratione carentis*. Bruno argues that curved and straight lines are fundamentally different in kind at the level of the minimum, making certain ratios impossible.</note>
*To the opposite part, as you see admitted on all sides*
*By how the proportion of the straight will be seen compared to the curved.*
*Therefore, let it be sufficiently clear that the same angle,*
*Once touched, never leaves more than two parts on either side.*
*But while the section of its base is being performed, do not*
*Miserably deceive yourself as if one and the same boundary*
<margin>50</margin> *Were to come divided for all those straight lines. But keep this*

*Now, if indeed the nature of a part consists of many equal*

*Again and again, by one and then another*
*And another flux of points: and if,*
*As I desire, that minimum is made your boundary,*
*The act of touching can no longer be repeated;*
<margin>60</margin> *For the point of the one touching is not smaller than the*
*Point touched: therefore this point, once struck, is occupied;* <note>original: *obiuit*. Literally "has gone" or "has met its end." Bruno means the point is "used up" by the first contact.</note>
*And it is not able to be a boundary again and again.*
*Rightly, therefore, Euclid was never found to have mentioned*
*That an angle could be distributed into many parts.* <note>Euclid’s *Elements* demonstrates the bisection of an angle, but not its trisection or general n-section, which Bruno takes as proof of his atomic theory.</note>
*But the voice is too dull from the excessive zeal of the foolish,*
*Who [busy themselves] with triangles, angles, chords, sines, and tables;* <note>Bruno is criticizing contemporary mathematicians who rely on complex trigonometric tables (sines and chords) without understanding the underlying physical reality of the discrete "minimum."</note>

<summary>Bruno argues that geometric angles cannot be infinitely divided because they are composed of indivisible "minima" (atoms). He asserts that a single point can only be touched by a limited number of lines, as the point itself has a finite nature that is "exhausted" by contact.</summary>

In Bruno's philosophy, these are the fundamental, indivisible units of reality, serving as both physical atoms and geometric points.</note>: for indeed, at a single point, no more than two lines can be terminated from a single underlying base, just as no more than two equal straight lines can be drawn <note>This refers to the geometric property that from a point to a line, you can only draw two equal lines (forming an isosceles triangle); any other line will be longer or shorter.</note>, and no more than one shortest line <note>The perpendicular line.</note>. We have expressed through multiple principles, rather than just one, the fact that the same point cannot be reached from every side by an infinite number of lines.

## CHAPTER XI.
### THE OPENING.
->I judge the quantity of an angle.<-

*The angle (as I have said), although it cannot be divided into many parts,* <note>original: *non multisecetur*. Bruno argues that because the "point" at the vertex has a finite size, there is a physical limit to how many lines can meet there without overlapping.</note>
*Is not therefore necessarily prevented from being measured*
*To see how great it is; and this*
*Can best be understood in an enclosed arc*
*Comprised of two curves, which complete the arc*
*Along with the rest of this **triangle** consisting of equal legs,* <note>original: *triquetrum*. A three-cornered figure or triangle.</note>
*As will be made clear to the doctrine of the minimum on all sides.*
*For a different angle will yield different parts in that place,*
*Since if it touches the circle with two legs from a right angle,*
<margin>10</margin> *It will surely encompass a quadrant of that circle from the middle region,*
*Which the radius, when applied to the triangle,*
*Clearly indicates as being one of six parts;* <note>original: *de senis partibus*. A reference to the geometric fact that the radius of a circle fits exactly six times into its circumference, forming a hexagon.</note>
*Thus it informs by various conditions,*
*And the orbit is then split by various sections,*
*Which my system requires to be shown in its proper place.*

Notwithstanding the things just said, we define the quantity and the power of an angle: not indeed by reason of its boundary and its "minimum," but partly by the angle itself; according to the third and partly according to the fourth manner of understanding <note>Bruno refers to his specific categorizations of "minima" and "measures" established earlier in the treatise.</note>...

## CHAPTER XII.
### THE COMPLEX.

->I double the angle, simplify it, and step by step<-
->I increase and decrease it.<-

**A**s this base of the <term>triangle</term><note>original: *triquetri*. While it literally means "three-cornered," Bruno uses it to refer to a triangle, often in a cosmological or symbolic sense.</note> is double where **Ganymede**
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Joins himself to **Hermes**, compared to the upper triangle
To this where **Bacchus** and **Apollo** touch each other in turn; <note>Bruno uses mythological names to label specific points or vertices in his geometric diagrams. This was part of his mnemonic system for remembering complex proofs.</note>
Thus that angle which this same base supports
Comes to the Center twice as large as that angle
Which comes from the height of the circle’s equal legs; <note>This is a poetic description of a famous theorem: the angle subtended by an arc at the center of a circle is twice the angle subtended by the same arc at any point on the circumference.</note>
Which **Berastus** <note>Likely a reference to a specific figure or authority Bruno cites in his geometric works.</note> clearly shows with the touching circle.
From there, I do not believe it is difficult for me that the
Line which flows from the edge to the center is found
<margin>10</margin> To be now single, now double; thus also with certainty
It can bear points from the middle points with distinction,
Points from which the angle emerges by fixed ratios.
Therefore, by whatever ratio it can be increased
Or diminished, if the simple below and the double [above] are clearly seen,
Greater and smaller, all the way to the twin extreme;
Because the maximum is here on the underlying plane,
And the minimum is where it is then seen to rise
Into a pure straight line, its vertex having been consumed. <note>Bruno is describing the physical limits of an angle: the "maximum" being a flat line (180 degrees) and the "minimum" being where the angle is so small the vertex effectively disappears into a straight line.</note>
Wherefore the vertex that is seen resting on the circle,
<margin>20</margin> When fitted to the Center, makes a triangle whose base
Is the <term>chord</term><note>original: *chorda*. The straight line segment whose endpoints both lie on a circular arc.</note> of half the arc. From the intermediate point
It will receive the middle ratios between both,
So that you can easily find for yourself any
Exposed part, and let it come from the circle thereafter

*Each polygon described internally in order;*
*One inscribed concentric circle and another*
*Help this work. It also holds true with <term>Quirinus</term><note>A Roman god, but here likely a mnemonic persona used in Bruno’s memory system to represent a specific geometric rule.</note> showing the way.*

**B**eware lest you think that just as doubling, tripling, and increasing an angle in any way happens according to the <term>continuous mode</term><note>The idea that space or magnitude can be infinitely divided or increased without breaks.</note>, so too it can be divided discretely and multiplied according to its parts. On this point, we have established many foundations in the present and in previous explanations; indeed, in the matter at hand, it will be permitted to gather the multitude and scarcity of parts from the increase and decrease of the continuum (which flows not from the actual state, but from the potential of magnitude), except in the underlying base subtending the angle. In that base, however many parts are found, they exist by the act of the power of the angle. For a distinction must be made between that which is such **formally, virtually, and actively**, and that which is such **materially, actually, and effectively**.

## SCALE OF UNDERSTANDING

<meta>This page continues the geometric and metaphysical exploration of the triangle. While the previous page established the triangle's historical and mystical significance through the Delphic tripod, this page moves into the formal logic of geometry, defining the triangle as the "minimum" polygon and the first departure from the "infinite" nature of the circle.</meta>

<page-num>190</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA</header>

*Therefore the <term>monad</term> <note>The "monad" refers to the number one or the primary unit of existence.</note>, after the rule was given in a straight flow,*
*And the twin confluence of the <term>dyad</term> <note>The "dyad" refers to the number two or duality.</note> into a single point*
*Has been established; behold the <term>triad</term> <note>The "triad" refers to the number three, here manifesting as the triangle.</note>, by binding the end of the two,*
<margin>30</margin> *Follows with a straight line closing the first figure,*
*Which holds the smallest field of those bounded by straight lines:*
*It restrains it with the final number, because with equal perimeters,*
*The area becomes larger the more sides the polygonal figure has.* <note>Bruno is describing a geometric principle: for a fixed perimeter, a shape with more sides (like a hexagon) encloses more area than one with fewer sides (like a triangle). The triangle is thus the "minimum" container.</note>

**E**specially from the preceding points, it can be made manifest that the triangle and the circle possess the character of a principle with respect to all figures, just as the pyramid and the sphere are the primary principles of solid bodies. Since the largest of the figures encompassing a circle is the triangle, and the smallest of those encompassed by a circle is the triangle. <note>In terms of area: a triangle circumscribed around a circle is the "largest" compared to other polygons, while an inscribed triangle is the "smallest" compared to other inscribed polygons.</note> From one perspective, the triangle is gathered to be the maximum in relation to the circle and all others, but from another, the minimum. Likewise, because the first unfolding from the <term>non-angled</term> <note>original: "agonae". Referring to the circle, which has no discrete angles.</note> figure (which is the circle) into a polygonal one is into the triangle, it is inferred that the triangle is that figure which most closely embraces the circle. But where a progression is made from the triangle to the square, to the pentagon, and to more and more many-sided shapes, reaching a final stability in a circular progression, we find the triangle to be the most remote of all figures contained by a circle. Therefore, from one side we find the circle to be the maximum relative to the triangle and other figures, but from another, the minimum. For this reason, the logic of the principle is divided between the circle and the triangle just as between matter and form, potential and act, the limitable and the limit, the container and the contained, the maximum and the minimum.

## CHAPTER II.
### THE PRISON. <note>original: "Carcer". In Bruno's terminology, this often refers to the "boundary" or "constraint" of physical form.</note>

-> angle, <-

**S** *hould you desire to know what the minimum in this category is,*
-> *into which the continuous plane is thinned and perishes,* <-
*As you know; as if the infinite were grasped*

<header>BOOK ON MEASUREMENT</header>

*Every side is equal, and every angle is equal.*
*Indeed, side, angle, and area are equal there,*
*And that <term>triad</term> <note>The number three or the triangle.</note> must be traced back to the <term>monad</term> <note>The primary unit or the number one.</note>. Nor further*
*Does the triangle differ from the circle, which enfolds all*
*Shapes. And you will be able to understand that same minimum*
*In this way; and the great above all, as I noted before.*
*But if anyone, with the senses as a guide,*
*Should seek the minimum triangle, it is easy to fulfill the wish:*
<margin>10</margin>
*For just as where it happens that two circles touch each other*
*At a single point, the smallest angle is formed there;*
*So if a third circle moves into that common contact,*
*The triangle is bounded by that triple contact and the arcs.* <note>Bruno is describing the shape of the gap left behind when three circles are pushed together. This "curvilinear triangle" is what he considers the physical "minimum" of space.</note>
*If you desire the minimum, it emerges from the touching of minimums,*
*For the minimum vacuum consists within the minimum bodies.*
*And since the circle is known to be more capacious than any form,*
*To which every <term>polygon</term> <note>A shape with many sides and angles.</note> approaches more closely*
*As more angles rise from a single straight line,*
<margin>20</margin>
*Which will become the common measure of their boundary,*
*Thus it follows that the vacuum which creates itself around them*
*Consists as a minimum, to which everything approaches*
*More closely, just as the polygon is more a partner to the circle.*

**T**o one making a distinction logically or mathematically, the circle and the sphere will be found to be simply the "minimum" (which is conceived by the casting away of all angles, and is included within every figure of any other kind). But to one contemplating physically, where it is necessary for a certain smaller thing—which is a **triangular void**—to mediate between meeting <term>atoms</term> <note>Bruno, following the ancient Epicureans, believed the world was made of tiny, indivisible, round "atoms."</note> (which are the minimum round bodies), the triangle and the curved, recurved pyramid will be found to be simply the minimums.

### CHAPTER III.
## JOY. <note>In this work, Bruno titles his chapters after various virtues or emotional states, reflecting his belief that geometry and the soul are interconnected.</note>

<vocab>measure, triangle, circle, monad, polygon, atoms, triangular void, pyramid, joy</vocab>

<summary>Bruno defines the triangle as the "minimum" shape in physics, describing it as the triangular gap (void) that is inevitably formed when three spherical atoms touch. He distinguishes this physical reality from mathematical abstractions where the circle is considered the primary unit.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, atomism, vacuum, triad, monad, physical minimum, mathematical minimum, spherical atoms</keywords>

## And I construct an equilateral triangle simply or upon a given line, etc.

**I**f one circle should revolve around a single point, it will be a cycle; let another equal circle be made adhering to it and sharing a common boundary, using a radius from the edge of the first turn. Let points be noted at the three extremes of the sides: For the radius by which nourishing Diana <note>In Bruno's geometric shorthand, Diana often represents the Moon or a specific point/circle in a diagram.</note> is joined to Bacchus <note>Another point or celestial representative in the construction.</note> is equal to that by which the Goddess approaches Hermes. The line *sig* <note>A label for a specific line segment in the accompanying diagram.</note> is given, bounded by Apollo <note>The Sun; likely one end of the base line.</note> on one side and Haemonian Bacchus on the other; then, by turning them in succession, let centers be made, and the house of Charis <note>Grace; here representing the third vertex or the intersection point of two circles.</note> will be found.
<margin>10</margin>
It is there that the mutual meeting occurs which terminates both straight paths leading from the twin divinity to her. By means of equal radii, Chremes <note>A character name Bruno uses to represent a plain-speaking demonstrator who makes the logic clear.</note> makes all things plain.

Of all the types of triangles, only the <term>equilateral</term> <note>A triangle where all three sides are the same length.</note> appears to have a certain <term>commensurability</term> <note>The ability to be measured by the same standard.</note> with the circle and to possess a principle of mutual demonstration. This is because a radius falling upon the <term>periphery</term> <note>The outer boundary or circumference of the circle.</note>—resting upon the end of a radius exiting from the center—acquires such an inclination that nothing more is needed for its completion than for the other extreme to flow together into the center.

Accordingly, in those things which were said concerning the discovery of the <term>minimum</term> <note>Bruno's "atomic" unit of geometry and matter.</note>, we have indicated that the order of the parts of a <term>hexagon</term> <note>A six-sided shape.</note>—seen through its component triangles, which are defined by the radius of the corresponding circle—corresponds in a plane to the order of the parts of the circle. This circle must be sought in the solid <note>Three-dimensional space.</note> as if in its own proper subject.

## AUGMENTATION.
### And I open the way for increasing the TRIANGLE conformably, etc.

**D**iana <note>The circle or the area of the construction.</note> becomes four times greater if she is made the center at the point opposite to Charis, and by flowing around...

*Let him take two points with the compass toward Bacchus <note>In Bruno’s geometric system, Bacchus likely represents a center point or a specific circle in the diagram.</note> by which*

By that same series in which the equilateral triangle is increased, any other species of triangle will also take its number and growth (provided the ratio of one angle and two sides is preserved).

### CHAPTER V
### DIRECTION

->And I designate a triangle of whatever kind even upon the diameter of the earth, etc.<-

*The axis of the Earth is given so that, by sending rays*
*Toward the sky from the two poles, an equilateral*
*Triangle, or any other kind, may be established:*
*It is enough for me if only the supply of the two extremes is given;*
*Since the practice and logic belong to Parthenius and Melampus. <note>Names of characters or authorities Bruno invokes to represent the practical application of theory.</note>*

The reason for this possibility is that all right and <term>isoscelic</term> <note>original: "Isoschelici". Meaning a triangle with two equal sides or, in this context, the equal angles associated with them.</note> angles are equal. It is sufficient, therefore, for one side of the triangle to be constructed to serve as the foundation; because two angles and one side define the species and the "moment" <note>In this context, "moment" refers to the specific geometric determination or weight of the figure.</note> of a triangle.

### CHAPTER VI
### APPLICATION

->And from three given straight lines I compose a triangle,<-
->any two of which are greater than the one remaining<-
->when joined together.<- <note>This is a restatement of the "Triangle Inequality Theorem," which states that the sum of any two sides of a triangle must be greater than the third side.</note>

*If ever three straight lines are given to me to be fused*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Into a triangle. <note>original: "triquetrum," a three-cornered shape or triangle.</note> Either they will come as equals. And then it will be*
*A single radius to be kept, which touches a radius, and an arc*
*Resting upon an equal chord, <term>chord <gloss>a straight line segment whose endpoints both lie on a circular arc.</gloss></term> whose other endpoint*
*Flows down into the center. Furthermore, if only one line*
*Of the three comes offered unequal, set this one back*
*Subtended to a part of the circle, <note>original: "gyri," literally a circuit or rotation.</note> whose radius is*
*Equal to the matching pair, since the base aptly*
*Comes attached to the circle, grasped by two radii.*
<margin>10</margin> *And the very convenient number of the sides comes out.*
*But if a triple unequal line is offered,*
*Then the larger radius of the cycle <note>original: "cycli."</note> will be yours, and that*
*Which is in the middle of the cycle which is made concentric to it;*
*For where from the end of the radius sloping into the internal*
*Orb comes that which is taken third in order,*
*It will meet the radius of the mediating one at that point.*

Here the number of angles which are conceived in the perimeter of a figure is perpetually defined by the same number of lines; it will not always be so easy to reason in the plane of the figure. Likewise, by that condition, three lines will be composed into a triangle, whereby any two of them, whichever are taken, are greater than the third. This did not need another demonstration for Zeno <note>Zeno of Elea, known for his paradoxes regarding motion and the continuum; Bruno often invokes him when discussing the "minimum" or the nature of space.</note>, because its evidence runs with the light by which we define a straight line as the shortest between two points.

*So also it opens abundantly with the radius subtended in arcs*

*Your six-sided hexagon, and a triangle with alternating points,*
*But the circle aptly receives the circumscribed triangle*
*With hardly any effort, if done as we showed before.*
*Let the tangent line be joined to the radius, and let that same*
*Which is the side of the inscribed triangle become the base outside,*
*With a similar and equal one added. This work requires nothing*

The **R**adius, doubled from the angle of the inscribed figure, here provides the circumscribed figure, as is also the case in most figures rising from an odd-numbered vertex. Likewise, the easiest way to circumscribe a similar figure is from the sides made into bases for a similar triangle, specifically for the one whose vertex is terminated from the center.

## CHAPTER IX.
### MATTER.

I give a right-angled <term>orthogonium</term> <note>From the Greek "orthogonios," meaning right-angled.</note> triangle, and I vary it unto infinity upon the same straight line.

*It is established that you form a triangle with a right vertex.*
*Here, where the right angle described stands as the top-angle*
*Above the underlying straight line, the dimension of the sides is certain.*
*For you shall take it with a varying difference of the sides,*
*Just as the diameter <note>original: "Dimetrus"</note> of the circle is made the underlying line,*
*From whose ends a twin flow may tend now toward this point,*
*Now toward that, just as the <term>Erector</term> <note>original: "Erector". In Bruno's geometry, the "Erector" refers to a specific mnemonic figure or a "constructor" tool used to generate vertical or angled lines from a base.</note> possessed.*

The same quantity is preserved collectively for the two sides containing the right angle; or (what is the same thing) while the measure of the one base subtending the right angle remains perpetually constant. This is made manifest in an angle described within a semicircle. <note>Bruno is describing a principle of Euclidean geometry: any triangle inscribed in a semicircle with the diameter as its base will always have a right angle at the vertex touching the circumference.</note>

## CHAPTER X.
### SEED.

# CHAPTER XI.
## GENERATION.

->**I multiply a right-angled triangle into others similar to itself and to each other.**<-

*From a right-angled triangle <note>original: "triquetrum". Bruno frequently uses "triquetrum" (three-cornered) as a synonym for triangle.</note> you shall also multiply a similar one,*
*If the right angle flows down into a shape, so that*
*A straight line, reaching the middle of the base, is perpendicular:* <note>Bruno is describing a fundamental theorem of geometry: in a right-angled triangle, a perpendicular line drawn from the right angle to the hypotenuse divides the triangle into two smaller triangles that are similar to the original and to each other.</note>
*For it is to make a triangle like a triangle, a part like the whole;*
*Which it is possible to show clearly in all figures.*

By the flow of the midpoint of the sides to the angles, or from the angles to the midpoint of the opposite sides, or with the sides produced all around, we have the ability both to increase the same figure and to multiply it around one center.

# CHAPTER XII.
## BIRTH.

->**I fit a line to any triangle whatever, and I cut it proportionally.**<-

*Every line fits itself to any triangle whatever,*
*If it is itself made the base of a similar triangle.*
*Which will first happen when the angle is not unsuitable*
*And is at hand, which a line of such size also subtends.*
*Thus exist the triangle of Bacchus, of the Graces, and of Apollo*
*In the larger triangle of Hermes, the Graces, and Ganymede.* <note>These mythological names refer to specific points and regions within Bruno's complex mnemonic diagrams. By mapping "Bacchus" or "Apollo" to specific geometric positions, the practitioner of his "Art of Memory" could "store" information within the shape of the triangle.</note>
*Then you shall divide the triangle into similar parts,*

*The parallel line is drawn whenever it is next to its side;*

## CHAPTER XIII.
### MOTION. <note>In Bruno’s geometry, "Motion" (Motus) refers to the transformation or "deformation" of a shape into another while maintaining a specific property, such as its total area.</note>

**I transform a triangle while maintaining an equal area, and I decrease and increase it by a certain ratio.**

*A single triangle will be varied in a thousand ways*
*Perpetually, so that the area remains the same for all,*
*Provided they are all contained within the same parallels*
*Entirely, and a single base is spread beneath them.* <note>Bruno is describing a fundamental rule of Euclidean geometry: triangles sharing the same base and height (resting between the same two parallel lines) have the same area, regardless of how skewed their sides are.</note> *And it is given*
*That within the parallels, a lesser or greater action occurs*
*According to whether the base is increased or always diminished.*
*Niclas is able to show this in many figures.* <note>original: "Niclas". This refers to Nicholas of Cusa (1401–1464), the German philosopher and mathematician whose work on the "coincidence of opposites" and the squaring of the circle deeply influenced Bruno.</note>

Thus, from the transformation of triangles into other triangles, a path is opened to equalize the ratios of differences between one polygon and any other; for in no species of polygon can an angle be equal to the angle of another species of polygon, just as a side of a polygon inscribed in a circle cannot be made equal to the side of another.
