# THE KEY OF TRANSMUTATIONS.

Firstly, I present the key, by which whatever figure
Is proposed can be seen converted into a triangle. <term>triquetrum<span>A three-cornered figure or triangle; in Bruno's work, this often carries symbolic weight alongside its geometric meaning.</span></term>
When its center, from the sides which shall be made equal,
Flows down into the middle of the side. And such
A line, being extended, meets with the remaining right vertex, <note>original: "cuspide recta." This refers to the "point" or vertex of the figure, likely at a right angle in the context of the "Key" diagram.</note>
Which is the one produced in length from the number of sides.

<note>The "Key of Transmutations" described here refers to the complex diagram below. Bruno uses this "Key" as a mnemonic and geometric device to show how different shapes—squares, circles, and polygons—can be mathematically or conceptually reduced to the form of a triangle. This process of "transmutation" was essential to his belief in the underlying unity of all geometric forms.</note>

# CHAPTER XVIII.

## THE BANNER OF THE SUN.

*Nicholas* <note>original: "Niclas." Referring to Nicolaus Cusanus (1401–1464), a German philosopher and mathematician whose work on the "squaring of the circle" and the nature of the infinite deeply influenced Bruno.</note> *taught this thoroughly. And it stands depicted*

*Recall to your mind how a single continuum extends itself*

*Or is shortened from a tall length into a broad one;*
*Furthermore, if a ready ratio of the divisible*
*Occurs to you—though not easily manageable for that*
*Whose mass you attempt to stretch out into an extraordinary size—*
*Namely, just as Queen Dido once drew out a bull's hide*
<margin>10</margin>*By such a cutting* <note>original: "segmine." Referring to the thin strips Dido cut.</note> *as much as would be enough*
*To complete the circuit of a city, which she had planned.* <note>According to the founding myth of Carthage, Queen Dido was offered as much land as could be encompassed by an oxhide. She cleverly cut the hide into a single long, thin strip to surround a large area.</note>
*Therefore, a square or any four-sided figure.*

### CHAPTER XIX.

### THE AUTHOR, THE DOUBLER, THE UNIFIER, from the common Euclidean principles.

**A** *right angle formed from a twin straight line arranges for you*
*A twin straight line, by which it may be connected on both sides;*
*So great is the base of the triangle <term>triquetrum</term> <note>original: "triquetri." An archaic term for a triangle, often used in astronomy and geometry.</note> closing the figure from there,*
*As the water <note>original: "aqua." Bruno uses "water" or "fluidity" here as a metaphor for area or the "content" of a geometric shape.</note> is equal to the twin figures from this twin source.*
*Wishing therefore to double this, arrange two*
*Equal straight lines, so that there is enclosed by them*
*That [right] angle, which brings forth an area equal to the two.*
*When the horoscope of Saturn <note>original: "Saturni horoscopus." In Bruno's system, this refers to a specific diagrammatic tool or "seal" used for calculation, likely associated with the square or the cross-quarter divisions of a circle.</note> receives this, from there*
*It will be present by a certain reason, both smaller and larger indeed:*
<margin>10</margin>
*Whether something of the sides be added to one,*
*Or be taken away, or be added to both,*
*The hypotenuse <term>subtensa</term> <note>original: "subtensa." Literally "the line stretched underneath," referring to the chord of an arc or the hypotenuse of a right triangle.</note> becomes no less one equal and the same;*
*Thus for a double circle, a double radius offers you*
*A triangle with a right angle, whose one larger side*
*Skillfully gives a field <note>original: "campum." Referring to the area of the figure.</note> equal to the two twin fields.*

<summary>This page concludes the "Banner of the Sun" section and begins Chapter XIX, which explains how to double the area of a geometric figure. Using the Pythagorean theorem as a basis, Bruno describes how the hypotenuse of a right triangle creates a new area equal to the sum of the areas of the two sides.</summary>

## CHAPTER XXII.

### On secret writing, the minimum, and explicable
### measurement.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A line stands accessible to the eye’s reach, compressed in a small space:
A clever youth will make this clear
By grasping it with equal straight lines stretched
All the way to a mark, by which the base concludes such a whole triangle,
In which, by being equally distant, a tiny mark—
Greater by twice twenty-two times <note>original: "binis vicibu’ atq́, vicenis." This likely refers to the 22 letters of the alphabet (a common count for Hebrew or specific Latin alphabets), suggesting a method where geometric points correspond to letters for a cipher.</note>—
Might include that [line] and, standing above it,
A crowd of sections <term>tomorum</term> <note>original: "tomorum." From the Greek 'tomos', meaning a cut or slice; here referring to the divisions on a geometric scale.</note> might divide it into just as many parts from its own boundaries.
Here the radius of the turn is the side of an equal triangle,
<margin>10</margin> To which one base explains the number of elements <note>Elements here refers to the letters of the alphabet.</note>
In parts; from which voices might be held through things seen
In their sequence, as the graduated sequence of sections
Indicates, so that the same radius will serve for various uses.

<meta>The table functions as a sliding scale where the alphabet shifts by one position for each philosophical concept. This layout is typical of Renaissance "Lullian" memory arts, where concepts are combined by rotating concentric wheels or using grids to generate new associations.</meta>

<page-num>214</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO</header>

# GIORDANO BRUNO

*Furthermore, countless mothers are multiplied for you,*
*Just as the species are varied, so too is the positioning of the element.*
*The number of daughters is also found under the mother,*
*But you shall derive thousands of mothers from the first elements.*
*Since they are connected in various ways through that varied number,*
*The works of the Art <note>Bruno refers to the "Art of Memory," a system for organizing knowledge in the mind.</note> can never be properly exhausted.*
*But now, for the sake of the triple light famous above all,*
*We take up three, the use of which runs far ahead of the others.*
*The order of nature prefers the second,*
*For first the voice moves out from the depth of the chest*
*And, gradually thinned, is finished in the narrow mouth;*
*The consonant is formed from the lips to the tooth, the tongue, and the palate.*

<header>THE BOOK ON MEASURE.</header>

# THE BOOK ON MEASURE.

**THE THIRD MOTHER, the elements in the order of instituting nature.**

*Let the Third Mother be most suitable for our study,*
*By which, the more it is extended, the rarer the erasure* <note>original: *litura*; in Bruno’s Art, this refers to a blot or an error in the shifting of characters within his memory wheels.</note> *becomes—*
*Indeed, having reached the outermost place. Let whatever is closest to it*
*By an equal condition also be most near;*
*Its convenience will assist you for the sake of brevity,*
*Unless varied shifts should cause erasures to occur,*
*Or unless it be by some other condition perceived by sight.*

### The Slanting <note>original: *obliquatio*; refers to the shifting or rotating of letters within the mnemonic grid.</note> of this Mother and Others.

*Therefore, first arrange the elements into three classes;*
*And let them be arranged with such varying intervals*
*That they are accustomed to return more or less rarely.*
*We shall mark one as the Archetype:* <note>The "Archetype" is the primary sequence from which all other variations are derived.</note>
*Then each person will be able to fashion a thousand variations for himself.*
*Let a certain distance also sometimes vary the terms,* <note>original: *voces*; referring to the mnemonic words or sounds generated by the table.</note>
*Although you see them placed here in a continuous order.*

<meta>This page transitions from the celestial mapping of the previous pages into the structural "Mothers" (foundational grids) of Giordano Bruno's combinatorial system. <page-num>218</page-num>
<header>GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA, ON MEASUREMENT, BOOK III.</header>

One "Mother" <note>In Bruno’s system, a "Mother" refers to a primary grid or wheel used to combine ideas.</note> from the remaining foreign ones, assigning the order from the titles of the Prince <note>original: *Titulis principis*; this refers to the primary category-headers that govern this specific grid.</note>.

> *From there the foreign mothers are born by law,*
> *So that for me, the order from the titles and name of the prince*
> *Has distinguished the triangle sent before from the front in the base.* <note>The "triangle" (trigonum) likely refers to the geometric way letters are combined or read within the grid to form "triadic" logic.</note>

[This page is blank and contains no manuscript text.]

<note>This page appears to be a flyleaf or endpaper. In historical bookbinding, blank sheets were often placed at the beginning or end of a volume to protect the written contents from the wear of the heavy wooden or leather covers.</note>

<note>The red staining or coloring noted on the right margin is a remnant of "edge staining." This was a common practice where the edges of the closed book were painted—not just for decoration, but to protect the paper from dust and to indicate the quality of the binding.</note>

<note>A modern watermark from the "Digitized by Google" project is visible in the bottom right corner, a digital addition from when the physical manuscript was scanned for archival purposes.</note>

<summary>This page is a blank endpaper or flyleaf, serving as a protective barrier at the conclusion of the alphabetical index of theologians.</summary>
<keywords>blank page, flyleaf, endpaper, bookbinding, edge staining, manuscript preservation</keywords>

--- Page 268 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of blank endpapers or flyleaves following the alphabetical index of theologians. It matches the physical state of the previous leaf.</meta>

[This page is blank and contains no manuscript text.]

<note>This page is a flyleaf, a protective blank sheet typically found at the very beginning or end of a bound volume. It serves as a buffer to protect the primary text from the friction of the covers and the elements.</note>

<note>The small, reddish-brown spots visible on the paper are called "foxing." This term refers to the age-related spots caused by mold or the oxidation of iron particles within the paper pulp over centuries of storage.</note>

<note>The visible grain and texture of the paper are characteristic of historical handmade papermaking, where fibers were pressed into a mold, leaving behind a physical record of the tools used to create the sheet.</note>

<summary>This page is a blank flyleaf or endpaper, showing signs of natural aging such as foxing and paper grain, but containing no written text or illustrations.</summary>
<keywords>blank page, flyleaf, endpaper, foxing, paper texture, manuscript preservation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of blank flyleaves at the end of the volume, following the previous blank leaf. It serves as a physical buffer at the back of the bound manuscript.</meta>

[This page is blank and contains no manuscript text.]

<note>Like the previous page, this is a flyleaf—a protective sheet of paper that separates the written manuscript from the heavy outer binding. These pages often show the most wear and tear because they act as the first line of defense against dust and friction.</note>

<note>The texture of the paper is visible here, showing the horizontal and vertical lines (often called "chain lines" and "laid lines") from the wire sieve used in the manual papermaking process. This indicates the paper was crafted by hand before being bound into this volume.</note>

<note>The presence of several blank pages at the end of a volume was common in historical bookbinding, providing space for later owners to add their own notes, or simply to ensure the main text was well-cushioned within the covers.</note>

<summary>This page is a blank flyleaf or endpaper, containing no text or illustrations, and serves as a protective layer for the bound manuscript.</summary>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page describes the exterior of the volume—the leather binding itself. It follows the final blank flyleaves, representing the physical closure of the manuscript.</meta>

->**JESUS, SAVIOR OF MANKIND**<-
<note>original: "IHS"</note>

<note>The central image on this cover is the emblem of the Society of Jesus, a Catholic religious order also known as the Jesuits. The letters **IHS** are a <term>Christogram<note>A monogram or combination of letters that forms an abbreviation for the name of Jesus Christ.</note></term>. While it originates from the first three letters of Jesus' name in Greek, the Jesuits often interpreted it in Latin as *Iesus Hominum Salvator*, which translates to "Jesus, Savior of Mankind."</note>

<note>The artwork is highly symbolic: a cross rises from the horizontal bar of the letter 'H,' and below the letters are three nails, representing the nails used in the Crucifixion. The entire symbol is enclosed within a "glory" or sunburst, signifying divinity and the light of God.</note>

<note>This cover is a prime example of historical bookbinding. It features dark leather decorated with "gold tooling"—a process where heated brass stamps were used to press gold leaf into the leather. In addition to the central Jesuit emblem, the corners are adorned with golden floral patterns, which served both as decoration and as a sign of the book's value and importance.</note>

<summary>This page describes the ornate leather binding of the manuscript, featuring the gold-stamped emblem of the Jesuit order (the IHS Christogram) and decorative floral corner stamps.</summary>
<keywords>Jesuit emblem, IHS, Christogram, gold tooling, bookbinding, Society of Jesus, religious iconography</keywords>
